# Introduction: Kant’s and Hegel’s Philosophy in the History of Metaphysics

## 1. Situating Their Own Philosophy in the History of Philosophy in Both Thinkers

In the formation of his own philosophy, the history of philosophy cannot be a matter of indifference to the philosopher, whether he sets himself apart from his predecessors or takes up their approaches. Although the history of philosophy has only relatively recently been recognized as a necessary discipline of philosophy, philosophers have often sought to relate their own philosophy to preceding philosophies and to determine it over against them. It has been not only customary but also always advantageous to clarify one’s own standpoint with reference to earlier philosophical doctrines and thereby also to explain wherein the merits of one’s own doctrine lie. Through this historical mediation, understanding of one’s own philosophy is also facilitated. For this reason it will also be helpful for the present study, which attempts to interpret Hegel’s logic from Kant’s *Critique of Pure Reason*, to examine and compare both philosophers’ historico-philosophical self-assessment.

### 1.1. Kant’s Determination of the Historical Place of His Own Critical Philosophy

In the *Critique of Pure Reason* Kant assigns a systematic place to the “History of Pure Reason,” with which he concludes his *Critique of Pure Reason*. We can gauge Kant’s self-assessment of the achievements he is just completing from the fact that he compares “the whole of previous treatments” of pure reason—thus his critique—with a building “in ruins” (A852/B880). This comparison makes clear that Kant ascribes to his critique not only a destructive but also a constructive function. His building of critique, with whose architectonic he is just concerned, is erected beside ruins, i.e., beside a mass of errant philosophical doctrines. But where one might have expected a presentation of the history of philosophy, Kant replaces this with a provisional, unhistorical sketch of the most important ideas that have occasioned revolutions in the history of reason (A853/B881). Thereby he indicates what place his own critical philosophy intends to occupy in the history of pure reason, and indeed in three respects, which Kant determines as (1) object, (2) origin, and (3) method of cognitions of reason. We can summarize the philosophical currents—both ancient and modern—that Kant here points out into two tendencies: the empirical and the rationalist.[^i1]

In particular, in treating the methodological, he names as those who represent scientific methods two philosophers important to him: Christian Wolff and David Hume. After he has mockingly criticized the naturalists, who apply the common way of thinking or experience to the investigation of nature, he then divides the scientists into dogmatic and skeptical: Wolff, in whose aftereffect and school Kant completed his philosophical training, is said to be the dogmatic scientist, and Hume, who “first interrupted [his] dogmatic slumber” (AA IV, 260), the skeptical scientist. And he then concludes by demarcating himself thus: that for us alone “[t]he critical path” still remains open (A856/B884).

This last section of the *Critique of Pure Reason* was mainly appended “in order to designate a place that remains in the system and must be filled in the future” (A852/B880). The only indicated presentation of the history of reason remains, as he puts it, fleeting in this last section. Moreover, it does not allow us to recognize precisely his picture of the position of his own philosophy in this history. And it seems that this “place” in his system remained unfilled altogether.[^i2] Instead, Kant develops more fully, in a writing that he composed not for purely systematic reasons but on account of an external occasion, his historico-philosophical conception and also determines more clearly the place that, within this conception, belongs to his own critical philosophy in the history of philosophy. What is meant is the writing *On the Prize Question Set by the Royal Academy of Sciences at Berlin for the Year 1791: What Are the Real Advances that Metaphysics Has Made in Germany since the Time of Leibniz and Wolff?*[^i3]

In the introduction to this writing, which attempts to present the historical course of philosophy from Wolffian metaphysics to Kant’s own critical philosophy as a “progress,” he divides the progress of metaphysics into three stages: 1. “the stage of dogmatism,” 2. “that of skepticism,” and 3. “that of the criticism of pure reason” (AA XX, 264).

He now first traces dogmatism back to the pre-Socratic period, yet beside this he also applies this concept to more recent philosophers such as Leibniz and Wolff,[^i4] who are said to have followed the following train of thought: Without knowing that theoretical reason cannot reach the supersensible, they crossed the boundary into this sphere with the means of ontological principles, which however could claim validity only in the domain of appearances and not beyond it. For supersensible objects this meant that experience was unable to refute them. In this ontological manner Wolff and Leibniz convinced themselves of the correctness of their doctrines concerning the supersensible (AA XX, 262). The path of thought of the second stage, that of the skeptic, for which Kant here adduces no example, was by contrast marked by doubt concerning dogmatism’s crossing-over. But the “retreat that destroys all further designs” (AA XX, 263), to which the skeptic holds fast, attains in the sense of sustained doubt only an unavoidable “standstill” (AA XX, 273). Over against both, dogmatism and skepticism, criticism must then thirdly clear for itself a secure path to the supersensible by entrusting itself to its own critique of practical reason.

With this presentation of the paths of thought found in the history of philosophy—not really chronological, but reconstructive—Kant attempts to answer the prize question of the Prussian Academy cited above and at the same time to defend himself against the dogmatic anti-Kantians then dominant in the Academy.[^i5]

In Kant one finds different formulations of this three-stage development, yet he always holds to this framework. It is noteworthy that Kant presents his own critical achievement as connected with skepticism. The first stage, that of dogmatism, is for Kant merely the object of his critique. He writes: “[O]ne only had to take good care not to let any contradiction with oneself enter into one’s judgments, which can also be done quite well, even though these judgments, and the concepts underlying them, may otherwise be quite empty” (AA XX, 262). Here Kant characterizes the dogmatist as a philosopher who regards the logical principle of non-contradiction as a sufficient methodological basis of cognition. For Kant, however, this logic is not sufficient for the cognition of objects.

The second stage of metaphysics is, as was noted, constituted by skepticism. But the reach of skepticism extends according to Kant only to the theoretical non-cognition of supersensible objects, i.e., God, freedom, and immortality, “in which reason takes a practical interest” (AA XX, 263). But it must not extend itself to experience (ibid.). This restriction is to rest on the achievement of the *Critique of Pure Reason*. For we can, as the Transcendental Aesthetic and Analytic establish, have secure cognition of sensible objects, whereas those supersensible objects, as the Transcendental Dialectic shows, are not cognizable, but are only to be presupposed as regulative concepts. This shows how Kant more precisely conceived the parallels that he draws between skepticism and his negative achievement in the Dialectic of the *Critique of Pure Reason*.[^i6]

To the “*Critique of Pure Reason*” is ascribed its own, thus third, step (AA XX, 263f.). Its achievement consists in “determining the extent, the content, and the limits of the same [of human cognition—T. O.]” (AA XX, 264). Kant had already earlier given a definition of metaphysics, if only a provisional one; he determined it as “the science” whose task it is “to progress by reason from the cognition of the sensible to that of the supersensible” (AA XX, 260). But the progress of German metaphysics at which the prize question aimed expresses itself in preventing the crossing of the boundary to the supersensible by human reason, for it is initially quite proper to reason itself, uncritically, to want to have cognition not only of the sensible but also of the supersensible. For Kant the achievement of his *Critique of Pure Reason* consisted essentially in the secure grounding of this drawing of boundaries. But what still remains to be noted here is that Kant, when he then engages with the inadequacy of that provisional definition of metaphysics, emphatically points to the “absolutely necessary practical use of reason” (AA XX, 261). Metaphysics “according to the concept of the school,” as “system of pure theoretical philosophy” (ibid.), contains of itself “no practical doctrines of pure reason” (ibid.), yet nevertheless prescribes to the practical use of reason its principles. Yet the critical limitation of the capacity of theoretical reason leads to it, as will be seen more precisely below, that Kant must necessarily confront the investigation of the practical path to the supersensible.

At a later place Kant goes into “the division of the stages of pure reason.” He holds that one is to regard 1. “the doctrine of science as a secure progress,” 2. “the doctrine of doubt as a standstill,” and 3. “the doctrine of wisdom as a crossing-over to the final end of metaphysics” (AA XX, 273).

Immediately thereafter he characterizes the first stage as “theoretical-dogmatic doctrine,” the second as “skeptical discipline,” and the third as “practical-dogmatic [discipline]” (ibid.). These designations can be related to the Doctrine of Method of the *Critique of Pure Reason*. There the doctrine is ascribed the positive contribution to formation, whereas the achievement of discipline is only negative (A709/B737f.). Discipline, i.e., that of reason, negatively determines the limit of cognition and is moreover distinguished from the canon of the same reason, which makes possible the correct use of the cognitive faculties (A797/B824f.). It departs from the determinations in the *Critique of Pure Reason* when the third “practical-dogmatic” stage is here called discipline, not “canon” as in the *Critique of Pure Reason* (A797/B825).[^i7]

With these divisions Kant attempts to exhibit the parallelism of his own achievements with the history of philosophy: The first coincides with his elaboration of the Transcendental Aesthetic and Analytic of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, the second with that of the Transcendental Dialectic, and the third with that of the *Critique of Practical Reason*, especially its Dialectic, which clarifies the objective possibility of supersensible objects.

From this it follows that Kant sees his own merit in the history of philosophy not only in the negative achievement of his Transcendental Dialectic—in particular, through his doctrine of the antinomies, having curbed the presumption of former metaphysics to cognize supersensible objects—but also emphasizes as his positive achievement that he has worked out a secure path of crossing-over to the supersensible, i.e., of metaphysics—through the moral postulates:

> “In theoretical respect we come not in the least nearer to conviction of the existence of God, the existence of the highest good, and the imminence of a future life, through the strongest exertions of reason, for into the nature of supersensible objects there is for us no insight at all. In practical respect, however, we make these objects ourselves, just as we judge the idea of them to be helpful to the final end of our pure reason […].” (AA XX, 299)

It is noteworthy that Kant also finds a correspondence of his three stages of the history of philosophy in the disciplines of former metaphysics. He reduces the first stage or dogmatism to ontology; the second is said to be “transcendental or pure cosmology,” which comprises rational physics and rational psychology; and the third he finds in theology (AA XX, 281). This does not seem convincing with regard to the *Critique of Pure Reason*, because the Transcendental Dialectic comprises three domains of former metaphysics—namely not only cosmology and psychology, but also theology—and his practical rescue of the metaphysical objects also relates to all three as special metaphysics.[^i8] But this is explained to some extent by the fact that, as concerns the second stage, Kant refers mainly to his doctrine of the antinomies, which deals with cosmological problems (AA XX, 288ff.).

### 1.2. Hegel’s Situating of His Own Philosophy in the History of Philosophy

It is Hegel’s achievement to have established the history of philosophy as a discipline of philosophy.[^i9] We have seen, on the basis of the Kantian version of the history of philosophy in the Progress essay, that Kant parallelizes his critical achievement with the course of the progress of metaphysics. Thus Kantian philosophy includes dogmatism (but as critically modified) and skepticism. Such a procedure with respect to past philosophical doctrines is still clearer in Hegel: “The history of philosophy exhibits in the philosophies that appear different partly only one philosophy at different stages of formation, partly that the particular principles, of which one lay at the basis of a system, are only branches of one and the same whole” (Enc. 1830, § 13; cf. Enc. 1817, § 8). His philosophy appears not only after the preceding philosophical doctrines, but must also include them. In the manuscript of the lecture on the history of philosophy he formulates:

> “Thus philosophy is system in development, thus also is the history of philosophy, and this is the main point,—the fundamental concept that this treatment of this history will present.” (GW XVIII, 48)

One must not, however, understand this sentence to mean that the presentation of the history of philosophy in Hegel is at the same time that of his own system, and that the systematic sequence of subdivisions is at the same time that of the history of philosophy, although he sometimes makes such concluding statements.[^i10] In the execution of the system, however, he clearly distinguishes the development of the concept from that in temporal sequence.[^i11] But we now wish to go not into the lectures on the history of philosophy, but into the historico-philosophical presentation of the “three attitudes of thought toward objectivity” in the “Preliminary Concept” of the Encyclopedia.

Kant’s historico-philosophical situating of his own philosophy is thoroughly comparable with Hegel’s presentation of the three attitudes of thought toward objectivity, even if the latter here does not place his own philosophy into this threefold division, but by means of these three “attitudes,” insofar as they form a whole in their connection, introduces into his own system. In the first attitude of thought toward objectivity Hegel makes dogmatic, former metaphysics the theme. Although by it the whole of philosophy before Kant—with the exception of empiricism—is supposed to be meant, it becomes clear in the presentation of this metaphysics, which is divided into four parts, that he aims especially at Wolffian school metaphysics, and one may in that respect proceed from a correspondence with the Kantian first stage of the history of philosophy.[^i12] This first attitude is characterized in Hegel by what he calls “the unsuspecting procedure” (Enc. 1830, § 26), which does not yet doubt the agreement of thinking with the object.[^i13]

This first attitude consists according to Hegel in the belief that the determinations of thought, without the critique of the cognitive faculties, count as one and the same with the determinations of things. It is “the mere understanding’s view of the objects of reason” (Enc. 1830, § 27/Enc. 1817, § 18). The attitude is, however, Hegel says, “higher than the later critical philosophizing” (Enc. 1830, § 28/Enc. 1817, § 19), insofar as it holds fast to that identity. Nevertheless Hegel criticizes its logical comportment toward metaphysical themes. This metaphysics attempts to describe the Absolute by attaching predicates to it as subject, without investigating whether this attaching comportment is appropriate to the Absolute (Enc. 1830, § 28/Enc. 1817, § 19). For Hegel the reason that this attitude became dogmatic lies in the presupposition that “of two opposed assertions […], the one must be true, but the other false” (Enc. 1830, § 32/Enc. 1817, § 21)—according to the principle of contradiction, the first principle of general logic. Hegel carries this critique further in each of the four domains of metaphysics, in ontology, psychology, cosmology, and theology. Especially in ontology he criticizes that in this kind of metaphysics untruth depends on contradiction: “If truth were thus nothing further than the lack of contradiction, then with every concept one would first have to consider whether it did not for itself contain such an inner contradiction” (Enc. 1830, § 33 Anm.). Hegel sees, like Kant, the inadequacy of former metaphysics in the fact that formal logic determines cognition in it. Hegel praises it on the one hand—now differently from Kant—in that it lays at the foundation the identity of determinations of thought and determinations of being. But the determinations of thought that are at the same time determinations of being are for Hegel no longer an object of formal logic, but of speculative logic.

Criticism together with empiricism constitutes for Hegel the second attitude of thought toward objectivity. Both agree according to Hegel—over against former metaphysics—in “taking experience for the sole ground of cognitions” (Enc. 1830, § 40/Enc. 1817, § 27). But their difference consists in the fact that these cognitions in criticism count as those of appearances, not of truths (ibid.). Empiricism, which seeks truth in experience, falls according to Hegel into a contradiction. For it still makes use of the formalia, such as categories or logical rules, which it cannot find in experience and which rather belong to metaphysics (Enc. 1830, § 38), and it uses them “in a wholly uncritical and unconscious manner” (Enc. 1830, § 38 Anm.). For this reason it is necessary that empiricism become Humean skepticism.[^i14] But this empiricism become skepticism has accomplished a great achievement, namely having found in perception two distinct elements: “the one the for-itself isolated, infinitely manifold material, the other the form, the determinations of universality and necessity” (Enc. 1830, § 39). On the basis of this distinction Hume had ascribed the form to habit. That Hume’s philosophy contributed much to the emergence of critical philosophy is a well-known insight.[^i15] But it is to be emphasized that Hegel sees this achievement especially in the distinction between the manifold material and the form of the universal and necessary, which is of great import especially for the present study.[^i16] Hegel thus sees in Hume a preparation of critical philosophy in that he takes up the material of cognition—as empiricism had already done—from experience, but ascribes the form of cognition, i.e., the determinations of objectivity, to the subject—yet then, differently from Kant later, only to mere habit. That is for Hegel a reclaiming of the unity of determinations of thought and of being that was lost in empiricism, which nevertheless remains limited by the fact that this unity first concerns not things in themselves but only appearance, and indeed second not its material but only its form.

Hegel then carries further his critical presentation of the Kantian doctrine—through a passage through Kant’s three Critiques. What interests us for the present is only the domain of theoretical reason. Hegel sees two sides of the achievements of the Kantian critique of reason: The one side, which can be ascribed to the Transcendental Analytic, consists in having found the source of the production of categories, which constitute the determinations of the object, in subjectivity.[^i17] But Hegel criticizes in this that objectivity has thereby fallen into mere subjectivity (Enc. 1830, § 46 Anm.).[^i18] The other side, which springs from the Transcendental Dialectic, is for Hegel more important (ibid.). Especially that Kant in the doctrine of the antinomies arrives at the cognition of the necessity of contradiction is to be regarded as one “of the most important and deepest advances of the philosophy of recent times” (Enc. 1830, § 48 Anm.), although Hegel does not accept Kant’s resolution of the antinomies. Moreover Hegel asserts that the antinomies are to be found not only in the four oppositions set up by Kant, but also “in all objects of all kinds, in all representations, concepts, and ideas” (ibid.). Precisely in this point consists “the dialectical moment” (Enc. 1830, § 81).

That Hegel here fixes the philosophical significance of Kant especially on the Transcendental Dialectic seems to support the parallelism between Kant’s three stages of the progress of metaphysics and Hegel’s three attitudes toward objectivity. But in situating his own philosophy Hegel distinguishes himself clearly from Kant. For the third attitude of thought toward objectivity is represented in Hegel not by his own system, but by Jacobi’s immediate knowing. Hegel’s speculative system is supposed to stand outside and above all three attitudes.

Hegel added to the Preliminary Concept of the encyclopedic logic the later famous section on the “three sides of the logical.” These three sides of the logical seem better to correspond to the Kantian characterizations of the three stages of the history of philosophy, apart from the fact that in Kant the third is not practical but only theoretical. The first “abstract and understanding” side of the logical in Hegel, which “remains standing by fixed determinateness and distinctness […] against others” (Enc. 1830, § 80/Enc. 1817, § 14), can be set parallel to pre-Kantian dogmatism. The second “dialectical or negatively rational” side of the logical is characterized by “the proper self-sublating of such finite determinations” and constitutes “skepticism” (Enc. 1830, § 81/Enc. 1817, § 15). The third “speculative or positively rational” side can then be regarded as the formation of the logical specific to Hegel (Enc. 1830, § 82/Enc. 1817, § 16). One can nonetheless also find in this the analogy that Hegel’s logic contains all three sides of the logical as moments, just as the Kantian critique comprised the second skeptical and the third practical-dogmatic side. But the difference consists further in the fact that every logical concept in Hegel is supposed to contain all three sides (Enc. 1830, § 79 Anm.).

## 2. Logic and Metaphysics

### 2.1. Hegel’s Concept of Logic

Hegel’s *Science of Logic* stands out from the history of logic in that it also claims to be an ontology. He says especially of objective logic:

> “Objective logic thereby rather takes the place of former metaphysics, which was the scientific edifice concerning the world that was supposed to be erected only through thoughts. — If we take into consideration the last shape of the formation of this science, then first immediately ontology, in whose place objective logic steps,—the part of that metaphysics that was supposed to investigate the nature of the *ens* in general,—the *ens* comprises both being and essence within itself, for which difference our language has fortunately preserved the different expression.” (GW XXI, 48f./XI, 32)[^i19]

That by “former metaphysics” and “the last shape of the formation of this science” Hegel means Wolffian school metaphysics is very probable, after we have above considered the “three attitudes of thought toward objectivity” in the Encyclopedia.[^i20] This system or, as Hegel here calls it, “the scientific edifice” was supposed to be “erected only through thoughts.”[^i21] It becomes clear from the context of the text that with this assessment he contrasts this system with Kantian criticism, which holds fast to consciousness, i.e., to the opposition of I and object. “Objective logic,” which has to do with the two kinds of *ens*, namely both with being and with essence, takes the place of the ontology of school metaphysics, which did not know this difference of the *ens*.

On the other hand, Kant announces in the sketch of his system to be found in the “Architectonic of the *Critique of Pure Reason*” a fourfold system of the metaphysics of nature, whose basis is well recognizable in school metaphysics: The first part is constituted by ontology; in the second part there follows, instead of rational psychology, rational physiology, which nevertheless comprises this psychology and rational physics; the third part is constituted by rational cosmology and the fourth by rational theology (A846/B874). This division of the metaphysics of nature follows, apart from the small but conspicuous deviation in the second part, exactly that of traditional metaphysics. Kant also calls ontology “transcendental philosophy”; this considers “only the understanding and reason itself in a system of all concepts and principles that relate to objects in general, without assuming objects that would be given (*Ontologia*)” (A845/B873). What this ontology means and what Kant intends with it is disputed in the literature. For it seems thereby not to mean the *Critique of Pure Reason*, to which Kant ascribes only a function as “propaedeutic (preliminary exercise)” (A841/B869) and which is in that respect distinguished from the metaphysics in which ontology is supposed to be contained.[^i22] If we take this architectonic as the measure, we must say that Kant never carried out this conception and nowhere wrote his ontology.[^i23] But one must concede that, in view of this problematic situation, Kant himself adopts a thoroughly wavering attitude when he says:

> “[T]he proud name of an ontology, which presumptuously claims to give in a systematic doctrine synthetic cognitions a priori of things in general (e.g. the principle of causality), must give way to the modest one of a mere analytic of the pure understanding.” (A247/B303)

It is thereby to be noted that the position of the Transcendental Analytic in Kant over against previous ontology is wholly analogous to that of objective logic over against the same in Hegel. The problem will not be solved here whether Kant in his system conception conceived transcendental logic as ontology. But it is hardly to be supposed that Kant intended to establish an ontology that would go beyond the achievement of the critique. In that respect we already find in the *Critique of Pure Reason* the principles with which we can interpret Kantian ontology.[^i24] Thereby we have arrived at the insight important for us that both, the Kantian Transcendental Analytic and Hegelian objective logic, claim to occupy the renewed place of that older ontology.[^i25]

It is still to be noted that Hegel says at a place somewhat preceding the last quotation:

> “What has here been called objective logic would in part correspond to what in him [Kant] is transcendental logic.” (GW XXI, 47/XI, 31)

Precisely for this reason objective logic could claim the position of ontology. It takes the place of that ontology by taking over the task of Kantian transcendental logic.

Hegel’s dissolution of ontology into the logic of being and of essence presupposes Kant’s transcendental logic: It is Kant who made the determinations of being of earlier ontology into determinations of thought and at the same time vindicated objectivity for these. In the system of Wolffian school metaphysics logic is indeed separated from ontology, but its determinations are derived only through the application of the rules of the former. In that respect the agreement of logic and ontology is not doubted in this tradition. Kant’s achievement consists rather in the fact that he called into question that unsuspecting assumption of the agreement of logic and ontology. He denies that this formal logic is an organon for appearance. Instead of it he has grounded the new logic that is applicable to appearances, i.e., transcendental logic. Hegel says: “Kant in more recent times has set over against what has ordinarily been called logic still another, namely a transcendental logic” (GW XXI, 46f.). “General logic,” Kant now says, “abstracts […] from all content of cognition, i.e., from all relation of the same to the object” (A55/B79). In it what is at issue is only the pure formalia, which have no reference to sensibility. If this logic is understood “as the organon of a general and unrestricted use” (A63/B88), it becomes the “logic of illusion,” i.e., dialectic (A61/B85f.). Its determinations are for truth a *conditio sine qua non*, and thus cannot positively construct the content. It has value only as a canon for truth. It is thus the task of transcendental logic to give the criterion of truth. It is the science “which determined the origin, the extent, and the objective validity of such cognitions [of a priori cognitions of objects—T. O.]” (A57/B81). This transcendental logic does not give the negative condition, but the positive construction of appearance.

In the amphiboly chapter, which we wish to bring into view in the present study, precisely this relation of being to logic becomes the theme. Kant there criticizes Leibniz (or Leibniz–Wolffian school metaphysics) because he applied logic as organon of ontology. Leibniz is said according to Kant to have derived the ontological principles only from general logic. Thereby he “intellectualized” (A271/B327) the world. This strong agreement of logic and being is called into question by Kant.

Hegel praises Kant for having subjected the cognitive faculty to a critique in transcendental logic: “[I]t is an essential proposition of Kantian transcendental philosophy that concepts without intuition are empty” (GW XII, 19; cf. *KrV*, A51/B75). As concerns this connection, Hegel expresses himself, in the introductory part to the logic of the concept, headed “Of the Concept in General,” on the problem of the correspondence of the structure of his logic with that of Kant’s *Critique of Pure Reason*. Hegel takes the systematic sequence of intuition, understanding, and reason set up in the *Critique of Pure Reason* for a ladder of stages that is supposed to show the development of consciousness, quite as Hegel’s system ascends from lower stages to the higher stages of the same, as it were as a “history of self-consciousness”:

> “First the stages of feeling and of intuition are sent on ahead of the stage of the understanding; […] Now as concerns first that relation of the understanding or concept to the stages presupposed for it, it depends on which science is being treated in order to determine the form of those stages. In our science, as pure logic, these stages are being and essence.” (GW XII, 19)

Hegel here surveys, from the height of the concept which the course of the logic has just reached, the path traversed, i.e., being and essence in their completeness, and compares this course with that of Kant’s *Critique of Pure Reason*. But one must insist that this interpretation of the *Critique of Pure Reason* does not hit Kant’s proper intention. We do not, however, wish to overemphasize the inaccuracy of Hegel’s interpretation of the *Critique of Pure Reason*. What is important for us is rather that Hegel sees a structural analogy between the two. The text immediately following what was just quoted shows us Hegel’s conception of the logic of essence in this ladder-of-stages version:

> “How incomplete this ladder of stages is at first, he [Kant—T. O.] already himself indicates by the fact that as an appendix to transcendental logic or the doctrine of the understanding he still adds a treatise on the concepts of reflection;—a sphere which lies between intuition and the understanding or between being and the concept.” (GW XII, 19)

This passage is significant not only because it is—so far as I see—the only place in which Hegel mentions the amphiboly chapter, but also because it makes recognizable the connection of this chapter with the logic of essence. Hegel here emphasizes the mediating function of the amphiboly chapter; more precisely considered, he ascribes to it having erected a mediating sphere between being and concept; thus this sphere occupies a systematic place that in Hegel’s logic essence occupies. The logic of essence takes the place of the appendix to the transcendental logic of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, i.e., of the amphiboly chapter. The systematic place that the logic of essence then occupies is not exhibited from the doctrine of the concept, as whose part and appendix the logic of essence would then have to count; rather, it corresponds to its mediating function only when it receives an objective sphere of its own “between intuition and the understanding or between being and concept.”

Despite this structural parallelism of Hegel’s *Science of Logic* and Kant’s *Critique of Pure Reason*, which Hegel himself emphasizes, it is not to be forgotten that the former distinguishes itself from the latter by the fact that the former realizes its concepts, whereas the latter throughout exhibits its concepts as something subjective, from which it is impossible to “pick out” reality from themselves (GW XII, 19; cf. *KrV* A603/B631). Moreover, feeling, intuition, and representation in their concreteness, as Kant takes them as cognitive faculties, are for Hegel forms of spirit and as such not objects of pure logic. They can be treated only—even if on the basis of the logic of being, of essence, and of the concept—in another part of the system, the philosophy of spirit. In his logic Hegel works out those determinations which from his viewpoint underlie the cognitive faculties that Kant had developed in the *Critique of Pure Reason*.

In this conception of pure logic, after the logic of being the logic of essence constitutes the second step of the genesis of the concept as unity of the subjective and the objective. Hegel’s logic is not a transcendental or ‘psychological’ logic, but a logic whose concept not only realizes itself in being, but also one that grounds within itself the unity of being and thinking. Hegel by contrast designates the manner in which Kant engages with objectivity as “psychological,” and this despite Kant’s own rejection of this designation for his critical procedure. For Hegel—spoken in his own language—the grounding of the determinations of being in Kant remains just as subjective as transcendental logic in general.

Hegel’s logic takes over the task of the “deduction of the categories” from the *Critique of Pure Reason*; a task with which after Kant not a few philosophers occupied themselves, which points to an unresolvedness in Kant himself. Kant’s deduction is for Hegel not sufficient, because Kant could derive the categories only externally and “empirically” from the table of judgments.[^i26] This deduction lacks inner necessity. From the same motive Fichte had first attempted in his *Wissenschaftslehre* to deduce the categorical forms consistently from the three “principles.” Hegel has critically followed this attempt in his logic. Thus it is no wonder that the categories that Hegel treats in his logic point, both in their derivation and in their reference to being, far beyond the Kantian “categories” as concepts of the understanding.

We can now, in resuming the sentence quoted above, regard the amphiboly chapter as the source of some determinations of reflection (in a wider sense) that become thematic in Hegel in the logic of essence. In Hegel’s logic of essence we can also find the concepts of reflection that Kant treats in this chapter, or yet concepts similar to them. The Kantian concepts of reflection are formed by four pairs of concepts: “identity and diversity,” “agreement and conflict,” “the inner and the outer,” and “the determination and the determinable” (“form and matter”). The last two pairs are very easily rediscovered in Hegel’s logic. Hegel goes into the third pair, the inner and the outer, in the treatment of “essential relation,” and “form and matter” are to be found both in the chapter on “absolute ground” in the *Science of Logic* and in the chapter “Existence” in this and in the first edition of the Encyclopedia and under “Thing” in the later editions of the Encyclopedia.[^i27] For the first two pairs there are no categories of the same name in Hegelian logic, but they nevertheless fit its “determinations of reflection.” *Einerleiheit* is a German translation of the Latin word *identitas*, thus identity. A chapter on “diversity” is to be found directly in Hegel’s logic. Conflict is thematized in the framework of “opposition” and also under “contradiction.” “Agreement” is not found as a separate determination of thought in Hegel’s logic, but is, as we shall see below, included in the treatment of identity and diversity.

It is still to be noted that objective logic takes the place not only of older ontology as *metaphysica generalis*, but also as *metaphysica specialis*. Hegel says in continuation of the place already quoted (p. 19):

> “But then objective logic also comprises the remaining metaphysics insofar as this sought to grasp with the pure forms of thought the particular substrates, taken at first from representation—the soul, the world, God—and the determinations of thought constituted the essential of the manner of consideration.” (GW XXI, 49/XI, 32)

Objective logic thus also comprises *metaphysica specialis*; thus in general and in particular the whole of former metaphysics. But it does not treat those representations that constituted its objects: soul, world, and God. These are not suited for the logic, in which what is at issue is only the pure determinations of thought:

> “But logic considers these forms free from those substrates, the subjects of representation, and their nature and value in and for themselves.” (ibid.)

For this reason the objects of that *metaphysica specialis* do not appear in Hegel’s mature logic in objective (nor in subjective) logic,[^i28] but rather their forms, i.e., their predicates such as substantiality and identity.[^i29]

### 2.2. Discussion of the Characterization of Hegel’s Logic

Hegel’s relation to Kant—and in particular that of Hegel’s logic to the *Critique of Pure Reason*—is no new theme in the extensive Hegel literature,[^i30] and the logic of essence is the part of Hegel’s logic to which most researches have been devoted in this connection.[^i31] But the connection between the logic of essence and the amphiboly chapter has only rarely attracted attention in the literature. Some have indeed already drawn attention to it, but have mostly only mentioned it, indicated it,[^i32] or only partially executed it in another connection,[^i33] but have not made it comprehensively as a whole into the theme. As small, albeit immensely important exceptions, only two works can count:[^i34] Jaeschke, in his introduction to the study edition of the logic of essence, investigated its genesis within the overall development of Hegel’s logic and in doing so especially drew attention to its connection with Kant’s amphiboly chapter.[^i35]

Already some time ago Belaval, in his attempt to compare Hegel’s logic of essence with Leibniz’s philosophy, occupied himself more fully with the text of the logic of essence and pointed to “the close relation that binds the logic of essence to the amphiboly chapter.”[^i36] He says moreover: “the doctrine of essence would be transcribed into Kantian: the doctrine of the concepts of reflection.”[^i37] But he was able to treat this theme only in passing, for his main theme was the relation between Leibniz and Hegel.

There are still the researches to be mentioned that deal with the characterization of Hegel’s logic in general. If Hegel’s logic, as we established in the introduction, intends to replace Kant’s transcendental logic, which itself attempts to replace traditional metaphysics or in particular ontology, then the characterization of Hegel’s logic must take place in this historico-philosophical context. In the literature there prevails not only a great diversity of opinion, but also disagreement about what is to be understood at all by ontology or metaphysics:

Horstmann interprets in his book *Ontology and Relation* Hegel’s logic as a relational ontology and thereby distinguishes, however, two concepts of ontology.[^i38] Ontology is said to be either categorical-analytic or a doctrine of what there truly is. He now understands Hegel’s logic as ontology in the second sense.[^i39] For ontology in the first sense, he thinks, can give no answer to the question of the status of relations. “For where concepts, and with them relations, are of concern only insofar as they can be conceived as fundamental determinations of beings, the question of what concepts, or more specifically what relations themselves are, has no immediately evident function.”[^i40] As an example Horstmann gives the substance–accident relation, which underlies the subject–predicate relation and which is therefore especially suited for the defense of Hegel against Russell’s critique that he actually intends.[^i41] He establishes that Hegel declares this relation untenable. But he concludes from this that this relation plays no ontological role in Hegel.[^i42]

Horstmann does not, however, deny Hegel’s logic its relational-ontological character, but only this determinate metaphysical kind of relation, when he says “that he [Hegel] conceives the monistic substrate favored in his own ontological theory—that, therefore, which according to this theory alone really exists—itself as relation, namely as a specific kind of self-relation.”[^i43] Horstmann thus advances the thesis that Hegel’s logic is an ontology of relation and indeed of self-relation, in that Hegel criticizes substance metaphysics or ontology, which is based on that logical subject–predicate relation. According to Horstmann, Hegel’s assertion, which he calls the “irrelevance of relation,” is not incompatible with the “relational-ontological monism of subjectivity.”[^i44] His presentation of Hegelian ontology concentrates on settling the apparent contradiction between Hegel’s thesis concerning the ontological irrelevance of relation and his conception of relational ontology. But despite this plausible posing of the problem, an analysis of the logic of essence is lacking in him, although he needed it, as it seems to me, for his conception of the interpretation of the logic.[^i45]

A far more decisive inadequacy of Horstmann’s interpretation of the logic springs from his already described version of Hegel’s ontology. Because he restricts it to the doctrine of what is real, he draws from this the consequence that only the “Idea” is ontologically relevant. For the same reason he rejects Hartmann’s categorical-ontological version of the logic, because then only the Idea could count as a category.[^i46] Behind this assertion hides the assumption that only the Idea in Hegel’s logic is in a position to fulfill the claim to truth. The asserted ontological irrelevance of relation is therefore also a consequence of this assumption: The substance–accident relation is therefore ontologically irrelevant in Hegel because this relation is declared untrue in the logic of essence. This assumption at first appears irrefutable when we read that Hegel says of the absolute Idea: “It is the sole object and content of philosophy” (GW XII, 236). But Horstmann misjudges the significance of this claim to truth of the Idea when he passes over the following passage: “In that it [the Idea—T. O.] contains all determinateness within itself, and its essence is this, to return to itself through its self-determination or particularization, it has various shapes, and the business of philosophy is to cognize it in these” (ibid.). The (absolute) Idea in Hegel’s logic does not stand for itself alone, but represents nothing other than the whole process of the preceding logical course that the logic has traversed. This course indeed also contained untruth. But Hegel’s monistic system must, as he makes clear for example in the introduction to the logic of the concept by the example of the Spinozistic substance-metaphysical system,[^i47] itself contain within itself the standpoints that it condemns as untrue. And not only this: It must even unfold their refutation out of itself.[^i48] Theunissen’s thesis that Hegel’s logic is both critique and at the same time presentation of metaphysics is therefore to be supported despite the sharp criticism of his interpretations of individual textual passages from Hegel’s logic.[^i49] Truth, separated from semblance (*Schein*), can fulfill no claim to truth. Critique of semblance is itself truth. A truth free of semblance is, as we shall investigate more precisely below, not tenable in Hegel.[^i50]

If truth is not reduced to the Idea in the narrow sense, the possibility opens of nonetheless understanding Hegel’s logic categorically-ontologically. Horstmann’s characterization of Hegel’s logic mentioned above is in fact directed especially against Hartmann’s categorical-ontological interpretation. The latter first sketched it introducingly in his essay “The Ontological Option” and further executed it in his posthumously published book on Hegel’s logic.[^i51] But he designates his categorical-ontological interpretation as “non-metaphysical.”[^i52] For he understands by metaphysics historically former (school) metaphysics or in principle a science that has the claim to posit existence.[^i53] Schick repeats almost the same objection as Horstmann against Hartmann’s categorical-ontological interpretation. In doing so she forgoes the designation ‘ontology’ for Hegel’s logic; instead she designates it as ‘metaphysics,’ which both, similarly to ontology in Horstmann, answers the question what is real,[^i54] and—now similarly to metaphysics in Hartmann—is existence-positing. For Schick Hegel’s logic has the intention of unifying logic and metaphysics in this sense; she condemns this project, however, as failed. This interpretation, which is directed against the categorical-ontological interpretation, understands ‘categorical’ only in a narrow Kantian sense and thereby too subjectivistically, against which Hartmann in turn turns, who understands by categories “concepts whose ‘grasp’ succeeds as unity of being and concept.”[^i55] Moreover one must say that this interpretation underestimates the Kantian categories and their claim to objectivity, on which Hegel too properly wants to rely.

Fulda gives an interpretation of Hegel’s logic in which it is presented as a more consistent critique of metaphysics than Kant’s transcendental philosophy.[^i56] According to Fulda, however, Hegel’s speculative philosophy is no ontology at all,[^i57] but still metaphysics: Hegel’s logic is “metaphysics without ontology.”[^i58] He understands by ontology especially the ontology of the Wolffian school, as science of the being in general or of its predicates, and criticizes especially Horstmann’s version of ontology as science of what is real. Thus he also rejects Horstmann’s interpretation of Hegel’s logic as ontology of relations.[^i59]

Fulda attempts in this connection moreover to refute three other types of interpretation of Hegel’s logic. These refutations are, however, especially for the relational-ontological and monistic conception of Hegel’s logic too hasty, for they precisely do not lead to “understanding Hegel’s logic throughout as an attempt to restore ontology against the intentions of Kantian critique.”[^i60] Hegel’s carrying-further of the Kantian critique of school ontology consists now precisely in dissolving substance in the monistic version of ontology into self-relation, since substance metaphysics, as our investigation of the logic of essence will sufficiently show, cannot fulfill the monistic claim.[^i61]

Problematic is also still another thesis of Fulda’s, namely that Hegel’s “ratification” of the Kantian critique of metaphysics is possible only in his theory of consciousness. For this Hegel’s own statement is supposed to speak, that Kant remained standing at subjectivity. One cannot deny that Hegel’s fundamental distancing from Kant’s transcendentalism becomes clear in statements such as this: “For the real progress of philosophy, however, it was necessary that the interest of thinking be drawn to the consideration of the formal side, of the I, of consciousness as such, i.e., of the abstract relation of a subjective knowing to the object, that the cognition of the infinite form, i.e., of the concept, was in this way introduced” (GW XXI, 48). Kant indeed stands in the series of modern philosophers for whom the subject was decisive; one must not, however, ignore that Hegel sees the Kantian achievement precisely in having, according to intention, procured objectivity for the subjective determinations of thought. Precisely in this point Hegel wanted to connect his logic to Kantian transcendental logic.[^i62] In this sense its task lies in really securing the objectivity of the determinations of thought in a manner other than Kant’s. “In order, however, to reach this cognition, that finite determinateness in which the form is as I, consciousness, still had to be stripped off. The form so thought out into its purity then contains it within itself to determine itself, i.e., to give itself content, and indeed the same in its necessity,—as system of the determinations of thought” (ibid.).

To this path Kant gave only a beginning, as Hegel says: “This philosophy also made the beginning of letting reason present its determinations out of itself. But the subjective bearing of this attempt did not allow it to come to completion” (GW XI, 19/XXI, 31). In this completion consists the task of Hegel’s logic. The beginning for it has, however, not come to unfolding in Kant owing to his conception of subjectivity. Its completion is reached only when this subjective bearing is for its part overcome.

Henrich in turn determines Hegel’s logic once as a doctrine of categories, but asserts at the same time, with a view to the Kantian “concepts of reflection,” that Hegel’s logic is more than a doctrine of categories, since it develops not “general characters of the real,” but only “our reference to objects.”[^i63] But this understanding misses the conception of the logic of essence, whose categories or, if one wishes to avoid this word, whose concepts indeed claim to count as objective. That relational determinations appear in the logic of essence springs precisely from Hegel’s conviction that relations, not substances, constitute objects. It is an expression of Hartmann’s insight into this object-character of relations when he finds in Hegel’s logic a corresponding “homogeneity” of the determinations. Here “then quite different things appear like categories of independents (e.g. something, substance, subject), categories of the dependent or inherent (e.g. property, magnitude, content and form, cause and effect), as well as structure- or principle-categories (e.g. being, determinate being, being-for-itself, essence, concept) in a sequence that links them.”[^i64]

There is, however, although they are developed through the necessity of a systematic sequence, a further, newly conceived heterogeneity of the relational determinations of ‘things’ over against Kantian transcendental philosophy: the determinations divide into those of being, of essence, and of the concept. The systematic derivation of the essence-logical character of “shining” already implies relationality and relativity. But our reconstruction of the logical course of the doctrine of essence on the basis of the most important essence-logical categories will show us that this logical course at the same time makes possible the overcoming of this relationality through the transition of external relation into self-relation.

This transition into self-relation is carried out by Hegel as critique of metaphysics. Hegel thereby summarizes the task of objective logic as follows, which recalls the critical formulations concerning the history of metaphysics in the three attitudes of thought toward objectivity in the Encyclopedia: Former metaphysics “therefore incurred the just reproach of having used them [the determinations of thought—T. O.] without critique, without the prior investigation whether and how they are capable of being determinations of the thing-in-itself, according to Kantian expression,—or rather of the rational. — Objective logic is therefore the true critique of the same,—a critique that considers them not according to the abstract form of apriority against the a posteriori, but themselves in their particular content” (GW XI, 32/XXI, 49). It is the aim of this study to follow this “true critique of metaphysics” on the basis of the logic of essence. In doing so it will, as already set out, refer especially to the amphiboly chapter of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, in which Kant presents his own critique of former metaphysics. The historico-philosophical situating of their own philosophies by Kant and Hegel themselves, which we investigated in the first section of this introduction, already served this aim preparatorily, insofar as we now wish to proceed from the amphiboly chapter in order to interpret Hegel’s temporally later logic of essence. Our task is thus, through this investigation, 1. to determine the reach of the critique of metaphysics in Kant and 2. to lay open Hegel’s position over against this. Only when we have clarified the connection between the amphiboly chapter and the logic of essence can we gain a clear picture of the position of Hegelian logic in the history of metaphysics.

This study divides into three parts. The first part attempts to clarify the systematic and historico-philosophical significance of the Kantian amphiboly chapter starting from the text of this chapter itself (Chapter 1) and looking back to the metaphysics of Leibniz, Wolff, and Baumgarten (Chapter 2); then I attempt in the second part, as preparation for the investigation of Hegel’s treatment of the Kantian “concepts of reflection,” to exhibit the connection between these and Hegel’s determinations of reflection through the developmental history of the logic of essence in Hegel’s logic (Chapter 3) and the systematic preconditions of this treatment in the *Science of Logic* (Chapter 4); finally we shall in the third part also present this connection in systematic respect mainly on the basis of the doctrine of essence of the *Science of Logic* (Chapters 5 and 6).

---------------

# Part I. Kant’s “Concepts of Reflection” and Their Prehistory

## First Chapter: Kant’s Presentation of the Concepts of Reflection and His Critique of Leibniz–Wolffian Metaphysics in the “Appendix: On the Amphiboly of Concepts of Reflection” in the *Critique of Pure Reason*

### Preliminary Remark: Problems Surrounding the Amphiboly Chapter

In the chapter “Appendix: On the Amphiboly of Concepts of Reflection through the Confusion of the Empirical Use of the Understanding with the Transcendental” (briefly: the amphiboly chapter) in the *Critique of Pure Reason* one finds, as we have already sketched in the Introduction, important indications for reconstructing Kant’s self-assessment or situating of his own philosophy in the history of philosophy, insofar as he there criticizes Leibniz and also Leibniz–Wolffian school metaphysics. The task of this Kantian chapter consists in “setting the ‘critical ontology’ apart from the previous, traditional ontology.”[^k1.1] Engagement with this chapter is also indispensable for situating Hegel’s logic of essence in the history of metaphysics, since it counts as “one of the germ-cells of Hegelian ‘logic,’” especially of the logic of essence.[^k1.2]

At the same time this chapter is the one that, compared with the other parts of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, has been relatively rarely taken into account by Kant interpreters. Cohen treats the amphiboly chapter in *Kants Theorie der Erfahrung*, his monumental overall presentation of the Kantian work, only in passing in connection with the epistemic-critical problematic of form, just as Paton in his commentary on the *Critique of Pure Reason* goes into it only with reference to the problem of the completeness of the table of categories.[^k1.3] Kemp Smith subjects the chapter to a somewhat more detailed investigation, but doubts its systematic appropriateness.[^k1.4] By contrast Heidegger thoroughly emphasizes its importance,[^k1.5] but he mentions it neither in his Kant book, which contains a metaphysical interpretation of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, nor in his lecture on the *Critique of Pure Reason*.[^k1.6]

It is understandable, however, that this chapter greatly interested Kant’s contemporaries and his direct successors, above all because the discussion of the advantages and disadvantages of critical philosophy over against Leibniz–Wolffian “school metaphysics” was still virulent.[^k1.7] Reinhold calls this chapter, in *Versuch einer neuen Theorie des menschlichen Vorstellungsvermögens*, one of the “most beautiful doctrines of the *Critique of Pure Reason*,” which “must be read there.” Yet he too forgoes a detailed commentary.[^k1.8] Maimon takes up the Kantian concepts of reflection in the *Kategorien des Aristoteles* as well as in *Versuch einer neuen Logik oder Theorie des Denkens*, especially with a view to the formation of judgments, but ultimately distances himself thereby from Kant.[^k1.9] That the chapter was present in the discussion of criticism in the nineties of the eighteenth century can be read off from the relevant title of a book that appeared anonymously in 1795: *Darstellung der Amphibolie der Reflexionsbegriffe nebst dem Versuche einer Widerlegung der Hauptmomente der Einwendungen Aenesidems gegen die Reinholdsche Elementarphilosophie*.[^k1.10]

In the twentieth century the amphiboly chapter was at least made the theme of their dissertations by a few. Behn attempts, like Knauer somewhat earlier, to structure and formulate this chapter better than Kant himself had done. Döring gives a historical investigation of the individual concepts of reflection.[^k1.11] More recently Broecken and Reuter presented detailed studies of the amphiboly chapter.[^k1.12]

Quite in contrast to that praise which Reinhold paid the chapter, there prevails in more recent Kant literature the common tone of lamenting the difficulty or obscurity of this chapter.[^k1.13] Already a first problem consists in the inner structure of this text, which makes understanding difficult. It consists of four parts, of which the first two are divided by headings and the two following only by asterisks.[^k1.14] The last part (A290–292/B346–349) thematizes the concepts “Something” and “Nothing,” which actually have nothing directly to do with the concepts of reflection; it is introduced only because it “might seem required for the completeness of the system” (A290/B346).[^k1.15] Consequently we can leave this part out of account in the framework of the present study. The first part (A260–268/B316–324) contains the main text of the chapter; the second part (A268–A280/B324–B336), which is headed “Remark on the Amphiboly of Concepts of Reflection,” gives further explanations of the individual concepts of reflection. The third, untitled part (A280–289/B336–346) seems to contain a further general remark on the concepts of reflection.

At first glance one might think that Kant, in these three parts, repeatedly presents the same content. Probably for this reason it has been claimed in the literature that Kant did not write this chapter in one stretch, but rather patched the chapter together from several texts that stemmed from different times.[^k1.16] But we shall see below that the organization of this chapter nevertheless follows a certain plan, although the project connected with it does not quite succeed for Kant.

Second, it is not unambiguous what the appendix is supposed to serve as an appendix to. Does it belong to the third main section of the second book of the Analytic: “On the Ground of the Distinction of All Objects in General into Phenomena and Noumena,” to the second book: “The Analytic of Principles,” or to the whole “Transcendental Analytic”?[^k1.17] Willaschek asserts the last of these on the basis of the parallelism of the structures of the Analytic and the Dialectic. For the chapters “On the Regulative Use of the Ideas of Pure Reason” (A642–668/B670–696) and “On the Final Aim of the Natural Dialectic of Human Reason” (A642/B670) are unambiguously called “Appendix to the Transcendental Dialectic.”[^k1.18] In terms of content he grounds his claim that this chapter as a whole belongs to the Analytic by the fact that it has a common content with all three main sections of the second book, namely that it relates to the formation of judgments, the principles of the pure understanding, and ontology.[^k1.19] But his characterization of the three main sections of the Transcendental Analytic and their contentual commonality with the amphiboly chapter leaves more questions than answers.[^k1.20]

To the problem of the systematic assignment of the chapter there correspond, moreover, contentual difficulties: For a chapter whose aim is to criticize former metaphysics and to steer its erroneousness seems thereby better suited to the critique of illusion of the Transcendental Dialectic. For this reason Kemp Smith asserts that this chapter systematically has a false place.[^k1.21] Broecken further emphasizes the analogy between the amphiboly chapter and the Transcendental Dialectic, especially the transcendental paralogisms.[^k1.22]

But despite these interpretations much speaks for the fact that the chapter has nevertheless received its appropriate place in the Analytic, for first Kant, in constructing the structure of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, follows the organization of former metaphysics: This divides first into *metaphysica generalis*, which constitutes ontology, and into *metaphysica specialis*, which consists of the three parts *psychologia*, *cosmologia*, and *theologia naturalis*. Just as the three parts of the Dialectic correspond to those of *metaphysica specialis*, so the Analytic is determined to take the place of ontology. Presupposing this as correct, it is convincing that the amphiboly chapter, which contests the truth of the ontology of Leibniz–Wolffian school metaphysics, belongs to the Analytic.[^k1.23] This connection of the Analytic with ontology is also attested by the concluding words of Reflexion 5552, where there is talk of the “Schluss der ontologie” (AA XVIII, 219); so it also becomes understandable why this Reflexion can count as an almost direct preliminary study of the amphiboly chapter. Referring to this, Adickes and Broecken hold that the chapter systematically occupies the right place.[^k1.24] Graubner sees, by contrast, that the method of the amphiboly chapter belongs to the Dialectic, but concludes from this that it has nevertheless found its correct systematic place, namely the “cut-point between Transcendental Analytic and Transcendental Dialectic.”[^k1.25]

Second, the *Critique of Pure Reason* in its construction follows the structure of general logic. The Transcendental Dialectic, however, follows from the necessity of inferences of reason. If the erroneousness in dealing with ontology that is treated in the amphiboly chapter did not derive from inferences of reason, then the chapter could not belong to the Dialectic. Whether this erroneousness belongs to reason as the faculty of inferring or is proper to the understanding as the faculty of the concept and of judgment, and whether this error is a “natural and unavoidable illusion” (A298/B354), as the dialectical one is, we shall have to investigate more precisely in what follows.[^k1.26]

A further problem, which stands in a close relation to the second, is that of the time of composition of this chapter. It has often been ascribed to the pre-critical period, because some assertions do not seem to do justice to the critical standpoint. That conjecture that Kant composed the chapter from texts written at different times would support this.[^k1.27] What is at issue here above all is the assertion that the ontological principles that derive from formal logic are not valid for appearances, but yet for objects of the pure understanding. This position seems to fit more that of the Dissertation of 1770 than that of the *Critique of Pure Reason*.[^k1.28]

Erdmann reacts with astonishment to the fact that Kant in the amphiboly chapter departs from the contents of the main text of the Analytic, namely that he here now appeals to an intellectual intuition, which leads Erdmann to the conjecture that Kant inserted this chapter later.[^k1.29] Vaihinger is likewise of the opinion that Kant, “for the sake of ‘popularity’ […] without many scruples,”[^k1.30] took this text from an earlier time into the *Critique*. Kemp Smith asserts, in noticeable agreement with this,[^k1.31] that the parts of this chapter were written separately and only later composed. He attempts—following this line—also to determine more precisely the time of composition of some passages of the text. The section A277–8/B333–4, in which the *substantia phaenomenon* is mentioned, he ascribes to an earlier time than the preceding and the following sections of this passage. By contrast the section A285–9/B342–6 is said to have been written later, because in it the results of the chapter on Phenomena and Noumena are taken into account.[^k1.32] Zilsel too is of the opinion that the amphiboly chapter is composed of texts with different times of composition; he presupposes, however, that each individual part has its own unity, and he holds those three parts to be differing ‘elaborations of the amphiboly.’ Further he attempts to date the composition of each individual part and to ground it in terms of content. He dates the “main text” (A260–268/B316–324) of this chapter to the year 1771 and holds the other parts for later written.[^k1.33] As the most important grounds that speak for this dating of the first part he names especially the following two: (1) This text can have been written only after Kant’s Dissertation of 1770, because only there are space and time determined as forms of intuition. (2) Yet the “main text” also cannot have been composed later than February 1772, because Kant, in presenting the concepts of reflection, does not follow his table of categories, and on the basis of his letter to Markus Herz of 21 February 1772 it is established that he had already developed the table of categories by this time. From this Zilsel concludes that the first part of the chapter must already have been written before 1772.

Yet Zilsel’s claim that Kant in the said main part of the amphiboly chapter does not yet refer to the table of categories is untenable. Admittedly one finds no exact correspondences of the concepts of reflection with individual categories—and the number of the concepts of reflection also does not agree with that of the categories. But this is by no means yet a proof that, as he claims, “Kant cannot have conceived the amphiboly according to the table of categories.”[^k1.34] For first it is easy to see that, as we shall investigate more precisely below, the conceptual pair of identity and diversity corresponds to quantity, that of agreement and conflict to quality, that of the inner and the outer to relation, and that of matter and form to modality. Second, Zilsel’s assumption contradicts Kant’s own statement in the *Prolegomena* of having derived the concepts of reflection from the table of categories.[^k1.35] The concepts of reflection do not correspond to all categories, but the connection of the two is unmistakable.[^k1.36]

In what follows I attempt to show that the amphiboly chapter can nevertheless be understood systematically consistently from the critical standpoint, and that precisely therein lies the weakness of Kantian transcendental idealism to which his successors, especially Hegel, drew attention in order to overcome it.

### 1. Transcendental Reflection and Transcendental Topic

The heading “On the Amphiboly of Concepts of Reflection through the Confusion of the Empirical Use of the Understanding with the Transcendental” (A260/B317)—already makes recognizable that what is at issue are two modes of the use of the understanding, where it is initially a matter of clarifying their difference: Kant already went into this difference in the chapter on “Phenomena and Noumena” immediately preceding this appendix:

> “The transcendental use of a concept in any principle is this: that it is related to things in general and in themselves, but the empirical use, when it is related merely to appearances, i.e., objects of a possible experience.” (A238f./B298)

The one use of the understanding is thus related to appearance, and the other to things in themselves; the latter, however, is strictly speaking impossible for Kant. The task of the amphiboly chapter is to distinguish these two modes of the use of the understanding precisely and to prevent the errors that their confusion produces. The concern of the amphiboly chapter is to take up this distinction, which was introduced in the Phenomena–Noumena chapter, and to steer the consequences of its non-observance. To “transcendental deliberation or reflection” falls according to Kant the task of carrying further the said critique of the use of the understanding with reference to the sources of cognition.

Kant distinguishes transcendental reflection at the beginning of this chapter from logical reflection.[^k1.37] He first ascribes to reflection in general a faculty of comparing. Logical reflection goes to the mere comparison of concepts, which concerns only the logical forms and therefore does not relate to the content of the concepts. All judging presupposes logical reflection, because the concepts can first be connected or not connected as a consequence of this comparing. In the universal judgment, e.g., the identity of the concepts can first be discovered through this comparison; the same holds in the particular judgment for diversity, in the affirmative for agreement, and in the negative for conflict (A262/B317f.).[^k1.38]

But Kant in this chapter is not really concerned with logical, but with transcendental reflection. This reflection likewise compares representations, but not in order thereby to guide the formation of judgments, but in order to determine to which cognitive faculty the given representations belong, to the senses or to the understanding, and what relations of given representations result therefrom. This determining reflection is called ‘transcendental’ because it no longer has to do directly with objects, but precisely with the cognitive faculties sensibility and understanding;[^k1.39] the expression ‘transcendental’ is thus used here in a different sense from that of the ‘transcendental’ use of the understanding. With reference to the use of the understanding the word ‘transcendental’ means that the understanding is applied ‘without regard to the sensible condition of cognition.’[^k1.40]

The difference between transcendental and logical reflection is analogous to that between transcendental and general logic (A55ff./B79ff.): In transcendental reflection the relatedness to the content of the concepts is emphasized (A262/B318), whereas logical reflection has to do only with the logical forms.[^k1.41] But this is not supposed to mean that transcendental reflection can determine the content of the concepts; here the analogy to transcendental logic should be taken seriously, which for its part was also not in a position to give itself the contents. Already at the beginning of the amphiboly chapter Kant says:

> “Deliberation (*reflexio*) has to do not with objects themselves in order to get concepts from them directly, but is rather the state of mind in which we first prepare ourselves to find out the subjective conditions under which we can arrive at concepts.” (A260/B316)

Although the specific designation ‘transcendental’ is not named here, by ‘deliberation (*reflexio*)’ transcendental reflection must be meant here, because Kant more closely determines this reflection as ‘the consciousness of the relation of given representations to our various sources of cognition’ (ibid.).[^k1.42]

Moreover, however, transcendental reflection must be distinguished from the other transcendental faculties that were treated in the main text of the Transcendental Analytic; this holds in particular for the demarcation of transcendental reflection from the transcendental power of judgment, which as the determining power of judgment[^k1.43] determines—constructively or regulatively—phenomenal objects. By contrast transcendental reflection investigates only “in which cognitive faculty they [the representations—T. O.] belong to one another subjectively” (A261/B317).[^k1.44]

But this faculty of transcendental reflection is also different from that of the reflecting power of judgment, which seeks for a given particular an unknown universal. For transcendental reflection the general genera are given under which every representation is to be subsumed, namely the objects of the pure understanding or those of sensible experience. Thus this reflection occupies a peculiar place between the determining and the reflecting power of judgment.

In the second part of this chapter, the “Remark on the Amphiboly of Concepts of Reflection,” Kant works out by means of this reflection “the transcendental topic”; he thus determines “the transcendental places,” i.e., he indicates whether the relations of the representations among one another belong in each case to the senses or to the understanding. The result of such a determination through transcendental reflection proves itself therefore as a topic, because against the confusion of pure concepts of the understanding with appearance it can be systematically indicated how separated places (*topoi*), precisely sensibility or understanding, result for the comparison of representations—precisely in the application of transcendental reflection. But this transcendental topic is of course immediately distinguished from that logical one which goes back to Aristotle (A268f./B324f.). In the latter a place, namely a logical place, is said to be assigned to every concept under which some cognitions can be subsumed, and it is thus said to be suited for the schoolteacher and orator, without being able thereby to engage investigatively at all with the truth of the concepts and cognitions. Over against this logical topic only two places are enumerated for transcendental reflection, and these exclusively against one another, namely the understanding and sensibility. In terms of content transcendental reflection contains those four conceptual pairs which Kant had already previously introduced in the appendix as concepts of reflection, and operates with them.

The importance of transcendental reflection for transcendental logic is unmistakable: “This transcendental deliberation is a duty from which no one can exempt himself if he wants to judge a priori something about things” (A263/B319). Thereby, however, the question arises why this “deliberation” is treated only in an appendix, if yet, as now turns out, transcendental reflection has a foundational significance for Kantian transcendental philosophy. If we did not know the presented difference of representations, it seems, we could not attain a true cognition, which Kant wanted to secure through the Transcendental Analytic. Transcendental reflection and the topic corresponding to it may moreover be regarded as means of the *Critique of Pure Reason* as a whole, in order with precisely this means to criticize former metaphysics, to undermine its ground of cognition, and over against this to place cognition on a firm foundation.[^k1.45] The chapter would become an Achilles’ heel of Kantian transcendental philosophy if the Kantian doctrine, in treating the amphibolous, became entangled in some kind of unclarity.

### 2. *Dictum de omni et nullo* and Critique of Leibniz

A further field of problems opens up when Kant criticizes Leibniz for also having committed a logical error. This error is said to consist in the inversion of the *dictum de omni et nullo*. This dictum properly says: “[W]hat belongs universally to a concept, or contradicts it, also belongs to, or contradicts, everything particular that is contained under that concept” (A280–1/B337).[^k1.46] This dictum properly consists of two statements: (1) All determinations that belong to the general concept also belong to the particular concept that is contained under that concept (*dictum de omni*) and (2) The marks that contradict those of the general concept also contradict those of the particular concept that is subsumed under that general one (*dictum de nullo*). But Leibniz according to Kant commits the error of taking the inversion of the *dictum de omni* for correct: “What is not contained in a general concept is also not contained in the particulars that stand under the same” (ibid.). This sentence must not be confused with the *dictum de nullo*, with the sentence: “[W]hat contradicts a concept universally also contradicts everything particular that is contained under that concept.” For that a mark “is not contained in a general concept” does not yet mean that the mark contradicts the concept. “Black-haired” is not contained in the general concept “human being,” but it does not contradict it. Some human beings can yet be black-haired. Leibniz is now said according to Kant to have supported his system on a sentence that is already false in formal-logical terms. The particular concept is first that which has more than a *differentia specifica*, i.e., more determinations than the general that contains it within itself. But already the sentence that counts as the inversion of the *dictum de omni et nullo* violated this logical definition.

From this it results that the logical erroneousness that is attacked by transcendental reflection or topic is differently situated from that which underlies the Transcendental Dialectic. What is at issue here is to lay open a logical, not a transcendental source of error; correspondingly what is at issue here is a direct inference, not an indirect inference or syllogism. Kant is of the opinion that Leibniz makes the error with respect to every pair of the concepts of reflection. This connection becomes understandable only when one presupposes that Kant here determines the difference between the thing as appearance and the concept of the same thing as that difference which—on the basis of the connection of the thing itself with its concept—results between its particular concept and its general one. He formulates in the Jäsche Logic the principle of categorical inferences of reason, from which the *dictum de omni et nullo* should easily be deducible, thus: “*nota notae est nota rei ipsius; repugnans notae, repugnat rei ipsi*.”[^k1.47]
