From this it first becomes clear that Kant assumes that the principles of appearance, which he contrasts by means of the concepts of reflection with the intellectual principles of the noumena, do not contradict these. They are not contained in the concepts, but they also do not violate these logical principles. Even if Kant’s conception of real opposition, e.g., seems to contradict the principle of contradiction, it is strictly speaking only a new determination that belongs to the being of appearance. Second, the assumption that the existent is particularized to a stronger degree than its concept seems to stand against his famous assertion in the “Transcendental Ideal” that being does not increase the determinations of the concept: Being is no real predicate (A598/B626). The determinations that belong to appearance are for Kant not being itself. Yet Kant must then have assumed that although being itself does not increase the content of the concept, yet through being some determinations are indirectly added to the concept. But to take being as a particular and the concept as a universal does not seem tenable. For then the concept in its universality would lose its reference to being, as Kant himself demonstrates by means of the concept of God.[^k1.48]

If Kant nevertheless insists on having found the error of Leibnizian ontology in the inversion of the *dictum de omni*, it is clear that he here does not aim with his critique of inferences at the syllogisms, but that what concerns him is the immediate inference, which he elsewhere calls “inference of the understanding” (AA IX, 116).[^k1.49] The sentence quoted above illustrates in the Jäsche Logic the principle of categorical inferences (AA IX, 123); but it itself, as the inference from the universal to the particular, is a direct inference (AA IX, 116). Insofar as this belongs to the understanding, it can well be grounded why the amphiboly chapter still belongs to the Analytic, since in this precisely the understanding is thematized. Moreover it has become clear that Kant traces the difficulties that result as a consequence of the inverted inference in the *dictum de omni et nullo* back to a clear logical error; i.e., he holds this erroneousness for avoidable, which stands in contrast to the dialectical errors that reason unavoidably commits. In that respect the place that Kant has assigned to the amphiboly chapter is nevertheless appropriate to the system-structure of the *Critique of Pure Reason*.[^k1.50]

The demarcation from the Dialectic and the assignment to the logic of the understanding, however, illuminates not only the systematic position of the amphiboly chapter as a whole, but also its inner structure and its hard-to-grasp consistency, which is responsible for those misunderstandings that we critically investigated above. Despite the seeming confusion each of its three parts has its own function. Kant has presented transcendental reflection and thereby at the same time the errors of Leibnizian ontology under three aspects: In the first part, the main text, transcendental reflection is generally compared with logical reflection; in the second part, the remark on transcendental reflection, the transcendental topic is especially presented; the third, untitled part concerns itself with the inversion of the *dictum de omni et nullo*. For Kant the grounds of Leibniz’s errors lie first in the fact that in his conception of ontology he was unable to develop a transcendental reflection, second in the fact that correspondingly he could not succeed in working out a transcendental topic; the first lack is properly the same as the second, even if with reference to the second lack the systematic difference between sensibility and understanding is emphasized. Third Kant criticizes Leibniz for having committed the logical error of falsely relying on the invertibility of the *dictum de omni et nullo*.

In the literature it was discussed wherein the emphasis of the amphiboly chapter lies. Thereby it is unmistakable that at least one aim of this chapter constitutes the critique of the ontology of the Leibniz–Wolffian school. Its principal error lies in the fact that it lacks the distinctions of the transcendental topic. It understands sensibility only as a confused mode of representation and does not see the difference of the two sources of cognition. Thereby Leibniz according to Kant “intellectualized” appearance, whereas Locke “sensified” the concepts of the understanding (A271/B327). Already in the Transcendental Aesthetic Kant expressed himself on this:

> “The Leibniz–Wolffian philosophy has therefore assigned a wholly wrong point of view to all investigations into the nature and origin of our cognitions, in that it regarded the difference of sensibility from the intellectual merely as logical, whereas it is obviously transcendental, and concerns not merely the form of distinctness or indistinctness, but the origin and content of the same, so that we through the former do not cognize the constitution of things in themselves not merely indistinctly, but not at all, and, as soon as we take away our subjective constitution, the represented object with the properties that sensible intuition ascribed to it is nowhere to be met with at all, nor can be met with, since precisely this subjective constitution determines the form of the same as appearance.” (A44/B62f.)

This passage shows what confusion according to the Kantian view has unnoticed befallen Leibniz.[^k1.51] Now the ontological principles from which these fundamental errors obviously stem are subjected to a critique in the amphiboly chapter.

### 3. Presentation of the Concepts of Reflection and Critique of Ontology

Reflection, both transcendental and logical, solves its tasks, as has already been mentioned, with four pairs of concepts of reflection. They are divided according to the table of categories[^k1.52] into: 1. identity and diversity, 2. agreement and conflict, 3. the inner and the outer, 4. determination and the determinable (form and matter).[^k1.53] These are also the concepts that Baumgarten treated in the *Ontologia* of his *Metaphysica*, without however distinguishing them from the categories.[^k1.54] Kant emphasizes over against this that these concepts must be distinguished from the categories (A269/B325).

If one more precisely determines the significance of transcendental reflection or of the transcendental topic by clearly distinguishing it from the transcendental deduction, then the distinctness of the concepts of reflection from the categories is also easier to understand.[^k1.55] But this distinctness Kant himself seems to have noticed relatively late, for both are treated together in the Reflexionen that can be regarded as preliminary works of the *Critique of Pure Reason*:

> “All concepts in general, from wherever they may take their material, are reflected, i.e., representations brought into the logical relation of multiple validity. But there are concepts whose whole sense is nothing other than one or another reflection to which occurring representations can be subjected; they can be called concepts of reflection (*conceptus reflectentes*), and, because every kind of reflection occurs in the judgment, they will absolutely grasp within themselves the mere action of the understanding that is applied in the judgment to the relation as grounds of the possibility of judging.” (Reflexion 5051: AA XVIII, 73)

The concepts here called concepts of reflection cannot already be equated with the later concepts of reflection. Rather they seem to be the archetypes of the categories.[^k1.56] In Reflexion 408 it is said still more clearly: “Our understanding is the faculty of reflecting, and pure concepts of the understanding (transcendental) are mere abstract [sic] concepts of reflection” (Ref. 409 in AA XV, 165f.). It is undoubted that the Reflexionen cited refer to the origins of the categories. But the understanding is also held for a faculty of reflecting, and insofar (1) the understanding as spontaneous faculty of unifying and (2) reflection as the faculty of comparing are not yet separated. But in the transcendental framework after Kant’s so-called Copernican turn the categories must be distinguished from the concepts of reflection. These relate only to the relation of representations and, in contrast to those, do not constitute determinations of the object itself.[^k1.57]

We took up in the Introduction Kant’s picture of the history of philosophy and its connection with his critical system in his drafts of the writing on the progress of metaphysics. Leibniz–Wolffian ontology, which was also subjected to critique in the amphiboly chapter, is—according to the statements of the Progress essay—to be regarded as the first stage of the progress of metaphysics, which was called the theoretical-dogmatic doctrine. Kant in fact exercises critique in that writing in several respects on Leibnizian ontology: 1. on the principle of the identity of indiscernibles, 2. on the principle of sufficient reason, 3. on the system of pre-established harmony, and 4. on monadology (AA XX, 282ff.). The content of the critique of Leibniz in this writing almost equals that of the amphiboly chapter.

In what follows we divide the conceptual pairs of transcendental reflection into two groups; this division in turn rests on the division of the categories: the first two pairs of concepts of reflection are ‘mathematical,’ and the last two ‘dynamical.’[^k1.58] This division seems appropriate not only because of its correspondence with that of the categories (B110), but also in view of the contents of these concepts, because the first two pairs have to do with ‘numbers’ and the last two with the appearances of matter, which is constructed dynamically.

Kant himself divides the amphiboly chapter according to the three kinds of errors that his critique of Leibniz–Wolffian school metaphysics finds wanting. He then exhibits these errors by means of each individual pair of the concepts of reflection: With a view 1. to transcendental reflection, 2. to the transcendental topic, and 3. to the misunderstood application of the *dictum de omni et nullo*.

#### A. The Mathematical Concepts of Reflection

##### 1) Identity and Diversity: Numerical One and Many in Space

First Kant makes clear (A262/B318f.) that identity relates to the universal judgment and diversity to the particular. Only when all concepts can be taken as identical under one mark can a universal judgment be formed; if by contrast they are held for diverse, the particular judgment results. In that respect it becomes clear that diversity relates to the category of “plurality.” The necessary connection of the concept of reflection of “identity” with the category of “unity” appears more convincing than that with the category “totality,” for “identity” has, as we shall soon see, rather to do with the self-sameness of the singular.[^k1.59]

In the first pair of concepts of reflection, “identity and diversity,” Kant takes up the predicates “*Idem* and *Diversum*” from Baumgarten’s ontology. Moreover there thereby comes under critique that famous principle of Leibniz, the *principium identitatis indiscernibilium*:

> “If an object is presented to us several times, but each time with the very same inner determinations (*qualitas et quantitas*), then it is, if it counts as an object of the pure understanding, always exactly the same, and not many, but only one thing (*numerica identitas*).” (*KrV*, A263/B319)

We here encounter Kant’s formulation of the Leibnizian principle: If several things had the same determinations—more precisely, what is meant here are the inner determinations—both according to quality and according to quantity, they would have to count as one and the same thing. There would thus be no several things that had absolutely the same inner determinations; rather every thing would have to have at least one determination that distinguished it from others.

With the second subordinate clause of the sentence in the last quotation, “if it [the object—T. O.] counts as an object of the pure understanding,” Kant gives a condition for this principle: This principle is said to hold for the objects of the pure understanding. In the following sentence Kant additionally makes clear that this principle does not also hold for appearance. Even if the inner determinations of a thing agreed wholly and completely with those of another, these things, as far as appearance is concerned, could be diverse through the places that they occupy. Space is, as the Transcendental Aesthetic shows, according to its transcendental position a form of intuition of the subject. In appearance the diversity of things can already be given through space, i.e., abstracting also from all inner determinations one can assert that two things are diverse insofar as they are located at different places in space:[^k1.60]

> “[I]f it [the object—T. O.] is however appearance, then it does not at all depend on the comparison of the concepts, but, however much everything may be identical with respect to them, the diversity of the places of this appearance at the same time is yet a sufficient ground of the numerical diversity of the object itself (of the senses)” (ibid.).

This diversity makes the distinction of the objects not only possible but necessary (A272/B328). The possible objection that Kant earlier maintained[^k1.61] would be that place is an inner determination of a thing. But he has already forestalled this objection in the Transcendental Aesthetic:

> “Space represents no property at all of any things in themselves, or [them] in their relation to one another, i.e., no determination of them that attached to objects themselves, and that would remain if one also abstracted from all subjective conditions of intuition. For neither absolute nor relative determinations can be intuited prior to the existence of the things to which they belong, hence not a priori.” (A26/B42)

According to Kant Leibniz’s principle is a result of the fact that the latter treated appearance intellectually and that he lacked a concept of the transcendental topic.[^k1.62] He is said to have applied the principle, which could be valid only for the objects of the pure understanding, also to the domain of experience.

In this principle Kant also finds that misunderstanding of the *dictum de omni et nullo*. For the *principium identitatis indiscernibilium* is according to Kant supposed to presuppose “that if a certain distinction is not encountered in the concept of a thing in general, then it is also not to be encountered in the things themselves” (A281/B337). The logical error consists in thinking that what is not contained under the universal also cannot be contained under the particular. For in the concept one precisely abstracts from the concrete. That some determination is not found in the concept thus does not exclude that it belongs to its being.

##### 2) Agreement and Conflict: Negative Magnitude and Real Opposition

Kant indicates also with the next pair of concepts of reflection that agreement has to do with the positive judgment, conflict with the negative judgment (A262/B318f.). Thus agreement is to be traced back to the category “reality” and conflict to the category “negation.” But if one considers Kant’s treatment of these concepts of reflection more precisely, what is at issue is rather two kinds of realities and two kinds of negations and thereby two kinds of their opposition. One cannot simply relate one of the two concepts of reflection in each case to one of the categories of quality. Also in what follows it becomes clear that these concepts of reflection have to do both with the categories of reality and of negation and with that of limitation.

The concepts of reflection of agreement and conflict refer first to the principle of contradiction. To its logical concept of opposition Kant here contrasts that of real opposition, which he had already attempted to introduce into philosophy in his earlier writing on negative magnitudes (1763): the *realitas noumenon* and the *realitas phaenomenon*. In the former “no conflict between the realities can be thought” (A264/B320). In the noumena there is opposition only between reality and negation, which means a logical contradiction. The *realitas noumenon* means the affirmation of a predicate; consequently only negation, negation, is opposed to it.[^k1.63]

In the phenomena by contrast the realities, which for themselves contain only positivities, can be opposed to one another. This opposition Kant in the pre-critical period called “real opposition” or “real repugnance.” It “also rests on a relation of two predicates of the very same thing against one another; but this is of a wholly different kind [from the logical]. Through one of them that is not denied which is affirmed through the other, for this is impossible, but both predicates A and B are affirmative; only since from each separately the consequences a and b would be, so through both together in one subject neither the one nor the other, thus the consequence is Zero” (AA II, 172). Null as the result of this opposition is said to be “Something (*cogitabile*)” or “*repraesentabile*,” whereas the result of logical opposition is Nothing (“*nihil negativum irrepraesentabile*”) (AA II, 171). In the amphiboly chapter Kant takes up this doctrine again with the second pair of concepts of reflection.

This real opposition is, as is to be gathered here, the opposition in which the realities “connected in one subject cancel one another’s consequences, and 3 − 3 = 0” (A265/B320). “[T]he real in appearance (*realitas phaenomenon*) [can] […] be in conflict and […] one [can] wholly or in part annihilate the consequence of the other” (A265/B321). These concepts are mathematical insofar as what is at issue thereby are the negative magnitudes that have long been known to mathematics.

Kant names here, as in the writing of 1763, as examples of real opposition “two moving forces in the same straight line, insofar as they either pull or push a point in opposed directions” and also pleasure and pain (A265/B321). In this opposition two realities are connected in one subject, but “not through the principle of contradiction” (AA II, 171).

In traditional metaphysics (and also in Kant’s “Ideal of Pure Reason”)[^k1.64] the real is so conceived that it has no opposition within itself, because it is always thought as purely positive, i.e., as the counter-concept of negation (as one also finds it in the table of categories). By contrast Kant now asserts that the realities that are for themselves positive can in appearance enter into a mutually opposed relation.

According to Kant Leibniz “did not exactly announce” the principle of logical contradiction “with the pomp of a new principle.” But he “yet made use of it for new assertions” (A273/B339). How new, one may ask. Should Leibniz, who was himself also a natural scientist, somehow not have known that forces that act on one another in opposed directions cancel one another? Yet Kant sees Leibniz’s error not in his conception of nature, but as grounded in his moral philosophy, namely in the assumption of his theodicy that evil or the bad is a result of the limitation of human creatures.

Kant exercises the same critique of Leibniz’s moral philosophy also in the Progress essay. Remarkable thereby is that he introduces the doctrine of real opposition not by means of the principle of contradiction, but by means of the principle of sufficient reason.[^k1.65] Leibniz is said to believe like Democritus that all things “were composed of reality and negation, of being and non-being” and that the ground of reality is only reality and the ground of negation only the lack of reality. (AA XX, 282) Thus he could not comprehend that a reality could also be a ground of a negation or that “the a priori cognizable consequence of this conflict of realities could be negation” (AA XX, 283)—namely real opposition. Kant traces this error of Leibniz back to the fact that he did not use the principle of sufficient reason “to go beyond the principle of analytic judgments, the principle of contradiction” (ibid.). The principle of sufficient reason could have functioned as a principle of synthetic judgments; Leibniz however is said thereby to have remained within the domain of validity of the analytic judgment.[^k1.66]

Kant ascribes the ground of this error in Leibniz to the lack of transcendental reflection and of the transcendental topic. Real opposition, which makes it possible to relate two opposing predicates to one subject, must be regarded as open contradiction if one knows only logical opposition and measures it by this standard. Here again there consists the misunderstanding of the *dictum de omni et nullo*, in other words in the assumption that opposition, because it may never occur in the concept, also does not occur in appearance (A282/B338). But in appearance still another kind of opposition is possible than the logical, even if it may not violate the principle of contradiction.

#### B. The Dynamical Concepts of Reflection

##### 3) The Inner and the Outer: Substance as “Sum of Mere Relations”

Kant gives no explanation of the concrete correspondence between these concepts of reflection and the categories, as he had done for the first two pairs of concepts of reflection. In view of his treatment of the concepts of reflection it becomes clear, however, that he equates the inner with substance, which belongs to the first category of “relation” (A80/B106). In the discussion of the concepts of reflection of the inner and outer it is striking that they relate to two different kinds of concepts of substance. Moreover one can easily see that the relation of substance with what inheres in it is conceived as inner relation and the relations of causality and of community as outer relations.

Kant thus attempts here to distinguish two concepts of substance: *substantia phaenomenon* and *substantia noumenon*. Substance was assumed in the tradition as the opposite of relation,[^k1.67] because relation is first possible through the prior existence of substances. A relation is said to consist between several substances; substance by contrast is something related in this relation or, as Kant says, “the substratum of all relational or outer determinations” (A282–283/B338–339). Kant determines the inner as *substantia noumenon* as follows:

> “In an object of the pure understanding only that is inner which has no relation at all (as to existence) to anything different from it.” (A265/B321)

It seems here that the liberation of substance from relations—in comparison with that more moderate expression of the “substratum of all relations”—is somewhat exaggerated. Yet it is to be borne in mind thereby that Kant in this connection already has Leibniz’s concept of substance before his eyes,[^k1.68] in which no relation between substances takes place. Leibnizian substance-doctrine, i.e., monadology, is according to Kant said to have arisen as follows: As substance something simple is sought, because something composite like matter is dependent on outer relation.[^k1.69] But in space one always encounters only the composite. Therefore nothing is found but the psychic states, which are the only thing that is located outside space. Thus Leibniz is said to have asserted the monad, which has no window, relates to no other, and only represents the world, as substance.[^k1.70]

How then does it stand with the *substantia phaenomenon*? Kant asserts here seemingly against the other doctrines in the Transcendental Analytic that the *substantia phaenomenon* is “wholly and completely a sum of mere relations” (A265/B321). From these relations, above all from the category of causality, Kant derives the concept of force, or of forces, through which we cognize substance in appearance. He names here attraction and repulsion or impenetrability. The substance that is made possible through these forces is, Kant says, that “which we call matter” (ibid.).[^k1.71]

In the “Refutation of Idealism” Kant had already spoken of matter as the substance in experience (B278). It was the recourse to the existence of matter in space through which Kant attempted to refute (material) idealism. But in the first Analogy of Experience he defines substance as “something permanent.”[^k1.72] Since in the “Refutation of Idealism” he found as the permanent in space matter,[^k1.73] this seems to contradict the reduction of substance to its relations inwardly and outwardly, i.e., the concept of substance in the Transcendental Analytic. But Kant here evidently sees no contradiction when he says: “What we also only know of matter are mere relations, (what we call inner determinations of the same is only comparatively inner); but among them there are independent and permanent ones, through which a determinate object is given to us” (A285/B341—emphasis T. O.).[^k1.74]

That Kant does not feel this conception of matter as a contradiction to transcendental idealism can also be seen from the *Metaphysical Foundations of Natural Science*, in which he makes the concept of matter and its construction the theme (AA IV, 472). Especially in the second main section on ‘Dynamics’ it becomes clear that Kant constructs matter by means of the forces of attraction and repulsion; this matter so understood he then treats in the amphiboly chapter, as already shown, in connection with the concepts of reflection of the inner and the outer. Through the original repulsive force, which is conceived in constant conflict with the original attractive force, matter fills space (AA IV, 499). The impenetrability of matter, which is a relative one (AA IV, 501f.), likewise stems from this opposition of forces (AA IV, 501). Attractive force is required so that matter does not extend itself into the infinite (AA IV, 508f.). Matter is then called the substance in space (AA IV, 503).[^k1.75] Kant thus conceives substance in appearance as constructed through relations or forces. It is thus only relatively (“comparatively”) inner.

Kant attempts with his determination of matter to refute still a further important doctrine of Leibniz. If one conceives the substances as matters in space, a physical influence of the substances on one another must be possible, as Kant has shown in the second Analogy of Experience. Leibniz, who forbids the physical influence of substances on one another, must by contrast demand a special instance that makes possible the agreement of the substances. “Precisely for that reason,” Kant says, “his *principium* of the possible community of substances among one another had to be a pre-established harmony, and could be no physical influence” (A274f./B331f.). In that respect the ground of their harmony must be ascribed to the third, namely God (A275/B332).[^k1.76] Thus Kant attempts with the concepts of reflection of the inner and outer to refute the two central doctrines of Leibnizian metaphysics—monadology and the doctrine of pre-established harmony.[^k1.77]

It had already become clear above that these errors are to be charged to the lack of transcendental reflection and correspondingly of transcendental topic. But this erroneousness is at the same time again that logical one which refers back to the *dictum de omni et nullo*. “[B]ecause, without an absolutely inner, no thing can be represented through mere concepts, so also in the things themselves that are contained under these concepts, and in their intuition, there is nothing outer to which something absolutely inner did not lie at the ground” (A284/B340). Leibniz again commits the error of thinking what is not contained under the universal also as not contained under the particular.

##### 4) Determination and the Determinable (Form and Matter): Form as Subjective Condition

The logical connection of the concepts of reflection ‘form’ and ‘matter’ with the modal judgments and the categories derived from them is not easy to grasp. That this fourth and last pair of concepts of reflection has to do with modality can be gathered neither directly from the table of judgments nor from the table of categories, but only the principles of modality from the ‘Postulates of Empirical Thinking’ make this clear.

Kant determines the modal predicates, possibility and actuality, in the empirical judgment through the difference of form and matter:

> “1. What agrees with the formal conditions of experience (according to intuition and concepts) is possible. 2. What is connected with the material conditions of experience (of sensation) is actual.” (A218/B265—emphasis T. O.)

Only on the basis of the determinations of both modal categories in the transcendental framework (not in the logical) can the reach of Kant’s critique of Leibniz be more precisely grasped; this shows itself especially in how Kant thereby makes use of the concepts of reflection of form and matter. Kant asserts in the Postulates of Empirical Thinking that the form of experience as pure concept, which nevertheless belongs to it, is to precede matter. For the forms of experience, such as space and time, categories, etc., spring from the subject; the matter of experience then only later joins this form. On this score it is convincing that Kant says: Matter and form are the concepts of reflection “which are laid at the ground of all other reflection” (A266/B322). Only the difference of these two concepts of reflection so determined provides the ground for the fact that a clear distinction can be made between the two kinds of use of the understanding (the empirical and the transcendental).

But according to Kant philosophy hitherto attempted to understand the relation between form and matter in the first place by means of formal logic: Matter is said to precede form just as in the logical judgment the concepts that are to constitute the subject and predicate do; for their relation, which is expressed through the copula, can only come about if subject and predicate are previously given. (A267/B323).[^k1.78] Leibniz conceives, when he takes space and time as relations of the monads, the relation of form and matter likewise so that first the substances must be given. But according to the Transcendental Aesthetic space and time may not be thought as outer determinations of things, but as subjective conditions of sensibility, which yet according to Kant hold objectively. Here matter does not precede its forms, but joins subsequently: “[…] so the form of intuition (as a subjective constitution of sensibility) precedes all matter (the sensations), hence space and time precede all appearances and all *data* of experience, and rather first make these possible” (A267/B323).

By drawing on this form of intuition one can then understand the connection of the concepts of reflection ‘form’ and ‘matter’ with the logical modalities such that the modalities relate to form as the connecting-subjectivity of the matters, in that in a sentence the copula as form connects (or separates) subject and object as matters. Modality, by the fact that it adds nothing to the content of the same, has to do only with the “value of the copula in relation to thinking” (A74/B100).[^k1.79] One cannot simply ascribe one of these concepts of reflection to a determinate modal category, but they refer overall to the modality of the transcendental judgment.

This conception of the concepts of reflection corresponds to Kant’s core thought in the *Critique of Pure Reason*, because the difference of matter and form makes it possible that Kant is able to demarcate his transcendental idealism from other idealisms, such as the Cartesian or Berkeleian. Whereas Descartes and Berkeley propagate a material idealism, Kant champions a formal idealism.[^k1.80] I.e., Kantian idealism is that which ascribes the form of experience to subjectivity; but it is at the same time an empirical realism, which neither denies nor doubts the reality of the matters.

Kant gives no thorough explanation of how Leibniz’s historically uncritical dealing with form and matter is connected with the inversion of the *dictum de omni*, or even would have arisen from it. But it is easy to see that Kant adduces as an example of the traditional logical conception of the relation of form and matter the latter as designation for the universal and the former as designation for the specific difference in the particular (A266/B322): In the *dictum de omni et nullo* and its erroneous inversion what is at issue is the general and particular concept. Through the misrecognition of the relation of the two the errors of the amphiboly arise. Thereby it is then also confirmed that the concepts of reflection of form and matter underlie the others.

In conclusion of this chapter some general results can be held fast:

1. With reference to every pair of concepts of reflection Kant asserts that Leibniz and the Leibniz–Wolffian school neglected the conditions of sensibility as a specific source of cognizing and conceived the ontological principles only according to formal logic. As Kant says at the beginning of the Transcendental Analytic, formal logic is indeed “the negative condition of all truth,” the “*conditio sine qua non*” (A59f./B84), but not its sufficient condition: It is no organon. The difference that can be recognized between the laws of the pure understanding on the one hand and sensible experience, which independently gives intuition thereto, on the other, is precisely the difference to which Kant attempts to hold fast in the amphiboly chapter and at the same time this difference serves to be able to distinguish between formal and transcendental logic.

2. Transcendental reflection and the transcendental topic Kant has introduced as a special procedure of cognizing, as a procedure that every contentual cognition is supposed to presuppose. The carrying-out of the *Critique of Pure Reason* is thereby, as has already been indicated, to be assumed as the decisive case of application of transcendental reflection itself. Transcendental epistemology is a cognition of cognition that grounds all cognition possible at all and measures out its limits. First the Transcendental Aesthetic and second the Transcendental Analytic and the Transcendental Dialectic, as subdivisions of transcendental logic, determine in which faculty of the subject which representations can occur how. Yet Kant has restricted the function of transcendental reflection in the amphiboly chapter to a special procedure that has above all to do with the comparing of representations and with their relations among one another.

3. Kant speaks in the amphiboly chapter of the for him alone possible two kinds of objects, those of empirical experience and those of the pure understanding. This distinction seems to correspond with that of noumena and phenomena which he had introduced in the last chapter preceding the amphiboly chapter. But he again distinguishes the noumena into noumena in the positive sense and in the negative sense and at the same time forbids speaking of the noumena in the positive sense: “[T]o the beings of sense there indeed correspond beings of the understanding, and there may also be beings of the understanding to which our sensible faculty of intuition has no relation at all, but our concepts of the understanding, as mere forms of thought for our sensible intuition, do not reach in the least to these; what therefore is called by us noumenon must be understood as such only in a negative signification” (B309). Elsewhere Kant says: “The division of objects into phenomena and noumena, and of the world into a world of sense and a world of understanding, can therefore not be admitted at all in a positive signification” (A255/B311; the addition “in a positive signification” was lacking in the first edition).

Confusing, however, is that Kant at some places in the amphiboly chapter seems to speak of the noumena in the positive sense. He asserts that Leibniz’s ontological principles, whose application to the world of appearance Kant rejects, nevertheless hold for the objects “of the pure understanding”:

> “If an object is presented to us several times, but each time with the very same inner determinations (*qualitas et quantitas*), then it is, if it counts as an object of the pure understanding, always exactly the same, and not many, but only one thing (*numerica identitas*).” (A263/B319)[^k1.81]

For the third pair of concepts of reflection, inner and outer, Kant formulates what was already adduced above: “In an object of the pure understanding only that is inner which has no relation at all (as to existence) to anything different from it” (A265/B321—emphasis by T. O.).[^k1.82] Yet at some places he deals more cautiously with that restriction of not speaking of the noumena in the positive sense. These places belong to the third part of the amphiboly chapter.[^k1.83]

But one can certainly not simply trace this problematic assertion of Kant’s back to the pre-Kantian period. In the *Metaphysical Foundations of Natural Science*, which was published in 1786, thus temporally between the two editions of the *Critique of Pure Reason* of 1781 and 1787, Kant reaffirms his view: Monadology indeed belongs “not to the explanation of natural appearances,” but it is “a Platonic concept of the world executed by Leibniz, correct in itself […], insofar as it is considered not at all as an object of the senses, but as a thing in itself, merely an object of the understanding, which yet lies at the ground of the appearances of the senses” (AA IV, 507—emphasis T. O.).

That is a contradiction which Kant had thus recognized at least a year before the second edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason* and also could not completely resolve in this. Moreover we must still draw attention to the fact that Kant took over the amphiboly chapter into the second edition unchanged, whereas he strongly modified other chapters, such as the transcendental deduction of the categories or the paralogism chapter. The idea of a correspondence between the order of the noumena and general logic Kant seems not to have given up.[^k1.84]

4. It remains still to be noted that Kant—differently from Hegel—in his critique of Leibniz does not go beyond the principle of contradiction, although he asserts that Leibniz should have gone beyond the principle of contradiction with the principle of reason: He remains with the analytic judgment and does not pass over to the synthetic.[^k1.85] Kant’s critique of Leibniz and of the Leibniz–Wolffian school results finally from the fact that these take general logic not as a canon but as an organon.[^k1.86] The principle of contradiction remains preserved as *conditio sine qua non*, which fundamentally, as we shall see more precisely below, distinguishes Kant from Hegel.

## Second Chapter: The Prehistory of the Kantian Concepts of Reflection in Leibniz, Wolff, and Baumgarten

That in the amphiboly chapter as a whole a proud tone of Kant concerning his achievement predominates is hardly to be overlooked: His transcendental philosophy is said to have put metaphysics, especially ontology, out of force.[^k2.1] It steps into the place of ontology, which presumptuously bore this “proud name” (A247/B303) of a doctrine of being.

Whether Kant has really reached his goal, however, still stands in question. He mostly simply names the object of his critique in this chapter “Leibniz.” But it has also very often been asserted that the doctrines that Kant criticizes in this chapter are not to be ascribed to Leibniz at all.[^k2.2] In particular the Kantian assertion is contested that Leibniz traces the difference between the understanding and sensibility only back to the degree of distinctness of the concept.[^k2.3] One can in fact also ask whether Kant really read Leibniz directly. It seems rather that he came to know Leibniz’s doctrines through the Wolffian school, especially through Baumgarten’s *Metaphysica*.[^k2.4] Moreover he himself had gone through the school of this school metaphysics and also used Baumgarten’s *Metaphysica* for his metaphysics lecture.[^k2.5] But it is also true that the most important texts of Leibniz, apart from e.g. the *Discours de métaphysique* and the correspondence with Arnauld, were accessible to Kant.[^k2.6] The *Nouveaux Essais* were published during Kant’s lifetime in 1765.[^k2.7]

It is worth the trouble in this chapter to go into the doctrine of Leibniz, Wolff, and Baumgarten. This is supposed to contribute to gaining clarity not only concerning the historico-philosophical reach of Kant’s amphiboly chapter, but also concerning that of Hegel’s logic of essence. Only then can it be made understandable to what extent Hegel in the transformation of metaphysics still went beyond Kant.[^k2.8]

### 1. Logic and Ontology: Two Principles and Their Ontological Connection

The Kantian critique of Leibniz’s ontology, or that of the Wolffian school, consists, as seen, in the fact that this takes general logic not as *conditio sine qua non*, but as constitutive for being (as appearance), i.e., not as a canon but as an organon, and derives the ontological principles from the logical rules. This has to do with the important difference between the analytic and the synthetic judgment that Kant introduces. In the former judgment the principle of contradiction, the highest principle of general logic, gives a sufficient ground for the truth of the sentence, but not in the latter.[^k2.9] That the principle of contradiction with reference to experience does not give a sufficient ground for a true sentence is, insofar as the empirical sentence or experience itself is synthetic, a view of Kant’s that does not first stem from his treatment of the second pair of concepts of reflection, ‘agreement and conflict,’ but rather constitutes the core assertion of the whole amphiboly chapter and of transcendental logic in general. To this it fits that Kant in the later drafts on the progress of metaphysics laments the lack of this distinction for the first stage of the progress of metaphysics already traced above, to which he reckons the ontology of Leibniz and of the Wolffian school.[^k2.10] The errors of ontology that remained undiscovered in their time are precisely ascribed to the fact that metaphysics did not work out the described distinction, which according to Kant led to regarding general logic as constitutive, i.e., as an organon.[^k2.11]

This Kantian understanding of Leibnizian philosophy has unmistakable commonalities with the works on Leibniz’s system from the beginning of the last century. It was the books of Russell and Couturat,[^k2.12] which together with the work of Cassirer[^k2.13] inaugurated the Leibniz renaissance. Russell and Couturat both attempted to ascribe to logic the decisive role in Leibniz’s metaphysics. Couturat’s famous thesis, “Leibniz’s metaphysics rests wholly and completely on his logic,”[^k2.14] seems wholly to accommodate the Kantian understanding of Leibnizian philosophy, although he based his Leibniz research on newly appeared and newly edited writings that Kant could not have known at all. According to Couturat the principle of reason is also a principle of analytic truth; the principle is said namely to mean “that in every true statement the predicate is contained in the subject.”[^k2.15] Leibnizian optimism is accordingly “an intellectualist and speculative” one.[^k2.16] Russell, who based his work on the writings now edited by Gerhardt, comes to a similar result. He criticizes Leibniz for reducing all sentences to subject–predicate sentences and allowing only analytic sentences apart from existential sentences. This viewpoint, which has since become dominant, is however contested by more recent Leibniz researchers such as e.g. Ishiguro and Fichant.[^k2.17] We cannot here go into this discussion in all detail. But the investigations of the Leibnizian texts themselves will show us that the Russell–Couturat and at the same time Kantian conception of Leibnizian philosophy is mistaken and rather hits only the Leibnizianism modified by Wolff and Baumgarten.

#### 1.1. Logic and Ontology in Leibniz

The principle of contradiction and that of sufficient reason are for Leibniz “two fundamental principles of our use of reason” (*Theod*. I, § 44).[^k2.18] First we investigate Leibniz’s understanding of the first-named principle.

It is according to Leibniz a condition of possibility in general: “*Tout ce qui implique contradiction est impossible, et tout ce qui n’implique point contradiction est possible*” (*Theod*. II, § 173, G VI, 217).[^k2.19] This “sun-clear maxim” (ibid.)[^k2.20] counts as unshakable, for otherwise every arbitrary sentence would be possible, which would overturn reason altogether.[^k2.21] In the *Monadology* Leibniz formulates the principle of contradiction as the principle “*en vertu duquel nous jugeons faux, ce qui en enveloppe, et vray ce qui est opposé ou contradictoire au faux*” (*Monad*. § 31); in the *Theodicy* as the principle “according to which of two opposed assertions the one must be true, the other false” (*Theod*. I, § 44).[^k2.22] Despite the difference of these formulations the principle always gives a criterion of possibility and impossibility. And insofar as it determines impossibility, the principle also contains the determination of necessity: If a sentence contains a contradiction, the sentence is false or impossible. The (contradictory) opposite of the sentence is then true and necessary. From the impossibility of a sentence there results the necessity of its opposite. This necessity Leibniz calls “logical,” “geometrical,” or “metaphysical” necessity. From this he distinguishes the “moral,” which is only necessary “*ex hypothesi*”[^k2.23] and insofar contingent.[^k2.24] In order to distinguish these two necessities, especially in the *Discours de métaphysique* for “necessary” in the second, moral sense the word “certain (*certain, asseuré*)” is used.[^k2.25] Moreover the first necessity is also called “absolute” necessity, the second, which concerns the will and is insofar conditioned (*conditionel*),[^k2.26] the “hypothetical” (G IV, 380 and *Theod*. I, § 37).

This distinction of the two different kinds of necessity Leibniz introduces especially in order to meet the critique of fatalism:
> “*Mais il semble que par là la difference des verités contingentes et necessaires sera détruite, que la liberté humaine n’aura plus aucun lieu, et qu’une fatalité absolue regnera sur toutes nos actions aussi bien que sur tout le reste des evenemens du monde. A quoy je réponds, qu’il faut faire distinction entre ce qui est certain, et ce qui est necessaires: tout le monde demeure d’accord que les futurs contingens sont asseurés, puisque Dieu les prevoit, mais on n’avoue pas pour cela, qu’ils soyent necessaire. Mais (dirat-on) si quelque conclusion se peut deduire infalliblement d’une definition ou notion, elle sera necessaire.*” (*Disc. metaph*. § 13—italics by T. O.)

For Leibniz those who designate his doctrine as fatalistic neglect to distinguish these two kinds of necessity (certainty and necessity). They know only the concept of necessity in the first sense, of which the opposite is impossible. But the necessity of God’s choice for this world among the many possible worlds does not consist in this necessity, but in moral necessity. God must have chosen the best world, because as all-knowing he must have known that this world is the best. But that does not mean that the other worlds must be (logically) impossible. It is according to Leibniz unimaginable that God would have chosen another, worse world if he yet knew the best. In that respect God acted freely, but he must have willed the best.

The same also holds for human freedom. The deeds of human beings are determined through moral necessity, but not through absolute necessity. In that respect they are necessary, but yet free. Yet freedom in human beings is not determined directly through the principle of the best, because their cognitive capacity is limited. But even if they know what is best, they must moreover still will to do it. At several places he expresses this state of affairs with the well-known expression “*incliner sans necessiter*.”[^k2.27]

The distinction of metaphysical necessity, which finds its expression in the principle of contradiction, from the moral, which stands above it, explains the insufficiency of the principle for the contingency of things and relations. According to Leibniz this distinction of necessities coincides with the distinction between “what is above reason and what is against reason”:
> “*La distinction qu’on a coutume de faire entre ce qui est au dessus de la raison, et ce qui est contre la raison, s’accorde assés avec la distinction qu’on vient de faire entre les deux especes de la necessité. Car ce qui est contre la raison, est contre les verités absolument certaines et indispensables; et ce qui est au dessus de la raison, est contraire seulement à ce qu’on a coutume d’experimenter ou de comprendre.*” (*Theod*. *Discours préliminaire*, § 23)

Against reason is contradiction. It is against the absolutely certain truths and therefore altogether impossible. But God’s deed and his wisdom stand above reason. In that respect it is clear that for God’s deed the principle of contradiction is only a *conditio sine qua non*, but not a sufficient condition.

This difference of necessities also brings with it a difference of concepts of truth. The truths that are secured through the principle of contradiction are called truths of reason (“*les Verités de Raisonnement*”) or necessary truths (“*les verités necessaires*”). In demarcation from these the truths that are contingent, i.e., whose opposite is also possible, are called truths of fact (“*les Verités de Fait*”) or contingent truths (“*les verités contingentes*”). These are the truths that stem from the free decision of God (“*decrets libres de Dieu*”) (G II, 39; cf. also G IV, 507). For the latter truths, namely those that are connected with the moral necessities, a further principle must be taken into account: the principle of sufficient reason. Leibniz expresses himself differently thereby as to whether this principle has validity exclusively for the truths of fact or yet applies to both kinds of truth.[^k2.28] In the formulation of this principle in the *Discours de métaphysique* it seems e.g. that Leibniz restricts this principle to the contingent truths of fact: “*toutes les propositions contingentes ont des raisons pour estre plustost ainsi qu’autrement*” (*Disc. metaph*. § 13, in G IV, 438).[^k2.29] In the *Monadology* Leibniz says cautiously: “*Mais la raison suffisante se doit aussi trouver dans les vérités contingentes ou de fait*” (*Monad*. § 36, G VI, 612—italics by T. O.). The last formulation suggests that the principle can also hold for the truths of reason, and at one place in the *Theodicy* he says clearly: “This important principle holds for all events, and no contrary example can be adduced for it” (*Theod*. I, 44). If the principle of sufficient reason holds for all events, then it also holds for the domain of the truths of reason. In these the application of the principle of contradiction is already supposed to lead to a sufficient reason.

But only in the contingent or truths of fact does the principle of sufficient reason play a specific role. Thereby it is emphasized that a fact needs not only freedom from contradiction, but also still other grounds, in order to be a truth. In that respect the distinction of the domains of validity of both principles seems to coincide with that of the analytic from the synthetic judgment.

On the other hand Leibniz traces his assertion that the complete concept of the human being ‘Adam’ as individual already contains everything that can happen to him or that he is to do back to the principle: *praedicatum inest subjecto* (*Disc. metaph*, § 13, in G IV, 437). In that respect he seems to hold all sentences in general for analytic and to deny the possibility of synthetic sentences. This interpretation, however, falls short, for the principle says that the concept of an individual was already at the creation of the world by God so perfect that everything that happens to this individual in the future is already determined and that this nevertheless does not exclude the freedom of the will of God and of human beings. Yet Leibniz asserts in a letter to Arnauld that one may not think the relation of the subject and its constitution with reference to the concept of the individual like species and genus of a logical relation.[^k2.30] From this it results that the principle *praedicatum inest subjecto* does not mean that other accidents as logically contradictory are excluded from the subject, but that these other accidents must also be able to be possible; but God at the creation decided for determinate accidents from all possible ones, in order to create the best world. Precisely therein properly consists the concept of the individual:
> “*Car si quelque homme estoit capable d’achever toute la demonstration, en vertu de la quelle il pourroit prouver cette connexion du sujet qui est Cesar et du predicat qui est son entreprise heureuse; il feroit voir en effect que la Dictature future de Cesar a son fondement dans sa notion ou nature, qu’on y voit une raison, pourquoy il a plustost resolu de passer le Rubicon que de s’y arrester, et pourquoy il a plustost gagné que perdu la journée de Pharsale, et qu’il estoit raisonnable et par consequent asseuré que cela arrivast: mais non pas qu’il est necessaire en soy même ny que le contraire implique contradiction. A peu pres comme il est raisonnable et asseuré que Dieu fera toujours le meilleur, quoyque ce qui est moins parfait n’implique point. Car on trouveroit que cette demonstration de ce predicat de Cesar n’est pas aussi absolue que celles de nombres ou de la Geometrie.*” (*Disc. metaph*. § 13)

The concept of the subject ‘Caesar’ contains everything that he is in a position to do and will do. Leibniz indeed believes that this is deducible from the concept. But what the concept contains are not only the logically necessary determinations that exclude their (contradictory) opposite. The concept ‘Caesar’ contains e.g. the determination of crossing the Rubicon. This deed is not logically necessary in that sense, because logically speaking Caesar also could not have crossed the Rubicon, i.e., it contains no contradiction against the concept Caesar. It is contingent, but certain (*certain*), because God has predetermined it. On this will of God the truths of fact rest:
> “*[J]e diray seulement, pourquoy je croy, qu’il faut philosopher autrement de la notion d’une substance individuelle que de la notion specificique de la sphere. C’est que la notion d’une espece n’enferme que des verités éternelles ou necessaires, mais la notion d’un individu enferme sub ratione possibilitatis ce qui est de fait ou ce qui se rapporte à l’existence des choses et au temps, et par consequent elle depend de quelques decrets libres de Dieu considerés comme possibles: car les verités de fait ou d’existence dependent des decrets de Dieu.*” (G II, 38f.)

Thus the significance of the perfect concept in Leibniz becomes clear.[^k2.31] Couturat understands “analytic” only in the sense that the subject contains its accidents. In that respect he is right when he asserts that in Leibniz all statements are analytic and that there is only the difference of finite and infinite proof.

It seems however that Leibniz himself holds all truths for analytic when he asserts in his *Meditationes de Cognitione, Veritate et Ideis* that a concept is confirmed as true through analysis, i.e., through the investigation whether it contains no contradiction, but that human beings cannot always carry out this analysis perfectly:

“*Et quidem quandocunque habetur cognitio adaequata, habetur et cognitio possibilitatis a priori; perducta enim analysi ad finem, si nulla apparet contradictio, utique notio possibilis est. An vero unquam ab hominibus perfecta institui possit analysis notionum […] nunc quidem definire non ausim.*” (G IV, 425)

But here first the true concept (*idea vera*) is defined as possible concept, not as actual.[^k2.32] Therefore this quotation can be understood such that a concept is possible, i.e., true, if it contains no contradiction. This is what Leibniz calls the truth of reason in contrast to the truths of fact.[^k2.33] Nevertheless Leibniz here second seems even not to conceive the concept of contradiction only purely logically when he adduces as example a body of which it is assumed that it moves with the greatest swiftness, in order to show that seemingly possible concepts can contain contradictions. For the swiftest motion is for Leibniz a contradiction, because if the spokes of a wheel that turned with the greatest swiftness were lengthened, their tips would have to turn still more swiftly, which however contradicts the presupposition. This explanation is not to be understood as purely logical alone. The concept of the swiftest motion itself contains no logical contradiction, because the concepts of motion and of “being-in-motion-most-swiftly” are not logically contradictory. Only the geometrical explanation makes the ‘contradiction’ or the impossibility of this concept recognizable.[^k2.34]

Leibniz himself probably did not bring this problem clearly before his eyes. But somewhat later he will express this thesis of the inhabiting of the accidents in the subject somewhat more cautiously: “[T]he concept of the predicate [is] contained in the concept of the subject in some manner” (emphasis, T. O.)[^k2.35] Moreover he holds that this principle “is common to every true, affirmative, universal and singular, necessary or contingent sentence” (ibid.—emphasis by T. O.). Then of course the question immediately arises: in which manner? In the treatise *Specimen inventorum de admirandis naturae Generalis arcanis* of 1688 he clearly names two manners of the containing of the predicate in the subject:
> “*In omni veritate universali affirmativa praedicatum inest subjecto, expresse quidem in veritatibus primitivis sive identicis, quae solae sunt per se notae; implicite autem in caeteris omnibus, quod analysi terminorum ostenditur, substituendo sibi definita et definitions.*” (G VII, 309)

In the first explicit manner the analysis of the concept of the subject reaches the “original, identical truths.” Remarkable is that Leibniz in what follows compares the two kinds of truths, namely the two manners of the containing of the predicate in the subject, with rational and irrational numbers.
> “*Essentiale est discriminen inter Veritates necessarias sive aeternas, et veritates facti sive contingentes, differuntque inter se propemodum ut numeri rationales et surdi. Nam veritates necessariae resolvi possunt in identicas, ut quantitates commensurabiles in communem mensuram, sed in veritatibus contingentibus, ut in numeris surdis, resolutio procedit in infinitum, nec unquam terminatur; itaque certitudo et perfecta ratio veritatum contingentium soli DEO nota est, qui infinitum uno intuitu complectitur. Atque hoc arcano cognito tollitur difficulties de absoluta omnium rerum necessitate, et apparet quid inter infallibile et necessarium interfit.*” (G VII, 309)

This difference is not relative, but principal. The irrational number is not dark because the analysis is still imperfect, but it is principally distinguished from the rational. In that respect one must assume that there are two kinds of the inherence of the predicates in the subject.[^k2.36]

Leibniz’s assertion that the individual concept e.g. of ‘Caesar’ or ‘Adam’ involves everything that will happen to them rests on his doctrine of judgment, which has been called ‘analytic’ by several researchers.[^k2.37] This doctrine of the individual concept was, as was mentioned above, the theme that was especially discussed in the already mentioned Leibniz renaissance at the beginning of the last century. But the validity of what was designated by the word “analytic” should be restricted. Here one must separately consider the two elements that Kant had brought into the analytic judgment. If one first looks to Kant, he defined the analytic judgment thus: “[T]he predicate B belongs to the subject A as something that is (in a hidden manner) contained in this concept A” (A6/B10). In this sense one might also call the Leibnizian doctrine that the subject-concept is already supposed to contain all accidents an analytic theory of judgment. But Kant means thereby that the analytic predicates are *logically* deducible from the concept of the subject, i.e., with the means of the principle of contradiction. Kant carries this thought further in the second edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason* (1787), when in demarcation from the empirical judgment he elucidates the analytic judgment: “For, before I go to experience, I already have all conditions for my judgment in the concept, from which I can only draw out the predicate according to the principle of contradiction, and thereby at the same time become conscious of the necessity of the judgment” (B12). Insofar as Leibniz holds his doctrine of the implication of the predicates (the accidents) in their subject (the substance) also for applicable to the truths of fact, it is not analytic in the last-described sense. For through the investigation (analysis) of the subject according to Leibniz all belonging accidents are supposed to be able to be found, whose opposites however are also possible, i.e., free of contradiction. Thus this analysis also includes synthetic cognitions in the Kantian sense concerning the subject. But these cognitions are not taken up empirically, but determined a priori. According to Leibniz the principle of sufficient reason requires a certain ground a priori:
> “*[Le principe] de la raison determinante: c’est que jamais rien n’arrive, sans qu’il y ait une cause ou du moins une raison determinante, c’est à dire quelque chose qui puisse servir à rendre raison a priori, pourquoy cela est existant plustost que non existant, et pourquoy cela est ainsi plustost que de toute autre façon.*” (*Theod*. I, § 44; see also *Disc. métaph*. § 13, in G IV, 438)

This cognition can be attained not only empirically, but also a priori. Therefore one can say that Leibniz already here introduces a theory of the synthetic judgment a priori—albeit of course not yet so elaborated and refined as Kant will later do.

From this it results that Kant’s critique of Leibniz, according to which Leibniz intellectualized the world in that he explains all events in appearance only logically, i.e., only through the principle of contradiction, cannot simply be accepted. It is not completely false, but it must be modified: Leibniz ignores—despite all too one-sided critique—not the difference between analytic and synthetic cognitions, between the logical ground and the real, but he recognizes that logical necessity does not suffice to grasp the truths of fact. But in that Leibniz asserts that also the accidents whose opposites prove themselves as not impossible are contained in the subject and through the investigation of the concept of the subject even become cognizable a priori, he extends the significance of analysis. For Kant by contrast it is impossible to cognize the individual or the singular synthetically a priori. In that respect Kant would argue that Leibniz crosses the boundary of human cognition.

#### 1.2. Logic and Ontology in the Metaphysics of Wolff and Baumgarten

It first holds once again to recall that Leibniz does not simply hold logic and the principle of contradiction for reality-constitutive. If he then presents the principle of sufficient reason as independent of the principle of contradiction, he must have allowed for the former a special domain of validity in which the principle of contradiction itself cannot be a sufficient ground.[^k2.38] Over against this Kant’s critique seems rather to turn against Wolff, who derives his whole ontology from the principle of contradiction.

In Wolff, as in Leibniz, both principles are the *principia philosophiae primae*,[^k2.39] and he treats them right at the beginning of his ontology. He first defines the principle of contradiction—with the over against Leibniz conspicuous shift of accent onto the existence of things and relations: “*Propositio haec Fieri non potest, ut idem simul sit & non sit, dicitur Principium Contradictionis*” (Wolff, *Ontologia*, § 29).[^k2.40] But he follows Leibniz when he then passes over to the definition of modality. The principle of contradiction is a measure of possibility and impossibility: “*Impossibile dicitur, quicquid contradictionem involvit*” (*Ontologia*, § 79); and: “*Possibile est, quod nullam contradictionem involvit, seu, quod non est impossibile*” (*Ontologia*, § 85).

This principle constitutes the foundation of ontology and appears immediately at the beginning of the *Ontologia*. Then Wolff treats the principle of sufficient reason with reference to the concepts that Kant treats at the end of the amphiboly chapter: Something (*ens*) and Nothing (*nihil*). Wolff here conceives the principle as a principle of existence or of actuality: “*Propositio, quod nil fit sine ratione sufficiente, cur potius fit, quam non fit, dicitur Principium rationis sufficiente*” (*Ontologia*, § 71).[^k2.41]

As Wolff makes clear in his *Deutsche Metaphysik*, it does not suffice as a foundation of existence that something contains no contradiction (*Deutsche Metaphysik* § 13), but: “There must thus outside possibility still something more come thereto […], whereby the possible obtains its fulfillment” (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 14). This fulfillment he conceives as “actuality.”[^k2.42] The principle of sufficient reason thus gives the ground why something rather exists than does not exist. It holds for actuality, whereas the principle of contradiction holds as ground of possibility and impossibility.[^k2.43] Wolff’s systematic clearly distinguishes from the domain of validity of the principle of contradiction that of the principle of sufficient reason, i.e., from the domain of possibility that of actuality.

But Wolff then departs from Leibniz when he derives the principle of sufficient reason from the principle of contradiction. Precisely at this point he criticizes Leibniz:[^k2.44]
> “He [Leibniz] has taken it [the principle of sufficient reason] as a sentence grounded in experience, against which one can bring no example, and therefore given no proof, although Clarke demanded it.” (*Deutsche Metaphysik* § 30)

Wolff indeed subsequently as proof of this principle only adduces that with it one can distinguish truth and dreaming, or the true world and the “land of Cockaigne” (*Deutsche Metaphysik* § 30; see also *Ontologia* § 77). But he also gives there an a priori proof. He calls that which neither exists nor is possible the Nothing (*nihil*). From Nothing something cannot arise, because the impossible cannot become something. For the impossible is something that contains contradiction within itself (*Deutsche Metaphysik* § 12; *Ontologia* § 79). That is Wolff’s negative grounding of his principle of sufficient reason: ‘Nothing’ cannot be the ground of something (*Deutsche Metaphysik* § 30, § 28).[^k2.45] But on the other hand he also says that the principle holds also without proof and then is to be counted among the axioms (*Ontologia* § 75).

This tracing of the principle of reason back to that of contradiction would make the Leibnizian distinction between the already exhibited two kinds of necessity impossible, because it would cancel the difference of the logical domain and the moral. But Wolff does not draw this consequence. He divides necessity, like Leibniz, on the one hand into “absolutely necessary” or “*absolute necessarium*” and on the other into “necessary under a condition” or “*hypothetice necessarium*” (*Deutsche Metaphysik* § 575; *Ontologia* § 302). Wolff also follows Leibniz here in that he first defines that first necessity through the principle of contradiction. Something is absolutely necessary whose opposite is impossible, i.e., contains a contradiction. “Hypothetically necessary” is by contrast something, now differently from Leibniz, whose opposite is impossible under a condition.[^k2.46] Beside this he also calls the absolute necessity the geometrical or metaphysical, and the hypothetical the necessity of nature and of morals. Further differently from Leibniz he does not call the first logical, for he could not regard only the absolute necessity as logical, because the principle of reason in the version in which it holds for natural or moral necessity is for Wolff likewise derived from the principle of contradiction. In that respect the hypothetical necessity also proves itself as a logical one. His whole metaphysics is derived from the principle of contradiction. In that respect logical necessity is thus to be regarded as generally predominant.

Wolff speaks instead of hypothetical necessity also of “certainty”—this terminology too he takes over from Leibniz—and contrasts this with necessity (in the narrower sense). In the German metaphysics the following is said of necessity: “If that which is opposed to a thing contains something contradictory within itself; then the same is necessary” (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 36). Over against necessity so defined stands the certainty of the mechanical course of the world in § 561:
> “Because the present state of the world is grounded in the preceding and the future in the present (§ 547, 548); the events in the world thereby obtain their certainty.” (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 561)

Events in the world are indeed necessary, but only insofar as they are through “the preceding,” but thereby they are “thus not absolutely” necessary (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 563). Exactly like Leibniz Wolff grounds with the difference between absolute and hypothetical necessity—or between necessity and certainty—the freedom of the will of human beings (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 521) and of God (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 978).

In that respect Wolff too distinguishes a logical and a causal necessity dependent on the will.[^k2.47] Thus it seems that Wolff conceives the principle of contradiction as a measure of possibility and the principle of sufficient reason as that of actuality. The principle of contradiction only makes recognizable whether something is possible. What of all the possible becomes actual depends on physical necessity or the will:
> “Since now possibility constitutes the essence of things (§ 35), and this is necessary (§ 38); so it is already understood from this that neither through willing nor power can something be made possible. What before and in itself is possible need not first be made possible through someone’s will or power, although what is possible can through will and power attain actuality.” (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 80)

The systematic separation of the domain of possibility or of the principle of contradiction from the domain of actuality or of the principle of sufficient reason enables Wolff to strengthen Leibniz’s doctrine of freedom. But on the other hand he thereby ignores the envisaged systematization of metaphysics that was to be based on the principle of sufficient reason being derived from the principle of contradiction. Through this manner of systematization moreover all sentences of this metaphysics could become analytic.

If we in this connection go into Baumgarten’s *Metaphysica*, we recognize immediately that he has taken up not a little from Wolff. He also begins his ontology with the principle of contradiction, which however here serves to define ‘Nothing’ (“*Nihil negativum, irrepraesentabile, impossibile, repugnans (absurdum)*” *Metaphysica* § 7). A negative, a Nothing is namely that which contains contradiction. By contrast that is possible which involves no contradiction. This is called ‘*aliquid*’ (Something). From Wolff he has also taken over the derivation of the principle of sufficient reason from that of contradiction (§ 20ff.).[^k2.48] In the definition of necessity he likewise follows Leibniz and Wolff (§ 101). He divides it into necessity in itself, “*necessarium in se*,” and hypothetical necessity, “*necessarium hypothetice*” (§ 102). That necessity is—again as in Leibniz—called the metaphysical, absolute, geometrical, and logical, whereas this is called physical or still moral.


### 2. Identity and Diversity, or *principium identitatis indiscernibilium*

After the general investigation of the connection of ontology with the logical principle, the principle of contradiction, in Leibniz, Wolff, and Baumgarten, we go into the individual themes that occasioned Kant to his critique.

Beside the principle of sufficient reason the “*principium identitatis indiscernibilium*” also stems from Leibniz. In a famous passage from the fourth letter to Clarke he tells an anecdotal story from the court of Herrenhausen, which Hegel mockingly mentions in his logic in the remark on “diversity.”[^k2.49] Leibniz’s acquaintance, who believed that he could find two wholly equal leaves and was challenged by Electress Sophie to the proof, searched the whole garden of Herrenhausen in vain (G VII, 372). From this Leibniz concludes the principle: “*il n’y a point deux individus indiscernables*” (G VII, 372). In our experience fine diversities of the individual things can be overlooked because of our limited cognitive capacity (G VII, 268). But if we could cognize everything, we would see that all things are diverse. This sentence is for Leibniz a new expression of the old principle of individuation, in which old philosophy or “*la philosophie superficielle*” erred in asserting that there are things that are diverse only according to number, “*solo numero*.”

Despite the empirical appearance of this explanation Leibniz holds that one precisely cannot understand this principle empirically. Leibniz asserts that this principle—and the sentence connected with it—is rather derived a priori. He traces the principle of individuation back to his principle of sufficient reason. If there were two wholly equal things, God would have created them twofold without ground, which obviously violates the principle of sufficient reason (G VII, 393f.). That the principle of individuation relates to the concept of the perfect concept is easy to see. If the individual is to be distinguished from every other through its determinations, its concept must already contain all its determinations, thus be a perfect concept.[^k2.50]

In Wolff this principle seems to play no important role in ontology. We can indeed find in his *Ontologia* a detailed treatment of the concepts ‘identity (*identitas*),’ ‘similarity (*similitudo*),’ and thereby ‘diversity (*diversitas*)’ in the third section of the first part (§§ 179ff.). But it is not in the *Ontologia*, but first in the *Cosmologia*, that he takes up the principle: “*Quoniam in omni mundo materiali non dantur duo individua, quae nonnisi numero different (§ 247); si duo entia A & B ponas, quae nonnisi numero different, consequenter indiscernibilia sunt, ens unum idemque sub duobus nominibus ponis*” (*Cosmologia*, § 248). Because he does not follow Leibniz’s monadology and against it allows material atoms, this principle now holds in the material world.

In contrast to Wolff, in Baumgarten’s *Metaphysica* this principle of the distinguishability of individuals occupies a systematic place in ontology. It is treated in the section ‘*Idem et Diversum*’ (§§ 265–279), which anticipates the Kantian concepts of reflection of identity and diversity. Yet this passage is far removed from the place where Baumgarten specifically treats the principle of individuation (§ 151), and no connection between the two is pointed to, although they, if one follows Leibniz, come to stand in a close connection.

Baumgarten here develops five principles that have to do with identity and diversity. The division of these principles rests on the definitions in § 70, which are given of “*similia*,” “*aequalia*,” “*congruentia*,” “*dissimilia*,” “*inaequalia*,” and “*discongruentia*.” In “*similia*” and “*dissimilia*” what is at issue is constitution (quality); in “*aequalia*” and “*inaequalia*” magnitude (quantity); in “*congruentia*” and “*discongruentia*” both, i.e., things are “*congruentes*” if they are equal both according to quantity and according to quality, and things are “*discongruentes*” if they are equal neither according to quantity nor according to quality.

The first principle: “*principium negatae totalis dissimilitudinis & diversitatis*” expresses that there are no wholly diverse things either according to quantity or according to quality, i.e., every thing has at least one determination of quality or of quantity in common with another (§ 268). The second principle: “*principium (identitatis) indiscernibilium late sumtum, aut negatae totalis identitatis*,” which fits the sentence of Leibniz quoted above, demands by contrast to the first that two things with wholly equal determinations be identical or, negatively expressed, that there be no wholly equal things. Baumgarten attempts to prove this principle on the basis of his § 74, in which plurality is defined. According to this “to be many” means that what belongs thereto is partly identical and partly diverse.[^k2.51] But individual things are identical according to number. Therefore they are not partly identical and partly diverse. This proof is no proof in the strict sense, because it already presupposes that individual things are identical, which properly was first to be proved (§ 269). The other three principles of diversity are derived from this second principle. The third is called the “*principium negatae totalis congruentiae*” (§ 270). Whereas in the second principle it was not said more determinately in which respect the things are to be compared, here the things are compared both according to quantity and according to quality (the inner determinations). There are no things wholly equal in these two respects at once. Two things must have at least one inner determination diverse from the other. In the fourth principle what is at issue is only quality and in the fifth only quantity. That is called: “*principium (identitatis) indiscernibilium stricte sumtum, aut negatae totalis similitudinis*” (§ 271) and this: “*principium negatae totalis aequalitatis*” (§ 272). We need not go into Baumgarten’s proofs of his five principles in all detail. But this much can be held fast: insofar as the last three principles rest on the second principle and this presupposes that individual things are diverse according to number, this explanation of Baumgarten’s would not have been convincing for Leibniz, because the latter, as shown, precisely criticized that his predecessors distinguished things only according to number.

It lies near to assume that this deviation of Baumgarten’s from Leibniz also affected Kant, because the latter designates identity as “*numerica identitas*” (A263/B319) or “*numero eadem*” (A281/B337) and diversity as “*numero diversa*” (A282/B338). Kant seems to believe that Leibniz’s principle consisted in making possible the one according to number through the equality of the inner determinations, whereas diversity of place already yields the many or diversity only according to number.
