But the point at issue in Leibniz lies in the fact that things are not identical or diverse according to number, but through their inner determinations.

### 3. Reality and Opposition

It is according to Kant not Leibniz, but “his successors,” who “carry into their Leibniz–Wolffian doctrinal edifices” the principle that Kant refutes with reference to his second pair of concepts of reflection, agreement and conflict (A273/B329): namely the principle that realities (as mere affirmations) never conflict with one another.

We have already seen that Kant in his prize essay on the progress of metaphysics relates his doctrine of real opposition, which he treats in the amphiboly chapter by means of the second pair of concepts of reflection, not to the principle of contradiction, but to the principle of sufficient reason. Leibniz is according to Kant said to have asserted that the ground of reality must be a reality and cannot be a negation. Kant’s understanding of the Leibnizian doctrine can be confirmed through Leibniz’s ontological assertion itself: Leibniz in fact asserted that the ground of existing can consist only in the existent.[^k2.52]

#### 3.1. Leibniz

Insofar as Leibniz holds contradiction for impossible and contrary to reason, it is for him impossible that mutually contradictory predicates belong to a subject. Yet Leibniz as a physicist must have known the phenomenon that Kant presents as real opposition. Thus he expressly objected against the Cartesian law of conservation, according to which the quantity of motion in the world in general is constant, that the direction of motion must also be attended to, for it likewise remains conserved. More precisely considered he asserts: First that the difference between the sum of the forces of the motions toward the east and that toward the west is always equal. Second it is said of this difference that it always amounts to null. Third it holds for Leibniz that likewise the sum of all forces toward east and west in the world in general is always equal to null.[^k2.53] It lies near to see in this difference an opposed relation of several forces that cancel one another. Moreover it may be remarked that Leibniz in the *Nouveaux Essais* argues for the positivity of the ground of rest:
> “*Autre chose est, quand on parle de la cause du repos, qui doit estre positive dans la matière seconde ou masse. Je croirois encor que l’idée même du repos est privative, c’est à dire, qu’elle ne consiste que dans la negation. Il est vray que l’acte de nier est une chose positive.*” (NE II, 8, § 2 in G V, 117)

What Leibniz here says of the cause of rest is furnished with the essential marks of Kantian real opposition. It is first “privative,” i.e., in it the realities directed against one another cancel one another. Second this cancellation is gradual, because motion has degrees. Most important is third its positivity. Privation is indeed a negation, but the act of “denying (*nier*)” itself is already for Leibniz positive.

Has Kant then not reached his critical goal over against Leibniz at all? Does Kant’s critique spring only from his own misunderstandings of Leibniz’s doctrine? But the Kantian critique concerning the second pair of concepts of reflection, agreement and conflict, aims not at all at natural philosophy, but at moral theory.[^k2.54] Kant contests the Leibnizian assertion that the ground of evil stems only from the limitedness of creatures, and comments on this thus: “According to this principle e.g. all evils are nothing but consequences of the limits of creatures, i.e., negations” (A273/B329). In order to free God from responsibility for evil in the world, Leibniz asserts in the *Discours de métaphysique*:
> “*On voit bien cependant que Dieu n’est pas la cause du mal. Car non seulement apres la perte de l’innocence des hommes le peché originel s’est emparé de l’ame, mais encor auparavant il y avait une limitation ou imperfection originale connaturelle à toutes les creatures, qui les rend peccables ou capables de manquer. […] Et c’est à quoy se doit reduire à mon avis le sentiment de S. Augustin et d’autres auteures que la racine du mal est dans le neant, c’est à dire dans la privation ou limitation des creatures.*” (*Disc. métaph*. § 30)

Also later in the *Theodicy* he formulates:
> “*Mais tous ceux qui reconnoissent que Dieu produit le meilleur plan, qu’il a choisi entre toutes les idées possibles de l’univers, qu’il y trouve l’homme porté par l’imperfection originale des creatures à abuser de son libre arbitre et à se plonger dans la misere […]; ceux là, dis je, font voir plus distinctement que l’intention de Dieu est la plus droite et la plus sainte du monde, que la Creature seule est coupable, que sa limitation ou imperfection originale est la source de sa malice, que sa mauvaise volonté est la seule cause de sa misere.*” (*Theod*. II, § 167, in G VI, 210—emphasis by T. O.)[^k2.55]

According to this doctrine evil has its ground in the lack of perfection that is proper to finite creatures. The essence of evil is “privative.” Leibniz speaks little ambiguously of “*la nature privative du mal*” (*Theod*. préface, G VI, 37).[^k2.56] This doctrine of evil as privation of being Leibniz traces back to Augustine.[^k2.57] If evil is the privation of the good, it increases when the good diminishes: “*Minus bonum habet rationem mali*” (*Theod*. II, § 194, in G VI, 231).[^k2.58]

Leibniz draws for the clarification of the character of evil as negative limitedness of creatures the comparison with the inertia of matter: Several ships sail with the current, without wind or rowing accelerating the journey. They have different loads, e.g. wood or stones, some more, others less. The ships that have loaded the most sail the slowest. Leibniz here compares the current with the ‘activity of God’ and the inertia of matter, which causes the retardation, with the limitedness of creatures:
> “*Le courant est la cause du mouvement du bateau, mais non pas de son retardement; Dieu est la cause de la perfection dans la nature et dans les actions de la creature, mais la limitation de la receptivité de la creature est la cause des defaults qu’il y a dans son action.*” (*Theod*. I § 30, in G VI, 120)

But although this comparison seems to ascribe to matter itself the cause of the retardation, one may according to Leibniz not think so, as if God, who created matter, were also guilty of its inertia.[^k2.59] The cause of evil must thus rather be ascribed to the form, i.e., precisely to the limitedness of creatures.[^k2.60]

Leibniz’s doctrine of evil interests us in connection with Kant’s amphiboly chapter with reference to the following points: First we have seen that Kant’s critique of Leibniz, insofar as it is exercised by means of the second pair of concepts of reflection, agreement and conflict, concerns the moral-philosophical domain; it aims at the fact that Leibniz holds evil for the consequence of the limitedness of creatures. Second: Evil as limit is a consequence of privation or of deprivation: this also holds in Leibniz. But he, differently from Kant, has let privation proceed only from negation and not—as Kant did—from reality. Third: Leibniz’s doctrine of evil, which derives from the limitation of creatures, becomes, insofar as he finds its ground not in matter but in form, for Kant an interesting object of critique in the treatment of the fourth pair of concepts of reflection, where Kant asserts that Leibniz conceived matter and form only according to the logical order of the pure understanding, namely that form subsequently joins matter. And we have seen that according to the Leibnizian doctrine of evil first matter was created by God and that form as limitation of matter through finite creatures comes afterwards.

But this form as the ground of evil is something that God is said to have “permitted (*permis*).”[^k2.61] In that respect God also participates in evil, “through the actual implantation of these forms into matter” (*Theod*. III, § 381, also I, § 27). Evil is finally regarded by Leibniz as “*une condition sine qua non du bien*” (*Theod*. préface, G VI, 47).

#### 3.2. Reality and Opposition in Wolff and Baumgarten

We have already seen with reference to the concept of necessity that Wolff and Baumgarten in their derivation of the principle of sufficient reason deny that Nothing can be a ground for something. *Realitas* is treated in Wolff only in passing, since *res* is defined almost like *aliquid*. Thereby it must be recalled that already the scholastics regarded *realitas* and *quidditas* as synonymous. *Res* is what is thought, what is or what can be (Wolff, *Ontologia*, § 243). Of negation there is here no talk, but *aliquid* was already previously (*Ontologia*, § 60) opposed to *nihil*. Wolff speaks it out clearly: “*Nihilum non posse esse causam alicujus*” (*Ontologia*, § 66, n.). On the other hand the concept of ‘*privatio*’ is introduced as lack of reality: “*Defectus alicujus realitatis, quae esse poterat, seu quam esse per se non repugnat, Privatio dici solet*” (*Ontologia*, § 273).

Baumgarten engages more thoroughly with *realitas*. He defined *realitas* as affirmative or positive determination (*determinatio*) and *negatio* as negative determination. Also for him realities can only agree with realities. Therefore he says: “*Rationatum enim merarum realitatum esse, est realitas*” (§ 140). That is the point on account of which Kant in his writing on the progress of metaphysics criticizes the principle of sufficient reason.

Negation and reality are thus in Baumgarten opposed to one another. For a merely negative being there is no possibility, therefore no reality. It is Nothing (*non ens*). By contrast: “*omne ens est reale*” (*Metaphysica*, § 136). Moreover he says at a place in the *Theologia naturalis*, where he determines God’s essence as “*omnitudo realitatum*”: “*Omnes realitates sunt vere positiva, nec ulla negatio est realitas*” (*Metaphysica*, § 807).[^k2.62] In that respect he leaves no room for real opposition. But like Wolff he distinguishes two kinds of negation: negation in the strict sense and privation. The first negation is necessary, thus belongs to the *essentialia* and *attributa*; privation is negativity as *modus* (§ 137).

Negation is the source of imperfection, and something imperfect is evil or bad (*malum*). Therefore negation is the evil. Negation in the strict sense is metaphysical evil (§ 146); *privatio* contingent or physical evil (*malum contingens, malum physicum late dictum*).

Wolff also divides *malum* into the metaphysical, the physical, and the moral. In every case *malum* arises from *limitatio*; it is an imperfection. The differences lie, as in Baumgarten, only in whether this limitation is absolutely necessary (metaphysical evil) or hypothetically necessary, i.e., contingent (physical and moral) (*Theol. natural*. II, §§ 282ff.; cf. *Theol. natural*. I, §§ 372ff.).

### 4. Substance and Matter

As concerns the concepts of reflection of the ‘inner and outer,’ Leibniz never made them comprehensively the theme; one is properly dependent on searching for scattered utterances. But Kant relates these concepts of reflection to the central concept of ontology: ‘substance.’ He uses the inner for the designation of substance and the outer for the designation of its relations. In that respect what is at issue in these concepts is the since antiquity traditional version of ontology.

A literal correspondence thereto can also be found in the ontology of Baumgarten’s *Metaphysica*. Here the distinction of the inner and the outer plays a great role. Ontology—in its presentation by means of the predicates of the existent (Baumgarten, *Metaphysica*, § 4)—is, apart from its thematization in the Prolegomena, right at the beginning divided into a part a) “(*praedicatis*) *internis*” and into a part b) “(*praedicatis*) *externis sive relativis*” (§ 6). Thereby the predicates that relate to the inner are again divided into the “*universalia*” and the “*disiunctiva*.” To the *interna universalia* belong e.g. the predicates such as *possibile, connexum, ens, unum, verum*, to the *interna disiunctiva* those such as *necessarium et contingens, mutabile et immutabile, reale et negativum* etc., and to the *externa* e.g. *idem et diversum, simultaneum et successivum, causa et causatum* etc. All possible predicates of being are either inner or outer, i.e., relational. These divisions of Baumgarten’s cannot be found in Wolff’s *Ontologia*.

#### 4.1. Substance and Matter in Leibniz

These concepts of reflection refer to the core doctrines of Leibnizian metaphysics, in particular to monadology and the doctrine of pre-established harmony. According to Kant the latter is a necessary consequence of the former. Monadology, which Leibniz himself never so called, is the doctrine of the plurality of substances from which every composite in the world is to be constructed and which themselves however are said to have no extension. These unextended substances can have no physical influence on one another, which is expressed in the famous image: “*Les Monades n’ont point de fenêtres*” (*Monad*. § 7, in G VI, 607). But they represent the whole world from their own “*point de vue*,” their own perspective, and are themselves furnished with perception and appetition. The individual substances can according to Leibniz also be addressed as beings that have a soul, even if they are distinguished according to the degree of distinctness of their representations. The substances with the more distinct representation are called spirit (*l’esprit*) (especially *Monad*. §§ 19ff., § 29; *Principes*, §§ 4 and 5).[^k2.63]

Kant seems to present this characteristic of the monadic substances in Leibniz appropriately when he in his way reconstructs Leibniz’s presumed trains of thought that the latter expresses in his substance-doctrine. Kant ties onto the determination that substance must be indivisible. But one cannot find something indivisible in space, because everything in space is extended. Consequently substance must be found in our soul. The following passage seems to agree with this Kantian presentation, although Kant could not know the corresponding writing of Leibniz:
> “*Ainsi on ne trouvera jamais un corps dont on puisse dire que c’est véritablement une substance. Ce sera tousjours un aggregé de plusieurs. Ou plustost ce ne sera pas un estre reel, puisque les parties qui le composent sont sujettes à la même difficulté, et qu’on ne vient jamais à aucun estre reel, les estres par aggregation n’ayant qu’autant de realité qu’il y en a dans leur ingrediens. D’où il s’ensuit que la substance d’un corps, s’ils en ont une, doit estre indivisible; qu’on l’appelle ame ou forme, cela m’est indifferent.*” (G II, 72)

This assertion that one can encounter the substances only outside the extended, because every extended is always an aggregate of several, we can also find in his later writings such as in the *Monadology* (§ 3) and the *Principles* (§ 2). We make the experience that in space we always encounter something composite. There must be simple indivisible substances from which this composite arises. Although Leibniz here indeed avoids simply calling substance soul, what is not composed belongs only to our inner sense; it may thus not be understood as matter or body, but rather precisely yet as form or soul.

But this doctrine is according to Kant marked by a double error. First: In the Refutation of Idealism, which he first inserts in 1787 into the second edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, Kant has recourse to the existence of something permanent, i.e., of substance in space. He finds here substance rather in our outer sense than in our inner sense. Inner experience rather already presupposes something permanent in space. If Leibniz second thinks with substance a thing in itself, this is for Kant not to be accepted, because for Kant inner experience also belongs to the world of appearance. That is the theme with which he occupies himself in the first part of the Dialectic, in the transcendental paralogism.

Kant’s turn against Leibnizian monadology aims in a double direction: Kant on the one hand concedes substance in space. But it is on the other hand not the individual, indivisible permanent, but it exists only as an effect of several forces, as sum of relations. This assertion contains a double critique of the Leibnizian doctrine: of the concept of substance and of the concept of matter.[^k2.64]

Beside that derivation of the simple monads from the existence of the composite, which one may call metaphysical, Leibniz still gives a physical derivation. We have already seen that Leibniz distinguishes two kinds of truths, the truths of reason, which follow logical and metaphysical necessity, and the truths of fact, in which moral or physical necessities are expressed. The principle of the truths of reason is the principle of contradiction; over against this the principle of the truths of fact is essentially the principle of sufficient reason. The grounds that are to be found on the basis of the principle of the truths of fact are of two kinds, “*la cause efficiente*” and “*la cause finale*” (*Monad*. § 36). That cause is found in the physical, mechanical explanation of nature, but this belongs to metaphysics. The domain of the truths of fact too is again divided into two domains, into physics and into metaphysics.[^k2.65]

But these two domains are not strictly distinguished like two worlds that cannot communicate with one another. For Leibniz it is rather the explanation of the physical world that itself requires the application of metaphysical principles. Therefore in this respect his doctrine of monads is physically derived.

Leibniz’s critique of Cartesian physics consists in the fact that Descartes and his successors saw the essence of the body in *extensio* and treated physical objects only geometrically. Leibniz in this connection rejects the Cartesian law of conservation: Connected with Descartes’s conception of the body is that he determined the fundamental law of nature with reference to the conservation of the quantity of motion as product of mass and velocity (*mv*). But what according to Leibniz is conserved is not this quantity of motion, but “*quod necesse est servari eandem quantitatem potentiae actricis, imo […] etiam eandem quantitatem actionis motricis*” (*De ipsa natura*, in G IV, 505f.). For Leibniz the nature of the body does not consist in extension. It is impossible to explain activity (and passivity) as physical phenomenon only through geometrical concepts (SD Pars II; GM VI, 246). But because the Cartesians cling to the criticized view, they had according to Leibniz to take refuge in God (occasionalism) in order to explain the communication between substances. Leibniz asserts against the law of conservation of the quantity of motion that goes back to Descartes the conservation of the product of mass and the square of velocity (*mv*²).

What is conserved through this new law he calls force (*force/vis*); meant is what today is called energy.[^k2.66] The essence of the body lies in its force. Mechanism, materialism or Cartesianism had held force, in determining what a body is, for indifferent. Force is said to be transferred outwardly through collision from one body to the other. Thus force itself is said to belong to the body only outwardly and not to belong to its essence. According to Leibniz the force that inheres in the substance is not a mere capacity of motion that needs an outer stimulus for its realization, like the scholastic ‘*facultas*’ or ‘*potentia nuda*’ (G IV, 469). The active force (*conatus, nisus*),[^k2.67] which Leibniz ascribes to the substance, consists “between this *facultas* for action and the action itself” (G IV, 469).[^k2.68] If this force is nevertheless not realized, it can only have been inhibited. In order for the force to be able to act, consequently only the inhibition needs to be cancelled (G IV, 469). Through this new concept of force a new science, dynamics, is made possible, and the objects of this science, the active forces, are, when it is asked how they stand in relation to the thought of God, so conceived that they were inserted into matter by God at the creation (G IV, 469f.).[^k2.69]

The extended, the *extensa*, is understood as something that has arisen thereby that something (what is different from it itself) extends itself. Therefore it presupposes something that is extended, i.e., a substance. Extension is therefore not itself a substance: “*extensionis notionem per se non completam esse, sed relationem ad aliquid quod extenditur cujus diffusionem sive continuatam replicationem dicat*” (SD, Pars II, in GM VI, 247). As Kant correctly sees, Leibniz—differently from Descartes—understands under matter or extension[^k2.70] nothing substantial. Extension is “its spreading or continuous repetition.”[^k2.71] He continues: “*adeoque substantiam corporis quae agendi resistendique potentiam involvit et ubique massa corporea existit praesupponi, hujusque diffusionem in extensione contineri*” (ibid.). Extension is only an effect of the actual substances that are capable of acting and of resisting. Precisely in these utterances Leibniz stands near the Kantian amphiboly chapter. Thus extension can be regarded as an effect of the substances understood as forces. Extension or matter is thus an effect of forces. In the following place Leibniz explains, exactly like Kant, extension through repulsion or repulsive force:
> “*[E]xtensioque nil aliud quam jam praesuppositae nitentis renitentisque id est resistentis substantiae continuationem sive diffusionem, dicit; tantum abest, ut ipsammet substantiam facere possit*.” (SD, Pars I, GM VI, 235)

In the *Specimen Dynamicum*, where he attempts a metaphysical grounding of his new science, dynamics, Leibniz introduces differentiations of the concept of force:[^k2.72] on the one hand he distinguishes between an active (*vis activa*) and a passive (*vis passiva*),[^k2.73] on the other between an original (*vis primitiva*) and a derivative (*vis derivativa*) force. These two distinctions according to Leibniz correspond with the traditional metaphysical divisions. The active force is the form, the passive the matter; the primitive force is called substantial, the derivative accidental. Then there are four kinds of forces, 1. *vis activa primitiva*, 2. *vis activa derivativa*, 3. *vis passiva primitiva*, and 4. *vis passiva derivativa*.

Leibniz’s dynamics underlies the conviction that all bodies move: “*cum corpus omnimode quiescens a rerum natura abhorrere arbitrer*” (SD Pars I, in GM VI, 236). Every corporeal substance essentially possesses this self-moving force, i.e., the original active force. This force, “which inheres in every corporeal substance” (ibid.), is however for itself no longer corporeal or material, but “the soul or the substantial form,” or, as Leibniz says, “nothing other than First Entelechy” (SD Pars I, in GM VI, 236; see also G IV, 395 et al.). It is also “*ens reale*,” “*unum per se*” (G IV, 395, 511).

It lies near that what Leibniz here designates with the original active force is a spiritual substance, the monad.[^k2.74] The monad is here conceived as a force. Leibniz says on this in *De ipsa natura*:
> “*Quantum ego mihi notionem actionis perspexisse videor, consequi ex illa et stabiliri arbitror receptissimum philosophiae dogma, actiones esse suppositorum; idque adeo esse verum deprehendo, ut etiam sit reciprocum, ita ut non tantum omne quod agit sit substantia singularis, sed etiam ut omnis singularis substantia agat sine intermissione, corpore ipso non excepto, in quo nulla unquam quies absoluta reperitur.*” (G IV, 509; cf. G IV, 470.)

The individual substance never rests. Everything that moves is individual substance. Substance is the name for the self-moving. This motion is not communicated from body to body, but it is already inscribed in the substance itself. God himself has laid this force into the substance at the creation:
> “*Etsi enim gravitas aut vis elastica mechanice explicari possint debeantque ex aetheris motu, ultima tamen ratio motus in materia est vis in creatione impressa, quae in unoquoque corpore inest.*” (G IV, 469f.)

But precisely for this reason this concept of the original active force does not serve for the explanation of the phenomenon (ibid.).[^k2.75] It belongs to the world of understanding. Therefore it is the understanding that distinctly cognizes this *vis insita* (G IV, 507). These original active forces do not always agree with one another. They can also collide against one another. When one collides with another, it is thereby limited and cannot realize all its potencies. This force limited through the collision is the derived active force. Directly following the place just quoted is the following passage: “*sed ipso conflictu corporum varie in natura limitatur et coërcetur*” (G IV, 470, also G IV, 511). The active force that we experience in the phenomenon is insofar a limited, namely derivative active force.

Something similar holds for matter. The forces that constitute matter are passive and again consist of the primitive and the derivative force. The original passive force is a resisting force that is called “first matter”: “*Et quidem vis primitiva patiendi seu resistendi id ipsum constituit, quod materia prima, si recte interpreteris, in Scholis appellatur*” (SD Pars I, in GM VI, 236f.). What he here designates as the original passive force is the impenetrability and the inertia of matter (ibid.).[^k2.76] Over against this the derived passive force constitutes second matter, namely modified matter. Of these two kinds of matter he explains himself in *De ipsa natura* as follows:
> “**[*M]ateriam** intelligi vel secundam vel primam; secundam esse quidem substantiam completam, sed non mere passivam; primam esse mere passivam; sed non esse completam substantiam; accedereque adeo debere animam, vel formam animae analogam, sive ἐντελέχειαν τὴν πρώτην, id est nisum quendam seu vim agendi primitivam, quae ipsa est lex insita, decreto divino impressa.*” (G IV, 512)

First matter, i.e., the original passive force, constitutes no perfect substance; it is rather second modified matter that constitutes it. In order to realize the being of a perfect substance, the original passive force must be modified. Then however the question arises how or by whom it is modified. The answer leads to the original active force (as entelechy) or the soul. In the text last quoted the concept of substance as monad is precisely thereby deduced that into matter a striving or a soul, i.e., an entelechy, is inscribed. In the letter to de Volder of 20 June 1703 Leibniz also distinguishes
> “(1) *Entelechiam primitivam seu Animam*, (2) *Materiam nempe primam seu potentiam passivam primitivam*, (3) *Monadem his duabus completam*, (4) *Massam seu materiam secundam, sive Machinam organicam, ad quam innumerae concurrant Monades subordinatae*, (5) *Animal seu substantiam corpoream, quam Unam facit Monas dominans in Machinam*.” (G II, 252)

After Leibniz has distinguished those two original forces, he elucidates three kinds of being in which those two are always already connected. The perfect monad is here a unity of entelechy as form and of the passive original force as matter. Second matter, which here is distinguished from that mere unity, is the organic machine that again contains within itself innumerable monads. These he calls in the *Monadology* “*les Machines de la Nature*” (*Monad*. § 64; see also G IV, 396 “*Machina naturalis*”), and he finds them in the earth or in the water of a pond (*Monad*. § 68). To the living being unity is given through a monad.

Here we must thus distinguish two conceptions of the monad. The monad that one sees at the beginning of the *Monadology* or of the *Principes* is a mere form that is not at all extended, i.e., has no body. It is purely spiritual. But first it gives the body or matter its unity. Matter without it is pure, in space splintered (but continuous) extension without unity. But the monad that Leibniz calls the more perfect substance is a corporeal substance as connection of form and matter, as well as of the original active and passive force. Here the two forces that belong to the physical world already appear modified, i.e., they are no longer originally given, but result derivatively:
> “*Proprie et rigorose loquendo forte non dicetur Entelechiam primitivam impellere massam sui corporis, sed tantum conjungitur cum passiva potentia primitiva quam complet, seu cum qua Monadem constituit, non vero influere potest in alias Entelechias, substantiasque adeo in eadem massa existentes.*” (to de Volder, 20 June 1703, in G II, 250)

The original active force, which belongs to metaphysics, does not act on the mass, thus on second matter, which belongs to physics. The thesis that matter is only an appearance and infinitely divisible seems to contradict his doctrine of monads. Thus the first two paragraphs of the so-called *Monadology* ran:
> “1. *La Monade, dont nous parlerons icy, n’est autre chose, qu’une substance simple, qui entre dans les composés; simple, c’est à dire, sans parties.*
> 2. *Et il faut qu’il y ait des substances simples, puisqu’il y a des composés; car le composé n’est autre chose, qu’un amas, ou aggregatum des simples.*” (*Monad*. §§ 1 and 2)

This turn gives us the impression that a composite, as one finds it in matter, consists of several simple substances, thus monads. But this interpretation Leibniz rejects in his letter to de Volder:
> “*Phaenomena igitur semper dividi possunt in phaenomena minora quae aliis subtilioribus animalibus apparere possent, nec unquam pervenietur ad minima phaenomena. Unitates vero substantiales non sunt partes, sed fundamenta phaenomenorum.*” (G II, 268)

The monads are not parts of matter, but its foundation.[^k2.77] But it has not yet become clear here what this word “*fundamenta*” is supposed to mean. But Leibniz says: “*Accurate autem loquendo materia non componitur ex unitatibus constitutivis, sed ex iis resultat, cum materia seu massa extensa non sit nisi phaenomenon fundatum in rebus, ut iris aut parhelion, realitasque omnis non sit nisi unitatum*” (G II, 268).

As résumé of this section we can hold fast: 1. Kant is right when he says that Leibniz in his metaphysics or the doctrine of monads conceives the concept of substance as spiritual. Yet 2. Leibniz also understands his monad as a kind of force, which however is not supposed as such to belong to appearance. 3. Leibniz like Kant holds impenetrability or resistance for one of the essential constitutions of matter.[^k2.78] Leibniz has denied matter itself its substantiality, but allows that it is animated through form or entelechy, whereas for Kant matter is the only possible substance in space, which however itself consists only of relations.


#### 4.2. Substance and Matter in Wolff and Baumgarten

If we now go into Wolff’s substance-doctrine, it turns out that the often asserted thesis is not tenable that Kant’s critique in the amphiboly chapter does not hit Leibniz, but Wolff. At least Kant’s critique of monadology cannot be reconstructed as an unintentional critique of Wolff.

Indeed Wolff conceived substance as a simple entity; the composite extended things constitute their aggregate (*Ontologia*, § 792ff.). It is defined as permanent and modifiable subject (*subjectum perdurabile et modificabile*) (*Ontologia*, § 768). And accordingly as Wolff defined simple being, quantity, *magnitudo*, and figure, which are all accidents, do not belong to it (*Ontologia*, § 780ff.). He even determines the soul as simple substance.[^k2.79] But, differently from Leibniz, he allows beside the psychic also corporeal substances and gives e.g. beside the soul as example of substance not only a stone (*Ontologia*, § 768f.; cf. §§ 765f.), but also unfolds a theory of corporeal substances in the *Cosmologia* with the concepts ‘*elementa*’ and ‘*corpusculum*.’

Also in Wolff the body is a composite being (*Cosmologia*, § 119): “*Corpora sunt substantiae compositae*” (*Cosmologia*, § 179). For in distinction from simple being it is extended (*Cosmologia*, § 122), has a determinate *magnitudo* and a figure (*Cosmologia*, § 123), and finally also fills space (*Cosmologia*, § 124). It is the *elementa* of which that body is composed. “*Elementa dicuntur principium internum corporum irresolubile in alia, sive primum*” (*Cosmologia*, § 181). “*Substantiae simplices sunt elementa corporum*” (*Cosmologia*, § 182). They are simple; therefore they are not extended, have neither magnitude nor figure, and fill no space (*Cosmologia*, § 184). They are even indivisible (*Cosmologia*, § 185). Over against the material atom, which *per se* is still divisible (*Cosmologia*, §§ 186f.), Wolff calls these for themselves indivisible *elementa* atoms of nature. Insofar the *elementa* are furnished with the constitutions that Leibniz already ascribed to the monads. But Wolff ascribes to those *elementa* no spiritual principles. Perception and desire (*appétit*) are the modifiable determinations of the soul, like color, moisture, cold, and figure in the stone (*Ontologia* § 765). In these corporeal *elementa* “*continentur rationes ultimae eorum, quae in rebus materialibus deprehenduntur*” (*Cosmologia*, § 191). The *elementa* stand opposed to matter. Matter is no substance (*Cosmologia*, § 178), but, as the *Ontologia* defines, “*[i]llud, quod determinatur in ente composito*” (*Ontologia*, § 948). This ontological definition of matter says that it first belongs to the composite and that second over against form something in it is determinate or determinable (*Ontologia*, § 944).[^k2.80] That is the definition of matter that Kant in the amphiboly chapter would have had to take over.

According to Wolff’s cosmological, rather physical definition in the *Cosmologia* matter is “*extensum vi inertiae praeditum*” (*Cosmologia*, § 141).[^k2.81] Matter consists of two components, extension and force of inertia. Here one sees that Wolff too defines matter with the help of a kind of force, yet differently from Kant. Whereas Leibniz and Kant describe matter or its extension as an effect of one or several forces, extension in Wolff is only a factor that together with the force of inertia constitutes matter. Extension (*extensio*) is in him defined only through a unity of several entities existing outside one another (*Ontologia*, § 548). The force of inertia has no inner connection with extension, but that is only set into this (“*in extensio supponitur*,” *Cosmologia*, § 131).

In Baumgarten both the ontological and the cosmological definition of matter are already found in the ‘*Ontologia*.’ In § 295 matter is called “*Extensum, cui vis inertiae tributur*.” Here a parallel to Wolff’s cosmological definition is found. It is “*phaenomenon substantiatum*” (*Metaphysica*, § 295). This matter, to which only the force of inertia is allotted, he calls against Leibniz the *prima materia* (*Metaphysica*, § 295). Similarly to Wolff in the next paragraph the body is designated as the matter to which a force is allotted, yet differently from Wolff this force is presented as a moving force. (*Metaphysica*, § 296)[^k2.82]

Baumgarten’s second definition of matter (*Metaphysica*, § 344) is very similar to that which we above already designated as the ontological. But with reference to ontology he here still gives two further distinctions that concern the concept of matter: Whereas matter as determinable (*determinabile*) is called “*materia ex qua*,” first matter as the stuff of the determining deed is called the “*materia circa quam*”; thereby it is brought out that matter here is essentially considered as object. And second matter in its mode of being as already determined matter is designated as “*materia in qua*.” Thereby matter is reflected especially with reference to the determining deed. But it appears in any case always in the signification of receiving a determination.

We have already seen that Wolff thoroughly distances himself from the contents of monadology—at least from calling his simple substance monad. But his pupil Baumgarten again introduces the concept of the monad into his own system of metaphysics. He again defines the monad as simple substance: “*Substantia vel est simplex, vel composita […]. Prior MONAS (atomus, perfecta unitas) dicitur*” (Baumgarten, *Metaphysica*, § 230). He had already previously defined substance as “*ens per se subsistens*” and also called it “*forma, ἐντελέχεια, οὐσία, ὑπόστασις, ἐνέργεια*” (*Metaphysica*, § 191). It seems indeed that he thereby also means the existence of the composite substance, but he then adds in § 234: “*Omnis substantia monas est, ens compositum strictius dictum non est monas. Ergo phaenomenon substantiatum*.” The composite being that cannot be a monad is properly no substance, but only its appearance. Only the monad as simple substance is substance in the proper sense. This monad arises, as in Leibniz—and in Wolff the simple things—only from Nothing (*Metaphysica*, § 236) and perishes only through the counter-running annihilation (*Metaphysica*, § 237).

Also in Baumgarten the concept of the monad likewise appears in the *Cosmologia*, the second part of his *Metaphysica*. It becomes clear here how closely it is connected with the Leibnizian. The concept of the monad is here brought into an elementary connection with the world. Monads are the substances of which the world is composed (*Metaphysica*, § 394). Baumgarten ascribes to the monad the same properties as Leibniz: It is indivisible, unextended, alone fills no space, has no magnitude, etc. (*Metaphysica*, § 396). But with a view to the history of the Wolffian school and also to the amphiboly chapter it is especially important to hold fast that here a faculty of representation is ascribed to the monad (*Metaphysica*, §§ 400ff.). Baumgarten against his teacher Wolff has again revived the doctrine of the monads as psychic substances. He also speaks of *elementa* (*Metaphysica*, § 423) and *corpuscula* (*Metaphysica*, § 426)—but these are derived from the monad.

Baumgarten’s concept of the monad is nevertheless different from Leibniz’s. Baumgarten first already ascribes impenetrability to the monad itself (*Metaphysica*, § 398). That then provides the ground for why extension is impenetrable (*Metaphysica*, § 418). Second it proves a great difference of Baumgarten from Leibniz that the former, as concerns the monads, asserts their mutual influence on one another and their conflict among one another (*Metaphysica*, § 408). From this the thesis is concluded that both action and reaction take place between the monads (*Metaphysica*, § 408ff.) and that there is likewise a mutually related, opposed equality of action and reaction (*Metaphysica*, § 411f.). Through the inevitable contact of the monads among one another and their thus manifesting mutual effect on one another they enter into a connection that proves itself as unity. Thus entering into relation the monads realize a motion that effects their continuous extension in space. Because already the parts of the extended are impenetrable, the extended as a whole exhibits an impenetrability that expresses its inertia (*Metaphysica*, § 418). We have already learned above that this extended—together with its inertia—is matter itself. If however a proper force of motion belongs to the extended, then it is called body.[^k2.83] The *elementa* are the parts that composed constitute the body. Baumgarten divides them into the absolutely first *elementa*, which are no longer divisible, and the relative, which are still divisible and therefore themselves consist of those first elements. Those absolutely first *elementa* are, Baumgarten says, monads. They are immaterial and incorporeal, as in Leibniz (*Metaphysica*, § 422), and *atomi naturae*, as in Wolff. The *corpuscula* are so small bodies that our senses can no longer cognize them (*Metaphysica*, § 425).

### 5. The Doctrine of Pre-established Harmony

The doctrine of pre-established harmony made Leibniz famous in the European society of his time. It formed an alternative to the then widespread views concerning the commerce of substances and procured Leibniz a place among the renowned philosophers. Characteristically then the title of his main work “New Essays on Human Understanding” is properly “*Nouveaux Essais sur l’entendement par l’auteur du système de l’harmonie préestablie*” (G V, 39); therein the author designates himself as the “author of the system of pre-established harmony.” That makes sufficiently clear how he understood his own philosophical position. He saw himself not as the philosopher of monadology, to which one later fixed him, but as that of the system of pre-established harmony. Also in other writings he gives with this turn to recognize with what he connects his own significance as author.

As seen, Leibniz ascribes to the substances however only the constitutions that Kant ascribes to inner sense. Leibniz forms a psychic or spiritual concept of substance and thereby robs matter of all realities. This Leibnizian substance-doctrine complicates the old problem of the commerce of substances among one another additionally by the fact that he thus must explain not only the influence of two different kinds of substances, the *res cogitans* and the *res extensa*, on one another, but of substances in general. Now the doctrine of pre-established harmony is supposed to resolve the problem of the commerce between body and soul by clarifying generally the relations among the substances.[^k2.84]

This doctrine constitutes an alternative to the two then known theses concerning the effects of substances on one another: to that of physical influence and that of occasionalism. With the philosophical controversies resulting against this background Kant also engages in the amphiboly chapter:
> “Precisely for that reason his [Leibniz’s] *principium* of the possible community of substances among one another also had to be a pre-established harmony, and could be no physical influence. For because everything is only inwardly, i.e., occupied with its representations, the state of the representations of the one could stand in wholly and completely no efficacious connection with that of the other substance, but some third cause flowing into all together had to make their states corresponding to one another, indeed not exactly through occasional and in each individual case specially applied assistance (*systema assistentiae*), but through the unity of the idea of a cause valid for all, in which they altogether obtain their existence and permanence, hence also reciprocal correspondence among one another, according to universal laws.” (A274f./B330f.)

Herewith Kant refers to the context into which the Leibnizian doctrine of pre-established harmony wanted to intervene resolvingly. “*Systema assistentiae*” is the designation that both Wolff and Baumgarten give to the doctrine of occasionalism.[^k2.85]

The view of the physical influence of substances on one another is excluded through Leibniz’s substance-doctrine. Between the substances, especially the soul and the body, no material influence takes place: “*on ne scauroit concevoir ny des particules materielles, ny des especes ou qualités immaterielles, qui puissent passer d’une de ces substances dans l’autre*” (G IV, 499). The successors of Descartes also struggle with the problem of the connection of substances, yet for them this problem mainly comes to a head in the relation between body and soul. The answer that they give consists essentially in relying on the assistance of God. Properly God on every individual occasion effects the connection where it seems that independently the soul acts on the body or conversely the body on the soul. This occasionalist doctrine for Leibniz ascribes too much to God and brings with it that we are to be compelled to underestimate our reason. It consists for him in the summoning of a “*deus ex machina*.”[^k2.86]

For Leibniz and also for his pupils it is the system of pre-established harmony that remains as the only alternative. He traces the *commercium* of the soul and the body like the occasionalists back to God. But God does not act anew on every occasion (“occasionally”), but according to Leibniz he has predetermined the world at the creation.

For Leibniz this doctrine is supposed to express God’s omnipotence better than occasionalism. But this only suffices to show that Leibniz disposes of a better hypothesis than the occasionalists, and this also only then if one follows the view that there is an omnipotent God. The three doctrines are discussed by Wolff and Baumgarten as three possible “*systemata*,” whereby the word “*systema*” designates a series of theoretically consistent hypotheses[^k2.87] that are especially supposed to aim at the clarification of this theme.[^k2.88]

Wolff and Baumgarten may precisely for this reason have been reckoned among the Leibnizians, because they held the doctrine of pre-established harmony, whereas they yet on the other hand took distance from many other of his doctrines, especially from his monadology. What specially concerns this doctrine, there are found in Wolff and Baumgarten no great contentual deviations from their predecessor: Only it now occupies a systematic place in the metaphysics of both. For Wolff, who holds the communication of physical substances among one another for possible, the problem with which Descartes already saw himself confronted also unavoidably presents itself, how then the connection of body and soul comes about. Therefore this theme is treated not in the *Cosmologia*, but in the *Psychologia rationalis*.[^k2.89]

The whole third part of this book is dedicated to this theme under the title “*De Commercio inter mentem & corpus*.” He first investigates the two hypotheses (*systemata*) concerning the *commercium* of substances that Leibniz already had before his eyes (Wolff *Psychol. rational*. § 553),[^k2.90] namely the “*systema influxus physici*” (*Psychol. rational*. §§ 558ff.), the “*systema causarum occasionalium*” (*Psychol. rational*. §§ 589ff.), and then the “*systema harmoniae praestabilitae*” itself (*Psychol. rational*. § 612ff.). Also in the *Deutsche Metaphysik* this problem is mainly treated in chapter 5: “Of the Essence of the Soul and of a Spirit in General.” It counts as *Psychologia rationalis* (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, §§ 760ff.): Here first the system of natural influences, thus the *systema influxus*, is refuted, because it violates the law of conservation (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 762); and the Cartesian occasionalistic seems weak, because it requires “everlasting miracles” (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 764). Wolff concludes from this that both are not tenable and by contrast only the doctrine of pre-established harmony can convince (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, §§ 766ff.).

Baumgarten’s treatment of this theme also counts as the systematization of the Leibnizian doctrine, because he—like already Leibniz—understands the communication of the soul and the body as a special case of the general connection of substances among one another. Thus those three *systemata* already appear in the ‘*Cosmologia*.’ There are according to him only these three modes of explanation for the problem (Baumgarten *Metaphysica*, § 458). But they now count as *systemata universalia*; *universalia*, because what is now at issue is altogether the relations of substances in general, not only those of body and soul. Baumgarten already there comes to the conclusion that both the doctrine of physical influence and that of occasionalism are false, and only that of pre-established harmony is possible.

In the ‘*Psychologia empirica*’ he attempts under the title “*Commercialium animae et corporis*” to prove the impossibility of concluding from experience to the doctrine of physical influence between the soul and the body. But first in the “*Psychologia rationalis*” those three systems are presented as *systemata psychologica* and more thoroughly investigated. Precisely insofar as these *systemata psychologica* appear as *systemata particularia*, the result is already given (*Metaphysica*, § 761), for the results of the investigation of the systems in general are here applied to the particularity; nevertheless Baumgarten engages in the enterprise of showing more precisely where the physical and the occasionalistic systems err (*Metaphysica*, § 766f.) and how the superiority of the system of pre-established harmony is to be grounded (§§ 768f.).

Kant has criticized in the doctrine of pre-established harmony first this, that Leibniz traces the communication of substances among one another back to a third, namely to God, which is a necessary consequence of his concept of substance, and that he thereby second lets the substances precede their relations. So far the characterization of this doctrine, which Kant judges as weak, agrees with that of Leibniz, Wolff, and Baumgarten. For Kant the problem properly lies in the fact that Leibniz and his successors like also the occasionalists have excluded physical influence among the substances (now as *substantiae phaenomenae*). Kant however is of the conviction that physical influence is possible in appearance and attempts to prove this in the Transcendental Analytic, especially in the treatment of the second Analogy of Experience. What especially concerns the commerce of the soul and the body, he propagates, as the *Critique of Pure Reason* shows, a cooperation of sensibility and understanding in cognizing, thus the commerce of the soul and the body, as he says in the polemical writing against Eberhard (AA VIII, 249f.).[^k2.91]

### 6. Space, Time, and Sensibility

The critique of the Leibnizian doctrine of space and time and of his doctrine of sensibility underlies the other critiques that Kant exercises on Leibniz in the amphiboly chapter. The sensible conditions are decisive for the construction of Kantian transcendental philosophy and are accomplished through the investigation of space and time as the pure forms of sensible intuition in the Transcendental Aesthetic. The ground of the intellectualization of the world in Leibniz consists according to Kant in his misrecognition of the fact that space and time constitute two subjective sensible conditions of cognition. The world as phenomenon is constructed thereby that matter as sensible stuff is first given through these formal conditions. Without thinking these along, i.e., in that one understands the world only purely logically, one always already misses the cognition of appearances, or of the appearing world.

#### 6.1. Space and Time as Relation in Leibniz

In the Transcendental Aesthetic Kant asserts that space and time are neither independent beings nor their properties.[^k2.92] This critique is also to be found in Leibniz. It is first the Newtonian doctrine of absolute space and absolute time that Leibniz especially contests in the polemical writings against Clarke. And second space and time are also for him no constitutions of the thing:
> “*Chaque chose a sa propre etendue, sa propre durée; mais elle n’a point son propre temps, et elle ne garde point son propre espace.*” (5th writing to Clarke, in G VII, 399)

But Kant also refutes the doctrine of space and time as relations that Leibniz propagates against Newton: “*l’espace n’est pas un être absolu, mais un ordre, ou quelque chose de relatif*” (G III, 595); space is said to be the order of coexistences (“*un ordre des coëxistences*,” G III, 622; G VII, 363), just as time is an order of successions (“*un ordre de successions*,” G VII, 363).[^k2.93]

But this seemingly sharp separation between Kant’s space–time doctrine and that of Leibniz must be revised in some points: First the relativity of space and time in Leibniz also gives them an ideal character.[^k2.94] Thus he asserts: “*[l’espace] qui ne seroit qu’ideal, si les corps n’y existoient point. […] Je dis encor là dessus, qu’il en est de l’espace comme du temps; que le temps separé des choses n’est pas un être absolu, mais une chose ideale*” (G III, 595; see also 622).[^k2.95]

The ideality of space and time gives for Leibniz the solution of one of the problems that he calls the “two famous labyrinths” (“*deux Labyrinthes fameux*,” *Theod*. Préface, G VI, 29), namely the problem of continuity and of the indivisibility of matter.[^k2.96] That is the riddle with which Kant occupies himself in his second antinomy, namely that in an extended, if it is considered as a whole, its parts must be presupposed, while it at the same time must be infinitely divisible. For Leibniz the ideality of space and time makes possible the solution of this problem:
> “*Spatium nihil aliud est praecise quam ordo coëexistendi, ut Tempus est ordo existendi, sed non simul. Les parties, autant qu’elles ne sont point marquées dans l’étendue par des phenomenes effectifs, ne consistent que dans la possibilité, et ne sont dans la ligne que comme les fractions sont dans l’unité.*” (G III, 612)

And also later he formulates:
> “*Dans l’ideal ou continu le tout est anterieur aux parties, comme l’unité Arithmetique est anterieure aux fractions qui la partagent, et qu’on y peut assigner arbitrairement, les parties ne sont que potentielles: mais dans le reel le simple est anterieur aux assemblage, les parties sont actuelles, sont avant le tout.*” (G III, 622)

The solution that Leibniz here gives rests on the distinction of the relation between whole and parts with reference to reality on the one hand and that with reference to ideality on the other. In reality the whole, as one commonly believes, presupposes its parts. It is composed of its parts. They always come before the whole, for before the whole comes to existence, its parts must already have existed. But in the ideal relation the whole, as in mathematical objects, comes before its parts: E.g. the line is the whole over against its part-lines or points of which it is supposed to be formed. But in fact its parts were not first given so that the line could be composed therefrom. The line is not an aggregate of part-lines, but these part-lines are first possible when that whole line is thought as divided. That is just the same in the relation between the one and its fractions. Leibniz asserts that “the mathematical body cannot be resolved into first constitutive moments, without further ado, that it is absolutely nothing real, but only a mental construct (*mentale quiddam*), which designates nothing but a possibility of parts, by no means however something actual” (G II, 268).[^k2.97] Its parts are thereby only possible, but not actual. Space is likewise “*res mentalis*” (ibid.).[^k2.98] It is not composed of its parts, but first given.

The problem of the priority of the parts before the whole according to Leibniz arose because one believed that space or matter were substances.[^k2.99] If it is real, it must be able to be decomposed into no longer divisible part-unities. But they are nothing other than phenomena, “well-founded appearances” (*des phenomenes bien reglés*).[^k2.100]

So far it has become clear that Leibniz conceives space and time as ideal relations. But as relations of what? Whether Leibniz, as Kant criticizes, thinks these *relata* as substances does not seem clear. He often speaks of the order of existences or especially with reference to space of the order of coexistences.[^k2.101] In a draft for a letter to de Volder he seems to hold the *relata* of the spatial and of the temporal order for monads, but then carefully adds the restriction “insofar as it [the order] is expressed through our appearances.”[^k2.102] That space and time act directly on the order of the monads is in Leibniz impossible, because the monads themselves are not extended (this too is correctly found in Kant’s presentation of Leibniz) and do not belong to the phenomena, but are “real.”[^k2.103]

In this doctrine of the ideality and apriority of space and time one may see a similarity to the Kantian space–time doctrine.[^k2.104] But the ideality of space and time is a correlate of the reality of the monads, which Kant would never allow. Space and time are ideal because they consist only of the relations between the monads. This ideality is thus not the transcendental that Kant ascribes to them. Correspondingly the objective reality of space and time would never be acknowledged by Leibniz.

#### 6.2. Space and Time as Order in Wolff and Baumgarten

Wolff makes no secret of the fact that in the definition of space and time he follows Leibniz, especially his third writing to Clarke.[^k2.105] He first defines time: “*Tempus adeo est ordo successivorum in serie continua*” (Wolff *Ontologia*, § 572) and space: “*Spatium est ordo simultaneorum, quatenus scilicet coexistunt*” (*Ontologia*, § 589).

In these definitions it is indeed clearly said that space and time both constitute orders, but it is not mentioned of what. But in the definition of *ordo* Wolff sets out that what is arranged beside one another or follows one another are things (*res*).[^k2.106] And things are defined as what is as existent or can be thought as possible (*Ontologia*, § 243).[^k2.107] A thing is, as we executed above, a Something in the sense of a possible that is thinkable. Possible thereby meant essentially not-being-contradictory. Therefore he says: “*Spatium adeo resultat ex possibilitate coëxistendi*” (*Ontologia*, § 591). Similarly he says in the *Cosmologia*: “*Res in universo connectuntur quoad spatium*” (*Cosmologia*, § 56) and of time: “*Res in universo connectuntur quoad tempus*” (*Cosmologia*, § 57). Insofar as space is actual only as order of the things in it, “there can be no space if things are not present that fill it” (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 46). This Kant criticizes in his Aesthetic (also in connection with time; see in Wolff *Deutsche Metaphysik*, §§ 94ff. § 547).[^k2.108]

Baumgarten defines space as “*ordo simultaneorum extra se invicem positorum*,” time as “*ordo successivorum*” (*Metaphysica*, § 239). He however likewise does not conclusively set out wherein the respective order consists and what at all is supposed to be meant by the concept of order (*Metaphysica*, § 239 and § 78). Alone the circumstance that these definitions occur in a section titled “*Monas*” lets one conjecture that they have to do with the order of substances. In the “*Cosmologia*” he however says more clearly that space arises from the aggregate of the monads (*Metaphysica*, § 399). Temporality (*aetas*) is then explained through the succession of the monads (*Metaphysica*, § 397).

#### 6.3. Sensibility as Confused Concept?

A view related to cognizing that Kant generally lays at the basis of his critique of Leibniz, which he carries out by means of the determinate concepts of reflection, consists in the fact that Leibniz understands sensibility only as confused idea and thereby does not assume sensibility as an original faculty independent of the understanding. It is this aspect of the critique that has very often been criticized as a misunderstanding of Kant’s.[^k2.109]

A classification of concepts or of ideas in Leibniz on which this critique of Kant’s could rely is to be found in the *Meditations*.[^k2.110] As Leibniz there explains, the cognition of sensible qualities is clear (*clara*), but ‘confused (*confusa*).’ Clear cognition means the cognition in which one cognizes again as such a thing that one earlier cognized; i.e., clear cognition is a sufficient cognition thereby that it can be distinguished from other similar cognitions. If by contrast one cannot recognize the thing again, the cognition remains obscure (*obscura*). Clear cognition can be either distinct (*distincta*) or confused (*confusa*). A cognition is distinct if one can indicate marks of the cognized thing through which it can be distinguished from others. Thereby Descartes’s maxim of cognition “*clare et distincte*” itself is distinctly defined, which according to Leibniz he had neglected. To have a clear but confused cognition then means to be able to distinguish a thing from others, but not to know exactly through which marks it is distinguished from them. Moreover a composite concept (*notio*) consists of several marks, namely of several part-concepts. Even if a composite concept is clear and distinct, i.e., its marks are cognized in order to distinguish it from others, the marks themselves can thoroughly not be clear and distinct. If all marks, including the marks of these marks, are cognized clear and distinct, up to the original concepts (*primitiva notio*), i.e., if they can no longer be analyzed, the cognition is adequate (*adaequata*), and if not, it has to count as inadequate (*inadaequata*). (G IV, 422ff.; also *Disc. metaph*. § 24, NE, II, § 29)

In the *Lettre touchant ce qui est indépendant des sens et de la matière* (G VI, 499ff.), which was addressed to Queen Sophie Charlotte of Prussia, Leibniz beside this still gives another explanation. There he divides the contents of cognition into three classes: 1. merely sensible; 2. simultaneously sensible and intelligible; 3. merely intelligible. The first, sensible cognition is clear but confused. He names as examples the cognition of colors, tones, smells, taste and touch qualities, which all belong to outer sense. The second sensible and simultaneously intelligible cognition concerns e.g. numbers and figures. This cognition does not belong to any individual outer sense, but it is common to several outer senses. He calls this cognition “*sens commun*” (G VI, 500).[^k2.111] This is by contrast to the first clear and distinct and belongs to the purely mathematical sciences, namely to geometry and arithmetic.[^k2.112] The third is also clear and distinct, but only intelligible; it concerns the objects of the pure understanding. This cognition is for Leibniz more important than the others, because it designates a reflective cognition or a self-consciousness: hereby cognizing includes the cognizer himself (“*moy*”) and makes him the object (G VI, 501f.). To this kind of cognition belong the metaphysical concepts such as substance, cause, effect, activity, similarity as well as fundamental concepts of logic and of morals (G VI, 502).

To this Leibnizian doctrine of concepts (*notiones*) three points are to be noted: First the *Meditations*, like the *Nouveaux Essais*, aim at criticizing Locke’s empiricism and bringing out the necessity of the intelligible concepts. In that respect in this connection the emphasis lies on the third merely intelligible cognition. But Leibniz also makes clear that cognizing from outer sense, although it always remains confused, must be principally distinguished from the other two kinds of cognition; and Leibniz insofar does not relate it to the relative degrees of clarity.

If he second in these two writings, the *Meditationes* and the letter to Sophie Charlotte, gives the perceiving of a color (in those the red, in this the blue) as example of a clear but confused comprehending, he wants thereby to make clear that something must be pointed out as immediately present if one wants to let someone cognize it. Otherwise formulated: It is impossible to explain a color to a blind person (G IV, 422). Color definitions are ostensive. That does not mean that in sensible cognition cognition remains behind its possibilities. Rather what is at issue is to understand in this way that the clear and confused concept is yet principally distinguished from the clear and distinct.

Third: If we take into account Kant’s view of the Leibnizian doctrine of concepts, one must say that Kant unambiguously ignores that Leibniz too like he himself ascribes mathematical cognition, which is clear and distinct, to sensibility, which however is a special form of sensible cognition, namely of the “*sens commun*.” Nevertheless one should exercise a certain caution if one now immediately reproaches Kant with the misrecognition of Leibniz’s epistemology. For that letter to Sophie Charlotte was first published in the second half of the nineteenth century by Klopp. Leibniz indeed also mentions this “*sens commun*” in his *Nouveaux Essais*. But he calls it, differently from in that letter, “the mind itself” (*l’esprit même*) and its concepts “concepts of the pure understanding” (*des idées de l’entendement pur*), which is unacceptable for Kantian criticism (G V, 116).

Overall one can say that Leibniz indeed determines sensibility as confused cognition, but this confusion consists not in the lack of cognition, but in its principal difference from distinct cognition. In Leibniz, one must concede, with a view to this difference much remains indistinct.[^k2.113]

Still to be noted is that Leibniz speaks of the differences of clear and confused, of distinct and indistinct concepts (*notiones, ideas*) or cognitions (*cognitiones*), but not of those of the faculties of these cognitions. He has thus not related that difference to these. It is first Wolff who has done that (*Ontologia*, § 50).

In the literature it was frequently rather ascribed to Wolff to understand the sphere of sensibility only as that of confused concepts.[^k2.114] Wolff takes over in the *Philosophia rationalis sive logica* Leibniz’s distinction of concepts. There he introduces the designations ‘*clara*’ and ‘*obscura*’ (*Phil. rational*. § 80), ‘*distincta*’ and ‘*indistincta*’ (*Phil. rational*. § 88), ‘*completa*’ and ‘*incompleta*’ (*Phil. rational*. § 92) and ‘*adaequata*’ and ‘*inadaequata*’ (*Phil. rational*. § 95)[^k2.115] as ‘*[n]otiones differentia formalis*.’ But it is already previously said by him that this formal difference “*modo cognoscendi desumitur*” (*Phil. rational*. § 77), is determined according to the mode of cognizing.

The human cognitive faculties themselves are made the theme in the *Psychologia empirica* and the *Psychologia rationalis*. There they are presented with the faculties of desire in a sequence of stages—from the lower faculties to the upper. Wolff even divides the cognitive faculties into lower (‘*Facultatis cognoscendi pars inferior*’) and upper (‘*Facultatis cognoscendi pars superior*’) and grounds this division and staging with the clarity and distinctness of the representations: “*Facultatis cognoscendi pars inferior dicitur, qua ideas & notiones obscuras atque confusas nobis comparamus*” (*Psychol. emp*. § 54) and: “*Facultatis cognoscendi pars superior est, qua ideas & notiones distinctas acquirimus*” (*Psychol. emp*., § 55). The lower cognitive capacity holds for the obscure and confused, the upper for the clear and distinct concepts. Further it seems to support the Kantian critique that Wolff draws attention to the following: “*A parte inferiori ad superiorem non progressus fit per saltum, sed per gradus intermedios*” (*Psychol. emp*., § 233). Wolff thus traces the difference of the cognitive faculties back to the degree of clarity and distinctness of the representations.

Here he takes over the classification of representations from Leibniz that we have just described; clear (*clarus*) or obscure (*obscurus*) and distinct (*distinctus*) or confused (*confusus*). He defines these in almost the same manner as Leibniz.[^k2.116] Thereby the representations are divided into three, namely into the obscure, the clear but confused, and the both clear and distinct (*Psychol. emp*., § 50).

Also in the *Deutsche Metaphysik* Wolff divides the cognitive faculties into three groups. Thereby he connects this doctrine with the gradations of the monads that Leibniz adduces in the *Monadology*. 1. A soul with exclusively obscure representations, i.e., that can distinguish nothing from one another, is encountered e.g. in the state of sleep (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 900; also *Psychol. Rational*. § 59). 2. The clear but indistinct representations, in which “one in the whole representation can indeed distinguish much from one another, but not in the particular [representations],” are produced through the “senses,” the “power of imagination,” and “memory,” which we are supposed to have in common with the animal soul (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 901). 3. Finally “understanding,” “reason,” and “will” appear, which are able to cognize clear and distinct; i.e., they cognize things both generally and in their particular marks as separated from one another. Wolff also follows Leibniz therein that he calls the souls with this kind of representations “spirits” (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 902, § 896).

Baumgarten connects the theory of cognition and of concepts with monadology. After he in the first part of the *Ontologia* has determined the monad as substance that is endowed with a faculty of representation, he subsequently also introduces the different degrees of the representing of these monads; he speaks of: *clarus, obscurus, distinctus*. Similarly to Wolff the monads whose representations are merely obscure or only in part clear are introduced as the mere or the sleeping monads (“*MONADES NUDAE (sopitae)*,” Baumgarten *Metaphysica*, § 401). The monads that represent the world not only clearly but at least in part distinctly have understanding (*Intellectus*) and are called spirit (*Spiritus*) (*Metaphysica*, § 402). All this is treated more precisely in empirical psychology. Confused (*confusus*) cognition one has if one cannot really distinguish the marks of an object. If one can, the cognition is distinct (*distinctus*). Baumgarten does not define clarity (*clarus*) and obscurity (*obscurus*), but introduces them and subordinates the stage of confusion and that of distinctness to that of clarity, as in Leibniz and Wolff. Baumgarten likewise according to the degree of clarity and distinctness of cognition divides the cognitive faculties into the upper and lower (*Metaphysica*, § 520, § 624).

After this passage through the doctrines of Leibniz, Wolff, and Baumgarten it must with a view to the amphiboly chapter be said that Kant’s “critique of Leibniz” in the amphiboly chapter de facto does not turn alone against Leibniz, nor against a single one of his pupils, but aims at a whole conglomerate of doctrines that stem from this milieu. Moreover Kant unfortunately has not made the objects of his critique explicitly and going into the particular identifiable. Nevertheless we have arrived at the insight that Kant himself in some points stands near Leibniz: In the polemical writing against Eberhard he designates the *Critique of Pure Reason* as “the proper apology for Leibniz” (AA VIII, 250). But we could also convince ourselves of the decisive differences between the two, especially in the treatment of sensibility. The “critique of Leibniz” in the amphiboly chapter hits Wolff and Baumgarten more often than Leibniz, but at least the substance-doctrine presented and criticized by Kant can be ascribed to Leibniz and can by no means count as that of his pupils. Kant seems to have laid at the basis of the amphiboly chapter cognitions of Wolffian and Baumgartenian metaphysics that he already long possessed, and newer, only just acquired cognitions of Leibnizian philosophy.

If Kant in particular attacks the doctrine of the Wolffian school concerning sensibility and lays the last pair of concepts of reflection matter and form at the ground of the others, he thereby elevates his transcendental mode of thought over against the rationalist: Sensibility may not be understood as a confused cognitive faculty distinguished only according to the degree of distinctness. Kant’s assertion that it is an independent faculty and thereby an autonomous, eminently important source of cognition makes former metaphysics and its rational conception of ontology impossible, which ascribed no proper value to sensibility; therein expressed itself how “rational,” formal-logically this ontology was structured. Leibniz developed an epistemology that could have led to the principal distinction of the cognitive faculties, but it remained fragmentary; thereby however every possibility of development toward a non-formal-logical logic was also cut off.

Hegel’s logic takes over from the Wolffian system the foundational function of logic. Over against Kant, who sets the critique of the cognitive faculties on itself and lets it precede the proper system, logic in the Hegelian system forms the foundation for the other system-parts, the philosophy of spirit included. Thus this logic is no longer a psychology, whereas Kant’s transcendental logic according to Hegel always proves itself as such. On the other hand Hegel no longer believes that after transcendental logic was introduced by Kant a systematic foundational function can still be ascribed to formal logic. For Hegel’s logic, which steps into the place of both former ontology and transcendental logic, there results therefrom the difficult task of having to carry out a metacritique of the Kantian critique of school metaphysics, without thereby again falling back into dogmatic rationalism.

---------------

# Part II: From the “Concepts of Reflection” to the “Determinations of Reflection”: Developmental-Historical and Systematic Preconditions of Hegel’s Presentation of the Determinations of Reflection

## Third Chapter: The Genesis of the Logic of Essence in the Development of Hegel’s Logic and the Concepts of Reflection

We turn first to the developmental history of Hegelian logic. Hegel began to occupy himself with logic only in the Jena period. He developed his conception of logic in this Jena period and further in the Bamberg and Nuremberg periods, and thereafter, as concerns its inner articulation and its place in the system of philosophy, retained it in the main and in principle. But in the logics from the Jena period there is still lacking 1. the system-concept in which logic is at the same time metaphysics, so that the two still appear separately, at least as concerns the outer shape of the articulations of the logic. And 2. there is still lacking an inner-logical conception that explicitly makes the logic of essence its theme. But the early engagement in Jena with the traditionally ontological problems that Kant took up in the amphiboly chapter can be read off from the metaphysics that was still independent of the logic. The concept of a logic that includes metaphysics first prevails at the end of the Jena period. The logic of essence appears for the first time at the beginning of the Nuremberg period in a new conception of the articulation of the logic, which is, however, still distinguished from that of the later *Science of Logic*. Hegel seems indeed not to have had from the beginning the intention of taking up the Kantian concepts of reflection into his logic, but that engagement with ontology drives him to it.[^r1.1]

### 1. Logic as Introduction to Metaphysics (Jena)

#### 1.1. Logic and Metaphysics in the First System-Concept (1801/02)

In his first semester in Jena (1801/02) Hegel announced two lectures: one on the introduction to philosophy and another on logic and metaphysics.[^r1.2] Fragments of these lectures are testimonies to Hegel’s earliest direct engagement with logic that is available to us today.

In the fragment on “Logica et metaphysica” Hegel lets logic precede metaphysics. The former is said to offer the “introduction to philosophy” (GW V, 272), while the latter is the “proper philosophy.” In this version of the relation of logic and metaphysics it is emphasized that logic belongs to reflection or to the understanding, thus to finite cognition. Over against this finite cognizing stands speculation as infinite cognition. But this standing-over-against must not, Hegel says, be understood as opposition. Reflection results from the abstraction from the identity of which only reason disposes. Insofar as this is so, reflection or the understanding presupposes speculation or reason, even if in the presentation logic precedes metaphysics.[^r1.3]

Thus the setting-up of the finite forms of thinking proves to be that of forms that are supposed to have arisen from speculation through abstraction,[^r1.4] and the carrying-out of the program of a setting-up of these finite forms constitutes the first task of logic. The second consists in the presentation of the endeavor of the understanding to dissolve the oppositions that it contains into identity. Hegel calls this “imitation” of reason by the understanding. But the identity that the understanding can reach is only the formal. In a third step the finite forms are sublated by reason. Yet the result can remain only a negative cognizing, insofar as it cannot leave the domain of logic, which still stands over against metaphysics (GW V, 272).

Concerning this triad, which Hegel describes as “object of a true logic,” it must be remarked that every stage of this triad already is supposed to presuppose the infinite cognizing of reason. As introduction to philosophy there serves “from this speculative side alone the logic” (GW V, 272).[^r1.5]

This concept of the transition from logic as the science of the finite to metaphysics as that of the infinite in the fragment on “Logica et metaphysica” has rightly been related in the literature to the concept of the self-destruction of reflection in the *Difference* essay:[^r1.6] In the first section “Various Forms that Occur in Present-Day Philosophizing” Hegel mentions that “the sole interest of reason” consists in sublating oppositions that the then philosophy of the culture of the understanding had fixed. It is always a time of division and separation that arouses “the need of philosophy.”[^r1.7] Hegel finds here the separation to be sublated especially between the subject and the object or between the subjective and the objective subject-object. The task of philosophy, to “sublate this sundering” (GW IV, 64), means at the same time: “[T]he Absolute is to be constructed for consciousness” (GW IV, 16)—the Absolute being thereby designated as “identity of identity and of non-identity” (GW IV, 64). Hegel presents this sublation of the oppositions—analogously to that fragment “Logica et metaphysica”—as transition from the understanding or reflection to reason or speculation.

When he speaks of reflection in this period, he uses the word in the negative sense. It is, with the understanding, the faculty of restricting itself to the finite and thereby of bringing out oppositions. It is however noteworthy that Hegel once also uses the word in the positive sense. Reflection “as instrument of philosophizing” is that of reason (GW IV, 16).[^r1.8] But insofar as reflection is the faculty of positing something in consciousness, it always remains entangled in the subjective and thereby in an opposition to the objective. The Absolute is thus already limited when it is reflected. Precisely for this reason that reflection which appears as that of reason and which sublates the oppositions produced by itself is driven precisely through itself into its self-annihilation.[^r1.9]

Hegel does not, however, speak in the *Difference* essay directly of the concept of his logic or of his system itself. But for the claim that Hegel here pursues the same concept of logic and of its relation to philosophy as in that fragment “Logica et metaphysica,” some evidences can be adduced. In Hegel’s critique of Reinhold in the *Difference* essay what is at issue is Reinhold’s assertion in the *Contributions to an Easier Survey of the State of Philosophy*, namely that the *Wissenschaftslehre* is to be reduced to logic. Criticizing this Hegel says:[^r1.10]
