> “If thinking is not posited as the absolute activity of reason itself, for which there is absolutely no opposition, but thinking counts only for a purer reflecting, i.e. such a one in which one only abstracts from the opposition, then such an abstracting thinking from the understanding cannot even come out to logic, which is supposed to comprehend reason within itself, much less to philosophy.” (GW IV, 18)

Here it is asserted that abstracting thinking or reflection, which seeks to procure identity for itself by only abstracting from the opposition, is itself not sufficient even for logic; rather according to the fragment “Logica et metaphysica” logic as science belongs to the understanding. But the two do not exclude one another, because logic in that fragment was consciously determined “from the speculative view” as introduction to philosophy. Second, the addition that abstracting thinking belongs neither to reason nor “much less to philosophy” suggests that Hegel here too conceives logic as introduction to philosophy, because then philosophy itself elevates itself above logic. At a later place—again in the context of the critique of Reinhold—Hegel says:
> “Logical cognition, if it really proceeds as far as reason, must be led to the result that it annihilates itself in reason; it must recognize, as its highest law, the antinomy.” (GW IV, 82)

Logic must finally arrive at self-annihilation—i.e. at antinomy—if it is treated with reason. Antinomy as self-annihilation of the understanding is the highest law of logic. This positive result is to be ascribed to metaphysics.

The systematics of logic and metaphysics in this period, which we can develop only on the basis of these meager materials, seems not yet to be entirely fixed. For while Hegel in the fragment Logica et Metaphysica lets logic as “introduction to philosophy” precede metaphysics as “proper philosophy,” he says in the second fragment from the lecture on the introduction to philosophy that logic, as first part of philosophy, is “as science of the Idea itself metaphysics” (GW V, 263).[^r1.11] It suggests itself that Hegel already here holds logic and metaphysics for One science. Moreover it appears inconsistent that he ascribes to logic an introductory function, if one takes seriously that he, as later in the Preface to the *Phenomenology of Spirit*, quite at the beginning of the first fragment says: “that philosophy as science neither needs an introduction, nor tolerates an introduction” (GW V, 259). One must in particular attend to the passage in which he says that, as concerns this collegium, he proceeds in “propaedeutic respect,” i.e. he begins with finite cognized and then passes over to the infinite. This makes it doubtful whether he delivered this collegium at all only according to a determinate system-conception.

After the definition of the object of logic Hegel indicates in this fragment its order: 1. the general forms or laws of finitude, to which he notes in the margin “general logic[,] categories,” 2. the subjective forms of finitude, of concrete concepts, judgments and inferences, even in this sequence of stages, and 3. the sublating of finite cognizing by reason (GW V, 273). This order of logic coincides with that which Hegel indicates in the announcement to this lecture: 1. “the general or transcendental logic, namely the system of the \<finitude> forms of finitude”; 2. “the ordinarily so-called logic or the construction of subjective reflection”; and 3. “the consideration of reason, which destroys the forms of finitude set up in the logic” and “makes the transition to metaphysics.”[^r1.12]

What he then would concretely have wanted to execute of this articulation can be inferred only through that transcript by Troxler, which however in comparison with this program remains incomplete. Some grounds can be adduced for the claim that the entire content of this transcript can be related to the first part of the presented articulation: 1. Troxler concludes it with “End of transcendental logic,” which Hegel in the fragment and in the announcement ascribes to the first part of logic.[^r1.13] 2. The transcript follows, if also not without deviations, the order of the *Critique of Pure Reason*: sensibility (space and time, pp. 65ff.), understanding (doctrine of categories, pp. 68ff.) and reason or dialectic (soul, world and God, pp. 74ff.). 3. The objects of ordinary logic—concept, judgment and inference—do not occur here. But if we investigate the text more closely, the matter becomes more complicated, because it seems that Hegel here did not remain with the presentation of the finite forms of transcendental logic, but dissolves them and relates them to his own concept. He thus seems already there to include or anticipate the third part.

It is interesting that Hegel nearly at the beginning of this lecture takes up the concept of matter. He had already in the *Difference* essay criticized the Kantian doctrine of the construction of matter in the *Metaphysical Foundations of Natural Science*:
> “[With] the poverty of attractive and repulsive forces, it [mechanics] has already made matter too rich; for force is an inner that produces an outer, a self-positing, = I, and such a thing cannot belong to matter from the purely idealistic standpoint; he [Kant] comprehends matter merely as the objective, that which is opposed to the I. […] If nature is only matter, not subject-object, no such scientific construction of the same remains possible, for which cognizing and cognized must be One.” (GW IV, 69f.)

One can already see here that the later essence-logical categories such as the relation of force and its expression and of the inner and the outer are developed in the treatment of the thematic of the construction of matter through the attractive and repulsive forces. But for Hegel the construction of matter is first possible when the identity of the subject and the object is secured. In Troxler’s transcript matter is “posited as identity and non-identity” (Troxler, 63). Hegel then poses the four questions concerning matter, the answering of which is supposed to unmask the inadequacy of the understanding. As the third he takes up the question of the divisibility of matter, in which the concepts of the whole and of the part occur (Troxler, 64). Still more important in connection with the amphiboly chapter is the fourth question: “Is it animated or dead” (Troxler, 64).[^r1.14] Thereby Hegel criticizes the doctrine of the construction of matter through the attractive and repulsive forces. According to Hegel Kant thereby explains nothing: “In general force posits nothing; it is a transforming of an outer into an inner, of a contingent into a necessary, with which however nothing is explained” (Troxler, 64). That the concepts outer and inner are here drawn upon also recalls to us the amphiboly chapter of the *Critique of Pure Reason*.[^r1.15]

Despite these points one cannot say that Hegel in this critical presentation of transcendental logic completely takes up the thematic of the amphiboly chapter. But one can at least see here that Hegel sees in the definition of matter a fundamental problem of transcendental logic, in the matter which Kant, together with form, lays at the foundation of all transcendental reflections (*KrV*, A266/B322). The execution of metaphysics is lacking both in the fragment and in the transcript.

#### 1.1.2. Faith and Knowledge

*Faith and Knowledge* (1802) does not indeed thematize logic as a part of the system. But here Hegel subjects, beside Jacobi’s and Fichte’s philosophy, the whole of Kantian criticism to critique, and indeed under the designation of the “philosophy of reflection.” Hegel here faults Kantian transcendental philosophy as a whole: It gives up the cognition of the Absolute itself and carries on only the critique of the cognitive faculty. According to Hegel Kant did not understand the genuine significance of his own question, “How are synthetic judgments a priori possible?,” for the answer to the question was supposed to secure the unity of thinking and of being, but in Kant it remains with subjectivity, i.e. with thinking.

But at the same time Hegel sees in Kantian epistemology a model of the mediation of thinking and of being. We saw in the Introduction that Hegel in the *Science of Logic* compares his concept of the logical triad with that of Kantian transcendental logic, and ascribes to essence, which takes the place of the amphiboly chapter, the function of the mediation of being and thinking. Here, in *Faith and Knowledge*, this mediating function is however performed by the productive imagination, which Hegel, departing from Kant, calls reason, if also “as appearing in the sphere of empirical consciousness” (GW IV, 329).

In this Hegelian writing nothing is to be learned about the concept of logic, and also in the belonging, peculiar analysis of the *Critique of Pure Reason* the amphiboly chapter is not mentioned at all. But the task that was also to drive the further work on his logic is clearly to be reconstructed: What is at issue is to assure oneself of the unity of being and of thinking. Kantian criticism had shown a transcendental path to mediate the two; but this result is for Hegel insufficient, insofar as it still remains in the subjective domain of the critique of cognition and finally leads not to knowledge, but only to faith. Hegel sees his task in carrying further this Kantian approach and thereby overcoming the boundary set by it.

#### 1.2 Execution of the First Logic- and Metaphysics-Concept in the Jena System Drafts II (1804/05)

##### 1.2.1. Logic and Relation

The developmental history of Hegel’s logic can first be taken up again where the transmission sets in again; under this proviso the logic and the metaphysics in the System Drafts II indicate a next developmental step. In this fair copy the structure of the logic seems to follow that sequence of the division that Hegel sketched in the fragment from the lecture on Logica et Metaphysica (1801/02). He first presents the Kantian categories. Then follow the logical forms concept, judgment and inference. Finally the logic passes over through proportion to metaphysics. But this articulation does not correspond quite exactly to that order. The Kantian mathematical categories, quality and quantity, are treated (in reverse order) in the first part with the additions of “C. Quantum.” The second part “Relations” comprises “A. Relation of Being” and “B. that of Thinking”; in the former the categories of relation and in the latter the logical concepts, concept, judgment and inference, are treated. It is striking that for the modal categories a proper point of articulation is lacking, but they are treated in passing in the relation of being. The third part “Proportion,” which corresponds to the doctrine of method of traditional logic and which in Hegel’s mature logic will be included in the absolute Idea, forms a bridge to metaphysics, which thus counts as that third stage of logic which has as its task the sublating of finite cognizing by reason (GW V, 273). This writing also contains the first and last execution of an independent metaphysics of Hegel’s that has been transmitted to us. It is therefore the last, because thereafter logic and metaphysics are brought into the logic and joined together.

In this concept of logic and metaphysics in the System Drafts II a ‘logic of essence’ is still just as little to be found as in the earlier logic. But Düsing’s remark is interesting in this connection, that the structure of the later logic of essence is prefigured in the logic of “infinity”: Düsing finds between the categories of simple relation on the one hand and of infinity as well as of the relations on the other a fundamental gulf. While the dialectic of the categories of simple relation is first brought to them by “our reflection” and is not contained in their own determinations, as later with the categories of being in the *Science of Logic*, the dialectic of the categories of infinity and of the relations includes a reflection belonging to them.[^r1.16] This claim is also supported by the fact that infinity is introduced into the “Relations,” and the logic of essence is a logic of relation. Moreover infinity in the Nuremberg Logic or also the Nuremberg Encyclopedia constitutes the third part of the logic of being, and essence is directly joined to it.

Beside this context to be inferred and the more unambiguous connection of the relation of being with the chapter ‘Actuality’ in the later logic, the places are to be named that anticipate the later logic of essence and are in that respect especially important, because they also stand in connection with the Kantian amphiboly chapter. Hegel takes up the concept of force with the category of causality in the ‘Relation of Being,’ whereby the concepts of reflection ‘the inner’ and ‘the outer’ are also included (GW VII, 46, also 59). The latter are however only mentioned in passing and not distinguished from force and its expression. In the second Remark on causality Hegel gives a further detailed treatment of the concept of force, where the concept of matter also appears as the substance in which force inheres (GW VII, 56). This critique of the concept of matter as the external connection of substance and force is later taken up again both in the Understanding chapter of the *Phenomenology* and in the Appearance chapter of the later logic of essence. Hegel carries out in 1804/05 his critique of the concept of matter exemplarily on the force of attraction and the so-called force of affinity and shows that both express only tautologies and are therefore “perfectly empty” (GW VII, 60). This exemplary critique can be regarded as a preliminary work to the Appearance chapter of the later logic.

The attractive and repulsive force, which together are supposed to construct matter, already appear however in “\[s]imple Relation”; their thematization at this systematic place can be connected in a forward-looking manner with the fact that they are later treated in a Remark of the logic of being, more precisely of being-for-itself. Hegel says in 1804/1805:
> “In that matter \[is] the absolute equilibrium of attraction and repulsion, which is nothing other than \[that] of differential unity and of differential plurality, they are thus purely ideal, pure thought-things, determinacies that are sublated in and for themselves, have no reality, \[and of which] the one cannot appear as singular.” (GW VII, 26f.)

Through the analysis of the forces Hegel arrives at the cognition of the ideality of matter.

##### 1.2.2. Principles in Metaphysics

Hitherto we have attempted to exhibit in the Logic (1804/05), especially in the “Relation of Being,” an anticipation of the later logic of essence. We now turn still to an anticipation of the logic of essence in the other part of the system, namely in metaphysics. In its first part “Cognizing as System of Principles” a prototypical treatment of the later determinations of reflection is to be found, in which Hegel takes up the traditional logical-ontological principles.

Hegel had already announced in the fragment to the lecture Introductio in philosophiam that in this lecture he would “dictate propositions concerning the Idea of the Absolute” (GW V, 264). But that these “propositions” could have related to those traditional logical-ontological propositions is improbable. For the Idea of the Absolute, which is to be set forth with these “proposition\[s],” will “be presented in its highest simplicity” (GW V, 264) and is therefore reachable only with “firm clear intuiting.” In the *Difference* essay Hegel already criticized the then philosophical tendency since Reinhold of beginning philosophy with a principle and of deducing from it. For a principle can first be posited through abstraction and thereby be opposed to something Other, insofar as it excludes the Other through abstraction from itself. The proposition of equality A = A e.g. excludes the proposition of diversity A = B or A = not-A. It is opposed to this. If however the former is posited, the latter must also be posited, and there comes to an antinomy of both propositions (GW IV, 24).[^r1.17]

This critical stance toward the form of the principle in the *Difference* essay appears quite to correspond to that of the later period. But a few months after the appearance of the *Difference* essay Hegel determines it in the sketch for his lecture Logica et Metaphysica as the task of his metaphysics “to construct completely the Principle of all philosophy” (GW V, 274). “Principle” is properly the German translation of the Latin word “principium.” Nevertheless it remains open here whether Hegel with “Principle” means that of a systematic principle.

It becomes clear, however, that Hegel did not implement this critique of propositional form in his system-concept, when he treats “the principles” in the Metaphysics (1804/05). This proves itself to be all the more important, because this metaphysics, which is here still conceived as separated from logic, is supposed to present the in-itself of reason, while logic can at most reach the cognition of antinomy or dialectic and remain standing there. The principles were thus here not supposed to be mere abstractions.

That Hegel in the Jena System Drafts II (1804/05) lets the logical (principl-) propositions belong not to logic, but to the metaphysics following it, also lets us learn that he holds the ontological significance of these propositions for essential. From this there results—against Hegel’s intention at that time—an unavoidable entanglement of metaphysics with logic. The “system of principles” at the same time gives expression to the “cognizing” that was reached at the end of logic (GW VII, 128, 111ff.), which becomes insightful when one takes into account that metaphysics was traditionally defined as the science of the first principles of human cognition.[^r1.18] Metaphysics proceeds in 1804/1805 in Hegel from proportion as relation between the relation of being and that of thinking. Cognizing is now however not a relation to an Other or External, but the “In-itself, which has withdrawn itself from all relation to another, and whose moments are themselves totalities, reflected into themselves” (GW VII, 124f.). But when once the difference of the moments is sublated and thereby self-sameness is reached, the transition to metaphysics has occurred. This course from reflection-into-itself to the logical-ontological propositions, with which the determinations of reflection in the elaborated logic also occupy themselves, recalls to us that of the beginning of the logic of essence in the *Science of Logic*, as we shall see more precisely still.[^r1.19] But this analogy must be restricted by the fact that the reflection that has here been reached is understood not as self-referential negation, but only as “indifference” (GW VII, 127).[^r1.20]

The propositions that Hegel treats here are A. the principle of identity or of contradiction (GW VII, 130), which he holds for one and the same, B. the principle of the exclusion of a third (GW VII, 132) and C. the principle of ground (GW VII, 134). Hegel seeks here on the one hand—corresponding to the then system-concept, according to which logic introduces into metaphysics—to determine the said principles so that they no longer, like the categories of logic, fall into an antinomy or dialectic; the principles are thereby supposed to be exempted from the passing-over into another. On the other hand he nevertheless tends to follow that approach of the *Difference* essay, according to which, when he relates these propositions to one another, it nevertheless results that thereby antinomies are found in and between them.[^r1.21]
The proposition A=A is for Hegel a formulation that expresses that indifference (*Gleichgültigkeit*) which was reached with ‘cognizing.’ Here it is presented with reference to ‘A’ that the determination loses its significance through the equating. In reflection it was “extinguished” and made ideal. The opposition has become in the proposition “pure shine” (GW VII, 131).[^r1.22] In this sublation of the opposition the proposition expresses its in-itself, i.e. its having-been-taken-out of the dialectic (in the negative sense).[^r1.23] But that the proposition A=A in its indifference must nevertheless each time take up with A a determination as determinationless shows the contradiction of the proposition, that what is not in-itself is posited as in-itself. Hegel already here comes to results that anticipate his later conception of logic: First he already speaks of the nullity of the content of the principle of identity: “The tree is tree; is the nothing of the cognizing of the tree” (GW VII, 131). Thereupon follows the transition to the uncovering of a further contradictoriness that this proposition contains. For Hegel at that time it is thus accordingly a contradiction that in this proposition a determination that does not reflect itself into itself, i.e. that can pass over into another, is treated as in-itself. The proposition expresses the contradiction that the not-in-itself proves itself as in-itself. This leads to the self-sublation of the proposition. This nullity that thus results betrays according to Hegel that the cognizing of what the principle of identity is must inevitably lead to antinomy or to opposition. He gives here a similar example as in the *Science of Logic*, but he also gives here an explanation departing from that for the example, which here runs: “the tree is tree.” At the beginning of the proposition: “The tree is” one expects that through the predicate the in-itself of the subject is expressed and cognized. “[B]ut: the tree is tree, precisely does not express the in-itself of the same, in that it does not present it as something reflected into itself” (GW VII, 131).[^r1.24] In this explanation it is presupposed that the predicate tree, like the subject, is no in-itself, i.e. no being reflected into itself, because it proves itself as a determination beside several others. Hegel in 1804/1805 does not see the relation of the predicate to the subject, which aims at a unity of self-sameness and reflection-into-itself and on the basis of which he will later carry out the transition from identity to diversity.[^r1.25]

Through the contradiction of the principle of identity already in 1804/05 precisely this identity falls apart, and the falling-apart leads over to diversity, which is characterized by the manner of speaking of “two sides” or also “two insofar-ases” (GW VII, 132); the “two sides” later become marks of the external reflection of diversity.[^r1.26] Differently from in the *Science of Logic* Hegel here already finds the contradiction: If “both \[are] in fact related to one another, and posited as one, then each sublates the other” (GW VII, 132). Thus they are first indifferent against one another and second “against the self-same” (GW VII, 132). Hegel does not derive from this untenable mutual exclusion and inclusion of both against one another the determination of reflection of difference, but here passes over to the Many.

Hegel’s engagement with the principle of the excluded third in the Metaphysics of 1804/05 leads him to the categories of ‘simple relation’ and at the same time to the later being-logical categories “One and Many.” The Many became thinkable in that identity divided itself into its sides and each side became independent. The Many now reflects itself in its independence into itself and becomes indifferent against Other. But this Other is now by contrast for its part the unity. The Many as indifferent thus excludes the Other as third (GW VII, 132). In this excluding relation of the Many over against the One that is doubly determined: The Many is on the one hand “equal to itself, and so it is opposed to unity” (ibid.). But it is on the other hand itself a unity and “not distinguished from it” (ibid.). The Many already itself unifies the many singulars that belong to it. Hegel emphasizes thereby that through the fact that the Many is now considered in metaphysics, its state as it were, in comparison with the immediate relation of logic, in which however the Many and the One also already appeared, has changed, i.e. has become other (GW VII, 7f.). There the Many “had been considered only in the relation to its opposed” (GW VII, 133), i.e. in its relation to the singular, which with other singulars constituted the Many. We have by contrast through the transition from logic into metaphysics reached a stage of being and of cognizing in which it becomes possible to consider the in-itself or the Absolute itself. For the Many this means that it now is able to enter into relation to the unity that belongs to it itself.

But the unity is in this relation doubly related to the Many: The Many and the unity are on the one hand opposed. And precisely thereby the unity is something opposed. It is as moment of the Many included in this; it is the “One of the Many” (GW VII, 133). Thus there arises the division of the Many “into a Many that is a Many, and into Many that is no Many” (GW VII, 133). Hegel continues:
> “This Many, as it is in itself, excludes every third; the third would be the unity of the two opposed of the Many, but this unity is itself one of its members.” (GW VII, 133)

The unity is first the Other of the Many. But it is also the Many itself. Thus the Many is “the other of itself” (GW VII, 134). These two mutually contradicting sides of the Many must not be so conceived as if they were expression of different manners of consideration, which in their diversity could avoid the contradiction. Thereby the Many falls into an antinomy. It is “in itself the Absolute Contradiction” (ibid.).

Thus the principle of the excluded third ends, as also already previously the principle of identity, in a contradiction. Moreover it also sublates itself, because this contradiction appears as the third that was supposed to be excluded in this proposition: “It is thus in fact not the Many, as an Either, Or, the Many that only divides itself into opposed members, but the third of these members, or the absolute, immediate unity of both, and a simple destroying into itself” (GW VII, 134). This unity as self-destruction of the excluding is called “the absolute concept” (GW VII, 134).

Hegel still expands the significance of the demand for a third: The in-itself as unity results neither from the first principle of identity nor from the second principle of opposition, but only the third, i.e. the principle of ground, leads to this result.

Ground as the third is “the unity of itself and of its opposed determinateness” (GW VII, 135). In that it at the same time became the Other of itself, it is as its returning unity precisely yet the “self-same determinateness” or the in-itself (ibid.). This ground is however also determined in relation to cognizing. Ground is the in-itself, for “[t]he ground shows itself hereby as the reflection of cognizing itself, as the simple closed into itself” (ibid.). The in-itself, which previously was present in cognizing for us, is now present for cognizing, and indeed as reflection of cognizing itself.

The course from the first principle, that of identity or of contradiction, to this third, the principle of ground, Hegel grasps as the “realization of cognizing” (GW VII, 136): In the first principle the in-itself was only formal. Only through the third proposition is a content gained. But this content was not simply to be received from outside. It is said to have come forth or become from the form of identity itself: “[I]ts content \[i.e. that of cognizing—T. O.] is the in-itself-being determinateness, posited as a self-same, according to the first principle; it is for cognizing first the formal In-itself, or it posits itself as its first moment, and indeed according to the determinateness of this first moment, in simple form” (GW VII, 135). It is however necessary for cognizing “to become itself once more out of its having-become” (GW VII, 135), i.e. to give this form its content for itself. Hegel makes here a comparison, that the content of cognizing can be conceived as a point that presents the negative unity, over against cognizing itself as the circle or the periphery. But the point of ground has itself come forth from the movement of cognizing that is expressed through the periphery.

From this there results first: Ground has the same double relation to cognizing as unity to the Many. Ground is a moment of cognizing itself, but at the same time it is as its object, what is to be cognized, but this it is only as an Other of itself. Second, thereby the first and the second principle, i.e. first the principle of identity and of contradiction and second the principle of the excluded third, are to be included in the process of cognizing, although with these themselves there was not directly talk of cognizing. But to this state of affairs the title of the part “I. Cognizing as System of Principles” (GW VII, 128) already drew attention. Third, here the task is to be solved that was posed at the beginning of metaphysics.[^r1.27] Grasped in terms of the theory of reflection this systematically means a process that leads from external reflection to immanent, inner reflection. In this “second becoming” (GW VII, 136), which owes itself to the reflection of the principle of ground, that externality is to be sublated which still characterizes metaphysics as long as our cognizing has not become cognizing for itself.

But despite the unity of ground and of cognizing the difference of both against one another initially remains preserved. “Or,” Hegel says, “it \[the difference] is sublated, but cognizing has not yet sublated it” (GW VII, 136). Ground and cognizing now pass over into “the total reflection into itself” (ibid.). According to Hegel their moments nevertheless remain standing indifferent against one another. Therefore they would now have to be related to one another. But the still remaining indifference of the moments is said to stem from the fact that each moment is at the same time totality. This still-to-be-sublated relation anticipates the opposition of the positive and the negative in the elaborated *Science of Logic*, in which each, the positive as well as the negative, presents the totality of both moments. Since the overcoming of the exhibited reflexive, contradictory relation between cognizing and ground for Hegel at that time presents a still unsolvable task, he must yet content himself with being able to indicate for the sublating of this reflexive relation scarcely more than a meagerly prefigured path that reflection necessarily has to take. Although the necessity of the sublating course of reflection to unity with itself is supposed, according to Hegel of 1804/1805 already as well, to have become actual through the fact that the path “has in fact already been embarked upon” (GW VII, 137), this path of realization remains a postulate.

Hegel thereby gives a failure to be recognized, the failure of his concept of metaphysics as sublation of dialectic. The “ground,” which relates to the “relation of being” in logic and insofar became one with it, is nevertheless distinguished from this, in that it now in metaphysics is able to appear as being in and for itself or as reflection-into-itself. This means, for Hegel at that time, that ground is “taken out of the vanishing in the dialectic” (GW VII, 137). But the path of ground that he drafts is itself “its dialectic” (ibid.). Hegel still attempts to distinguish this dialectic—so one would properly have to say: this dialectic of metaphysics—from that of logic. “[B]ut that ground is in this relation posited as in itself, i.e. itself although moving, yet as indifferent against it and as unchangeable thereby” (GW VII, 137). But ground is still something moving. For the resolution of this problem Hegel appeals to the traditional concept of substantiality, according to which substance remains indifferent against the change of the accidents. Like substance, ground is indifferent against its (still changing) moments, which it however ideally includes within itself.

This solution is in comparison with the later little convincing and indicates that it still holds itself within the framework of traditional metaphysics. This is not irrelevant for the fact that Hegel in this metaphysics after the discussed “principles” treats the objects of traditional *metaphysica specialis* (“B. Metaphysics of Objectivity,” GW VII, 138ff.), which will no longer occur in his mature system. Hegel will rather hold fast to the contradictory structure of totality and of its moments, i.e. to dialectic as movement or unrest. This he will include as truth in his theory. He was at that time not yet in a position to gauge the full reach of the contradiction of totality with itself; only later will it stand before his eyes what distance from traditional metaphysics becomes necessary in order to be able to confront the problems of dialectic and of its sublation at all. Over against this in 1804/1805 in Hegel “indifference” or indifference is still predominant.


#### 1.3 Dissolution of Metaphysics into Logic in the ‘Logic’ of the *Phenomenology of Spirit*

The theme of the reconstruction of the ‘logic of the *Phenomenology of Spirit*,’ which Fulda posed for himself in his book *The Problem of the Introduction*, was then taken up by Pöggeler and provoked between the two in the sixties of the last century a heated discussion. In his counter-writing *The Composition of the Phenomenology of Spirit* Pöggeler, after the attempt to relate the conception of the Phenomenology to the articulation of speculative philosophy in the Jena System Drafts III, comes to the following concluding thesis: “There is no Hegelian logic whose course could open up for us the course of the Phenomenology as one uniformly and from the outset unambiguously articulated. Rather the Phenomenology helps us to infer a logic for Hegel’s last Jena period.”[^r1.28]

In what follows we do not go chapter by chapter into the problem of the correspondence of the Phenomenology and one of the Jena logic-drafts. But we briefly take up one of the focal points of this discussion that especially concerns our interest.

Fulda asserts, for which he adduces as evidence the Nuremberg Logic, the correspondence between the categories of relation and the course of the Self-Consciousness chapter of the Phenomenology. Insofar as thing, force and appearance in the Nuremberg Logic precede the categories of relation, self-consciousness is supposed to correspond to these. Against this Pöggeler asserts that Hegel did not yet at that time have the composition of the Nuremberg Logic that Fulda lays at the foundation of the Phenomenology. One must therefore rather orient oneself by Logic and Metaphysics in the System Drafts II and by the articulation of speculative philosophy in the system-sketch of the System Drafts III.[^r1.29] Life and Cognizing in this e.g. correspond to self-consciousness. What we wish to take up from this discussion concerns however not self-consciousness, but the understanding. Both authors are thereby agreed that the essence-logical categories, especially those of force, correspond to the understanding in the *Phenomenology of Spirit*. Fulda ascribed the categories of relation to self-consciousness, while Pöggeler sees the categories of relation already entangled in the presentation of perception and of the understanding. The latter is further of the opinion that “the shape of the understanding in the Phenomenology […] is already beyond the categories of relation and at the attempt to think the Idea of relation.”[^r1.30] In principle I agree with this. But I wish to draw attention to a point that both overlook. As we have seen above, the concept of force appears in the Jena Logic where causality is at issue. Hegel says “that force properly expresses the whole causality-relation within itself” (GW VII, 45). In the same section he even designates it as “the Idea of the relation” (GW VII, 52).[^r1.31] By the relation is thereby meant not relation in general, but the causal relation. If one takes this correspondence of the concept of force with the category of causality seriously, it suggests itself to connect it with a prospect onto the further development of Hegelian thinking; thereby it strikes one that Hegel those concepts such as thing and properties, force, the inner and the outer, the supersensible world etc., which later are treated in the chapter ‘Appearance’ of the logic of essence, still occur in the Phenomenology in such a way that they are entangled with the categories of relation. Thus (1) the Perception chapter corresponds to substantiality; (2) by contrast force and its expression, the inner and the outer, which are treated in the Understanding chapter of the Phenomenology, correspond to causality. And (3) infinity—as alternation of the extremes—corresponds to reciprocal action.

Against this background it then also explains itself that perception is included in the being “that becomes other (relation),”[^r1.32] as the system-sketch of speculative philosophy in the System Drafts III expresses it, because namely obviously the categories of relation of substance and of its accidents correspond to perception. The correspondence between substantiality in the Jena Logic and perception of the Phenomenology can also be evidenced by the fact that he there speaks of the “And” (GW VIII, 39, and elsewhere), which corresponds to the “Also” in the Phenomenology.[^r1.33]

If this hypothesis, in my view correct, of the correspondence between the two last chapters of consciousness in the Phenomenology and the relation of being in the Jena Logic holds, the following also suggests itself: Hegel in Nuremberg divided the two series of categories, the later appearance-logical categories on the one hand and the categories of relation on the other, into the two parts of ontological or objective logic. To the appearance-logical categories are joined—still separated from them—the logical-ontological propositions, and together they form essence, which constitutes actuality. Both will later be brought together through the alteration of the composition of the logic and will jointly structure the logic of essence.

Second it is to be remarked that Hegel in the *Phenomenology of Spirit*, by means of the appearance-logical categories, arrives at the cognition that in each case two opposed categories determine one another, thereby enter into reciprocal action and then form a unity, which he will later present purely logically in the logic of essence of the *Science of Logic*.

### 2. Genesis and Development of the Logic of Essence (Nuremberg Period)

From Bamberg Hegel writes to Niethammer that he is working further on his “logic, as it now begins to become, to which I in Jena scarcely laid the foundation and have not lectured extensively,”[^r1.34] Insofar as this is so, one is to proceed from the assumption that Hegel in the Bamberg and especially in the Nuremberg period occupied himself intensively with logic. The realization of his plan to publish the logic soon then, despite the promise toward Niethammer, lasted until the year 1816.

Of a doctrine of essence as part of Hegelian logic in the narrower sense one can speak first in the Nuremberg period. Because we, on account of a largely unclarified transmission of text of the logic from the Bamberg period, where Hegel certainly also worked on it, cannot follow the developmental process of his logic between the end of the Jena period and the beginning of the Nuremberg period, there arises the impression that it suddenly appears with a new articulation, as it were as a wholly new logic.

Hegel had already announced in the “Self-Announcement” of the *Phenomenology of Spirit* that after the first part of the science the system of logic (and the philosophy of nature and of spirit) was to follow as its second part (GW IX, 446), which lets us with good reason conjecture that Hegel already in this period had given up the division into logic and metaphysics and had included the latter in the former. This is also recognizable in the Philosophical Encyclopedia 1808/09 for the upper class, insofar as Hegel here calls the first part of logic “ontological logic.”[^r1.35]

#### 2.1. Proto–Logic of Essence in Ontological Logic (1808/09)

Ontological logic, which is followed by subjective logic and the doctrine of Ideas, articulates itself into the three parts: I. Being, II. Essence and III. Actuality. The proto–logic of essence—if one wishes to designate it so—does not here, as later, constitute an independent part of the logic, but only a subsection of Ontological Logic, which moreover is still distinguished from actuality. But that “essence” is here intercalated between “being” and “actuality” and is still distinguished from the latter lets a decisive developmental step since the Logic and Metaphysics of 1804/05 be recognized. For Hegel there, following Kant’s categories, joined directly to simple relation, which contained the later categories of being, the relation of being (the categories of relation). But here as in the mature logic essence finds its place between the two parts, of which the one treats the categories of quality and quantity and the other the categories of relation.

The proto–logic of essence again contains three parts: “a.) The Concept of Essence,” “b.) The Proposition” and “c.) Ground and the Grounded.”[^r1.36] Concerning this three points are to be remarked: First: The core of this proto–logic of essence are the logical or ontological propositions, out of which the “determinations of reflection” are to be worked out. Hegel had treated these propositions in 1804/05 first in metaphysics. But here they stand between the categories of quality and quantity on the one hand and of relation on the other. This lets one conjecture that the inclusion of metaphysics in logic is accompanied by the intercalation of essence between being and actuality. Hegel adds to the propositions that he already took up into the Jena Metaphysics still the principle of diversity, which he formulates by means of the *principium identitatis indiscernibilium* (§34, GW X, 66). The propositions that Kant assigns to the two first pairs of concepts of reflection are thus here placed.

Second: Under “c.) Ground and the Grounded” Hegel here treats the categories that later become thematic in the logic of essence of 1813 in “essential relation.” These are the categories that stand in close connection with the third pair of the Kantian concepts of reflection.[^r1.37]

Third: The last pair of concepts of reflection “matter and form” is here not to be found in “c.) Ground and the Grounded,” and no independent part-title is allotted to it either. But Hegel takes up the concepts at the beginning of b.) “Proposition.”
> “The proposition contains the moments of remaining-within-itself, or of self-sameness, and of pure distinguishing. That would be pure matter, this pure form. But pure form is the doing remaining within itself, thus the same self-sameness which was called pure matter, just as this conversely is the undistinguished outside-one-another, and is not distinguished from pure form” (§ 31, GW X, 66).

Form and matter are here treated as two moments of the logic of the proposition. To form is ascribed distinctness and to matter equality. Hegel in the later logic has taken up these categories such as equality or identity, difference etc. into the chapter on the determinations of reflection, but not form and matter, whose thematic has rather left some traces in the chapter on ground. It is also to be remarked that Hegel already here rejects these categories of traditional metaphysics and as also later lets the concept of matter pass over into that of content (§32, GW X, 66), which likewise marks a step toward the mature logic. Pure form, which is said to be “the doing remaining within itself,” is as equality with itself also matter; matter, which is distinguished from form, is also itself form. Hegel seeks to replace this pair of categories through the new one of “form and content.”

The categories that he here draws upon in the treatment of “ground” and of the “grounded” also stand in close connection with matter. The problem of the whole and of the parts is in traditional metaphysics a problem of matter, which Leibniz called one of the labyrinths of philosophy and with which Kant engages in the second antinomy.

In the “Logic or Doctrine of Spirit for the Middle Class” from the same school year Hegel, after he lets the doctrine of consciousness introduce into logic, divides logic in the same manner into three parts as in the already mentioned Encyclopedia, apart from the fact that ontological logic was there called “Objective Logic” (Middle Class Logic: Doctrine of Spirit, Manuscript 1808/09, GW X, 29, also in the Dictation GW X, 116). But the articulation of this ‘Objective Logic’ is wholly different from that of the Encyclopedia for the upper class. Hegel introduces an articulation according to the cognitive faculties, which recalls the Kantian *Critique of Pure Reason*. In the chapter “A. Understanding,” which as a whole occupies itself with the categories (§33, GW X, 30 (Ms.); 116 (Dict.)), essence is intercalated between the parts that treat the first three Kantian categories, i.e. between “Being” and the “independent relation” (§34, GW X, 30 (Ms.)). As categories of essence mainly form, matter and ground are named (§39, GW X, 31 (Ms.); 116 (Dict.)). But Hegel introduces, similarly as in the already mentioned Encyclopedia, the categories of essential relation further under the main point of “ground” (§ 43, GW X, 31 (Ms.); 117 (Dict.)). The logical and ontological propositions are however here not contained, but first in the second part of Objective Logic, “b. Power of Judgment” (GW X, 32 (Ms.); 117 (Dict.)). This seems convincing, insofar as Hegel here articulates objective logic according to the cognitive faculties: The propositions are objects of the power of judgment, insofar as they as propositions have the form of judgment, while the understanding occupies itself with the categories as concepts of the understanding.

It is noteworthy that here the ontological character of the propositions is emphasized. Corresponding to this character, with every paragraph the ontological versions of the propositions are indicated beside the logical. Hegel divides the relations, following Kant, into the identical (analytic) and the synthetic (GW X, 32/117). The principle of identity or of contradiction expresses an ‘identical relation’ (GW X, 32f./118). Of the ‘synthetic relation,’ where “something is related to another determination” (GW X, 33/118), there first speaks the principle of diversity. The second synthetic relation is opposition, which corresponds to the principle of the excluded third. Opposition in general Hegel here designates as that between the positive and the negative. Ground or the principle of ground is then determined as the unity of diversity and of opposition (GW X, 33/118).

As the understanding corresponds to the categorical and the power of judgment to the ontological principles, so reason, again following Kant, corresponds to dialectic. Reason is even “the dialectical movement of the same \[of the determinations of the understanding and of judgment]” (§ 54, GW X, 34 (Ms.); 118 (Dict.)). It is noteworthy that Hegel here takes up again the categories of being, of essence and of the independent (here unconditioned) relations and presents their antinomies. The categories of each part of objective logic have their own dialectic or antinomy.[^r1.38] Hegel here lets the categories of being correspond to the first, those of essence to the second and those of the unconditioned relations to the third Kantian antinomy. Especially to be noted however is that Hegel first in the treatment of reason as dialectic presents his logic and the systematics proper to it. The inner logical progress from one category to another, such as the dialectic of being and nothing and their passing-over to becoming, is thus first to be found here, while the categories in the understanding were determined formally, i.e. without connection with one another or transition. Thus Hegel’s own logic first sets in here, while in the Encyclopedia of the same school year a beginning is already made directly with it. What he presents in the logic of this Encyclopedia fits the doctrine of reason of the logic that has its place here in the doctrine of spirit. This is illuminating when we take into account the connection of logic and dialectic. In that Hegel in this logic of the doctrine of spirit does not begin directly with his logic itself, but orders logic according to the stages of the cognitive faculties and ascribes his own logic to a determinate stage within this order of stages, he paradoxically thereby makes clear that his proper, at least however the objective, logic rests on a totalization of the dialectic or of the antinomies of the categories.

While the understanding only determines each category, the dialectic of reason consists “in the exhibiting of the nature of these determinations, or rather in the nature of these determinations themselves, of not being in themselves that as which they are posited in their determinateness, but of passing over into their opposed” (§ 55, GW X, 34 (Ms.); 118 (Dict.)). The categories of essence were matter, form and ground, of which the last contains the whole and the parts, force and its expression, and concludingly the outer and the inner. The antinomy of essence contains the second Kantian, thus that of the divisibility of matter (§ 73, GW X, 44 (Ms.); 123 (Dict.)).

Hegel derives the categories of matter and of form, which he had taken up directly in the understanding, from the concept of essence. Essence is determined over against being, which is characterized through manifold determinations, as follows: “Essence 1) is the in-itself-being whole of manifold determinate being taken out of the changeableness and perishability of the finite” (§ 70, GW X, 42 (Ms.); 122 (Dict.)). The beginning of the dialectic of essence already shows a close kinship with that of the logic of essence in the *Science of Logic*. As first stage Hegel introduces the “inessential,” which embodies an own and new manner of viewing being, yet without even mentioning the concept of the “essential” opposed to it. Essence is “the whole of the determinations of determinate being” and necessarily has the inessential within itself, which here is called not “shine,” but “appearance” (§ 70, GW X, 42 (Ms.); 122 (Dict.)). When essence (unity) is grasped in its appearance (manifoldness), the difference of matter and form becomes recognizable in it. That was previously, i.e. in the “Understanding,” determined as “equality with itself,” this as “the active, or the positing of difference in general” (§§40f. GW X, 31 (Ms.); 116f. (Dict.)). Matter is also in “Essence” on the one hand the equality with itself that excludes determination or inequality. “It is the remaining determinate being” (§ 71, GW X, 43 (Ms.); 123 (Dict.)). The active altering form remains outside it, and matter only receives the form. It is the indeterminate, formless unity. But it is on the other hand also “the many being absolutely outside one another within itself” (§ 71, GW X, 123 (Dict.)), which properly belongs to distinguishing, i.e. to form. Then matter is a unity of indifferent, self-same continuity and of formal diversity. Likewise it is form that is many; thus it is also a unitary name of this many. Thus both would have the same determination. “Their truth is thus the unity of matter and form, the forming and the form of matter or the form which has its matter in itself” (GW X, 43 (Ms.); 123 (Dict.)).

Hegel here gives the formed matter or the material form, this unity of matter and form, in contrast to later works no name.[^r1.39] Nevertheless matter and form follow almost the same logical course as in the *Science of Logic*. Distinguished from this by contrast is that in the doctrine of spirit 1808/1809 to this dialectical presentation of the categories—matter and form—the Kantian second antinomy is joined, which however seems plausible, because it too already concerned the concept of matter. Precisely from the described ambiguity of the concept of matter as continuity and plurality resulted the Kantian antinomy of the divisibility of matter.

The first essential relation as also the second are treated by Hegel in connection with the second antinomy. The relation of the whole and of the parts thematizes the problematic of the antinomy of matter. For what is at issue in this antinomy is whether matter consists of parts or not (§ 74, GW X, 45 (Ms.); 124 (Dict.), cf. *Critique of Pure Reason* A434ff./B462ff.). The second essential relation, that of force and its expression, indeed joins to this, but it does not properly relate to the antinomy, but to the problematic of matter that Kant takes up in the amphiboly chapter and executes in the *Metaphysical Foundations of Natural Science*. Hegel here joins together the themes that concern matter and that Kant had treated separately, even if the inner and the outer are lacking.

The logical course of the two first essential relations in the doctrine of spirit (1808/09) is again almost the same as that of the Encyclopedia from the same year. Moreover “Ground and the Grounded” in the doctrine of spirit contains the complete three parts of the later essential relation. Therefore the ground of the omission of the last relation, that of the inner and outer, seems to be sought in the fact that what was at issue were different teaching-concepts. The outer and the inner could not be related directly to the second Kantian antinomy, but the dialectic of each pair of categories, which shows itself therein that one determination of reflection passes over into the other, is here—if still in an immature form—already unfolded.

Both in the doctrine of spirit and in the Encyclopedia of 1808/09 it is to be found in agreement that the categories of the later chapter on “Ground” are bound into the process of the passing-over of the categories that Hegel here develops for essential relation. Matter and form underlie every category; this holds both for the dialectic of the categories of essence in the doctrine of spirit (1808/09) and for the executions under “II. Essence” of the Encyclopedia from the same year. The concept of content, which expresses the unity of matter and form, first appears in the chapter on “Force and its Expression” (Doctrine of Spirit 1808/09, § 75, GW X, 45 (Ms.); 124 (Dict.); Enc. 1808/09, §§ 38ff., GW X, 67). Thus the transition from the relation of form and matter to that of form and content occurs parallel to the transition from the whole and its parts to force and its expression. The categories “ground,” “condition” and “the unconditioned” are also to be found here. With force and its expression in the doctrine of spirit (1808/09) at the end “the unconditioned” is reached (§ 76, GW X, 46 (Ms.); 124 (Dict.)), for which reason the next stage, which earlier was called “the independent relation” (§34 and §44, GW X, 30 and 32 (Ms.); 116f. (Dict.)), is now called the “unconditioned” relation (§77, GW X, 46 (Ms.); 124 (Dict.)). In the Encyclopedia for the upper class (1808/09) the relation of the inner and of the outer counts as “the unconditioned” (§ 42, GW X, 68).

To exhibit the dialectic of reason, which consists “in the nature of these determinations themselves, of not being in themselves that as which they are posited in their determinateness, but of passing over into their opposed” (§ 55, GW X, 34 (Ms.); 118 (Dict.)), therein the doctrine of spirit (1808/09) sees the proper task of reason. The same function falls to the logic of the Encyclopedia (1808/09) as a whole.

The two logics from this school year let one recognize which categories Hegel in his first version of a logic of essence held for belonging to essence. They are first form and matter as well as the inner and the outer beside two pairs of other categories that however stand with them in a close connection. The “proto–logic of essence” of the Encyclopedia (1808/09) includes the ontological principles that are connected with the two first pairs of the Kantian concepts of reflection, (1) sameness and diversity and (2) agreement and conflict. Hegel has transferred the Kantian concepts of reflection into his logic and with them formed his first transmitted logic of essence, yet not without the addition of other categories and modifications, wherein the own necessities of the Hegelian concept of logic make themselves valid, which however here is just beginning to arise.
#### 2.2. From the Proposition to the Determinations of Reflection (1810/11)

In the school year 1809/10 Hegel lectured in the upper class beside the philosophy of nature on logic.[^r1.40] Its transmitted content however contains only subjective logic. In the middle class he again made the doctrine of spirit the object of instruction; for this he used the dictation of the previous year in a revised version. He excluded by contrast the proper logic, namely the logic-part that would have been to be ascribed to reason, and passed over from the doctrine of consciousness directly to psychology.[^r1.41] [^r1.42] In this year Hegel thereby followed the prescriptions of the so-called general Normativ, which prescribed lecturing on logic in the lower class.[^r1.43] But this instruction then in practice comprised only the introductory treatment of the stages of the forms of representation, such as sensation, representation itself, the sensible object, space and time, categories and concepts; the latter then join themselves to logic. But the repeated lecture of this logic mostly remained standing with offering an introductory explanation of traditional formal logic, an explanation of its terminology and of some laws. It is significant that Hegel here in the explanation of the logical propositions prefers the determinations such as identity, diversity etc. to the propositional forms (GW X, 144f.).

It is the Logic for the Middle Class (1810/11) that transmits the next developmental stage of Hegel’s logic and also the last before the publication of the *Science of Logic*. As concerns the main articulation, it is to be remarked, 1. that he indeed divides the whole logic into three parts, but no longer into (1) objective or ontological, (2) subjective logic and (3) the doctrine of Ideas, but into being, essence and the concept (\[§5], GW X, 220 (Dict.)).[^r1.44] The doctrine of Ideas was already in the philosophical Encyclopedia for the upper class of 1808/09 included in subjective logic. But it stood in this subjective logic beside the doctrine of the concept (§ 1, GW X, 264).[^r1.45] 2. “The independent relation,” which was here renamed into “Actuality,” constitutes, as in the *Science of Logic*, the third section of essence (GW X, 68 (Ms.); GW X, 233 (Dict.)). Although the first two sections are lacking and the transmitted manuscript first sets in with the third section and also in the dictation of the instruction of this school year the designation “objective” for this first and second ‘part’ is not to be found, the existence of a bipartite articulation into an objective and a subjective logic can nevertheless be conjectured from the fact that Hegel precisely calls the logic of the concept the subjective logic (GW X, 181 (Ms.); 246 (Dict.)). Thereby here the framework of Hegel’s mature logic has arisen.[^r1.46] If we look into the second part “Essence,” which comprises the former ‘essence’ and ‘the independent relation,’ it is immediately to be remarked that the section ‘Appearance’ is newly intercalated, which then contains “A. The Thing,” “B. Appearance” and “C. The Relation” (GW X, 160ff. (Ms.); 229ff. (Dict.)). Under the last subsection ‘C. The Relation’ there also appear those relations that in the ‘proto–logic of essence’ named and presented by me above were treated under the point of articulation “Ground and the Grounded”: “the whole and the parts,” “force and its expression” and “inner and outer.” This section still retains the same role as in the year 1808/09, namely of passing over to ‘actuality,’ the former ‘independent relation,’ but ‘thing,’ ‘appearance’ and ‘relation’ now make each only a subsection of the section ‘Appearance.’ It is important that the content that in the Encyclopedia of 1808/1809 was treated under the theme of the “proposition” now takes the place of the first section of “Essence” and is now provided with the title “The Determinations of Essence” (GW X, 158 (Ms.); 227 (Dict.)). Here the place can be marked at which Hegel has freed himself from the traditional logical-ontological principles and instead has passed over to treating the determinations of reflection that underlie those principles. Admittedly there can still be read off from the section that occupies itself with the determinations of reflection a division that hangs together with those propositions, i.e. the principle of identity, the principle of the excluded third and the principle of ground; it is now articulated into four and not into three subpoints: namely the determinations of identity, of diversity, of opposition and of ground.

It is moreover worth mentioning that the Kantian doctrine of antinomies, concerning which the General Normativ for the middle class prescribes lecturing, first appears in an appendix. From this it results that the doctrine of antinomies, as it was formulated by Kant, does not for Hegel belong directly to the objects of his logic. As the logical-ontological propositions reformulate themselves into the determinations of reflection, so Hegelian logic thematizes the Kantian antinomies not directly, but indirectly, and this can count as their concrete application, which first results through Hegelian logic.[^r1.47]

The conceptual pair matter and form is made thematic in the Logic of 1810/11 in “B. Appearance” (GW X, 162ff. (Ms.)), i.e. it follows after the determinations of essence and its determinations of reflection. This procedure departs both from the *Science of Logic*, which on the one hand treats matter and form in the chapter on ground and on the other treats matter in the chapter on existence, and from the published Encyclopedias, which thematize matter and form only in the section “c. Thing,” which is now contained in the chapter on “\[e]ssence as ground of existence,” and in the section “a. The World of Appearance” in the second chapter of the doctrine of essence, “Appearance.” If one considers the dictation-transcript of the Logic of 1810/1811, which Hegel revised in the years 1812/13 and 1814/15, one can follow the trace of this alteration: Admittedly the concept matter, which already appeared in the section “1. Thing” also in the manuscript, remains standing (§§45, 46: GW X, 230 (Dict.)). But the paragraphs in the section “2. Appearance,” in which Hegel thematizes the relation of matter and form and derives from it the concept content, are wholly struck out in the later versions (§§49–52, GW X, 230f. Anm. (Dict.)). Thereby the elaboration of the concept of the logic of essence for the *Science of Logic*, with whose composition Hegel was just occupied, has obviously exercised a certain influence.

From this it results that the systematic place of the conceptual pair matter and form in the Nuremberg period still wavers. In the *Science of Logic* Hegel has once more placed it at the old place, namely in “Ground,” and treated it as extensively as never. But in the Heidelberg Encyclopedia he has abandoned this concept and included matter and form, together with what is treated under the theme of “content,” in the thematic of the “thing.”

In 1810/11 Hegel has come to the concept of the tripartite logic and thereby to the concept of the logic of essence that he thereafter in principle retains. The subarticulation of the logic of essence however still changes after the *Science of Logic* of 1813 in the three published Encyclopedias. But with this tripartite main articulation of the logic its objects are brought into an order no longer altered. According to the text transmitted by Rosenkranz the thought that is supposed to constitute the object of logic is threefold: “1) The Categories; 2) Determinations of Reflection and 3) Concepts.”[^r1.48] This can also be confirmed through Hegel’s own instructional manuscript of 1810/11: He determines the object of subjective logic as follows: “Subjective logic has no longer the category and the determination of reflection but concepts as its object” (GW X, 181—emphasis T. O.).[^r1.49] It is here obvious that Hegel sees in the categories and the concepts of reflection the objects of objective logic, namely of being and of essence.[^r1.50]

That it was Kant who led Hegel to the concept of the logic containing metaphysics is recognizable from a passage from the private expert opinion to Niethammer; which Hegel wrote in the year of the publication of the logic of being and shortly before the publication of the logic of essence.[^r1.51] There Hegel says:
> “According to my view of the logical the metaphysical falls wholly and entirely into it. I can cite Kant hereto as predecessor and authority. His critique reduces the hitherto metaphysical into a consideration of the understanding and of reason. Logic can thus according to the Kantian sense be so taken that besides the ordinary content of so-called general logic, that designated by him as transcendental logic is connected with it and sent on ahead” (GW X, 825)[^r1.52]

Kant has made the metaphysical into the mere investigation of the cognitive faculties, especially of the understanding and of reason. But Hegel here nevertheless names Kant as predecessor who also conceived a metaphysical logic. Transcendental logic, in which the metaphysical becomes the theme, must now be sent on ahead of ordinary logic. Then Hegel names the new content of this metaphysical logic: It is “the doctrine of the categories, concepts of reflection, and then the concepts of reason. — Analytic and Dialectic” (GW X, 825). As concerns the sequence of categories, concepts of reflection and of reason, it suggests itself that Hegel with the concepts of reflection means those that are set up and worked through in the Kantian amphiboly chapter, and thereby had the amphiboly chapter before his eyes in the composition of the logic. That mention of the amphiboly chapter in the logic of the concept which I cited in the Introduction (p. 23) thus contains no subsequent justification; rather the working-through of the logic in the Nuremberg period led Hegel to the engagement with Kant’s transcendental logic, including the amphiboly chapter.

But neither the categories, nor the concepts of reflection and of reason, that Hegel takes up into his logic prove themselves as such as were merely deduced from the critique of the cognitive faculties. Hegel’s logic distinguishes itself from the Kantian “psychological” logic, whose categories (in the wide sense) suffice only to engage with the appearing world. Hegel’s determinations of reflection have objective significance, while Kant’s concepts of reflection aim only at subjective comparing. The understanding and reason, which both, both in Hegel and in Kant, belong to the objects of “logic,” present however in Hegel no mere cognitive faculties, but are the Logos itself, which determines being in general. Hegel takes up the concepts of reflection, by means of which Kant subjects Leibniz–Wolffian ontology to critique, into his logic of essence, but this is part of a concept of logic wholly other over against Kant.


## Fourth Chapter: Determination and Reflection. The Approach to the New Treatment of the Concepts of Reflection

### 1. The Concepts of Determination and Reality in Kant and Hegel

If one takes into account the fourth pair of the concepts of reflection, which Kant lays at the foundation of the others, not in the connection of ‘form and matter,’ but of ‘determination and the determinable,’ one has to go not into the logic of essence, but into the logic of being. ‘Determinateness’ is the title of the First Section of the logic of being and is for Hegel synonymous with the concept ‘quality’ (GW XI, 41/XXI, 66; GW XI, 43/XXI, 68).[^r2.1] In the first edition of the logic of being the second subsection of the second chapter ‘Determinate Being’ is titled “B. Determinateness” and again its second part “2. Determinateness,” which now contains “a) Determination.” In the second edition the chapter is reformulated. ‘Determination’ then also appears in a subsection beside ‘constitution’ and ‘limit’; ‘determinateness’ itself however no longer appears as title of a subsection, but only as the title of the first section ‘Determinateness (Quality).’

Regard for the logic of being is also relevant for this reason, because understanding of the logic of essence is first possible through that of the logic of being, especially of the mode of being of determinate being, which is subordinated to determinateness. Essence is, as will still be executed below, determined over against being.[^r2.2] But this being is not pure being, which constitutes the beginning of the logic of being as of logic in general, but “determinate being,”[^r2.3] i.e. determinate being, to which only a subsection of the logic of being is devoted (GW XI, 59/XXI, 96), which however at the same time determines this as a whole.[^r2.4]

Hegel identifies in the logic of essence—ignoring the mentioned contentual diversities—the logic of being with quality. Neither quantity nor measure nor their articulations, but quality and especially determinate being is supposed to give a typical picture of the logic of being in general. The logic of being is however not simply to be summarized under this one mark, as Hegel pretends at the beginning of the logic of essence; rather it contains various contents.[^r2.5] Hegel says, summarizing the whole logic of being: “Being is the Immediate” (GW XI, 241). But of immediacy in the precise sense one can speak only with pure being. For determinate being is already mediated through becoming, which however hides itself behind it (GW XI, 59). Hegel’s well-known formulation that the logic of being is the logic of transition and the logic of essence that of shining is also not entirely illuminating, because the reflexivity of the categories of essence, which is their most important mark, already appears with some categories of being, such as determinate being and being-for-itself.[^r2.6]

Despite these systemic inconsistencies we take seriously Hegel’s thesis from the beginning of the logic of essence, that essence is the successor of being, which is understood in the sense of determinate being or of determinateness. In the following investigation Hegel’s radical alteration of the significance of the traditional metaphysical categories such as determination and of the concept “reality” closely hanging together with it is to be made transparent.

#### 1.1. The Concept of Reality in Kant and Hegel’s Critique

Reality and determination hang closely together in Hegel. Reality is the character that determinate being has as determinate being. It is Kant who, as Hegel himself mentions (GW XI, 64/XXI, 99), has taken up these two concepts in his critique of the ontological proof of God. The concepts and their relation separate Kant and Hegel so deeply that their differing manners of conception in this connection are to be clarified.

Hegel takes up in the first chapter ‘Existence’ of the second section of the logic of essence the theme of the proof of God, thus a traditional theme in the history of metaphysics, with which Kant also occupies himself in his third theoretical dialectic. At the beginning of the section ‘Appearance’ Hegel presents a process in which ground as unconditioned sublates itself and determines itself to existence as groundless. Thereby logic again reaches the immediacy that it left upon entry into essence, but now through the self-negation of essence or of ground. That is the arising of the world which we—from its ground—have immediately before our eyes: “Essence is Being” (GW XI, 323), whereas the logic of being was the process in which being becomes essence. “Being is Essence” (ibid.). Reflection leads being through essence back to being, but to the being of appearance:
> “In that this \[essence—T. O.] however is ground, it determines itself really, through its self-sublating or into-itself-returning reflection.” (ibid.—emphasis by T. O.)

What “its self-sublating or into-itself-returning reflection” is supposed to be must still be clarified in the present work. Hereto we look at the word ‘real.’ Appearing or existing (in the sense of standing-out) means that essence determines itself really. It is precisely the concept ‘reality’ on account of which Kant criticizes the traditional ontological proof of God and Hegel for his part exercises a metacritique.

What is reality? We today use the word ‘real’ in the sense of ‘actual’ or ‘factual.’ It means that something is present outside our consciousness, especially in space, and is experiencable for us. The Hegelian use of this word in the above quotation can on the one hand be understood in this sense. “Essence determines itself really” then means that essence steps out into empirical actuality. On the other hand the concept ‘reality’ in Hegel has a specific significance.

Attention has often been drawn to the ambiguity of the word in Kant.[^r2.7] The word has mainly two significances, a traditional and a modern. One ground for why the two significances occur in Kant can be seen in the fact that he philosophizes in the time of the change of significance of this word; he stands as it were between two significances.

When Kant e.g. speaks of the “empirical reality” of the pure forms of space and time, he uses the word “reality” in the sense of actuality.[^r2.8] They are not merely subjective representations, nothing imaginary, but objective and opposed to ideality.[^r2.9] Reality is moreover one of the qualitative categories, beside negation and limitation. The category of reality corresponds to the affirmative judgment and is opposed to negation, which for its part corresponds to the negative judgment.[^r2.10] Here it is determined that reality is sensation in the form of intuition, while negation is the lack of the same: “What now in empirical intuition corresponds to sensation is reality (*realitas phaenomenon*); what corresponds to the lack of the same, negation = 0” (A168/B209f.). Reality is thus initially understood as objectivity or as givenness in appearance and in this sense of the word opposed to ideality.[^r2.11]

One sees here that Kant already uses the word ‘reality’ in the sense customary today,[^r2.12] but of course within the framework of his transcendental philosophy. With this concept of reality what is at issue is the object outside us.[^r2.13] But this is not completely independent of us, but given to our sensation; thus what is at issue is the object only in appearance. Real is what is present in space and time.

The other significance of the word “reality” was customary in traditional metaphysics before Kant. Reality is here the essence of the thing (*res*) or its determination, which as predicate can belong to the subject. Kant himself also uses the word in this sense when he refutes the ontological proof of God. Heidegger, who draws attention to the ambiguity of the word, traces this significance back to Baumgarten.[^r2.14] Yet the significance has been generally accepted since the Middle Ages.[^r2.15]

If one formulates the ontological proof of God, as Kant criticizes it, as a syllogism, it runs:
 - Minor premise: Existence (or determinate being) is a reality.
 - Major premise: God comprises all realities, or God is the epitome of all realities (*omnitudo realitatis*).
- Conclusion: Thus God has existence (or determinate being), or he exists.

Kant accepts the definition of God in the major premise: God is said to be the one who comprises all possible determinations. It is the minor premise that Kant refutes, the proposition that subordinates existence to reality. That famous sentence of Kant’s, that being is no real predicate, says that existence is no reality in this sense. Kant carries the sentence further: “Being is obviously no real predicate, i.e. a concept of something that could be added to the concept of a thing. It is merely the position of a thing, or of certain determinations in themselves” (A598/B 626). Being or existence is not what can be added as predicate to the subject. In the judgment “S is p” nothing is said concerning the existence of “S.”[^r2.16] Reality thereby means only the possible property of a possible subject: The word “reality” is used in the traditional sense.

Kant explains with the famous example of the hundred thalers that the actual hundred thalers are according to their content not distinguished from the ideal hundred thalers. For if the concept “hundred thalers” had more content than the actual, it would no longer be the concept of the hundred thalers. A concept of something has the same content as its actual object. Being is something that joins synthetically to the concept and does not alter the concept itself. Being is not an accident of something: “For the object in actuality is not merely analytically contained in my concept, but joins synthetically to my concept (which is a determination of my state), without through this being outside my concept these thought hundred thalers themselves being in the least increased” (A599/B627). Being thus does not belong analytically to the concept, but synthetically. Hegel criticizes precisely this conception of being when he says:
> “[I] as possessor of hundred thalers, or as non-possessor of the same, or also, I as representing hundred thalers or not representing them, is a different content. On the one hand it is a difference whether I only represent these hundred thalers to myself or possess them, whether they thus find themselves in the one or the other state, because I have once presupposed these two states as different determinations. On the other hand, each of these states taken particularly, they are within the same a particular determination of content that enters into relation to another, and whose vanishing is not a mere non-being, but constitutes an other-being. It is a deception that we shove the difference merely out onto being and non-being, whether I have the hundred thalers or not. This deception rests on the one-sided abstraction that leaves aside the determinate determinate being that is always present in such examples and holds fast merely to being and non-being.” (GW XI, 47f. Cf. XXI, 74f.)

In this hard-to-understand assertion of Hegel’s the radicality of his concept of being and of reality betrays itself. He equates, as is well known, pure being, which contains no determination and thus no negativity, with nothing. If there are no determinations or differences, there is only emptiness, nothing. Determinate being is in general only because it is distinguished from an Other. Only diversity or negativity lets something be there for Hegel.
