The difference that appears with determinate being is called by him “real diversity” (GW XI, 48) or “the real difference” (GW XXI, 75).[^r2.17] Therefore he also says: “Only determinate being contains the real difference” (2nd ed., 75).[^r2.18]

What Hegel criticizes in Kant in the last quotation is that the latter confuses the real difference that appears in determinate being with another, namely with the supposed difference of pure being and of pure nothing. When Kant speaks of the difference of having or not-having of hundred thalers, this is a real difference that Hegel sees between ‘Something’ and the ‘Other.’ Hegel already earlier faults that Kant applies the categories being and nothing to concrete states of affairs, because thereby one can speak only of “determinate being.”[^r2.19] But for Hegel being in the sense of existing in relation to an Other is “determinate.” For ‘being’ or existing means in Kant to be posited in the cognizing subject:
> “As Kant expresses himself, through existence something comes into the context of the entire experience; we thereby get one more object of perception, but our concept of the object is thereby not increased. — This means, as results from what has been explained, in fact so much: through existence, essentially because Something is determinate existence, it enters into connection with another or stands therein and among other things also with a perceiving.” (GW XI, 48/XXI, 75)

Kant meant that one can abstract from the cognizing subject when one speaks of being itself, while being can first be explained in relation to the cognizing subject. The cognizing subject grounds being (as phenomenon), but is itself no being. The transcendental level of the subject is strictly separated from the objective level of being. Hegel says thereto:
> “For if being and nothing had any determinateness through which they distinguished themselves, they would be, as was recalled previously, determinate being and determinate nothing, not pure being and pure nothing, as they still are here. Their difference is therefore wholly empty; each of the two is in equal manner the indeterminate; it therefore does not consist in them themselves, but only in a Third, in meaning. But meaning is a form of the subjective that does not belong in this series of the presentation. But the third in which being and nothing have their subsistence must also occur here; and it has occurred, it is becoming” (GW XI, 49f.).

The supposed difference of being and nothing consists in meaning, in subjectivity, which does not belong in the logical presentation. Here being and nothing are instead mediated through becoming and become determinate being, being-determined. Thus the problem of being or of existence in the customary sense becomes thematic in Hegelian logical systematics first with reality as determination. Here the peculiarity of the Hegelian concept of reality announces itself.

#### 1.2. Thoroughgoing Determination and Reality

The problem of existence, which Kant distinguishes from the problematic of reality, is drawn by Hegel again into the latter. According to him reality as determination and that as determinate being are the same. But before we go into this, it is to be investigated whether Kant pregnantly distinguishes the two concepts of reality.

We have above adduced as an example of the fact that Kant uses the word reality in the traditional sense his concept “epitome of all realities.” But does the concept of reality in this definition of God really here have only the significance as determination or attribute? Thereto we must more precisely look at the function of the “Transcendental Ideal” in the *Critique of Pure Reason*.

The third dialectic, the dialectic of the transcendental ideal, issues in a critique of ontotheology,[^r2.20] of the proofs of the existence of God. With this ideal, as with the other dialectics, it is criticized that theoretical reason oversteps its boundary. But it is to be remarked that the concept ‘God’ is at the same time called the “transcendental ideal,” while it is “transcendent” insofar as it surpasses our cognition. The adjective ‘transcendental’ means that what is determined therewith participates in the making-possible of the constitution of the object and of cognition. Thus the ideal in Kant is indeed unprovable, but it is mediately necessary for cognition. Kant calls the correct use of the transcendental ideal like that of the other transcendental ideas “regulative” (A644/B672).

How can the ideal be used regulatively? The possibility of every thing must rest on the “principle of thoroughgoing determination.” The principle demands that to every thing “of all possible predicates of things, insofar as they are compared with their counterparts, one must belong” (A571f./B599f.).[^r2.21]

Kant has taken over this principle from the school philosophy of the eighteenth century. Yet thoroughgoing determination, which Kant relates to the concept of God as epitome of all realities, in Wolff does not have directly to do with the concept of God, but with the singular individual. It is in him the principle of individuation: “*Est nempe principium individuationis omnimoda determinatio eorum, quae rebus insunt*” (*Philosophia rationalis sive logica*, pars II § 75).[^r2.22] The thing becomes an individual when all accidents that it has are determined. It is easy to see that this principle hangs closely together with the *principium identitatis indiscernibilium*. Wolff indeed holds thoroughgoing determination for the *ratio sufficiens individui*.[^r2.23] But the *principium identitatis indiscernibilium* is the presupposition for the fact that it is the sufficient ground of the individual.

In Baumgarten conversely the individual (*singulare*) is defined through thoroughgoing determination.[^r2.24] In him the principle of individuation means above all a *differentia numerica*. This he himself translates: “the precise determination” “of each singular” (§151). He even equates it with the scholastic “*haecceitas*,” which Wolff holds for the synonym of reality (ibid.; Wolff *Ontologia* §243).

Thoroughgoing determination is related neither in Wolff nor in Baumgarten to the concept of God as in Kant. Insofar as God is an individual, God too must be thoroughly determined, if one does not wish to pay homage to Spinozism. But in Kant this relation is thought still more closely. For in him every singular thing too must be thoroughly determined: “[A]ll that exists is thoroughly determined” (A573/B601). Thereto one needs a model that comprises all possible predicates and with which every thing is to be compared. It is the transcendental ideal as epitome of all realities:
> “Thus the transcendental major premise of the thoroughgoing determination of all things is nothing other than the representation of the epitome of all reality, not merely a concept that all predicates according to their transcendental content under itself, but that comprehends them within itself, and the thoroughgoing determination of every thing rests on the restriction of this All of reality, in that some of the same is ascribed to the thing, but the remaining is excluded, […]” (A577/B605).

In order to cognize or determine a thing, there must a priori be a concept that comprises all possible predicates. In Kant the concept of God is used as prototype (A578/B606), which contains all realities. A thing is compared with this ideal and thereby determined with a view to every possible predicate. Thus it is thereby thoroughly determined, in that it is measured out what in comparison with that ideal is lacking to it. Determining thus already becomes in Kant limiting and negating. He distinguishes the universality of the ideal-concept from the logical genus (*genus*), which has its particulars, the species (*species*), under itself. It has, in distinction thereto, not the particulars under itself, but its predicates within itself.[^r2.25]

But from the necessity of the ideal for our cognizing one may not conclude to its existence. It is only a “transcendental ideal”: an ought-rule of cognition, without an objective existence being to be ascribed to it.

Thus far reality is still understandable as the determination of the thing. But when Kant asserts that the realities that the ideal comprises concern not the logical form, but content or matter, he still betrays a further significance of this concept:

“The principle of thoroughgoing determination thus concerns the content, and not merely logical form. It is the principle of the synthesis of all predicates that are supposed to make the complete concept of a thing, and not merely of the analytic representation, through one of two opposed predicates, and contains a transcendental presupposition, namely that of the matter for all possibility, which a priori is supposed to contain the data for the particular possibility of every thing.” (A572f./B600f.)

The transcendental ideal constitutes, so Kant says, “the highest and complete material condition of its \[of that which exists] possibility,” “to which all thinking of objects in general according to their content must be led back” (A576/B604, emphasis by T. O.). Thus what is at issue in thoroughgoing determination is the content or the matter of the thing. Matter for theoretical cognition is nothing other than sensation, which is given to the senses through affection by the thing in itself. If the ideal, as Kant says, concerns the material condition of things, the realities whose epitome it is must be those of things in appearance. Kant already elucidates reality in the Schematism chapter:
> “Reality is in the pure concept of the understanding that which corresponds to a sensation in general; that therefore whose concept in itself indicates a being (in time); […] Since time is only the form of intuition, hence of objects, as appearances, that which in these corresponds to sensation is the transcendental matter of all objects, as things in themselves (the thinghood, reality)” (A143/B182—emphasis by T. O.).

Reality, sensation and being are in the world of appearance the same. But when Kant equates reality with thinghood, he remains true to the tradition. He ascribes this reality as thinghood to things in themselves. What he now calls “the transcendental matter of all objects” corresponds to the previously cited “matter for all possibility.” It is a thing in itself and for us not cognizable, so that one cannot assert its being. But the realities that it is supposed to contain are phenomenal. On account of this gulf one may not derive these from those: “The general concept of a reality in general cannot a priori be divided, because without experience one knows no determinate kinds of reality that would be contained under that genus” (A577/B605). That is the ground for the fact that Kant distinguishes the allness of the concept, the epitome of all realities, from logical universality. The ideal contains the singular realities, but this subordination is not logical; therefore these cannot be derived from that, as the species from the genus.

But with both significances of ‘reality’ Kant remains in one respect with the tradition.

> “A transcendental negation means by contrast \[over against logical negation] non-being in itself, to which transcendental affirmation is opposed, which is a Something whose concept in itself already expresses a being, and is therefore called reality (thinghood), because through it alone, and so far as it reaches, objects are Something (things), whereas the opposing negation means a mere lack, and, where this alone is thought, the sublation of every thing is represented.” (A574f./B602f.)

Admittedly Kant attempts to set himself apart from traditional metaphysics by distinguishing being as transcendental affirmation like non-being or nothing as transcendental negation from the logical. But he is still of the opinion that being or reality simply corresponds to affirmation. For Kant reality is always positive, being a position, while negation is negative, nothing a lack. This is the point where over against the Kantian concept of reality that of Hegel stands.[^r2.26]

It must still be drawn to attention that Kant’s doctrine of thoroughgoing determination hangs together with the concepts of reflection in the amphiboly chapter: Thoroughgoing determination, which in the Wolffian school was named as principle of individuation, hangs first, as already mentioned, together with the *principium identitatis indiscernibilium*. If Kant also declares the singular as thoroughly determined, he too seems to follow the *principium*. That assertion of Kant’s that singular things are already distinguished according to space, even if they have the same determinations, does not exclude that they also distinguish themselves through their determinations. If they with all determinations could thoroughly be compared with God, they would also have to be distinguished through their determinations, which however is impossible for our limited cognitive capacity. In the concept of God as epitome of all realities second real conflict must be excluded, when Kant says that the concept contains no negativity and thereby no contradiction. That the epitome of all realities is also called transcendental matter hangs thirdly directly together with the fourth, but indirectly also with the third pair of concepts of reflection.

#### 1.3. Determination and Reality in Hegel

The Kantian transcendental ideal as the epitome of all realities contains, as we just saw, only the positivities that strictly distinguish themselves from negativity. Pure positivity without all negativity would according to Hegel be a nothing, like pure being with which his logic begins. Kant follows the traditional concept of reality and excludes negativity from it:
> “Now no one can think a negation determinately without having the opposed affirmation lying at the ground. The one born blind cannot make for himself the least representation of darkness, because he has none of light; the savage not of poverty, because he does not know prosperity. The ignorant has no concept of his ignorance, because he has none of science, etc. Thus also all concepts of negations are derived, and the realities contain the data and so to speak the matter, or the transcendental content, for the possibility and thoroughgoing determination of all things.” (A575/B603)

For Kant negativity is only derivative. It must presuppose reality as positivity. Negativity shows itself only as restriction of positivity or reality, or it is, as Kant says, nothing but limit, which it could not be called “if the unrestricted (the All) did not lie at the ground” (A576/B604). That Kant here equates the realities with matter also lets us recognize that he ascribes negativity to form.

Thus according to Kant the epitome of all realities (*omnitudo realitatis*), which constitutes the principle of thoroughgoing determination, is formed only from being that contains no nothing or no negativity. We reach a cognition of a finite, singular thing by adding from outside restrictions to complete, pure being (in the Hegelian sense) as “prototype,” the unconditioned. Being is opposed to nothing.

The epitome of all realities, as which Kant, following the tradition, defines God, counts in Hegel as pure being, which at the same time is said to be nothing. He criticizes in the *Science of Logic* several times this concept of God, especially with a view to the fact that this version of the concept of reality is false. The unconditioned without all restrictions is said first to be only a nothing that has no determination within itself and against something other. But it is said second also to be restricted, in that it has to receive its restriction or negativity from outside. It is thus restricted through having no restriction.[^r2.27] Hegel draws attention to the ambiguity of the concept of reality:
> “At one time thus under reality is understood external determinate being, at the other time being-in-itself. But this is not a different or opposed significance of reality, but rather only One, because reality essentially includes those two determinations within itself.” (GW XI, 63, cf. XXI, 99)

Hegel finds precisely in the ambiguity the essence of reality: “This both is united in determinate being or reality. Determinate being is both in itself, as it has something on it, or being-for-other” (GW XI, 64/XXI, 99). Determinate being, which proceeds from becoming as passing-over from being to nothing and conversely, is a unity of these two (GW XI, 57/XXI, 94; GW XI, 59f./XXI, 97). It thereby stands over against an Other or is itself other-being (GW XI, 61/XXI, 106). Thus it has within itself two determinations, being-for-other and being-in-itself instead of being and nothing (GW XI, 62f./XXI, 106ff.). What Hegel calls “reality” is not pure being that holds itself far from all differences, but determinate being that not only stands over against an Other, but includes this being-for-other beside being-in-itself as moment within itself.[^r2.28] To reality negation is indeed also in Hegel set over against (GW XI, 75/XXI, 101), but when one speaks of the positive character of reality over against negation, only an accent shifts. “Reality,” as Hegel says, “itself contains the negation” (GW XXI, 102, also 99 and 100).[^r2.29]

Behind this of course sticks the famous dictum “*omnis determinatio est negatio*,” which Hegel has taken up from a letter of Spinoza’s (GW XI, 76/XXI, 101).[^r2.30] Hegel remains true to school metaphysics when he holds reality for determination. While however school metaphysics had given the concept of reality only a positive character, Hegel writes negativity into it. The positive determining of a subject negates at once the contrary determination of the same subject. Thus the in-itself and the for-other are inseparable. To determine the in-itself is to exclude the Other from the affected being. Only in that something stands over against another is it itself. According to Hegel the in-itself corresponds to that former pure being and being-for-other to non-being. Through becoming as mutual transition of being and nothing determinate being arose.

For Hegel something exists first through negation, through delimitation against the Other. Determinate being, which is first there through determination, is a being consisting through nothing. For if being is restricted through no negativity, it is pure being that was equal to nothing. That is the point at which Hegel criticizes the traditional concept of God as epitome of all realities:
> “There can here be mentioned the former metaphysical concept of God that was laid mainly at the foundation of the so-called ontological proof of the existence of God. God was namely determined as the epitome of all realities, and of this epitome it was said that it contains no contradiction within itself, that none of the realities sublates the other; for it is only to be taken as a perfection, as a Positive that contains no negation. Thus the realities are not opposed to one another and do not contradict one another. \[Paragraph] With this concept of reality it is thus assumed that it then still remains, insofar as all negation, thereby however all determinateness of the same, is sublated. But it is determinate being in general; it contains non-being as being-for-other, and more closely the limit or determinateness. Reality that is to be taken in the so-called eminent sense or as infinite,—in the ordinary significance of the word,—is expanded into the determinationless, and loses its significance” (XI, 64f.—emphasis by T. O.).

Thus Hegel connects with one another the two significances of the concept of reality on which Kant also indeterminately relies. For him determination or reality as the principle of being is negativity. Insofar as this is so, reality hangs on the relation to the Other. This is the fundamental trait that Hegel ascribes to the logic of being in general.

#### 1.4. The Question of Ground and the Problem of Subjective Mediation

We have investigated the concepts of determination and of reality in Kant and Hegel because they make present in a special manner that from which essence proceeds. Over against being as quality essence is also determined. Before we go into its arising, we return to the textual section from the Existence chapter that was cited at the beginning of this chapter. There reality proceeds from the unconditioned. Subsequently Hegel mentions once more the ontological proof of God. He recalls the Kantian critique: “Thus existence is here not to be taken as a predicate or as determination of essence” (GW XI, 326). But in contrast to Kant existence is of course in Hegel not a purely positive positing. Position is rather negation (ibid.). Hegel conceives existence, distinguishing it from determinate being, at its systematic place in his logic. Essence does not have existence, but has passed over into existence: “existence is the reflection of ground into itself” (ibid.).

What Hegel rejects of the Kantian critique of the proof of God concerns less the content than rather the formal-logical proving: “Proving is in general mediated cognition” (GW XI, 324). He points in this connection however to the logic of the concept, where the proof itself is a stage of cognizing. But here Kant is already criticized for this reason, because he is not conscious of the mediating character of proving:
> “Now in what Kant calls the concept, namely in Something insofar as it is taken as only simply related to itself, or in the representation as such, there does not lie its mediation; in the abstract identity with itself the opposition is left aside. The ontological proof would now have had to present that the absolute concept, namely the concept of God, comes to determinate determinate being, to mediation, or how the simple essence mediates itself with mediation.” (GW XI, 325)

Here there is talk of the mediation that proving lays into the matter to be proved, thus here into God’s determinate being. In that determinate being grounds its absolute object and thereby mediates it, it degrades it to the non-absolute. For God, who is supposed to be an absolute ground, is thereby grounded in an Other. Admittedly this ground is not an objective one (*ratio essendi*), but only one for cognition (*ratio cognoscendi*). Herein however consists the problem of Kantian transcendental philosophy:
> “But that proving reflection does not know this nature of its mediation; it takes itself on the one hand for a merely subjective, and thereby removes its mediation from God himself, but on the other hand therefore does not recognize the mediating movement, that and how it is in essence itself.” (ibid.)

According to Hegel proving cannot remain external over against what is to be proved. The proof of God however shows a non-divine ground for God, who yet himself is supposed to be the and thereby also his absolute ground. This aporia, of grasping through mediation the God who is supposed to be immediate, displaces knowing into immediate faith, which is a renunciation of thinking itself. Against this Hegel undertakes the difficult path of developing a speculative logic that internalizes mediation within itself.

Yet it is the achievement of the *Critique of Pure Reason* to found the objectivity of the phenomena in transcendental-subjective mediation. But Kant distinguishes the instance of the transcendental subjectivity grounding experience from the reality of experience itself, insofar as in the “Paralogism” he strictly separates self-consciousness and self-cognition and declares the latter for impossible.[^r2.31] The transcendental I, in which the empirical reality of all phenomenal objects is founded, itself has no reality. Kant indeed seeks to ground that the determinations of thought as categories are at the same time the fundamental determinations of content of experiences, but thinking itself is grasped outside experience. Only Hegel undertakes the further step: to the speculative principle that determinations of thought are themselves determinations of being.
### 2. Essence as Reflection

While for Kant’s *Critique of Pure Reason* commentaries have already long been available, a commentary on Hegel’s *Science of Logic* was long a desideratum.[^r2.32] Today however we have some commentaries on this main work as well as on the encyclopedic logic.[^r2.33] Still more often a part was commented. Above all Hegel’s logic of reflection has since Henrich’s renowned essay often been commented, because it is regarded as the core of Hegelian logic.[^r2.34] The question how far the logic of reflection reaches over the whole logic we must here leave open.[^r2.35] But as concerns the logic of essence it is unmistakable that the logic of reflection is foundational for it,[^r2.36] not only because what is at issue thereby is the concept of reflection that is central in the amphiboly chapter of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, but also because the logic of reflection contains the dialectic of the essence-logical categories that Kant treats as concepts of reflection in the amphiboly chapter.

If questioning after the ground or the essence that lies behind what is present gives the beginning of metaphysics, then the approach of the logic of essence is precisely the place where metaphysics begins. But this questioning does not exhaust itself in metaphysics, for modern science attempts to cognize the essence of appearance also with the concept of force or of law, which is also a category of the logic of essence.[^r2.37]

In the last section we investigated Kant’s and Hegel’s concepts of determination and of reality. At the beginning of the logic of essence essence is determined over against being, which culminates in determinate being, to which determination and reality belong.[^r2.38]

The aim of the logic of essence is the development to the concept of the concept, which is then formulated at the beginning of the second book, subjective logic. But being and essence are already themselves forms of the concept, that as in-itself and this as posited.[^r2.39] The task of the logic of essence consists in realizing the potential concept. Thereby, as its characterization as for-itself lets one recognize, self-relation, reflection, is most important. Reflection, as which essence determines itself, already becomes thematic at the beginning of the logic of essence; it gives the following essence-logical categories a framework.

#### 2.1. Prehistory of the Concept of Reflection

We investigated in the first chapter how Kant determines logical and transcendental reflection. What Kant generally calls the faculty of comparison is taken up by Hegel as external reflection, which is subordinated to reflection and is only an external form inferior to it.[^r2.40] Hegel does not name transcendental reflection as example of external reflection, but the reflecting power of judgment, which Kant introduces in the *Critique of the Power of Judgment* with the determining power of judgment, and criticizes thereon that it takes the immediate only as given. The same is also supposed to hold for transcendental reflection, insofar as the representations that it compares are not produced by reflection itself, but are gathered up.[^r2.41] Against this Hegel introduces a concept of reflection that does not take what is related to one another as given from outside, but itself produces it in its movement. That external reflection is first deduced in this movement and again led back into the inner relation. Such a structure of reflection Fichte already conceived as self-relation of the I. But this was for Hegel inadequate, because Fichte indeed attempts the transcendental grounding of his *Wissenschaftslehre* without the concept of the thing in itself, but with the concept of the check lets something given remain.[^r2.42] Moreover reflection for Hegel is not a deed of the subject, but an objective movement of being in the wider sense.

With a view to modern philosophy Hegel’s concept of reflection is peculiar. It is customary to name Locke as the one who first distinctly determined this concept, although it was already since Descartes a principle of modern philosophy. For Locke reflection (*reflection*) is one of the two origins of our representations (*ideas*). While “sensation” designates a perception that is given to the understanding by outer objects through the sense organs, reflection is a “Perception of the Operation of our own Minds within us.”[^r2.43] He defines reflection as “that notice which the Mind takes of its own Operations, and the manner of them, by reason wherof, there come to be Ideas of these Operations in the Understanding” (ibid.). It is a perception of the understanding of itself. In this definition a self-relatedness is already to be found. Thus he himself says: “[T]he Understanding turns inwards upon it self, reflects on its own Operations, and makes them the Object of its own Contemplation” (loc. cit. § 7, p. 107).[^r2.44] In reflection what is at issue for Locke is “the acquisition of cognition,”[^r2.45] and neither comparing, which “the knowledge” as “the perception of the connexion and agreement, or disagreement and repugnancy of any of our ideas” (IV, I, §2) is supposed to demand, nor “discerning,” both of which point ahead to the Kantian concept of reflection, are in Locke reflection itself.[^r2.46]

Although Locke already emphasizes the self-referential character of reflection, this attribution of significance is however to be distinguished from that of German Idealism: As Locke distinctly explains, reflection is just as much an ‘observation’ as ‘sensation.’ It is also called “internal sense” (§4, p. 105) and therefore rather counts as an inner experience through the inner sense, whose form, according to Kant, time is supposed to constitute.[^r2.47]

Leibniz, who condemns Locke’s doctrine of a *tabula rasa* as fiction, also determines reflection in his polemical writing against Locke’s *Essay* as “*une attention à ce qui est en nous*.”[^r2.48] He agrees with him therein that there is no source of cognition besides sensation and reflection.[^r2.49] But when he thereby assumes innate concepts (ibid.), his conception of reflection nevertheless distinguishes itself very much from Locke’s, regardless that Leibniz means to agree with Locke. Reflection for Leibniz is not inner sense or inner experience, as Locke understands it, but rather a thinking.[^r2.50]

Thus Leibniz has taken up the self-relatedness of reflection from Locke, but thereby distinguished reflection from perception. This Leibniz first says only in the critique of Locke, in order to make clear that Locke has admitted a non-sensible source of cognition and that his position insofar is not far removed from his. But the systematic status of reflection is said in Leibniz to have remained unclear.

Wolff finally gives reflection in the *Psychologia empirica* a distinct systematic status. He follows Leibniz insofar as he distinguishes reflection as a higher cognitive faculty from the lower ones such as *sensus*, *imaginatio* and *memoria*. His definition makes use of *attentio*: “*Attentionis successiva directio ad ea, quae in re percepta insunt, dicitur Reflexio*” (Wolff, *Psych. emp.*, § 257). Previously he had determined *attentio* as a capacity to bring a part of the composite representation to greater clarity than other parts (Wolff, *Psych. emp.*, § 237). Reflection is a special kind of attention that successively directs itself to something in the perceived, while the direction of attention remains dependent on our arbitrariness and indeterminate (Wolff, *Psych. emp.*, §§ 234, 256f.). Although from the thesis that reflection relates not directly to objects, but to their representations, and that attention is an apperception (Wolff, *Psych. emp.*, §§ 235ff.),[^r2.51] it results that reflection implies a self-relatedness, Wolff seems still to understand the self-referential intention as a special case of reflection (Wolff, *Psych. emp.*, § 261).

His successor Baumgarten also treats the concept of reflection in the *Psychologia empirica* of his *Metaphysica* and likewise counts reflection among the higher cognitive faculties, which he calls *Intellectus*. He defines reflection as “*Attentio in totius perceptionis partes successive directa*” (Baumgarten, *Metaphysica*, § 626), which is not different from Wolff’s definition. In connection therewith he defines *comparatio* as the “*attentio ad totam perceptionem post reflexionem*” (ibid.). Wolff has already established that we bring the representations together or compare them (*inter se conferimus*) when we reflect on them.[^r2.52] He has not separated reflection and comparison, because the former implies the latter. But in Baumgarten comparison follows after reflection. Reflection directs itself only to each one part of perception; then *comparatio* brings the reflected parts together.

If we relate the result of this short historico-philosophical retrospect to Kant, we come to the conclusions: First Kant, who has philosophically trained himself in this school metaphysics, indeed takes up the concept of reflection as comparison from this tradition. But he does not separate comparison and reflection like Baumgarten, but follows Wolff. Second Kant seems to weaken the self-relatedness of reflection, which is clear with the concept of reflection in the amphiboly chapter of the *Critique of Pure Reason*. But Kant also gives a concept of self-referential reflection when he says in his Anthropology:
> “If we represent to ourselves the inner action (spontaneity) through which a concept (a thought) becomes possible, reflection, the receptivity (*receptivity*) through which a perception (*perceptio*), i.e. empirical intuition, becomes possible, apprehension, but both acts with consciousness, then the consciousness of oneself (*apperceptio*) can be divided into that of reflection and that of apprehension. […] Here now the I seems to us to be double (which would be contradictory): 1) the I as subject of thinking (in logic), which means pure apperception (the merely reflecting I), and of which nothing further is to be said, but which is a wholly simple representation; 2) the I as the object of perception, hence of the inner sense, which contains a manifold of determinations that make an inner experience possible.” (AA, VII, 134)

He here relates the concept of reflection rather to apperception, which recalls Leibniz.[^r2.53] Moreover he distinguishes the object of thinking, i.e. the inner sense, which Locke called reflection, from this reflection. Reflection in this sense is also to be distinguished from transcendental reflection. For this does not reflect itself, but the representations given through the other faculties. Insofar as this is so, the concept of apperception stands nearer to the Hegelian concept of reflection than transcendental reflection. But the subjective character must still be stripped from it.[^r2.54]

#### 2.2. The Logic of Reflection in the *Science of Logic*

##### 2.2.1. From Being to Essence

Hegel’s concept of reflection in the *Science of Logic* departs from this tradition thereby that it is no longer a mental or transcendental, thus subjective act. In him the self-reference in reflection is emphasized. The reflection directing itself outward is by contrast subordinated to it, like logical and transcendental reflection in Kant. Hegel’s reflection is a pure self-relation that precedes the related. It has now become an ontological process.

The logic of being was the domain of immediate being. But this does not mean that no being-logical category would be mediated. Determinate being, in which Hegel finds the fundamental character of being, is rather always already mediated, because in it being and nothing were mediated through becoming or Something through its Other. The logic of being is thus only relatively, first in comparison with essence, immediate, because what is at issue thereby is only what is present and one does not ask after the truth standing behind it. Therefore being is not called appearance as in Kant. Shine is it first as the Other of essence. Over against this being can be assumed as phenomena, appearance or shine.[^r2.55]

But essence, to which being is thus set over against, arises according to Hegel not through the deepening of the cognition of a subject, but from being itself. That this objective process corresponds to the subjective Hegel already betrays at the beginning of the logic:[^r2.56]
> “Being is the Immediate. In that knowing wants to cognize the true, what being is in and for itself, it does not remain standing by the Immediate and its determinations, but presses through the same, with the presupposition that behind this being there is still something other than being itself, that this background constitutes the truth of being.” (GW XI, 241)

At the beginning is the Immediate. But is it its own truth? From this elementary astonishment or doubt the logic of essence proceeds. One cannot directly cognize essence; its cognition “begins from an Other, being” (ibid.).

But genuine essence may not remain in the external relation to being. Being sublates itself immanently into essence. Thereto Hegel draws upon the concept of ‘recollection’: “Only in that knowing recollects itself out of immediate being, through this mediation it finds essence” (ibid.—italics by T. O.). When he etymologically traces essence back to the past participle (*gewesen*) of the verb ‘to be’ and calls it “timeless past Being,” Hegel appeals, without formulating it expressly, to the Platonic doctrine of *anamnesis*:[^r2.57] The truth of appearing things, the Ideas, are said to be cognizable for us for this reason, because our soul has already beheld them before birth into present life. Our cognition is said to be the re-cognition of what was formerly known and forgotten. Hegel thereby plays with the German word: recollection as internalization. Plato already with that doctrine of *anamnesis* demanded that we seek the truth in ourselves, because we have already known it.[^r2.58] The search for essence beyond being thus presses into the inner. But Platonic *anamnesis* obviously does not suffice for Hegel, since it lets the world of phenomena stand outside the truth, the Ideas. Insofar as this is so, the phenomena are excluded from the truth. But essence must include its Other, being, within itself.[^r2.59]

The transition from being to essence is thus according to Hegel not an operation of the cognizing subject that stands externally over against the affected objects. It is rather a self-sublation, “the movement of being itself” (GW XI, 241). That recollection is the “going-into-itself” of being itself. If an external cognizing or external reflection finds essence, this is only a product of this contingent operation and insofar has its producer and its determinate being as Other outside itself. Then essence falls again back into the domain of being and is no genuine essence.

Therefore essence must produce itself not through an operation of the subject external to it, but through the movement of being itself. But the self-producing of essence from being would miss itself if essence were only a pure unity that had no determinations within itself, while being (in the wider sense) contains manifold determinations. Then it would have left the determinations of being outside itself and would still be determined by this. Therein consists the error of the proof of God with the epitome of all realities (GW XI, 242). Through this empty abstraction essence is never reachable (GW XI, 241f.). It would have no determination and thereby no determinate being. Essence must rather itself become determinate being, in that it determines and delimits itself.[^r2.60]

Therefore Hegel indicates in the introductory part of the logic of essence that the concept of essence has to sublate two kinds of other-being. First it must sublate being as the Other of essence, from which it has developed. Second the Other as external operation of abstracting reflection is to be sublated. This is supposed to occur through the sublation of the being-logical determination that we analyzed in the last section.

##### 2.2.2. The Essential and the Inessential

The concept of essence is, as we have just seen, also presented by Hegel with the terminology such as ‘in itself,’ ‘for itself,’ ‘in and for itself’ and ‘for other.’ Essence is said to be “being-in-and-for-itself” (GW XI, 242). This must be understood literally and indeed in the sense that essence is both ‘in itself’ and ‘for itself.’ It is in itself, because from it every relation to its Other is excluded, thus it is not ‘for other.’ It is insofar indifferent against the determinations of being. But if essence were only itself, it would have to be the indeterminate immediacy like pure being. It is thus also supposed to be for itself, because it includes those determinations within itself. The relation to other or being-for-other is here the general mark of being that we have found especially in determinate being. The progress of the logic of being is designated by Hegel, over against the “shining” of essence, as “passing-over” (Enc. 1830, §161). The whole process of being was a transition from one to another, thus a processual relation between diversities.

In order to sublate the relation to other, the transition must thus be sublated. But this seems a difficult task, because essence itself arose through the transition from being. Essence itself has the determination of being the Other of being.

Also in the subsection “The Essential and the Inessential” this difficulty of the transition from being to essence comes to light.[^r2.61] If being remains an Other for essence, essence falls again into the domain of being. It has its determination against being, but this manner of having a determination against another belongs to determinate being:
> “Essence itself is in this determination being, immediate essence, and being only a Negative in relation to essence, not in and for itself, essence thus a determinate negation. Being and essence comport themselves in this manner again as Others in general toward one another, for each has a being, an immediacy, that are indifferent against one another, and stand according to this being in equal value” (GW XI, 245).

In this relation of essence to being that cannot be essence, but only “the essential.” Being is then no longer an immediate being, but the inessential, whereby it is determined over against that. “The difference of essential and inessential has let essence fall back into the sphere of determinate being” (ibid.). The essential that stands over against the inessential is not the proper essence, but only a determinate being. It is only essence-like, but no essence itself. Hegel elucidates the lack of the essential in the following steps:

1. It is only Other against being. For insofar as it is determined against this, it is only a determinate real being. Determinate being is still laid at the foundation of this relation (ibid.)

2. It still has an Other outside it. It stands “in determinate respect” (ibid.) as Other against being. The difference of the two consists in a Third. Hegel already says in the logic of being concerning determinate being that the difference of (determinate) being and (determinate) nothing consists in a Third (GW XI, 50). But now first it is betrayed what the Third is. It is “an external positing, a separation of a part of the same from another part that does not touch determinate being itself” (GW XI, 245).

3. Insofar as this is so, it does not belong to the essential or inessential itself whether it is determined as essential or inessential.

4. According to Hegel this determinate-being-like essential is only the first negation, “which is determinateness” (ibid.). Admittedly he has previously said of determinate being that it is a double negation, insofar as it is the negation of its Other, of its Negative. But to the double negation there here underlay the negativity that is determinateness. Hegel thereby indicates that the negativity of proper essence must be of another kind than that of the sphere of being, namely a negativity that does not separate the matter and produce Something and the Other, but that frees itself from this relation.

Through the lack of the essential the proper essence shimmers through. It is the double negation that goes beyond simple determination. With this negativity essence can no longer stand over against being as its Other. Rather the Third that made possible the relation of Something and of the Other must be sublated.

##### 2.2.3. Shine as Essence

What Hegel calls shine is being over against proper essence. It is “the in and for itself null Immediate” or “unessence” (GW XI, 246). This concept of shine is wholly different from the Kantian. The latter is an error that reason necessarily commits in that it overflies sensible givenness. Hegel’s shine by contrast is being in essence. He thereby recalls beside the “phenomenon of skepticism” the “appearance of idealism” (ibid.). Hegel then more precisely points to Kantian appearance as well as to the representations of the Leibnizian monad and the Fichtean check (GW XI, 246f.).

Now the logic has the problem, in determining being and essence, of having to fall back on the instrumentarium of the logic of being—thus the aporem of having to determine essence and shine, while determinateness itself belongs in the logic of being. If essence is determined over against shine, it falls back into the sphere of determinate being; essence would be only the essential and shine the inessential. Hegel finds the solution of the aporem in his concept of reflection. Reflection is here no longer a faculty of the subject, e.g. of comparison. This reflection has the function of mediating being and essence, immediacy and mediation. Instead of, as in Kant, determining the transcendental places, reflection unifies: as unity of both it is for Hegel the proper essence. Hegel does not want to dissolve the ambiguity (amphiboly), but to find the truth in it.

That aporia is solved thereby that shine is determined, and through the determining of shine determinateness itself is annihilated. To determinate being or to the other being-logical categories immediacy or being always underlay. Determinate being was being in its being-determined, with the negation or limit.[^r2.62] Negativity delimited being or immediacy, which brought forth determinate being. It was something that was added to being. In order to overcome this being, negativity must now underlie. The immediacy of being is now to be understood as product of negativity. Not immediacy, but the mediation of negativity triumphs. Essence as reflection is absolute negativity that negates itself.[^r2.63]

Negativity is now to be found in shine. It is first the remnant of being and insofar immediate. But this being or immediacy is in essence nullity: “[O]utside its nullity, outside essence it \[shine] is not” (GW XI, 246). This immediacy is “the reflected immediacy, that is, which is only by means of its negation” (ibid.). In this shine there still seems to be something independent of essence. But “[s]hine is the in itself null” (GW XI, 247). And “[i]ts nullity in itself is the negative nature of essence itself” (ibid.).

In the identity of shine with essence the determinateness of determinate being is surpassed. Something stood over against the Other. Therefore it had on the one hand being-for-other as moment. On the other hand it had the in-itself that was not exhausted with this being for other and let Something be itself. Determinate being consisted of these two moments. But in shine no being-in-itself is to be found. It is, in that it is wholly null in essence. It has only being for other, the null, as its determination. Over against this shine essence is essence, insofar as it annihilates shine. Thus it is shown “that the determinations that distinguish it from essence are determinations of essence itself, and further that this determinateness of essence, which is shine, is sublated in essence itself” (ibid.). “But the immediacy or indifference that this non-being contains is the own absolute being-in-itself of essence” (ibid.). Thus with shine being for other is at the same time being-in-itself. Insofar as this is so, shine and essence are identical. That reflected immediacy of shine is “nothing other than the own immediacy of essence” (GW XI, 248).

In this connection the actual state of affairs of shining is clarified: “[S]hine in essence is not the shine of an Other, but it is shine in itself, the shine of essence itself” (GW XI, 248). Essence shines in shine; shine is the shining of essence. Insofar as this is so, the relation of both is not that of the one to the other, but a relation to itself. In it negativity (mediation) and immediacy are united.[^r2.64]

It is reflection as self-relating negativity that makes this unity possible. And in it first determinateness, which characterizes the logic of being as a whole, is sublated. Self-relating negativity is “the absolute sublating of determinateness itself” (ibid.). Determinateness, which is the negativity that is given to immediacy and restricts this, is sublated in the One of determining and of the determined. Immediacy, which always underlay the being-logical categories, is now explained as result of the self-relation of negativity: “This relation of the Negative or of unselfsufficiency to itself is its immediacy” (ibid.).[^r2.65]

##### 2.2.4. Three Forms of Reflection

The logic of shine results in that essence is reflection as self-relating negativity. Hegel calls this “absolute negativity.” It is absolute in the sense that it needs nothing outside itself to which it relates. It negates only itself. But through self-negating it makes itself into an Other. Hegel calls this a repelling from itself. Reflection is thus also the negativity repelling itself from itself. It has now shown itself that immediacy is only a result of the self-repelling of negativity. Position is thus double negation. The primacy of negation is the truth of the logic of being, a truth that first lays itself open in the logic of essence.

In the concept of essence as reflection that task must be solved. Positing, which in the external relation of being had necessarily been left out of account, is now to be thematized. It is included in this reflection. Being-logical determinateness itself essence has developed within itself, because otherwise it would remain determined against it and so fall back into determinateness. Essence may not be only positing. Insofar as the negation lies between what is thereby posited among one another and between positedness and positing, it must also be determining. These tasks find their solution in three forms that will underlie the following presentations of the essence-logical categories.

###### 2.2.4.1. Positing

One of the tasks of the concept of essence was, as we have seen above, to internalize the operation of positing itself, which in the sphere of being lay outside its presentation, thus to thematize positing. The one side of reflection, of mediation, is now positing. Its other side, the side of immediacy, is over against this a positedness. The word “positedness” makes clear that that immediacy is the result of an operation, so that what we seem immediately to find before us we in truth see through as something mediated or posited.[^r2.66]

This recalls to us that Kant in the Transcendental Ideal explains being as a “position.”[^r2.67] Being is a positedness of the object in relation to its concept.[^r2.68] Determinate being for Kant is a positedness of the object in relation to the cognizing subject. Fichte takes this up into his doctrine of the deed-act: He derives in the *Foundation of the Entire Wissenschaftslehre* from the secure fact ‘A=A’ the first principle: The I posits itself absolutely. Thereby he holds being for the ‘being posited in the I’: “Everything that is, is only insofar as it is posited in the I, and outside the I there is nothing.”[^r2.69] Fichte thereby goes a step in the direction of Hegel’s later doctrine of reflection. For positing in Kant does not mean that the subject lets the object exist. Whether something exists or not does not depend on the (cognizing) subject, but on whether it is given to its senses or not. Positedness is thereby not the subject or the I. But in Fichte here the I is posited by the I itself. The positing of the object becomes first in him a self-positing, a self-relatedness. Thus the positing and the posited in the Fichtean first principle are one and the same. Hegel’s “absolute reflection” is also the being-one of the positing and of the posited, but positing is not a deed of the I.

Still to be analyzed remains the distinction of these two moments. It makes as the self-relation of negation both moments independent. Therefore it is not positing, but “presupposing.” The transition from positing to presupposing or their being-one is the necessary consequence of reflection as self-relating negativity. It negates itself, i.e. repels itself from itself. Thus the positedness of positedness is negated, so that it becomes immediate. Reflection is “the negation of the Negative as of the Negative” (GW XI, 251). Thus negativity in positedness is negated and is no longer posited, but is found, as if it had been immediately there from the beginning (GW XI, 252). Positing for its part negates itself. As the equality of the Negative with itself it is immediate. Thus it again sublates itself as Negative: “The return of essence is thus its repelling itself from itself. Or reflection-into-itself is essentially the presupposing of that from which it is the return” (ibid.).

These two sublations that bring forth presupposing reflection are no new addition to that reflection, but the necessary result of reflection as the infinite self-mediation of negation. Reflection sublates its own mediatedness and thereby becomes immediate. Thereby there arises the external relation of reflection and of immediacy, but this externality is already sublated thereby that it was brought forth from the negativity of reflection. This sublation itself brings forth immediacy. Reflection is a movement always reversing itself. Positing and presupposing are only two sides of the one movement of reflection.

It is this movement in which positing and presupposing are one and which explains the logical mechanism that brings forth that immediacy. We have arrived from immediate being into essence. But the presupposed immediacy is, as just shown, only what is posited in advance, only a product of the reflection of essence: “The going-beyond the Immediate from which reflection begins is rather first through this going-beyond; and the going-beyond the Immediate is the arriving at the same” (GW XI, 252).

###### 2.2.4.2. External Reflection

The concept of essence as reflection that was just derived has two sides, positing and presupposing reflection. In the process of this absolute reflection Hegel allots a place for that reflection with which one began: for external reflection.

External reflection is a further determination of absolute reflection in which especially the side of presupposing comes into the foreground. It is important that positedness is determined in this reflection.[^r2.70] Namely the relation of reflection and of positedness or of a positedness to another is determined. Positedness is thus determined against another and thereby has become real. Reality can as determinate being wholly first here, from reflection, come into view.[^r2.71] Therefore this reflection is called “external or real reflection” (GW XI, 252).

Thereby that being which filled the whole logic of being and was sublated in the transition to essence is included in external reflection. This has two moments within itself, the Immediate as Other and reflection-into-itself. In this reflection the relation of the two has become external, in that the moment of presupposing surpasses that of positing. But insofar as reflection is absolute, external reflection again becomes inner, because the Immediate that is presupposed in this reflection is only the being posited through reflection, the pre-posited.
> “It is thus present that external reflection is not external, but just as much immanent reflection of immediacy itself; or that what is through positing reflection is the essence being in and for itself” (GW XI, 254).

###### 2.2.4.3. Determining Reflection

External reflection has returned into inner reflection; it has again reached the equality of positedness and of positing. Determining reflection is insofar a unity of positing reflection and of external reflection, which in this unity is an absolute presupposing (GW XI, 256). Positing and positedness, in that they went through external reflection, which was their real relation, have become determining and determined. The again internalized reflection that has now arisen must be the unity of determining and of the determined or of determinateness. But this last mode of being, determinateness, belongs to determinate being or reality, which essence is supposed to have overcome. Therefore determining reflection has to show how it is possible that it is an identity with itself, while it as determination is a relation to an Other.

Positing reflection was an absolute reflection that depended on no external given, while the external, which arose through presupposing, found the Immediate before itself. (The word ‘positedness’ points to the fact that it depended on positing and not conversely.) Insofar as positing reflection is independent and has no negative relation to the Other, it has no reality in the Hegelian sense shown above. Positedness for its part has no independence; it was only in relation to reflection. It is insofar null:
> “[P]ositing does not posit its determination in the place of another; it has no presupposition. But for that reason it is not the completed, determining reflection.” (GW XI, 255)

Over against this independent reality first arose in external reflection. Positedness has become a determinate being, in that its equality with reflection was negated and it was found only as externally given. Only in determining reflection, which is the unity of those two, arises the reflection that is both independent and brings forth reality as Other.

Positedness, or better: being-determined, in this reflection, is however neither that determinate being to which being underlay, nor shine or positedness that is negated as the Other by essence and is dependent on it. It is rather the positedness that itself is essence. The determinations that this reflection brings forth are not the being-logical, but essentialities or determinations of reflection.

Here the task of sublating determinateness or reality reaches a paradoxical solution: reflection that determines itself and makes itself into determinateness. But the determinateness here reached does not equal that of determinate being. Therefore Hegel distinguishes these concepts of determination, qualitative determination and the determination of reflection (GW XI, 256f.): Determination as negation is supposed to be related to something. In quality it is an Other that is qualitatively diverse from Something to which this determination belongs. By contrast the Other of the determinations of reflection is “being-reflected into itself” (GW XI, 256). Thus in determinate being being constituted the foundation or the element of negation. Negation thereby negated being. But what in the determination of reflection is negated is now being-reflected into itself. Insofar as this is so, it is the product of reflection as self-relating negativity. In qualitative determination inequality prevails. It is a unity of being with negativity. Insofar as this is so, it has the potential to alter itself, to pass over into an Other. Therefore the logic of being is characterized through passing-over. But in the determinations of reflection equality with itself prevails.[^r2.72] Insofar as this is so “determinateness has through the relation to itself fastened and infinitely fixed itself” (GW XI, 256). But this does not mean that the determination of reflection is a resting determinateness (GW XI, 257); rather there is lacking in it a firm foundation, as being to determinate being. Rather reflection betrayed that being, which seemed like a firm foundation, is a product of the hovering movement of reflection. But paradoxically determination is here brought to a standstill through this movement, because it does not pass over into another. Hegel designates this shape of the determinations of reflection as “the relation to their other-being on them themselves” (ibid.). Determination reaches independence in that it includes its Other within itself and thereby elevates itself into the hover.

-----------------

# Part III: Exposition of the Principal Moments from the Logic of Essence in the *Science of Logic* – with Particular Retrospective Regard to the Kantian Concepts of Reflection

## Fifth Chapter: Hegel’s Determinations of Reflection as Speculative Conception of the First Two Pairs of the Kantian Concepts of Reflection

The course from being to essence as reflection was at the same time that from being-determined to self-determining. Only at the last stage of reflection have we reached the element of this reflexive determination. This determining reflection has finally developed itself into certain determinations: It has thereby again been passively determined, yet itself contains the reflexive activity. This determinacy of self-determining, the unity of determining and of being-determined, Hegel calls “determinations of reflection.”

The chapter “Essentialities or Determinations of Reflection” is at the same time the place (*topos*) at which Hegel situates the first two pairs of the Kantian concepts of reflection—as well as the traditional logical and ontological principles connected with them. Hegel found the model of this logical conception in Kant’s amphiboly chapter when, in his developed logic, he replaced the traditional principles through the determinations of reflection. The Kantian concepts of reflection appear in Hegel’s logic as “determinations of reflection.” Sameness (*Einerleiheit*) is the German term for the Latin *identitas* (identity); diversity is already contained in Hegel’s determinations of reflection; opposition and contradiction are to count as conflict (*Widerstreit*). The moment of agreement (*Einstimmung*) Hegel did not take up into the main titles of the determinations of reflection, but identity in the Hegelian version already implies the state of affairs of agreement. Moreover one of the moments of diversity that has arisen from identity also counts as agreement, namely likeness. The correspondence is not only verbal but also contentual, for the problematic that Kant takes up with the concepts of reflection in the amphiboly chapter is likewise treated here, even if it occurs only in Remarks, because the traditional principles of logic and ontology, as we have made clear, are no longer a direct theme of Hegelian logic. Hegel’s stance toward Kant first becomes intelligible when we have presented what he develops in the main texts.

Concerning the structure of the chapter “Determinations of Reflection” the following is still to be remarked: Hegel first in the Logic for the Middle Class (1810/11) replaced the named principles through the “determinations of essence”; the principle of identity and at the same time of contradiction through identity, that of diversity through diversity, that of opposition—i.e. of the excluded third—through opposition and that of ground through ground. In the later editions of the Encyclopedia this division into four moments is tailored to the triad customary in the mature Hegelian system:

Diversity and opposition (*Entgegensetzung* / *Gegensatz*) are drawn together into difference. But in the *Science of Logic* the structure of the chapter elevates itself still further from the propositional division: Ground forms a proper chapter beside the determinations of reflection; the determinations of reflection are first divided into three stages—identity, difference and contradiction. The last place is thereby now occupied by contradiction, not by ground. Difference has in this conception become threefold: absolute difference, diversity and opposition. In this division the principle of identity or of contradiction is treated at identity, the principle of diversity or the *principium identitatis indiscernibilium* at diversity and that of ground at ground, as in the Logic for the Middle Class (1810/11) (GW X, 159 (Ms.); 228 (Dict.)) and in the Encyclopedias. The principle of the excluded third appears however first in a Remark to contradiction. To opposition is devoted the problem concerning real opposition.

Hegel praises Kant for the achievement of having “drawn attention above all to the antinomies.” Yet he at the same time finds fault that Kant “has not exhausted the antithetic of reason, in that he has set up only some forms of it” (Logic for the Middle Class (1810/11) § 79, GW X, 176 (Ms.); 238 (Dict.)): The antinomy is to be shown under all logical categories also in the concepts of reflection.

In what follows Hegel’s exposition of the determinations of reflection is to be reconstructed. Thereby we shall draw upon the text of the *Science of Logic*, because he here gives its, if not definitive, yet most complete presentation, since on account of his sudden death no second edition of the logic of essence came about, and the Encyclopedia—which Hegel as is known twice reworked[^s1.1]—as a compendium gives no such precise executions of the logic of essence as the *Science of Logic* of 1813.[^s1.2]

### A. Exposition of the Determinations of Reflection 1: Identity and Diversity

The determinations of reflection are presented in a continuous course of inner reflection. Each of them is a moment of this one course. Insofar as this is so, it seems superficially requisite to treat them continuously. Yet for the attainment of our goal, to interpret Hegel’s logic in retrospective regard to the Kantian concepts of reflection, it has proved expedient to divide them into three parts, namely into: 1. identity, absolute difference and diversity, 2. opposition and 3. contradiction.[^s1.3]

#### 1. Identity as Negative Self-Relation

##### 1.1. Identity as Restored Immediacy

The determinations of reflection have arisen in that determining reflection determines itself, i.e. makes itself into a determination. It, as self-referentiality, has for itself two sides as determining and determined. Since with respect to the determinations of reflection the aspect of determination is emphasized, what is at issue is thus the “determinate reflection” (GW XI, 258). Identity can therefore here not be taken only for mere sameness, but must also be understood in the sense of the agreement of what is distinguished, because identity means not only a being-one, but also an agreement of what has been distinguished through the determinations, i.e. of the realities: Identity thus means both sameness and already implicitly agreement. The reflection that now appears as identity is first determined as “the immediacy of reflection.” Thereby the moment of the mediation of reflection disappears:
> “This identity with itself is the immediacy of reflection. It is not that equality with itself which being or also nothing is, but the equality with itself which is as producing itself into unity, not a restoring from an other, but this pure producing from and in itself; the essential identity.” (GW XI, 260)

With this concept of identity the customary version of identity (that of “external reflection”) as “abstract identity” (GW XI, 261) and its form ‘A = A’ as “expression of empty tautology” (GW XI, 262) are criticized. External reflection, which holds fast to abstract identity, abstracts from the distinguishing in identity. It does not thereby take into account the principal admixture of the former into the latter, namely “that identity is a diverse” (ibid.).[^s1.4] In this concept of identity contradiction is already implicitly contained. Truth consists “only in the unity of identity with diversity” (GW XI, 262f).[^s1.5]

##### 1.2. Absolute Difference as the Reverse Side of Reflexive Identity

Insofar as this identity is not determined from outside, i.e. does not arise through abstraction, but reflexively negates itself, it is in this immediacy already a difference. Hegel calls this difference the absolute difference.

The adjective “absolute” means in this case that a concept contains its counter-concept and has no other outside itself. This difference is thus called absolute because it itself is at the same time an identity.[^s1.6] It is the self-relating negativity that we have already considered as identity. Precisely through the fact that identity is at the same time an absolute negation, identity itself is difference. Identity as a self-relating absolute negativity or absolute reflection is both a unity with itself and the activity that repels itself from itself: “Identity is thus in itself absolute non-identity” (GW XI, 262). “Difference in itself” is accordingly “the self-relating difference; thus it is the negativity of itself, the difference not of an other, but of itself from itself” (GW XI, 266). In this reflection identity and absolute difference are one, as in positing reflection the positing and presupposing reflection were one (GW XI, 250ff.). The former designated the one side of this reflection as unity of positing and of positedness, the latter the other side as the non-unity of both.

Therefore this absolute difference adds nothing contentually to what was already present in identity.[^s1.7] The difference between the two concepts lies in the different emphasis on equality with itself or on negativity, which both are properly contained in the same concept of essence as self-relating negativity.

Thus far it is clear that the identity that first seemed like a simple sameness is a unity of identity and of difference, and that absolute difference itself has identity and difference as moments within itself. Here one finds a reflexive relation whose moments are also their whole relation itself: Identity and difference are the extremes of relations and the relations themselves, the whole and its parts at once:
> “Difference is the whole and its own moment; as identity is just as much its whole and its moment.” (GW XI, 266)[^s1.8]

This relation, in which the moments of the whole are also the whole itself, is a mark of absolute reflection. It makes possible that movement of the categories of the logic of essence which rests only upon itself.

From the identity of identity and of difference on the one hand and their difference on the other there arises the concept of diversity. Now the moments of difference are themselves reflections into themselves.

#### 2. Diversity

##### 2.1. The Difference of the Diverse in General

The concept of identity and of absolute difference executed above, which are properly one, makes it possible here to thematize at the same time both the diversity of what is pre-given such as things on the one hand and the relation of the relations themselves on the other, such as sameness and diversity.

Identity falls apart through its own reflection itself into diversity.[^s1.9] Identity as self-relating reflection is on the one hand identical with itself, but on the other hand also distinguished from itself. In that it is thus both identity and difference, it divides itself and falls apart again into diversity, whose extremes are identity and difference.

Into this diversity Hegel first places the domain of reality, where qualitative difference was predominant, namely the domain of the logic of being at the level of the determinations of reflection. Second, external reflection thereby appears here again in this manner.[^s1.10] The logic of being is namely contained in the determinations of reflection as diversity, just as in the logic of reflection as external reflection. But being or determinate being may no longer, as already with the latter, emerge as immediate being, because the development from being to essence has brought forth the result that immediacy is a product of doubled mediation, of doubled negation. Therefore being is now introduced as identity or reflection.[^s1.11]

Thus the moments of diversity are not something and its other, but identity and difference, which are both identical with themselves. But the diversity thus arisen is not yet “real” diversity. For while with qualitative difference, i.e. with real negativity, a determinate being relates itself to an other as other determinate being and both mutually determine one another, the two extremes of this diversity, identity and difference, exist independently of the other. They have no relation to one another and are indifferent against one another. Each extreme of this diversity is first the identity existing for itself:
> “The moments of difference are identity and difference itself. They are diverse as reflected into themselves, relating themselves to themselves; thus they are in the determination of identity, relations only to themselves.” (GW XI, 267)

Each extreme of this diversity is thus a simple identity that does not exhibit difference within itself. Precisely for this reason diversity falls into the structure of external reflection. Because both identities are indifferent against one another, what distinguishes both and determines them as diverse stands outside themselves:[^s1.12]
> “[I]n that each of these moments is thus related only to itself, they are not determined against one another. – Because they are now in this way not distinguished in themselves, difference is external to them. The diverse thus do not relate themselves to one another as identity and difference, but only as diverse in general, which are indifferent against one another and against their determinacy.” (ibid.)

Thus reflection as a whole has become, with diversity, an external reflection, in that each moment has become reflection-into-itself. Difference itself stands outside these extremes. Each extreme is indifferent against its own determination, insofar as it is indifferent against the other. Their determinacies they gain from an external reflection.

##### 2.2. Two Reflections

In the manner presented here reflection has according to Hegel “become external to itself” (GW XI, 267). Because the moments themselves as reflections are indifferent against one another, they cannot in this diversity be determined through themselves, but must be determined by a third. But this dependence of diversity upon a third Hegel develops only later. First he investigates still more closely the moments themselves analyzed here.

In external reflection its moments are doubly determined: once as reflection-into-itself and positedness; and then as reflection-in-itself and external reflection. These two pairwise determinations are properly not to be joined directly to one another, as the text suggests. First identity is determined as reflection-into-itself and difference as positedness. But it already resulted that the two moments are reflections, as is also again established here.[^s1.13] Insofar as this is so each is the whole, and as the whole they lose their determinacies of being identity or difference. They have no determinacy, i.e. no negation against one another. But as the logic of reflection has clearly shown, reflection negates itself and thereby posits itself as positedness. From this the double character of the moments becomes clear:
> “There is thus that doubled present, reflection into itself as such, and determinacy as negation, or positedness.” (GW XI, 268)

When reflection makes itself into positedness, it becomes external to itself. Therefore Hegel can here say: “Positedness is the reflection that has become external to itself” (ibid.). Moreover the reflection that has become external is now only in itself reflection-into-itself.[^s1.14] It is the reflection-into-itself that however still remains potential. Then these two sides of positedness form the moments of diversity:
> “Reflection in itself and external reflection are thus two determinations into which the moments of difference, identity and difference, posited themselves.” (ibid.)

In this connection it is to be borne in mind that these two moments here do not directly concern that reflection-into-itself and positedness, but are alone the two sides of the latter.[^s1.15] This is made possible through the structure of diversity as external reflection. For in this diversity the extremes that constitute it are not only a simple determinate being as positedness, but they are themselves reflections. They are identities, insofar as they are identical only with themselves, and they are differences, insofar as they posit themselves as an other and are diverse from this. The extremes are thus the identity of identity and of difference on the one hand and the difference of identity and of difference on the other. For this reason diversity is that of the two reflections that were presented in the “logic of reflection” as stages of its development: of inner (absolute) and of external reflection. From this it results that the logic that is here conducted in determinations of reflection stands at the meta-level of the logic of reflection itself. But in this diversity this relation between the reflections themselves is external. In externality lies the specific character of diversity. Then it has become possible to treat at the same time both the diversity of the two forms of positedness and that of the reflections. Hegel determines these two reflections as follows:
\1) “Reflection in itself,” i.e. the reflection-into-itself that remains implicit, is identity. It is “indifferent against difference” (GW XI, 268). Precisely thereby however it is the relation of the diverse (of identity and of difference): “[I]t is diversity” (ibid.). Thus reflection is only in itself and consequently only an implicit identity. This identity is first identity in that it relates itself against difference. Both moments of diversity are themselves identities, “both are reflections into themselves” (ibid.). They are each diverse in general. But each of these identities has “so reflected itself into itself […] that it is properly the one reflection of the two moments into itself” (ibid.), i.e., that it itself as reflection already includes two moments within itself.[^s1.16]

\2) “External reflection” is by contrast a difference, but not “the absolute difference” that we have seen above, but a “determinate difference,” because it is indifferent against reflection in itself. Identity and difference, which both constitute the difference, are externally determined, thus “not determinations that are in and for themselves”[^s1.17] (GW XI, 268).

It is noteworthy that here these two reflections are not immediately conceived as two extremes of diversity itself. Each extreme is both the whole and a part, and thus a reflection-into-itself that is at the same time identity and diversity. In the determination of reflection in itself or of external reflection one of the two sides has been abstracted from, which in principle however both possess.
