It can be established that Hegel, through this conception of diversity as two reflections, brings into connection with one another the relation of the domain of the pure understanding and that of sensible experience, which according to Kant were supposed to remain separate. “Reflection in itself” as reflection that does not presuppose givenness and relates itself only to itself counts according to Kant for the pure understanding. External reflection counts by contrast for the empirical stance of thinking, which proceeds from givennesses. Moreover we can see in the “whole reflection” (GW XI, 267), which treats the relation of the two, a concept of reflection that is comparable with Kantian transcendental reflection.

##### 2.3. External Reflection as Comparison: Likeness and Unlikeness

At this point first the second aspect of diversity as external reflection is considered. This can be assigned to the empirical use of the understanding that Kant introduced in the “Transcendental Analytic,” then however at the same time to “transcendental reflection,” which is related to that and to pure thinking. Through this presentation of diversity as an external reflection Hegel attempts to show that this is nevertheless supposed to develop itself into inner absolute reflection. These two moments are external to one another. For this reason they are on the one hand “the external identity,” which is thereby itself diversity, and the external difference on the other. These external moments Hegel now calls likeness and unlikeness.[^s1.18]

In this connection the specific structure of diversity now finally becomes clear, the structure that consists of the three members likeness, unlikeness and the reflection external to these. The determinations of likeness and unlikeness demand a third of them: “Whether something is equal to another something or not concerns neither the one nor the other. […] [T]he identity or non-identity as likeness and unlikeness is the regard of a third, which falls outside them” (GW XI, 268). At this point Hegel now introduces comparing, which Kant ascribes to reflection in general.

This difference, which over against that absolute difference is called “the determinate difference,”[^s1.19] is no longer itself the reflection into itself, but “this it has outside it” (ibid.). This reflection-into-itself, related to this difference, is also no longer the simple reflection, but “the reflection into itself standing over against them [the moments of difference]” (ibid.).

This external reflection is an external deed of comparing. In order to characterize this, Hegel uses the words “insofar,” “sides” and “regard” and thereby points to the fact that judgments made do not hold generally, but only under a determinate standpoint. Of “regard” there was already talk at “absolute difference.” But there the things are distinguished “in one and the same regard” (GW XI, 266). The other or non-equality had a reflexive character, and the difference admitted no other regard, because it belonged to it itself. But now with diversity the “regard” belongs in a third, so that it is relativized. Thus the measure of the comparison belongs neither to what is to be compared nor to likeness or unlikeness as the moments of this comparison itself, but to a third standing over against both. The diversity that Hegel here introduces is thus a relative difference. Precisely for this reason “[t]his relation, comparing, […] goes over from likeness to unlikeness and from this to that and back again” (GW XI, 268). Whether something is equal or unequal with another depends upon according to which “side,” in which “regard” they are compared. According to one side a thing is equal with an other, but perhaps according to another unequal (GW XI, 269). The relativity or the externality of reflection in comparing falls into fluctuations of determination.

##### 2.4. Dissolution of Diversity

First Hegel paradoxically asserts that already in this diversity the dissolution of the two extremes of diversity, of likeness and unlikeness, has arisen: “Through this their [of likeness and of unlikeness] separation from one another they only sublate themselves” (GW XI, 269). Likeness and unlikeness are free of contradiction only through the fact that they are determined in a distinguished manner according to certain “sides” or in certain “regards”—i.e. through external reflection. But these moments of diversity themselves must not only be determined through external reflection, but now have the relation to one another. For likeness is likeness in that it is diverse from unlikeness and determined over against it, and conversely.[^s1.20] But if likeness and unlikeness, as we have hitherto seen, are to be thought without reference to one another—i.e. independently as reflection into itself—they can according to Hegel no longer be the likeness and the unlikeness that must be determined against one another, but both likenesses:
> “Through their indifference however against one another, likeness is related only to itself, unlikeness is just as much an own regard and reflection for itself; each is thus equal to itself; difference has disappeared, since they have no determinacy against one another; or each is hereby only likeness.” (ibid.)

In order to avoid the threatening contradiction, external reflection must reduce the difference of the determinations—of likeness and of unlikeness—to the difference of regards. But according to Hegel there already follows upon this to-and-fro of determination through the change of regard the destruction of these determinations. “This indifferent regard, or the external difference, thus sublates itself, and is the negativity of itself in itself” (ibid.). Thus the determinations of the moments are in themselves untenable and dissolve themselves.

Now the third as the comparing is thematized. Hegel attempts through this concept of reflexive negation to include the instance of regard—i.e. subjective reflection—into the movement of objective reflection. “It [the external difference as indifferent regard] is that negativity which in comparing belongs to the comparing” (ibid.). The comparing is with external reflection distinguished from the compared, but this difference itself is not thematized and therefore stands “beyond the compared as well as beyond the moments of comparison” (ibid.), i.e. beyond likeness and unlikeness. As instance of regard reflection is “the negative unity of both” (ibid.). That to-and-fro of the determinacies of its moments concerns this third, which itself remains equal and makes those moments unitary. Hegel here clearly calls it “a subjective doing falling outside them” (ibid.). Insofar as this doing determines its moments as likeness or unlikeness, this unity is “the nature of likeness and unlikeness themselves” (ibid.). In this negative unity now consists the likeness of likeness and of unlikeness.

##### 2.5. From Infinite Regress to Opposition

Diversity is dissolved in that each of its moments has become equal to the other and the unity of both moments falls into the external subjective doing. There thus arises here the likeness of likeness and unlikeness. But this likeness is still distinguished from the moments themselves, only “posited in them”:
> “But this their negative unity is further also posited in them; they namely have the reflection that is in itself outside them, or are the likeness and unlikeness of a third, of an other than they themselves are.” (GW XI, 269)

Indeed Hegel here goes a step further when he understands the negative unity as “posited.” In that it is conceived as posited, it no longer stands “beyond the moments,” but is itself a moment. But the fundamental structure of diversity thereby remains preserved, insofar as the unity still remains distinguished from both moments and a third.[^s1.21]

Hegel wants from here to show that the three-poled structure of diversity passes over into the two-poled structure of opposition. This transition is first supposed to be possible when each moment is again reflected into itself and thereby includes that external subjective instance as third within itself. Hegel however gives here in this regard no sufficient grounding; he merely asserts:
> “The like and the unlike is thus the unlike of itself. Each is thus this reflection, likeness, that it is itself and unlikeness, unlikeness, that it is itself and likeness.” (GW XI, 269f.)

Thereby each moment suddenly gains back the reflexive character. Previously the reciprocal determination of likeness and of unlikeness is supposed to have arisen, so that each moment includes its other within itself, which Hegel here however likewise does not elucidate in detail. In the Encyclopedia this process is formulated more convincingly—but very roughly—as process from the indifferent relation of the diverse sides or regards into “a shining into the other” (Enc. 1830, § 118). A more precise presentation however still remains lacking here as well. This course can however be interpreted as follows: In that the negative unity was grasped as positedness, it becomes possible to grasp it as a moment of diversity. It is a likeness that is distinguished from the diversity (unlikeness) of likeness and unlikeness. But this process can be infinitely repeated and fall into an infinite regress. For this reason both determinations—likeness and unlikeness—would in this infinite regress be mutually determined and invert themselves against one another.

In this manner Hegel includes into the diversity of likeness and unlikeness the instance of the comparing. Now beyond this the instance of the compared as substrate of both determinations is also to find consideration.

Hegel appeals in this connection to the difference between reflection-into-itself and positedness. He now formulates: “Likeness and unlikeness constituted the side of positedness, against the compared or the diverse, which had determined itself as the reflection that is in itself against them” (GW XI, 270). When he here speaks in the preterite, he recurs to the named difference between reflection-into-itself and positedness. The compared or the diverse had thus been determined as “the reflection that is in itself.” It is compared with another, and it is determined whether it is equal with it or not (1st level). Likeness and unlikeness designate the relations between these compared (2nd level). Reflection stands in diversity at both levels outside the moments; it namely stands on the one hand externally over against the compared and on the other hand over against likeness and unlikeness (3rd level).[^s1.22] Through the designation “being” it is suggested that the reflection that is in itself belongs to the level of determinate being, thus to the 1st level.

But likeness and unlikeness are at the same time themselves the compared or the diverse, when one takes into account the second and the third level. The reflection that is in itself (1st level) is then as reflection at the same time likeness. Now reflection is “positedness itself” (GW XI, 270). Thus that determinate difference has become the “indeterminate difference,” since the reflection that is in itself relates itself only to itself and is not determined against one another or does not determine itself.

Hegel summarizes the process just presented in one sentence: “The merely diverse thus passes over through positedness into negative reflection” (ibid.). Problematic about this is that he has presented only the course from the diverse to positedness, not however that further one which reaches “into negative reflection.” In order to close this gap, it is helpful to point out that Hegel sees behind this course from the diverse to positedness a reverse course from positedness to the reflection that is in itself. Only when both courses take place at once can the “reflection that is in itself” and the determinations in “negative reflection” be united: “Thus likeness and unlikeness themselves, positedness, goes through indifference or the reflection that is in itself back into the negative unity with itself; into the reflection which is the difference of likeness and unlikeness in itself” (ibid.).[^s1.23]

There are thus now no longer three, but only two factors: likeness and unlikeness. For each moment now has likeness and unlikeness at once and is insofar reflection-into-itself. Both moments—both likeness and unlikeness—are themselves negative unities. This relation, in which two independent extremes are opposed to one another, constitutes opposition.

### B. Exposition of the Determinations of Reflection 2: The Logic of Opposition and the Speculative Conception of Kantian Real Opposition

Diversity counted as external reflection; opposition now counts as determining reflection, namely that reflection which has again become self-referential. For this reason opposition should also be designated as “determining,” although Hegel finds in opposition “the determinate reflection” (GW XI, 272). But reflection with opposition is still only “determinate,” because here only “difference is completed” (ibid.), but does not dissolve itself, as will happen with contradiction.[^s1.24]

The moments of diversity were likeness and unlikeness; now the moments of opposition are “the positive” and “the negative.” From this we must first investigate how those develop themselves into these. The opposition of the positive and of the negative is presented in the *Science of Logic* in three stages. But it is first the third stage that in Hegel appears as the proper shape of opposition.[^s1.25] Indeed the first two stages therefore seem superfluous, but Hegel attempts with this threefold division to show in what manner his concept of opposition elevates itself from Kantian real opposition.[^s1.26] Kantian real opposition counts for Hegel not as proper opposition, but as the opposition that still belongs to diversity. Therefore it has its systematic place in the second stage of opposition.[^s1.27] It will thus here be presented that and how opposition passes over from relative diversity through real opposition to proper opposition in the Hegelian sense and dissolves itself into contradiction. In this manner Hegel’s engagement with the Kantian qualitative concepts of reflection agreement and conflict and with real opposition is also to be presented.

#### 1. Moments of Opposition: The Positive and the Negative

Diversity as external reflection had—as already shown—the third outside itself, which was the negative unity of its extremes—likeness and unlikeness. This third constitutes the essence of the moments of diversity. An extreme of diversity as diverse is thus in a double sense diverse from an other: on the one hand from the other extreme of diversity, namely from yet another diverse, and on the other hand from the unity of both, which constitutes its essence—i.e. its being-in-and-for-itself:
> “[T]heir [of the moments of difference—T.O.] identity with themselves is not only the indifference of each against that distinguished from it, but against being-in-and-for-itself as such; an identity with itself against that reflected into itself.” (GW XI, 272)

Each moment of diversity—i.e. likeness and unlikeness—is an identity with itself. Insofar as this is so each is “indifferent” against the other. But they are diverse in that yet an other outside them compares and distinguishes them. This “third” was in the previous section assumed as a subjective doing. An extreme does not relate itself to this third, but it treats and compares those with one another. It is the “reflection into itself” that relates itself externally to the diverse. Thus this reflection of diversity is here called “the alienated reflection” (ibid.).[^s1.28]

Likeness and unlikeness as extremes of diversity are—as likewise already shown—not immediate being. The former is the likeness of likeness and unlikeness, and the latter is the unlikeness of likeness and unlikeness. Therefore the third is to count as the unity of both—namely as the likeness of the likeness of likeness and unlikeness on the one hand and of the unlikeness of likeness and unlikeness on the other. But this likeness again means an extreme of diversity over against the unlikeness of its extremes. At this point an infinite regress arose. The overcoming of external reflection again and again yielded yet a new external reflection. But it is precisely this structure of the categories as the infinite regress where each extreme of diversity gains the structure of self-referential reflection, in which each moment is at the same time the unity of the two extremes. Here both extremes are no longer moments that depend upon the external third, as in diversity, but each extreme has now become an independent “reflection into itself.” Likeness and unlikeness are now independent—and precisely thereby diversity becomes opposition:
> “[T]hey both [likeness and unlikeness—T. O.] reflected into themselves constitute the determinations of opposition. Their reflection into themselves consists in that each in itself is the unity of likeness and unlikeness.” (GW XI, 272)

Now opposition, which has arisen directly from this diversity and indirectly from identity, is the “unity of identity and of diversity” (ibid.). This unity has arisen through the fact that the extremes of diversity have internalized into themselves their essence, their unity external to themselves and thereby the other extreme. “Each of these moments is thus in its determinacy the whole” (ibid.).

These independent extremes—likeness and unlikeness—which are however opposed to one another, Hegel now calls “the positive” and “the negative.” At this point he thus introduces the concept of the negative—although not negative magnitude—as moment of opposition. On account of their two-sidedness Hegel here gives two definitions of these concepts: unity and positedness:
> “This equality with itself reflected into itself, which in itself contains the relation to unlikeness, is the positive; thus unlikeness which in itself contains the relation to its non-being, likeness, is the negative.” (GW XI, 273)

In this first definition of the concepts—as positive and negative—the emphasis lies especially upon their character that each contains the relation to the other within itself and insofar is the unity of itself and of the other. Beyond this they still have another side as positedness:

“Or both are positedness; insofar now the distinguished determinacy is taken as distinguished determinate relation of positedness to itself, so opposition is on the one hand the positedness reflected into its equality with itself, on the other hand the same reflected into its unlikeness with itself; the positive and negative.” (ibid.)

The positive and the negative as moments of opposition are now “positedness.” But this positedness is not posited by another “positing,” but has the relation only to itself. Therefore the positive is not the likeness with something other according to an external measure, but likeness with itself; correspondingly the negative is the unlikeness with itself. Both are at the same time also the unities of themselves and of the other, independent reflections-into-themselves.
> “Both thus, the positedness reflected into equality with itself has unlikeness, and the positedness reflected into unlikeness with itself has also likeness in it.” (ibid.)

Hegel summarizes this in one sentence: “The positive and the negative are thus the sides of opposition that have become independent” (ibid.). In this connection it is to be emphasized that both, in that each of them is at the same time their unity, no longer demand the third that was necessary in diversity, but now stand in a two-poled relation. Each pole thus also gains the two-sided character that “positing reflection” had: both as positedness and as the activity of positing. The positive and the negative are now not only posited, but also determined: Thus “each has its determinacy not in an other, but in itself” (ibid.).

#### 2. The First Two Shapes of Opposition

As has already been executed several times in the literature,[^s1.29] opposition is unfolded in three shapes. Opposition is first an opposing of both negative moments against one another, second the relative, external opposing and third that of the moments that are themselves reflexive. Hegel moreover treats in the Remark to this section the concept of the positive and of the negative, “as it occurs in arithmetic” (GW XI, 275), namely the concept of positive and negative magnitudes, whose opposing Kant presented as real opposition. Hegel thus divides this concept into two kinds: “the two real determinations of the positive and negative” (ibid.). The first (1) holds for the first and second shape of opposition in the main text, and the second (2) for the third. Hegel further divides the first determination into the “opposed magnitudes in general” (GW XI, 276, also 277) (1.1) and the “real or indifferent” (GW XI, 277) (1.2). It seems convincing to ascribe these three kinds of opposed magnitudes to the three stages of the concept of opposition itself. Hegel himself initially also does this, but fails at the correspondence of the first shape of the concept of opposition with 1.1.

Despite this inadequacy, which we shall now investigate more precisely, one can clearly recognize Hegel’s intention of integrating the Kantian concept of real opposition into his logical system. Hegel attempts with this division of the first logical opposing into the two arithmetic applications to clarify the two factors that Kantian real opposition implies. In this treatment it is indeed not conceived dynamically, as with Kant, but again grasped logically—yet not formal-logically, but reflection-logically.

Only in the third shape of opposition is that opposition sublated: But this specific concept of opposition is according to Hegel not his own creation. Rather this concept is already used in arithmetic calculation.

##### 2.1. “Opposed in General”: Negative Opposing Against One Another

“The positive” and “the negative” exhibit—as has already been presented—each two sides, namely the side of reflection on the one hand and that of positedness on the other. Each is at times positedness, because it exists only insofar as the other exists. Without this the former cannot consist, because it has its own determination over against the other. At times however each is a reflection-into-itself as whole, because it can exist only when the other does not exist. Only when it takes the other into itself and makes it its own—i.e. when it cancels the other—can it consist. Hegel here calls the positive and the negative “absolute moments of opposition” (GW XI, 273). But these two moments initially have only the same determination, so that each consists through the non-being of the other and thereby is at the same time dependent upon this. Here it is not yet posited which of the moments is positive or negative. Insofar as this is so they are neither the positive nor the negative, but “both are negative against one another” (ibid.). They are therefore initially called only “opposed in general” (ibid.).

These moments however have only a consistence as reflection. Each is “the one mediation of opposition in general” (GW XI, 274). Hegel relates this consistence of the moments of opposition to the absolute value in arithmetic (“$a$” or “$|a|$” in today’s designation[^s1.30]), whereby however no closer determination of the negativity or positivity of “$a$” has yet been undertaken.[^s1.31]

##### 2.2. Relative Opposing of the Positive and Negative

In the first concept of opposition, whose moments are independent and only negatively opposed to one another, and which insofar lack the determinacy of being positive or negative, external reflection returns, as the Remark has already anticipatorily shown. Opposition is again a diversity: “The two sides are thus merely diverse” (GW XI, 274). That diversity itself stemmed from absolute difference, which had two reflections-into-themselves as its moments. This path from absolute difference to diversity is now repeated at the level of opposition.

In that the moments cannot determine themselves as positive or negative, they demand a third that is to determine them; otherwise there arises “that first identity in which they are only moments” (ibid.); Here the unity of both falls outside themselves. They are now “indifferent” against their unity. But on the other hand this unity was supposed to belong to them themselves. Therefore “each [moment—T. O.] is indifferent against this its reflection into its non-being,” and, insofar as it posits the other in its reflection, “against its own positedness” (ibid.). In this indifference the determinations of the moments are given by external reflection through the closer determination “insofar”—thus according to an external measure. These determinations are only relative, i.e. according to another criterion they could be determined otherwise:
> “[T]o each side there thus indeed belongs one of the determinacies of positive and negative; but they can be interchanged, and each side is of such a kind that it can just as well be taken as positive as as negative.” (GW XI, 274)[^s1.32]

In this formulation we find the character of Kantian real opposition, according to which determining also belonged to an external measure. Hegel attempts to integrate the concept of real opposition, which Kant had introduced into philosophy, into his system and to overcome it in the systematic development, which however is still to be further executed.

#### 3. Arithmetic Application of the First Two Shapes of Opposition

As already elucidated, Hegel shows in the Remark to this paragraph in what manner the concept of opposition, which he divides into three kinds, occurs in arithmetic. He attempts for each shape of opposition to find an application in arithmetic or rather through this concept of opposition to ground the calculus with positive and negative numbers.

To these three shapes of the concept of opposition in the kinds of arithmetic calculus the research literature dedicates itself extensively;[^s1.33] it is however not so simple as it appears *prima vista* to clarify the correspondences of the logical forms with the kinds of application. Hegel in the first paragraph of the Remark-text first subdivides the determination of opposed magnitude into the indifferent opposing and the opposing of “position in itself” and of “the negative in itself.” It is initially unambiguous that the second corresponds to the third stage of logical opposition. With the first stage by contrast it is more complicated. The first of the two determinations of the positive and negative is that “namely that the first time, a merely diverse, immediate determinate being underlies, whose simple reflection into itself is distinguished from its positedness, the opposing itself” (GW XI, 275). Insofar as Hegel here distinguishes the positedness of the opposing from reflection, this opposing is to have the determination of external reflection. But he continues:
> “This therefore counts only as not being in and for itself, and indeed belonging to the diverse, so that each is an opposed in general, but also exists indifferently against it for itself, and it is all one which of the two opposed diverse is considered as positive or as negative.” (GW XI, 275f.)

From this determination of the first opposed magnitude it results that it is a mixture of the two first shapes of the concept of opposition. For each moment of this opposing is only an opposed in general, which is indifferent against the determination whether it be positive or negative. But this indifference leads to its being externally determined. Only thereby can Hegel speak of the diverse, since diversity is supposed to be dependent upon external determination or external reflection.

Hegel in the second and third paragraphs of the Remark-text divides this determination of opposed magnitudes once more into two divisions and executes in the fourth and fifth paragraphs the first sub-determinations and in the sixth and seventh the second. But this division does not correspond exactly to that of the first two shapes of opposition, as is customarily asserted in the research literature. The second shape of opposition as diversity is already present in the first subdivision of this determination of magnitude. For the first shape of the concept of opposition only the fifth paragraph holds, in which absolute value becomes thematic. In this connection one can however properly not speak of the real determination of opposed magnitudes.[^s1.34] In what follows we investigate three arithmetic examples of the first determinations. Thereby it first holds to reconstruct Hegel’s argumentation.

##### 3.1. Opposed Magnitudes in General

The magnitudes are first simply opposed; against this it is indifferent whether the one is designated positively as + a or negatively as − a. First “+ a” and “− a” count as “opposed magnitudes in general” (GW XI, 276).[^s1.35] With them the simple “a” or “|a|” underlies, which is indifferent for the opposed, thus absolute value. But this foundation is in this opposition only “unity that is in itself” or “dead foundation” (ibid.). “[T]he one is just as much an opposed as the other” (ibid.). Hegel also calls this “their [of the opposed—T.O.] identical relation […], which is indifferent against opposition itself” (ibid.). Thereby there is only one indifferent. The same sum of money can designate both wealth and debt—depending upon whether it is a matter of a debtor or a creditor. A stretch of way can be understood both as positive and as negative, but it is not two ways, but remains one way. Thus this state of affairs can be presented as “+ y − y = y” (ibid.), which expresses only the ordinate in calculus form.

##### 3.2. Real and Negative Opposing of Magnitude as Diversity

The second arithmetic form of opposition Hegel connects with the first. Both yield one form. But this division spoils the division of the concept of opposition in the main text. Insofar as this is so this form would have to be distinguished from the first, in order to achieve the exact correspondence of the Remark-text with the main text.

That first opposing is not yet real, as Hegel later clearly says.[^s1.36] Although Hegel in the fourth paragraph attempts to explain the first sub-determination of the first real determination of the positive and negative—i.e. opposed magnitudes in general—he already ascribes to this the indifference against the determination that is to be given through the external regard (cf. GW XI, 276). Here is the systematic place for the mutually sublating opposed magnitudes that Kant presents as real opposition.

We have already seen that Kant with the second pair of concepts of reflection “agreement and conflict” introduces a new concept of opposition over against the logical one: real opposition or real opposing. With appearance (*phaenomenon*) two magnitudes, although both are for themselves positive, can be opposed, while this according to formal logic and the ontology that was built upon it was impossible, because opposition here was possible only between the real and the negative and negation meant only the “lack” of position. With this new concept of opposition, to which the new natural science is said to have led Kant,[^s1.37] it depends upon the standpoint which of two realities is held for positive and which for negative. The decision about this is accordingly only subjective and cannot be determined by these realities themselves.

The opposition thus understood Hegel finds in the arithmetic opposing in which one moment sublates the other, as “+ y − y = 0” or “− 8 + 3 [= − 5].” In the last example Hegel however does not name the right side of the calculus formula, but he says: “in − 8 + 3, the 3 positive are negative in the 8” (GW XI, 276). In this calculation 3, although a positive number, is subtracted from 8, although a negative number. 3 is sublated by − 8, although the latter is a negative number, which already shows the relativity of this opposing.

The result of this opposition is sublation (in the usual sense) or privation. Hegel also explains this with similar examples as Kant gave them in the writing on negative magnitude: “An hour’s way made toward the east, and just as much back toward the west, sublates the way first made” (ibid.).[^s1.38] An hour’s way toward the east and the same way toward the west are for themselves neither positive nor negative. That that way is determined positively and this negatively depends upon in which direction one wants to go. These determinations themselves are first given to each through this subjective stance; they are not related to them themselves.

In this opposing Hegel sees three factors that he also found with diversity. One of these factors is here the “identical relation.” To it there corresponds in the last example the “a” without sign.[^s1.39] Hegel still names “two indifferent” factors that likewise constitute this opposition. Here it is to be remarked that they are also called “diverse” (GW XI, 277). Correspondingly “− a” and “+ a” are the indifferent diverse. Which of the two is called negative is at this point still arbitrary.

Here the determination of the positive or of the negative is thus only relative. It is determined “according to regards falling outside it” (GW XI, 276) which of the opposed is positive and which negative. Hegel thus “smuggles” external reflection into the first opposition, which properly first belongs to the second opposition as diversity. This is a result of his attempt to understand opposing in general as opposition of + a and − a, although the moments were supposed to relate themselves only negatively against one another. Insofar as this is so this attempt of Hegel’s is to be assessed as not especially convincing.

But this opposing of magnitude is nevertheless different from the second, because the positivity of each moment is not yet recognized. Therefore Hegel reminds us that in these opposed “their identical relation is present.” With the opposing of + a and − a by contrast there is “only One a” (ibid.).

Here we thus clearly see that what Hegel unfolds as second opposition has much in common with Kantian real opposition. This opposition is relative; it is external to the realities that determine whether they are positive or negative, and each moment consists for itself, i.e. it is real (in the Kantian sense) or positive.

##### 3.3. Real and Positive Opposing of Magnitude

In the last two kinds of arithmetic opposing there was talk only of one indifferent; now however two indifferent become thematic. This opposing is expressed through “− 8 + 3 = 11” or “+ y − y = 2y.” This kind of opposed magnitude Hegel calls “real or indifferent” (GW XI, 277). “Real” must here be understood not in the specifically Hegelian, but in the usual sense, namely as “positive.” This means that that absolute value which in the opposition is assumed as positive or negative is for itself positive: “[B]oth have a special consistence and are positive” (GW XI, 276). “Also the way covered toward the east and toward the west is” thus for this reason properly only “the sum of a twofold effort or the sum of two periods of time” (ibid.). We can understand Hegel’s intention in this regard that he here attempts to place the positivity of each opposed. That was an important mark of the Kantian concept of real opposition. Hegel thus offers in the Remark an analysis of Kantian real opposition, especially applied to arithmetic.

#### 4. Third Shape of Opposition: “Opposition” as Double Reflection

At the last stage it was shown that the second opposition designates an opposing that still remains standing with external reflection. At the third stage of opposition the proper opposition is now introduced, which is finally free of the determination of diversity. The last relative opposing has arisen through the fact that the unity or reflection falls outside its own moments. But these moments were properly each supposed to have reached this unity in themselves. For this reason they would however have to carry the measure for their determination in themselves.[^s1.40] “Each,” Hegel now says, “is in itself positive and negative; the positive and the negative is the determination of reflection in and for itself” (GW XI, 274 – emphasis T. O.). The formula “in and for itself” one may here too not understand simply only as a specifically Hegelian terminology, but must take it literally, as he himself explains somewhat later (GW XI, 275). The positive and the negative are in themselves positive and negative, i.e. each has its own determination without relation to its other.[^s1.41] Yet the positive and the negative are not wholly relationless, but they have their relation to one another through the exclusion of the respective other. In that this exclusion at the same time constitutes their determination, they are in and for themselves positive and negative. The other is thus an essential constituent of the one. Insofar as this is so they are in this relation to the other self-referential (for themselves). With this self-referentiality the two determinations, the positive and the negative, must be determined:
> “The positive has the relation to the other, in which the determinacy of the positive is, in itself; just so the negative is not negative as against an other, but has the determinacy whereby it is negative likewise in itself.” (GW XI, 274)

The three-stage execution of the concept of opposition has already at the beginning of this chapter led us to its proper determination. But only here has it become clear that the positive and the negative each have own determinations that distinguish themselves from that of diversity. Now we have the following determinations of the positive and of the negative:

\1) The positive is on the one hand first positedness. But it is a unity of the two positedness, “positedness as sublated” (ibid.), which posits them both. It is no longer negatively opposed to an other, namely “the non-opposed” (ibid.). But it is only one side of opposition, insofar as it is positive and the contrary of the negative. Yet this is first so when it negates otherness and at the same time reflects it into itself. It thus belongs to the determination of the positive to exclude the negative from itself. Only thus is it independent. The positive contains the negative relation to the negative; only insofar is it “in and for itself” positive (GW XI, 275).
\2) On the other hand the negative is also now independent as “sublated positedness” (GW XI, 274). This negative relates itself positively to itself, insofar as it is also a reflection-into-itself and itself retains its own identity. This negative excludes the positive from itself, because it is “the whole opposition resting upon itself,” while the positive is the “sublated opposition” (GW XI, 275).

Now the positive and the negative, which both constitute opposition, are each for themselves absolute reflection. Opposition is namely that of the reflections-into-themselves, while diversity, as we have seen, was a relation between reflection-into-itself and an external reflection. With opposition the process is repeated in which external reflection becomes absolute reflection. Thereby an opposing of the two absolute reflections has arisen. Each pole of the opposing now consists as “positive.” It is determined neither through the other pole nor through a third, but only through itself. In this new conception of opposition Hegel goes further than Kant with his determination of real opposition, which depends upon external reflection.

With the other conception, which corresponds to the third stage of opposition and designates proper Hegelian opposition, “the positive is the positive in itself, the negative the negative in itself, so that the diverse is not indifferent against this, but this is its determination in and for itself” (GW XI, 276). Hegel first gives the example: “8 − (−3)” (GW XI, 277). The first minus designates only the relation between two numbers. It indicates that the two numbers are opposed to one another. The second minus by contrast indicates that this number −3 itself is a negative number, i.e. an “opposed in itself, outside this relation” (ibid.).

The next example of multiplication seems still more convincing.[^s1.42] In this example it becomes clear that a mistake befalls the understanding when it proceeds from the assumption that if “− $a \cdot + a = -a^2$” is, conversely “+ $a \cdot - a = + a^2$” is, since it in this way misrecognizes the qualitative determinations of the two numbers and takes “plus and minus only as opposed magnitudes in general” (GW XI, 278). According to Hegel however minus means the opposing in and for itself and plus the non-opposing in and for itself. “− $a \cdot + a = -a^2$” accordingly means: There are “− $a$” units of “$+a$,” i.e. according to Hegel the same as “$a$” units of “$+a$,” yet in an opposed manner. For this reason “− $a \cdot + a = -a^2$” must be. If this opposing were only relative, i.e. dependent upon the relation, “+ $a \cdot - a = + a^2$” would result, because “− $a$” is to be multiplied with a in the opposed manner of “− $a$.” Yet “+ $a \cdot - a = -a^2$” must be, because according to Hegel “+” and “−” must be understood in and for themselves as positive or negative. The plus has no power to alter a respective quality, while the minus has it. “− $a \cdot - a = + a^2$” can also be understood in the same manner, because here “$-a$” is multiplied with “$a$” in the opposed manner. In this calculus of the multiplication of two negative numbers Hegel sees an example for his theory of the negation of negation: “The negation of negation however is the positive” (ibid.).

### C. Contradiction and Ground as Result of the Determinations of Reflection

The presentation of the determinations of reflection from identity up to opposition we have related especially to the first two pairs of the Kantian concepts of reflection, “sameness” and “diversity,” “agreement” and “conflict.” In the chapter “The Essentialities or the Determinations of Reflection” in the *Science of Logic* Hegel takes up the thematic again, although he does not explicitly refer to it: He gives the Kantian version of this thematic a place in the reflective movement of the categories. In this manner he attempts to integrate Kantian external reflection and the comportment corresponding to it into his logic. Moreover he unfolds his determinations of reflection, which replace the Kantian concepts of reflection, not ordered beside one another as with Kant, but in a continuous progress in which the comportment of external reflection is “sublated” in the double sense determined by Hegel.[^s1.43] Contradiction is now the last moment of the determinations of reflection, into which all previously treated flow.

But the third phase of the opposition of the positive and negative has already shown the structure of contradiction: The positive and the negative appeared as independent determinations of reflection. Independence means here that each includes the other as its moment. In that sense each moment of opposition is at the same time its whole; this relation of mutual inclusion constitutes contradiction. Its independence lies in the contradiction that it is independent through the fact that it contains the other within itself and at the same time excludes it. The surrender of own independence through the determination of independence thus marks the contradiction:
> “In that the independent determination of reflection in the same regard in which it contains the other, and thereby is independent, excludes the other, it excludes in its independence its own independence from itself; for this consists in containing within itself the determination other to it and thereby alone not being relation to an external, but just as immediately in being itself and excluding from itself the determination negative to it. It is thus the contradiction.” (GW XI, 279)

Important here is that this contradiction is not understood formal-logically, i.e. that it does not lie in the relation of both moments, but each moment of this contradiction already contradicts itself: The independence of each moment consists in including the other and at the same time being independent over against the other. “The positive and negative however are the posited contradiction” (ibid.).[^s1.44]

Hegel also indicates the contradiction in the relation of both moments: The positive negates the negative and excludes it from itself. In this negative relation against the negative it is now the negative. Formulated otherwise: The positive is first positive when it is negative.

Just so the negative is also first the negative in the relation to the positive and in that it is opposed to this. But the positive, to which it is opposed, and on account of which it was negative, is now for its part negative. Insofar as they mutually determine themselves against one another, the negative is now positive (cf. GW XI, 280).

It has been shown that both the positive and the negative each contradict themselves and also that they mutually contradict one another. In this contradiction each is constantly exchanged with the other through mutual determination. Here—in this mutually excluding independence—each moment loses its provisionally established independence: It cannot hold through its determination against the other: both become identical. This movement of contradiction, which is at the same time that of identity, harbors reflection into itself as absolute negativity: “This is the absolute contradiction of the positive, but it is immediately the absolute contradiction of the negative; the positing of both is One reflection” (GW XI, 280).[^s1.45] The determination of the independence of the positive and of the negative means at the same time their dissolution into identity:
> “[E]ach is sheer passing-over or rather the translating of itself into its contrary. This restless vanishing of the opposed in themselves is the next unity which comes about through the contradiction.” (GW XI, 280)

For this reason the dissolution of the contradiction must not be understood—as Hegel’s presentation itself suggests: that both moments first fall into contradiction and this contradiction then dissolves itself at the second stage. The arising of the contradiction means insofar at the same time its resolution.

What Hegel calls ground is this identity into which the contradiction dissolves itself. Ground means not a firm substantiality or a quiet foundation—*ὑποκείμενον*—of those restless moments of contradiction. Rather ground is the “restless vanishing of the opposed in themselves” (ibid.). This “dissolved contradiction is thus the ground” (GW XI, 282).

The contradiction into which Hegel lets all determinations of reflection flow surpasses all concepts of opposition that were already treated at the previous stages.
> “It has in general emerged from the consideration of the nature of contradiction that it is for itself still, so to speak, no damage, lack or fault of a thing if a contradiction can be pointed out in it.” (GW XI, 289)

This contradiction designates 1. not the formal-logical contradiction, 2. yet also not only real opposition, but also the opposition of likeness and unlikeness, of agreement and conflict. This logic of the determinations of reflection makes it possible to treat the real level and the meta-level at the same time. This again makes possible a conception of self-relating reflection that precedes reality and produces this only as its effect. In Hegel reality stands in opposition with another, as in Kant’s real opposition. This logic of contradiction disputes that the realities, as Kant assumes with regard to the noumena, agree and are not contradictory. But on the other hand reality and determination already mean for Hegel a negation, while Kant conceives reality as purely positive and admits no negativity for his concept of reality, even if the realities are really opposed to one another. That Hegel criticizes the positive version of the concept of reality in a Remark on the concept of God as the sum of all realities, to which the ontological proof of God refers, shows once more the connection of reality and contradiction:
> “It has already been recalled concerning the ontological proof of the existence of God[^s1.46] that the determination laid at its foundation is the sum of all realities. Of this determination it is customarily first shown that it is possible, because it contains no contradiction, in that reality is taken only as reality without limits. It was recalled that thereby that sum becomes simple indeterminate being, or if the realities are in fact taken as several determinate, the sum of all negations. Taking more closely the difference of reality, it passes from diversity into opposition and thereby into contradiction, and the sum of all realities in general into absolute contradiction in itself. […] The ordinary horror that representing, non-speculative thinking, like nature before the vacuum, has before contradiction, rejects this consequence; for it remains standing with the one-sided consideration of the dissolution of the contradiction into nothing, and does not recognize the positive side of the same, according to which it becomes absolute activity and absolute ground.” (ibid.)

This passage mirrors the whole path that we have hitherto traversed. We proceeded from the concept of reality in the logic of being. There we already gained the cognition concerning the negativity of reality. Hegel there explains that the concept of God, which has traditionally been determined as the sum of all realities, must rather be understood as the sum of all negations. From the logic of reflection it further resulted that determinacy was not supposed to remain a one-time negation or a determinate being, but appears as an effect or a side of the movement of essence in the sense of reflection. Essence is the reflection that negatively relates itself to itself, absolute reflection. Finally we find in the last determination of reflection, namely in contradiction, an element in which this self-relation of negation is grasped and formulated as contradiction, which constitutes the essence of things and also that of God. With this contradiction the goal of metaphysics, ground, is reached. Ground or God is the dissolving contradiction.

### D. The Logical-Ontological Propositions in the *Science of Logic*

Hitherto in the following of the logical course of the determinations of reflection we have left aside the problematic of the logical propositions, which Hegel mainly treats in the Remarks. Kant thematized them in connection with the determination of the concepts of reflection. In what follows, after we have reconstructed Hegel’s speculative presentation of the determinations of reflection, we shall enter upon his critical treatment of the logical and ontological propositions.

We have already seen in the course of the investigation of the development of Hegel’s logic (in the 3rd chapter) that he first treats the logical-ontological propositions in the Metaphysics in the Jena System Drafts II and then in “Essence” in the logics of the earlier Nuremberg period, but finally replaces them through the determinations of reflection. In the Remark of the introductory text of the chapter “The Essentialities or the Determinations of Reflection” in the *Science of Logic* Hegel lets this both developmental- and historico-philosophical trail be recognized: “The determinations of reflection were otherwise customarily taken up into the form of propositions, in which it was stated of them that they hold of everything” (GW XI, 258). That the logical-ontological propositions are there spoken of in the preterite already points to Hegel’s conviction that in science one must no longer speak of the propositions, but of the determinations of reflection. But this does not mean that the “propositions” would no longer be taken into account in Hegel’s logic. Rather his attention, as he later says in the Encyclopedia concerning the relation of his logic to traditional logic,[^s1.47] now lies especially upon the relation of the concepts of reflection to the propositions:[^s1.48] “[I]n that they [sc. the determinations of reflection] are determinacies which are relations in themselves, they insofar already contain the form of the proposition within themselves” (GW XI, 259). And further: “To the determinations of reflection […] the form of the proposition itself lies near” (ibid.).[^s1.49]

But how does Hegel arrive at this consequence? Why is the form of the proposition supposed to transform itself into the form of the determination of reflection? First the principles that are supposed to hold generally may according to him not be restricted to those principles that were traditionally treated as logical or ontological principles. Hegel in this connection also mentions the being-logical categories, in order to show for which “propositions” this claim was still supposed to hold.[^s1.50] Yet it can be established that the corresponding also has to hold for all other categories in his logic. In this sense the whole Hegelian logic takes the place of those propositions. Their categories are to hold for thinking in its whole extent and at the same time for all being.[^s1.51]

At the same time this finding does not yet say that the propositions must be replaced through the determinations of reflection. In this respect Kant is a forerunner of Hegel, because he took up the propositions in the form of the concepts of reflection. Hegel however goes quite further than Kant, for for Kant the logical principles—especially the principle of contradiction—are still the presuppositions against which one must not offend. Only their ontological implications, their applicability as organon, are disputed by Kant. Hegel however is—more radically than Kant—of the conviction that the propositions are only modifications of the determinations of reflection and belong alone to the “in-and-for-itself.” The form of propositions is for his logic “something superfluous” and has a “skew side” (ibid.).[^s1.52] The ground for this may be found in the logical process itself that we have just reconstructed. For the logic of shine and of reflection, through which the determinations of reflection were derived, gives a grounding for the fact that it is the determinations of reflection, not however the traditional propositions, that belong in speculative logic or in the logic of essence.

This thesis is also supported by Hegel’s assertion that the form of the proposition belongs not to essence, but to being, insofar as every proposition demands a something like ‘A’ as subject. Essence is now supposed to have stripped from itself the remainder of being. For this reason not the propositions, but the pure relations that are treated in them, now become the theme. Being has already passed over into essence. The form of the proposition is not that of essence.[^s1.53]

As last and most important ground for the replacing of the propositions through the determinations of reflection Hegel repeatedly names that the form of the proposition hinders one from recognizing the necessity of contradiction among the categories. “Thoughtless consideration” (GW XI, 260), which took possession of the propositions, left them standing unmediated beside one another, although from Hegel’s view contradictions had to appear between them.[^s1.54] Hegel has already demonstrated in the logic of being that a category ultimately contradicts itself and thereby passes over into another; he has however attempted to show this by means of other categories than those that the propositions thematize. Now in the logic of essence the propositions are to be related to one another and the transition from the one to the other and also their contradiction and its dissolution are to be shown. But for this the form of the proposition must be replaced through the form of the determination of reflection.

From Hegel’s decision to treat them in the form of the determinations of reflection it results that the propositions themselves are treated in the Remarks.[^s1.55] Hegel adds in a Remark to contradiction still his own formulation of the principle of contradiction, which is to serve not only this chapter or the logic of essence, but also the whole logic or the whole philosophy of Hegel as principle. The principle of ground then appears in the Remark to the introductory text to the chapter Ground:

#### 1. Dissolution of the Principle of Identity and of Contradiction

Hegel, following the tradition, has held the principle of identity and that of contradiction to be contentually identical. This is “[t]he other expression of the principle of identity” and has over against that a “negative form” (GW XI, 265). Therefore the principle of contradiction is also treated in the Remark to identity (ibid., Enc. §115).[^s1.56]

Hegel’s critique of the principle of identity ran, briefly summarized, that it is only an empty tautology. The manner of thinking that holds fast to this law of thought fails to recognize that identity contains difference within itself and thereby contradiction. The principle of contradiction is also expression of abstract identity.[^s1.57] But the propositions even contain “more than is meant with them” (GW XI, 265), for example—as already seen—the negation of negation.[^s1.58]

For Hegel already the traditional formulation of the law of thought of identity, “A is A,” expresses the return of negation into itself as double negation. When one begins a proposition with the subject and the copula: “A is…,” an assertion about the subject “A” in the form of a predicate that is diverse from the subject is already expected. In this manner the subject “A” is at the same time negated. When one then completes the proposition with “… is A,” one negates this first negation, and A returns from its negation into itself.[^s1.59] That is according to Hegel nothing other than essence as unity of shine and essence, namely essence which itself is being:
> “There thus lies in the form of the proposition in which identity is expressed more than simple, abstract identity; there lies this pure movement of reflection in it, in which the other appears only as shine, as immediate vanishing.” (GW XI, 264)

As demonstrated in the logic of reflection, essence cannot have its being outside itself. Being rather “is” essence; identity is a designation of essence in this sense. Therefore Hegel asserts that this identity is first the “whole reflection” (GW XI, 261). Now however the principle of contradiction contains reflection in “more developed” form, because the proposition once posits the difference and then again negates it (GW XI, 265).

Hegel’s conception of identity, which underlies this conception of the principles of identity and of contradiction, directs itself against traditional logic and ontology when it ascribes to both principles a synthetic character: “It becomes clear from this that the principle of identity itself and still more the principle of contradiction is not merely of analytic, but of synthetic nature” (ibid.). Both the principle of identity and that of contradiction, which were held fast to both in pre-Kantian school metaphysics and in Kant himself as the principles of thinking, contain within themselves the contradiction. They have in Hegel become a mere moment of the movement of reflection, which finally flows into contradiction. Hegel’s stance toward contradiction becomes still clearer in what we want to call the speculative principle of contradiction.[^s1.60]

#### 2. Principle of Diversity or the *principium identitatis indiscernibilium*

In Hegel diversity, as already presented, is no longer related to space (as with Kant), but to determination (as with Leibniz). But in that he shows how diversity dissolves itself, which represents the simple determination of the logic of being in the essence-logical movement of reflection, he also shows the inadequacy of the Leibnizian concept of diversity. Hegel thereby takes a stance against his two great predecessors. He comports himself neither purely formal-logically nor purely transcendental-logically, but his concept of diversity is, like the other determinations of reflection, grasped speculatively.

What constitutes the essence of diversity as determination of reflection is relativity. The measure according to which something is judged as like or unlike over against another stands with this diversity outside the diverse itself. It necessarily demands a third that makes it into an external reflection. This diversity presupposes a qualitative difference, namely a determination, which was supposed to belong to the domain of the logic of being, while the concepts of reflection “sameness and diversity” in Kant belong to quantity.

The Leibnizian *principium identitatis indiscernibilium* Hegel takes up in the Remark to this determination of reflection. Here he also mentions that anecdote which plays in the garden of Herrenhausen, and is communicated by Leibniz in the fourth letter to Clarke,[^s1.61] and is humorously commented upon by Hegel: “Happy times for metaphysics, when one occupied oneself with it at court, and when no other exertion was needed to examine its propositions than to compare tree-leaves!” (GW XI, 271).

Hegel here indicates for the principle of diversity the following two formulations: “All things are diverse” and “There are not two things that are equal to one another” (GW XI, 270).[^s1.62] The first proposition he immediately rejects as tautology, because according to him the subject of the proposition—“all things”—designates plurality and in it diversity is thereby already implied.[^s1.63] By contrast Hegel concedes significance to the last proposition and continues in his analysis:[^s1.64]
> “The proposition however: there are not two things that are perfectly equal to one another, expresses more [than the first proposition—T. O.], namely determinate diversity. Two things are not merely two; numerical plurality is only sameness, but they are diverse through a determination.” (GW XI, 270f.)

According to Hegel what is at issue here is the problematic of determination, which—as already executed—is a central theme of this chapter. Thereby Hegel’s interpretation of this proposition however approaches that of Leibniz. For two things are to be distinguished “through a determination.” To this the parenthesis in the quotation executed above runs: “numerical plurality is only sameness.” Insofar as this is so one sees that “to be two” according to Hegel means no diversity, but sameness. In order to understand this, one must recur to Hegel’s conception of number as unity of continuity and discretion in the doctrine of being.[^s1.65]

Over against this Kant understands under numerical plurality diversity. That is the manner of thinking in which Hegel here finds the ground for the fact that to ordinary representing, i.e. to external reflection, which begins only with givennesses, the second proposition: “There are not two things that are equal to one another” appears strange. Hegel says:
> “Representing, in that it also passes over to determination, takes up these moments themselves as indifferent against one another, so that the one without the other, the mere likeness of things without unlikeness, suffices for determination, or that things are diverse even if they are only numerically many, diverse in general, not unlike.” (GW XI, 271)

For representing thinking diversity is a numerical many, i.e. that there is only diversity in general, but not an unlikeness of the singular moments. Representing thinking does not see that each moment of diversity is qualitatively distinguished from others through its determination. But diversity or plurality is first possible when things are qualitatively distinguished and then again abstracted from their qualitative diversity.
> “The principle of diversity by contrast expresses that things are diverse from one another through unlikeness, that the determination of unlikeness belongs to them just as much as that of likeness, for only both together constitute the determinate difference.” (ibid.)

To representing thinking diversity thus does not appear in this speculative sense. Twoness requires at once being both like and unlike, which—if one lays the principle of contradiction at the foundation—is not possible. In view of this however the question arises how it can be proved that the determination of unlikeness belongs to all things? At this question it becomes clear according to Hegel that one cannot remain standing with external reflection. For non-speculative consideration demands a third in which subject and predicate are mediated, in order to prove a synthetic proposition in which a predicate unlike this is added to the subject. External reflection proceeds from the assumption that the identity of the two determinations is secured through a third. But this external proof of identity brings forth a new difference between both moments on the one hand and between both moments and the third on the other:
> “This proof would have to demonstrate the transition of identity into diversity, and then the transition of this into determinate diversity, into unlikeness. This is however customarily not performed.” (ibid.)

The course of proof of identity leads to a diversity. The attempt to unite this diversity again demands a third, namely as we have seen, “determinate diversity.” This process corresponds to the logical progress from identity through absolute difference up to diversity and its dissolution. Through this impossibility of the proof it comes about “that diversity or external difference is in truth difference reflected into itself, difference in itself, that the indifferent consistence of the diverse is mere positedness, and thereby not external, indifferent difference, but One relation of the two moments” (GW XI, 271). The identity of the third that unites the two moments is itself an identity of identity and of difference: Unlikeness is an unlikeness of likeness and unlikeness. Here reflection-into-itself arises as infinite self-reference. Diversity relates itself to itself. Its moments themselves are accordingly two shapes of self-relation that are themselves unities of both moments.

Thus the proposition: “There are no two things that are wholly equal” dissolves itself, because it in principle also states that things are at once like and unlike.[^s1.66] The proposition itself already contains a contradiction that dissolves it from within and lets it pass over into another determination. “Thus it has passed over into opposition” (ibid.).

According to Hegel non-speculative thinking remains standing with external reflection, in order to preserve a stance from which subjectivity and object are to be distinguished. But this manner of thinking
> “forgets here as elsewhere that thereby the contradiction is not dissolved, but only shoved elsewhere, into subjective or external reflection in general, and that this in fact contains the two moments, which through this removal and transposition are pronounced as mere positedness, as sublated and related to one another in One unity.” (GW XI, 272 – emphasis T. O.)

Kant asserts that the principle of contradiction in the domain of formal logic, i.e. of the pure understanding, presents a sufficient condition of truth, not however in “transcendental logic,” in which the subject relates itself to being as appearance. Hegel by contrast asserts the necessity of contradiction, in that he admits no external, subjective reflection, in which the understanding finds a refuge before contradiction, outside the logical moments. For this reason the concepts of reflection—sameness and diversity in Kant or likeness and unlikeness in Hegel’s terminology—must jointly with the other determinations of reflection flow into contradiction. Diversity here already marks a contradiction, although this is first later really introduced and developed.

The *principium identitatis indiscernibilium* Kant has conceded for the object of the pure understanding; he however at the same time denies the validity of this principle of the old metaphysics for the domain of sensible experience. Rather the identity of two things that have equal determinations is ascribed to the understanding, and their diversity to sensibility. The relation between identity and diversity is thereby not thematic. The operation of transcendental reflection itself, which distinguishes both domains and determines to which transcendental place the representations belong, remains outside. Kant does not see the diversity between reflection itself and the two extremes. Just as little does he thematize the subject of transcendental reflection itself. It is thus in Kant not entirely clear what relation the subject of this reflection[^s1.67] has to the understanding (pure apperception or empirical apperception). If Kant had speculated about this, he would presumably have discovered the speculative relation between the subjects as moments on the one hand and the subject as their unity on the other and thereby also that structure of self-reflection. Kant here halts before infinity and external reflection, although transcendental cognition already contains a self-referential structure.

#### 3. The Principle of the Excluded Third

Both in the Logic for the Middle Class (1810/11) and in the three published Encyclopedias the principle of the excluded third is treated at the determination of reflection of opposing. Only in the *Science of Logic* does the principle appear in the second Remark to contradiction. But Hegel says also there that the principle has arisen from the “determination of opposing” (GW XI, 285). Moreover Hegel at the beginning of the third Remark also claims the principle of the excluded third for opposing (GW XI, 286). Insofar as this is so it is also to be related in the *Science of Logic* to opposition, all the more since it also contentually considered corresponds more unambiguously with opposition than with contradiction.

“Something is either A or not-A; there is no third” (GW XI, 285). This proposition according to Hegel says that everything is to be determined either as positive or as negative. This assertion of Hegel’s must be related to the third stage of the concept of opposition, at which relative opposition as diversity had already been overcome and had thus developed itself into two-poled opposing. Precisely for this reason Hegel can here assert that the significance of the proposition consists in “that identity passes over into diversity and this into opposing” (ibid.). Opposition presents the sharpening of the previous determinations of reflection, which for their part will again sharpen themselves into contradiction.

Hegel also thematizes this proposition against the background of his executions concerning the “determinations” in the logic of being. The principle of the excluded third customarily says “that to a thing of all predicates either this predicate itself or its non-being belongs” (ibid.); i.e. a thing must be determined either as “a” or as “not-a.” “[T]he something is determinate, the proposition states” (ibid.). Behind this understanding of the proposition there hides for Hegel the ontological prejudice—which Kant also disputes with the concept of real opposition—namely that an opposing of being and non-being (as lack of a determination) consists. In this customary version one is to be led back to indeterminacy; but in Hegel determinacy is rather to be carried further to self-determination, as he has shown in the logic of reflection and in the determinations of reflection. Through the self-determination of determinacy the determination of reflection has become opposing.

Moreover Hegel disputes the possibility of the conceptual reduction of the principle of the excluded third to the other propositions, as it comes to expression in the view that with the principle it is merely a matter of a modification of the principle of identity and of contradiction. But in the principle of contradiction: “There is not something that is at once A and not-A,” there is from Hegel’s view contained the third “A,” which constitutes the “dead” foundation of both determinations. According to Hegel this “third, which here has the shape of a dead something, taken more deeply, is the unity of reflection into which, as into the ground, opposing goes back” (GW XI, 286).

#### 4. Contrary and Contradictory Opposing

Hegel says in the logic of the concept that diversity underlies contrary and opposing underlies contradictory opposition.[^s1.68] In the research literature the inappropriateness of this formulation of Hegel’s is however criticized: Contrary opposition must not be equated with diversity.[^s1.69] But if our interpretation of the determinations of reflection diversity and opposition is accurate, this conception of Hegel’s is to be defended. For diversity is precisely the relation of the extremes that admits a third external to these extremes. Opposition by contrast is according to Hegel’s conception the relation of the two extremes that allows no third. This difference is dissolved in the transition from the one to the other (ibid.), when diversity passes over into opposition.

At the same time Hegel’s contradiction must not, as Kang asserts, simply be held for contradictory. It is indeed on the one hand contradictory, insofar as its moments exclude one another and admit no third; on the other hand the contradiction, as Hegel determines it, can also be understood as contrary, since it dissolves itself into ground: The result of the contradiction is ground as the—over against the extremes—third.[^s1.70] Hegel’s concept of contradiction thereby itself makes obsolete this distinction of the concept of opposition by means of the determinations ‘contrary’ and ‘contradictory.’[^s1.71] I agree with Iber that he ascribes contradictory opposition to the second stage of opposition, i.e. to the third form (in his terminology),[^s1.72] and asserts that the second form corresponds to real opposition.[^s1.73] If however he ascribes to the first stage, which brings together the first and second form, as a whole contrary opposition, our conceptions part for the following reasons:

\1. The first form cannot be grasped as contrary opposition, because on account of the becoming-independent of each moment it still has no third. It depends upon whether one ascribes what Wolff calls the “reflection-logical substrate” to the first form or not.[^s1.74] As already shown, this substrate first appears in the second form of opposition, which means the falling-back of opposition into diversity. Iber and Wolff let themselves be misled by the inappropriate mathematical examples of application in the Remark.[^s1.75]

\2. It is further a matter of whether the second form can count as contrary. It further depends upon whether real opposition, to which Hegel here gives a systematic place, is a contrary opposition. It could indeed be held for contrary, insofar as it has the third as result. Kant however emphasizes that it, considered according to formal criteria, appears contradictory. Both A and −A could in this opposing be ascribed to the same subject. Real opposition is therefore already the opposing in which that traditional distinction is invalid. It can therefore just like Kant’s dialectical concept of opposition be understood as a forerunner for Hegel’s concept of contradiction. The same also holds for the second stage of Hegelian opposition. It is a merely as contrary appearing contradictory opposing.

#### 5. The Principle of Ground

“Ground” is in the *Science of Logic*—differently from in the Encyclopedias—separated from the determinations of reflection and Hegel dedicates to it an independent chapter. Ground is however still designated as “one of the determinations of reflection of essence,” yet as a “sublated determination” (GW XI, 291). The following proposition is ascribed to it: “Everything has its sufficient ground” (GW XI, 293). The proposition is in Hegel understood as a principle of general mediation. It says: “[W]hat is, is not to be considered as being immediate, but as posited” (ibid.). It marks the sublation of immediate givenness in reflection: “In the principle of ground thus the essentiality of reflection into itself against mere being is pronounced” (ibid.). For Hegel Leibniz’s intention, as the author of the principle of sufficient ground, lies in that the pre-given, being, is a posited and as such the untrue, and that first ground is “the true immediate” (ibid.).[^s1.76]

From this Hegel’s version of this proposition distinguishes itself from the Leibnizian. For this mediation means not only positing, but also determining. Already the first sentence of the chapter—“Essence determines itself as ground” (GW XI, 291)—is an expression of the result of the logic of reflection.[^s1.77] Hegel no longer understands the ground-relation in a manner—as was traditionally the case—that the positive ground brings forth a positive consequence and the negative a negative, which Kant had already criticized in school metaphysics.[^s1.78] Yet Hegel does not assert—as Kant—that the positive ground can also have a negative consequence, but that the relation of ground and of the grounded always consists in negative mediation. The consequence of ground always results through the fact that ground negates itself and through this negative mediation gains immediacy again.[^s1.79]

Hegel mentions the principle of ground once more at the beginning of the second section “Appearance”—and here he recapitulates his argumentation in the chapter on ground. The formulation of the proposition shows more clearly the character of the proposition described above: “Everything that is has a ground, or is a posited, a mediated” (GW XI, 324). But being (“is”) is a category of the logic of being. Now existence takes its place: “What exists has a ground and is conditioned” (ibid.). Existence thus results from ground, in that this negates and sublates itself. It takes the place of that immediacy, in that ground mediates itself and negates the mediation itself. Therefore Hegel can now with equal right assert: “[I]t [what exists—T.O.] has no ground and is unconditioned” (ibid.). Existence is the negation of ground or the coming-forth of ground itself as reflection. Ground and existence are as the reflection of a self-relating negation one.[^s1.80]

#### 6. Speculative Principle of Contradiction

If we had followed the sequence that Hegel gives, we would have treated this proposition before the principle of ground, for ground is first developed by Hegel after contradiction, i.e. more precisely: in the unfolding of the dissolution of contradiction. But this concept of contradiction plays a fundamental role not only in the determinations of reflection, but in the whole philosophy of Hegel. The proposition that we now want to call the speculative principle of contradiction is a proposition that departs from the tradition. In this term the core of his executions is to come to expression—executions that critically engage with the philosophy of the understanding, school metaphysics and Kantian criticism.
