In the *Science of Logic* a proposition holds for contradiction as also for the other determinations of reflection, a proposition that previous philosophy, which from Hegel’s view merely made use of “external reflection” (GW XI, 288), had not known: “All things are in themselves contradictory” (GW XI, 286). This proposition is for Hegel the only one that is able to express the essence or the truth of things. “Ingenious reflection, to mention this here, consists by contrast in the grasping and pronouncing of the contradiction” (GW XI, 288). Representing or external reflection by contrast holds fast to the principle of contradiction, namely to that of the contradiction to be avoided. From this view there may be nothing contradictory. Hegel is of the view that this avoidance of contradiction, as he has already indicated this in his determination of diversity,[^s1.81] had to lead to the contradiction being “shoved into subjective reflection.”[^s1.82] If the contradiction appears, it counts as a fault of consciousness as “an abnormality and transient paroxysm of illness” (GW XI, 287):
> “Speculative thinking consists only in that thinking holds fast to the contradiction and in it to itself, not however that it, as happens to representing, lets itself be ruled by it and through it lets its determinations dissolve themselves only into others or into nothing” (ibid.)

Contradiction is for Hegel the root of vitality, the movens of the process that logic intends to present.

If all things carry the contradiction within themselves, it at the same time dissolves itself in them, since it, as we have seen, necessarily carries its dissolution within itself:
> “The thing, the subject, the concept is now precisely this negative unity itself; it is an in itself contradictory, but just as much the dissolved contradiction; it is the ground that contains and carries its determinations. The thing, the subject, or the concept is as reflected into itself in its sphere, its dissolved contradiction […].” (GW XI, 289)

With the concept of contradiction, whose content the speculative proposition brings to expression, we reach the core of the method of Hegelian logic, which not only guides the ensuing course of the logic, but was already present *in nuce* in the previous course of the logic of being and of essence, which shows itself when Hegel for example already in the Introduction of the *Science of Logic* formulates:
> “The sole thing, in order to gain the scientific progress, and about whose quite simple insight one has essentially to endeavor,—is the cognition of the logical proposition, that the negative is just as much positive, or that the self-contradictory does not dissolve itself into null, into the abstract nothing, but essentially only into the negation of its particular content, or that such a negation is not all negation, but the negation of the determinate thing that dissolves itself, thus determinate negation; that thus in the result there is essentially contained that from which it results.” (GW XXI, 38; also GW XI, 25)

Behind this consideration stands Hegel’s central assertion of “determinate negation,” which he had already formulated in the Introduction to the *Phenomenology*. The concepts of contradiction and of reflection in the logic must be understood as compatible with this determinate negation.[^s1.83] But in fact the reflection that leads over to this concept of contradiction offers a logic that can describe the contradiction precisely not as nothing, but in the sense of determinate negation, which for its part first makes possible the development of the logical process itself, a form of negation that again includes something negated through it as negated. “In this dialectical,” so it runs in Hegel, “and thereby in the grasping of the opposed in its unity, or of the positive in the negative, consists the speculative” (GW XXI, 40f. also GW XI, 27).


## Sixth Chapter: The Last Two Kantian Pairs of Concepts of Reflection in the Logic of Essence: Matter and Form, the Outer and the Inner

### A. Form and Matter

#### 0. Logic as Science of Absolute Form

We now want to follow not the sequence of the Kantian concepts of reflection, but Hegel’s essence-logical course. We thus enter upon the fourth pair of concepts “matter and form” before the third. Beforehand however a clear picture of the general stance of Hegel’s logic toward these concepts—of form and of matter or of content—is to be drafted.

Traditional logic treats the forms of correct thinking. It has not to do with its content, but only with the laws of thought that regulate the formal relations of the contents. “The previous concept of logic” (GW XI, 16/ XXI, 28) rests upon the separation of content and of form. Hegel’s Introduction to the *Science of Logic* criticizes this in traditional logic:
> “If logic is assumed as the science of thinking in general, it is thereby understood that this thinking constitutes the mere form of a cognition, that logic abstracts from all content, and the so-called second constituent that belongs to a cognition, the matter, must be given from elsewhere, that thus logic, as from which this matter is wholly and entirely independent, can only indicate the formal conditions of true cognition […].” (GW XI, 15f./ XXI, 28)

Logic, so understood, is said to contain no “real truth itself” (ibid.). By contrast what is at issue for Hegel is to develop logic as in itself contentual. That is “the path to real truth” (GW XI, 16/XXI, 28) that that logic has lost. One can say that it has been ‘lost,’ because “the older metaphysics” once went the correct path and knew “that thinking in its immanent determinations, and the true nature of things, is one and the same content” (GW XI, 17/XXI, 29).[^s2.1]

In connection with Hegel’s critique the question poses itself: Does it also hold for Kant’s transcendental logic? Indeed Kant elevates his transcendental logic from the traditional. His transcendental logic, differently from the traditional, does not abstract from all content of cognition, but determines its empirical objects with a view to their a priori conditions (A55ff./B79ff.). That transcendental logic also contained the critique of traditional ontology as the metaphysical application of general logic, we have already seen. The laws of thought with which traditional logic occupies itself form only the necessary conditions of all possible objects—transcendental logic elevates itself from this in that it has to do with the necessary determinations of possible empirical objects—even if they are only appearance.[^s2.2]

Despite this critique of traditional ontology, which Kant especially exercises with the fourth pair of concepts of reflection form and matter, there is still lacking according to Hegel in Kant’s transcendental logic the content that logic as science of the true determinations of thought is to thematize. Kant criticizes that traditional ontology presupposes its content and lets form as its relation, which logic treats, first follow the content. By contrast Kant asserts that form precedes matter. Thereby Kant presupposes that matter is only given from outside. Hegel indeed esteems in Kant having shown that not “the object is a for itself completed, finished” (ibid.), but is determined through thinking. But Kant has left “truth”—the thing in itself—outside thinking, so that this can no longer be “the material of cognizing.” Hegel thus takes a divided stance toward Kant, especially in the Introduction of the *Science of Logic*: While Kant degrades reason or, as Hegel sharpeningly says, lets reason renounce itself, when he denies the cognition of things in themselves, Kant’s transcendental logic on the other hand considers concepts “that relate themselves a priori to objects” (GW XI, 31/XXI, 46). Insofar as this is so it does not abstract “from all content of objective cognition” (ibid.).

Insofar Hegel’s critique of traditional logic nevertheless strikes Kantian transcendental philosophy: this too, although differently from traditional logic, distinguishes form and matter. “Thinking therefore in its receiving and forming of the material does not come beyond itself, its receiving and accommodating itself to it remains a modification of itself, it does not thereby become its other” (GW XI, 16/XXI, 29). The lack of dialectical movement lets Kant seek a “substantial essence” outside: the thing in itself (GW XI, 19/XXI, 32). Hegel’s object of logic is rather the form that is itself content. The content of logic is the concept itself. In the Preface to the second edition of the *Science of Logic* Hegel says:
> “[T]he determinacy of the concept however is a form-determination of this substantial unity, a moment of form as totality, of the concept itself, which is the foundation of the determinate concepts.” (GW XXI, 17)

The object of logic, the concept, is “the reason of what is” or “Logos” (ibid.).

The thing in itself is according to Hegel only the “abstract shadow separated from all content” (GW XI, 19/XXI, 31). To “destroy” this “specter” is a task of Hegel’s logic. Explicitly this occurs at a stage of the logical process itself, in the Existence chapter of the logic of essence. As thing in itself there the empty substrate of the properties that are perceived is designated.[^s2.3]

This peculiarity of the logical science also leads to its methodological peculiarity over against other sciences: No reflections upon its method may precede it. The comprehending thinking that is both the beginning and the result of this science must be grounded through the logic itself (GW XI, 15/XXI, 27). It is at once both the subject and the object of logic. Method and content cannot be distinguished in this science.[^s2.4]

The same Hegel means incidentally in the lectures on the philosophy of history: Kant’s theory of cognition corresponds to “wanting to swim before one goes into the water” (HW XX, 334), as also with the critique of “a natural representation” that prepares a means or an instrument for the cognition of the Absolute, in the Introduction of the *Phenomenology of Spirit* (GW IX, 53f.).[^s2.5] Thereby it confirms itself that he also faults Kant for the error of the separation of form or method from content. For Hegel the thinking that logic presents is itself its content. To the content as the absolute true the form does not remain external (GW XI, 21/XXI, 34). Thus Hegel’s logic can as it were be understood as “the presentation of God,” “as he is in his eternal essence, before the creation of nature and of a finite spirit” (GW XI, 21/XXI, 34). It is in comparison with the philosophy of nature and of spirit, the following system-parts, still the formal science. But its form contains as absolute form “in itself its content or reality” (GW XII, 25).[^s2.6]

Kant’s concepts of reflection of matter and form are in the amphiboly chapter the last of the four pairs of concepts of reflection, and their difference is fundamental for transcendental reflection (and in general in Kantian theory of cognition). In Kant’s transcendental idealism matter is given alone by sensible affection; it is not ideal, but only its form. For all four distinctions of the transcendental and empirical use of the understanding that the amphiboly chapter undertakes, the fourth pair of concepts is important, because with each it depends upon the sensible, material condition being taken into account.

That Hegel lays no special weight upon these concepts already says that he does not take up Kant’s external reflection. They are only concepts that in the course of the logic of essence are derived and dissolved. And in the place of the traditional concept of matter there later steps the concept of content.[^s2.7]

#### 1. Absolute Ground

The Ground chapter joins itself directly to the “Determinations of Reflection.” Contradiction has “gone to ground.” In this manner of speaking one finds Hegel’s approach for the further progress. It has come to ground in that it has “gone under.” Ground has arisen from the reciprocal determination or the identity of the mutually contradicting, but thereby mutually determining determinations, of the positive and of the negative. “Essence determines itself as ground” (GW XI, 291).

In order to take up at this place of the logic of essence[^s2.8] the traditional concepts form and matter, which Kant treats as the concepts of reflection underlying all others, Hegel must derive them from the concept of ground, into which the course of the determinations of reflection just flows from contradiction. Hegel remarks in this connection the difficulty of the “presentation of reflection in general.”[^s2.9] Yet conversely it can be said that he with the logic of reflection and the logic of the determinations of reflection has precisely the means in hand to overcome this difficulty. For ground must first exhibit the grounded as its correlate and be mutually determinable with this, whereby the difficulty lies in that ground itself has its determination through that grounded through it and is thereby conversely determined and grounded by this. Second ground and the grounded as distinguished must have their substrate. This would however as foundation of the ground-relation of ground and of the grounded rather have to be called the ground. This relation causes an infinite regress. Ground is rather a moment of the ground-relation and at the same time the whole as substrate of this relation. Hegel has however already developed a logical presentation both of that reciprocal determination and of the dialectical relation of the whole and of the part, and indeed both with his concept of reflection as self-relation of negation and with that of contradiction, which facilitates the understanding of the chapter.

The second place in the enumeration that the pair of concepts form and matter occupies in this chapter already indicates that this distinction belongs to external reflection. Form and matter are for Hegel also concepts that are derived from the movement of reflection and dissolved.

##### 1.1. Deduction of Matter – Form and Essence

Hegel criticizes in the lectures on the history of philosophy that Kant has not shown the necessity of the fundamental concepts of his doctrine of nature, such as attractive and repulsive force.[^s2.10] The first part of the section on absolute ground, “Form and Essence,” derives these concepts reflection-logically.[^s2.11] For the interpretation it is however more favorable first to illuminate terminologically the constellation of the concepts around ground, which Hegel later derives through the movement of reflection.

Ground has, as seen, that grounded by it as its correlate. Only in this relation can it be determined as such. Hegel calls this relation of both—somewhat later—“the ground-relation” (GW XI, 295). ‘Form’ first designates this relation of ground and of the grounded. Their constituents—ground and grounded—are “form-determinations” (ibid.), which demand a substrate that is “determined” by them. Hegel in this connection first calls “the foundation,” which is to constitute their “essence.” It is thus the relation of “determination” and of the determined or “determinable,”[^s2.12] from which Hegel derives form and matter.

This three-poled structure of the two form-determinations and of their foundation again grounds itself upon the “simple identity of essence with its absolute negativity in immediate unity” (GW XI, 291), or upon “essence that in its negativity is identical with itself” (GW XI, 294). Essence as identity arose through the dissolution of contradiction. In this immediate identity, the melting-pot of all determinations, there first do not yet consist the determinations of ground and of the grounded—just as in absolute reflection reflection and positedness were undistinguished, whose unity nevertheless formed ground itself. It is obvious that Hegel here refers to that logic of positing and presupposing reflection. The determination of reflection that has gone to ground has thereby come back into an immediate determinate being. Because it is still a positedness, it presupposes its ground, which properly is not at all separated from it. But this presupposing of ground is not assumed as its positing, because it has properly been posited by this: “[T]his positing [is] a sublating of itself” (GW XI, 294). Ground is thus “as the sublated positedness,” i.e. determined as “indeterminate.”

The double character of essence, at once to be posited as non-positedness and as positedness, brings forth those form-determinations ‘ground and the grounded.’ But these two mutually opposed determinations are “one and the same identity” as that of the positive and negative in contradiction (ibid.). This identity may first however not be called ground, since ground as a form-determination is posited “as non-posited against positedness” (ibid.). It is therefore first called “essence in general” and distinguished from its determinations, “from its mediation” (ibid.).

Here already the germ of the fundamental structure of that three-poled constellation is present. This unity first contains the unity or the immediacy itself (1), whereby it must be distinguished from the mediation. Therefore it contains the difference or the mediation of ground as the as indeterminate determined (2) and of the grounded as the as determinate determined (3).

This structure of ground Hegel carefully distinguishes both from “pure reflection” as the immediate self-relation, which he previously presented as positing reflection, and from “determining reflection,” which presents the unity of positing and of external reflection (GW XI, 294f.). This mediation of ground[^s2.13] is on the one hand distinguished from pure reflection, because its determinations have independence. On the other hand it is not simply determining reflection, because this independence of its determinations has in it “gone to ground” (GW XI, 295), i.e. is melted into its immediacy. These determinations, which are still embedded in their foundation, essence, but are nevertheless relatively become independent of it, Hegel now calls “form.” Essence and form thus finally stand over against one another here.[^s2.14]

Thus however essence and form are grasped reflection-logically. Essence has its forms, its determinations. It is something in which these consist. Insofar as this is so it is “the substrate,” which constitutes the foundation of the forms. It grounds the form, which itself is the relation of ground and of the grounded. Thus essence is both a form-determination and the immediate substrate indifferent against the form-difference.[^s2.15] Herein consists the structure of reflection: “that positedness as sublated and positedness as such,—ground and the grounded,—is One reflection, which constitutes essence as simple foundation, which is the consistence of form” (GW XI, 295). But this foundation or the consistence of the form-relation is not simply independent of this, but “this consistence is posited in ground; or this essence is itself essentially as determinate; thus it is also again the moment of the ground-relation and form” (GW XI, 295f.). Thus essence and form lie in the “absolute reciprocal relation.”[^s2.16]

Thereby here the subordination of form under essence is revised. For it is no longer essence that in this relation constitutes the whole, but form, because that as ground, as as indeterminate determined, has become a mere form-determination. “Form is therefore the completed whole of reflection” (GW XI, 296). External reflection is therefore criticized here, because it sees in essence only the indifferent, quiet substrate of the forms and lets it be distinguished from form. Essence, which has become a mere form-determination, as as indeterminate determined, is matter. It negates form and stands over against it, but is itself a form.

##### 1.2. Form and Matter

##### 1.2.1. The Mutual Presupposing of Form and Matter

We have now come to the classical stock of ontological principles, ‘form and matter.’ The traditional pair of concepts was derived from the relation of essence and of form, which was a reflexive identity, and explained with those three factors: Form is form-determination, matter is also form-determination, but at the same time its substrate: Matter results from essence as the identical foundation of the form-determinations:
> “Matter is thus the simple undistinguished identity, which essence is, with the determination of being the other of form. It is therefore the proper foundation or substrate of form, because it constitutes the reflection into itself of the form-determinations or the independent, to which they relate themselves as to their positive consistence.” (GW XI, 297)

Matter is a form that essence assumes, which is properly indifferent against form. Form is determination, and matter is the substrate of this determination, i.e. the determinable. It is however not indifferent against form, but the posited, what is posited as the foundation or the substrate of form through the sublation of form. Hegel in this reflection-logical course of the deduction of matter points to the corresponding in the process of our cognition:
> “If one abstracts from all determinations, all form of a something, indeterminate matter remains” (ibid.).

Undoubtedly Aristotle’s *Metaphysics* hovers before Hegel with this sentence.[^s2.17] Herein however there also lies a decisive critique of the Kantian version of the concept of matter. For for Kant it is matter that is perceived by our sensibility and first gives our cognition its content, but itself has no determination, no form, before it is formed by our cognitive faculty. But such matter is “a sheer abstract.” “One cannot see, feel etc. matter—what one sees, feels is a determinate matter, i.e. a unity of matter and of form” (ibid.). Pure matter one could not perceive, since the determinations through which one first cognizes things are by definition lacking in matter, although according to Kant they are supposed to constitute the content of our perception: Matter as wholly indeterminate is nothing.[^s2.18] To Kantian formal idealism this sharp distinction of form and matter underlies. Kant despite his transcendental turn remains with this traditional version of matter.[^s2.19] But such an abstract without any determination is for Hegel not matter, but pure being, which is the beginning of logic in general, because matter cannot be presupposed (in the usual sense), but results from essence.[^s2.20] It arises through the movement of form itself: “form reduces itself through itself […] to this simple identity” (ibid.).

Hegel grasps this relation of matter and form as mutual presupposition, similarly as with presupposing reflection. As results in positing reflection, presupposing is an activity of assuming an other as given or found, while this was posited by that as pre-given. That is the result of the fundamental function of reflection, to repel itself from itself. Something posits itself as other and assumes that it had given this before it.

The presupposing of matter through form is to be understood in this double sense: as identity and as difference. This relation now presents itself on each of the two sides:

###### 1.2.1.1. Matter as Form-Determination

Form and matter are the form-determinations of ground. They thereby stand in the double relation of presupposing. On the one hand: “Form presupposes matter,”[^s2.21] and on the other hand “form is presupposed by matter” (GW XI, 297).[^s2.22] In the first case it is meant that form posits matter as the sublated form. This is a substrate indifferent against form. But to be indifferent against form is again the determination of matter. Therefore matter and form are both dependent upon one another. In the second case matter is “the positive,” but as first so determined: Matter is thus “sublated negation” (ibid.). Form is a determination and as such also a negativity. Matter, as the positive, needs form, the determination, as whose negation it can be positive. Insofar as this is so matter is posited by form as its ground.

###### 1.2.1.2. Matter as Consistence

But “from the other side,” Hegel says, matter is not a ground, i.e. not a form-determination in the ground-relation. It is “determined as groundless consistence” (GW XI, 298), thus not a side of the ground-relation, but the fundament of the relation itself. This is first possible when its fundament is divided into its determinations. “Form and matter are thus determined, the one like the other, not to be posited through one another, not to be ground of one another” (ibid.). In this relation not only matter is indifferent against the other, but also form.

###### 1.2.1.3. The Active and the Passive

In 1.2.1.2. it has been shown that form and matter contain one another and cannot be the ground of the respective other, because each as the unity of both is indifferent against the other. But the determination of being indifferent is the determination of matter. “This their common determination of indifference is the determination of matter as such, and also constitutes the relation of both to one another” (GW XI, 298). Both now have the determination of matter.

But matter is also a form-determination that stands over against form, in that it is determined as indifferent. Indifference thereby also contains a relation to the other. “Just so the determination of form, of being the relation as distinguished, is also the other moment of the comportment of both to one another” (ibid.). Both form and matter have the determinations, 1. to be indifferent against the other and 2. to relate themselves to the other. Matter is matter or form is form first through the fact that each contains the determination of the other. “Matter must therefore be formed, and form must materialize itself” (ibid.). Here matter is also determined as passive, form as active. Matter is what receives form and is formed; form is what determines matter and materializes itself.

##### 1.2.2. Dissolution of Form and of Matter

###### 1.2.2.1. Dissolution of Both Determinations

The difference just developed cannot remain preserved and is sublated:
> “Because form itself is the absolute identity with itself, thus contains matter within itself; just so because matter in its pure abstraction or absolute negativity has form in itself, so the activity of form upon matter, and the being-determined of this through that, is rather only the sublating of the shine of their indifference and distinctness” (GW XI, 298).

The double relations of matter and form, “indifference and distinctness,” unmask themselves as shine that sublates itself. But what then is their essence? It indicates itself with the expression “the recollection of their alienation” (ibid.). In the form-matter relation essence, or ground, alienated itself. This outer relation must now be internalized or recollected: Essence must be recollected as having-been.[^s2.23]

Recollection is however already present, because that relation, as has resulted, is “the mediation of each of the two with itself through its own non-being.” “[T]hese two mediations are One movement and the restoration of their original identity” (ibid.). The second section Hegel again divides into three: In the first it is shown that form and matter in their mutual presupposing are identical. This identity is the ground that we have seen in the relation of essence and of form, “absolute ground” (GW XI, 299).

In the second it is presented that form through self-sublation makes itself into matter: a self-sublating contradiction—independent, i.e. indifferent against its other and also related to it. This self-sublation means “to make itself into matter.” The materialization of form is at the same time “the own movement of matter itself” (ibid.).

Rohs has in this connection posed the question how the thesis of the “movement” of matter can be explained historico-philosophically. He points to Aristotle and Thomas Aquinas.[^s2.24] But at this point attention must likewise be drawn to the doctrine of matter in modern philosophy. As we investigated in the second chapter, Leibniz already defined matter as passive force. Yet especially in Kant’s *Metaphysical Foundations of Natural Science* matter, whose fundamental determination is movement (AA IV, 476), is set up as the principle of the philosophy of nature.[^s2.25]

The difference of matter from form was held fast to only by external reflection, but matter is first brought forth through the self-sublation of form and then dissolved into this movement. “Matter is the same contradiction in itself that form contains” (GW XI, 299f.).

###### 1.2.2.2. The Derivation of the Concept of Content

The process of the self-determining of form and of matter has again arrived at absolute ground as the original unity: “[T]he doing of form and the movement of matter is the same” (GW XI, 300). This unity owes itself to that reflection of the negativity that repels itself from itself. But according to Hegel the returned unity is not equal to the original, but it is “a posited” (ibid.) and as such again “the unity of being-in-itself and of positedness” (ibid.). Thus it is both a unity of form and matter and one of the first and second pairs of the determinations of ground, ‘form and essence’ and ‘form and matter.’

The posited determinations, form and matter, betray over against this unity their finitude. They are as singular insofar no actual ground, therefore have no truth: “[O]nly their unity is their truth” (ibid.) and their ground.
> “Matter is therefore only insofar ground of its form-determination as it is not matter as matter, but the absolute unity of essence and of form; just so form is only ground of the consistence of its determinations, insofar as it is the same one unity.” (GW XI, 300)

The unity is now posited, as “[t]he formed matter or the form having consistence [materialized]” (GW XI, 301). The unity that constitutes the “determinate foundation” of both is now designated as content.

##### 1.3. Form and Content

Hegel thus converts the concept of matter traditionally opposed to form, which is to be traced back to the Latin *materia* and semantically to the Greek *hyle* and to which Kant’s transcendental philosophy also held fast, into the concept “content.” This one may not hold for a German translation of *materia*. It is rather a unity of form and matter, materialized form or formed matter, and as this unity a foundation of both, while it is posited by form. This structure of contradiction was already found with matter. But in the concept of matter, which as a hidden form-determination stands against form and first consists in the abstraction from forms, it is not obvious. This it is first in the concept of content.[^s2.26]

Insofar as the unifying concept of content forms the foundation of form and matter, form, which now stands over against this content, is its determination. “What previously was the identical with itself, first ground, then consistence in general, and lastly matter, steps under the dominion of form and is again one of its determinations” (GW XI, 301). Content has first arisen “under this dominion of form” over its other. Matter is always already as double negation a form.

What Hegel says about this concept ‘content’ is properly not new. Content is first the unity of matter and form, now however ‘posited and determined’ and so opposed to form itself, which constitutes its moment. Then content is considered as an identity: It is ‘the identical in form and matter’ (ibid.). As this identity content also has that double character that essence like matter had *in nuce*, i.e. of being indifferent against form and as ground a determination of the ground-relation, i.e. of being a form. “Content therefore has this [ground-relation] for its essential form” (ibid.). Content however does not remain external to its form, the determination of ground, like matter. In its posited structure of reflection content makes form its own. The form-determinations are now essential to it. And “ground conversely has a content” (ibid.), and indeed a determinate content (GW XI, 302), because content according to Hegel’s version of this concept, differently from matter, is explicitly determinate. “The content of ground is thus the ground returned into its unity with itself” (ibid.).

In content ground and grounded are united. These determinations have “disappeared” in it. Content is their negative unity. Therefore Hegel can finally in the “absolute Idea” formulate that “this [content] is the form-determination gone into itself and sublated in identity so that this concrete identity stands over against that developed as form” (GW XII, 237). He then continues: “[I]t [content] has the shape of an other and given against form, which as such stands sheer in relation, and whose determinacy is at the same time posited as shine” (ibid.).[^s2.27] Only through the sublation of the shine of the distinction of form and content is ‘the Concept’ and “the Idea” possible.

The derivation of content, which takes the place of essence and especially of matter, is decisive for the further process of ground.[^s2.28] What is at issue for the next section “Determinate Ground” are the determinations of content: Ground is determined according to its determination of content, as “a. Formal Ground,” “b. Real Ground” and “c. Complete Ground.” With formal ground ground and the grounded have an indifferent content. They are only formally distinguished and form a tautology. Ground however with real ground is “no longer the tautology,” but “realized” (GW XI, 307). “Each [side of ground, i.e. ground and the grounded] thus has a content diverse against the other” (ibid.). Here first is possible what we ordinarily understand under the ground-relation: something grounds an other. The third, thus “complete” ground is the ground that constitutes the unity of the previous two. It designates a ground-relation returned into itself, no longer externally related, which becomes the unconditioned. In the following, last section of the ground chapter “Condition” the concept of content plays a still more important role. Indeed content first appears as that “in itself,” which in this connection instead of matter is called “material” (GW XI, 315), but finally the unity of form and of content is reached, in that each of both is recognized as the whole (GW XI, 317). The “thing” (*Sache*) thus arisen goes out into “existence” (GW XI, 319ff.).

#### 2. Matter in the Appearance Chapter and in the Encyclopedias

We have investigated the section “Absolute Ground,” where the traditional pair of concepts form and matter, which Kant also treats as concepts of reflection, is thematized. The beginning of logic, the sublation of the difference of form and of matter or of content, is grounded reflection-logically in the presentation of logic itself. Thereafter not matter, but content constitutes the reflection-logical correlate of form.

But in the logic of essence the concept of matter comes again in the first chapter of the second section ‘Existence,’ which joins itself to the third chapter “Ground” of the first section.[^s2.29]

Here the matters (now in the plural) arise from the properties of the thing, into which thinghood has passed over, since the “thing in itself,” without properties, is an abstract or empty (GW XI, 333). The matters are the become-independent properties of the thing, which are now things themselves (GW XI, 333f.). The thing thus consists of matters. Hegel calls this “one of the most current determinations of representing” (GW XI, 337) and thereby criticizes theories of the then chemistry, which explain perceptions through the affected concept of matter such as light-stuff, color-stuff, smell-stuff etc. (GW XI, 334).[^s2.30] The thing is thereby assumed only as an external connection of the properties or matters, which are determinations of content (GW XI, 337). These theories finally assert the porosity of the thing, which entangles itself in a contradiction. In this contradiction of the relation of the thing to its matters these are no longer impenetrable, as is assumed in the dynamics or doctrine of matter of Kant, but a thing is rather porous or penetrable (GW XI, 338). The determination of matter inverts itself.[^s2.31]

In the Heidelberg Encyclopedia (1817) already Hegel attempts to correct the awkward circumstance that the concept “matter” appears twice in the main text. It seems to have become clear that the thematic of matter and form and that of the thing stand in a connection; Hegel therefore attempts to connect both. The corrected articulation is again altered in the later Encyclopedias (1827 and 1830).

In the Heidelberg Encyclopedia (1817) Hegel already includes ‘ground’ in the chapter ‘The Pure Determinations of Reflection,’ so that this concept appears in the third part of the chapter, which again requires including ‘contradiction’ like ‘diversity’ and ‘opposition’ in the second part ‘Difference.’[^s2.32]

‘Ground’ is here only formal, “has as yet no content.” It is not active, like that in the *Science of Logic*. With this formal concept of ground the chapter ‘The Pure Determinations of Reflection’ closes. Thus in the next chapter Appearance it can be said of the ‘thing’: “Ground is hereby posited as thing-in-itself” (§ 74, Remark). This is like that ground “the indeterminate and inactive”; it lacks the content (ibid.).

Now as the determinations of the thing the matters appear, “the things as abstract determinacies” (§ 76). Matter is thereby the foundation of the thing, and ‘form’ is the determinate difference against the identity of matter (§ 77). Thus Hegel in § 78 equates the pair of concepts of ‘form’ and ‘matter’ with that of ‘thing in itself’ and ‘matters.’ The concept ‘content’ is first treated later and even only incidentally in ‘Relation.’

In the Encyclopedias of 1827 and 1830 Hegel has further developed the articulation. ‘Existence’ and ‘Thing’ no longer belong to the section ‘Appearance,’ but together with the ‘pure determinations of reflection’ occupy the section “A. Essence as Ground of Existence” (§§ 123–130). The second subsection of “c. Thing” dedicates itself to the ‘matters’ (again in the plural), which arise from the properties of the thing through their becoming-independent (§ 126). But matter as consistence of the thing is, as set forth in the *Science of Logic* and the Heidelberg Encyclopedia, “abstract” and “indeterminate”; Hegel calls it the “existing thinghood”: “The thing […] consists […] of the matters” (ibid.), which however are only externally connected.

The matters become one matter, in that they are indifferent against every determinacy (§ 128), while form is demanded only as an external relation of their determinacies: “The thing thus falls apart into matter and form” (§ 129). Also the further investigation of both determinations yields: “Both are in themselves the same” (ibid.).

Form and matter pass over through this identification into content, but not directly thereafter, but first at the second stage of the next section, B. Appearance (§ 133): “The relation of appearance to itself is thus completely determinate, has form in itself, and because in this identity, as essential consistence. Thus form is content” (ibid.). In the Remark to this paragraph it runs: “With the opposition of form and content it is essential to hold fast that content is not formless, but just as much has form in itself, as it is an external to it” (ibid.). Here Hegel again similarly as in the *Science of Logic* places the concept of content under the primacy of form, as “the doubling of form” (ibid.). The relation of content and of form is here described as turning-into-one-another (§133, Remark). But Hegel here for the positing of this “absolute” relation points to the chapter, C. Actuality.[^s2.33]

#### 3. Critique of the Kantian Doctrine of the Construction of Matter: The Remark to “Being-for-Itself”

There is still a place where what is at issue is matter. It is only a Remark to the last, third part of the chapter “Being-for-Itself” in the doctrine of being, but has a direct connection with the Kantian doctrine of the construction of matter, which we investigated in the first chapter.[^s2.34] There Hegel criticizes the Kantian construction of matter through the two fundamental forces.[^s2.35] Hegel here treats attraction and repulsion as logical, not as dynamic categories. The concept of force itself first appears in essential relation (which we presently want to investigate). Beforehand we shall take up that Remark on the construction of matter, in order to clarify the connection of essential relation with the problem of the construction of matter.

“The relation of repulsion and attraction,” which in the second edition constitutes the last paragraph of the chapter on “Quality,” builds a bridge to “Quantity.” Although the concept of matter itself does not appear in the main text, it is easy to see that what is at issue here is the concept of matter, for here a transition from individuality, i.e. from discontinuity, into continuity must be made. In the second Remark to “Pure Quantity” the problem of the divisibility of matter, namely the second antinomy, is treated, which shows us that this series of being-logical categories stands in connection with the traditional problems of the concept matter.

Hegel likewise lets repulsion precede attraction (GW XI, 94/XXI, 156). The former is for Hegel the moment through which the one becomes the many. This one counts as the monad of Leibniz (GW XI, 94/XXI, 157), is for Hegel however no pre-given substance, but has arisen through affirmative infinity as self-related negation. In that it contains this negation within itself, i.e. is being-for-itself, the one becomes the many. The negativity that the one contains within itself and whereby it unfolds itself into the many designates repulsion. In this relation of many ones, which have no relation among one another, a one stands as a one among several. But the plurality of the ones has nothing to do with the determination of each one, belongs rather to an external reflection. The ones, which have no relation among one another, do not determine one another and therefore have no reality: The many ones are “ideal” (GW XI, 95). Repulsion itself, through which the many arose, inverts itself into “attraction.” For the dissection into the many is at the same time the return into itself and insofar “repulsion of repulsion” (GW XI, 98), since repulsion itself is a relation of the one to itself.[^s2.36] With this attraction the one gains reality, because through the sublation of plurality it includes the difference from plurality within itself.

What has been said corresponds to Hegel’s interpretation of the monadology of Leibniz. After we have gone through the doctrine of substance and the theory of matter connected with it of Leibniz, it presses itself upon us that Hegel here takes up the problem of the construction of matter, to which the logic of repulsion and of attraction underlies.

According to Hegel Kant’s merit consists in having sublated the externality of the traditional concept of matter as impenetrability through the introduction of attractive force. Kant indeed, as we investigated in the first chapter, ascribes impenetrability to matter, but according to Hegel Kant through the introduction of attractive force has banished this “common-mechanical manner of representation” (GW XI, 104/XXI, 169).

Hegel thus praises Kant for the merit of “recognizing matter from these two opposed determinations as its fundamental forces” (GW XI, 104/XXI, 169).[^s2.37] Yet there remain in Kant the “two fundamental forces, within matter, external and for themselves independent against one another” (GW XI, 105/XXI, ibid.). The Kantian deduction of attraction fails to ground its unity with repulsion. Attraction is said to be demanded only in order to supplement repulsion, since matter cannot be explained only through repulsion. Thus the Kantian construction of matter is said not to be worthy of its name, since his procedure remains analytic and is not constructing (GW XI, 103/XXI, 167). Repulsion and attraction would have to be derived through a deductive course and considered as unity.

Through the non-recognition of the unity of both forces Kant is said to fall into a confusion in the construction of matter, which however “lies in the nature of the thing […]” (GW XI, 106f./XXI, 171). Kant determines, so Hegel, repulsive force as a surface-force, i.e. as force that acts at the direct contact of the surfaces of matter, while attractive force is a penetrating force that acts in empty space without contact. Insofar as repulsive force acts, space is filled with matter. The bodies touch one another; attractive force by contrast acts between the distant bodies, i.e. in the space that matter does not fill. This however does not mean, Hegel thinks, that this force acts between pre-given bodies, but rather that the force itself produces the empty space, the distance of the bodies, itself, insofar as the force presupposes the distance and this belongs to the determination of the force. In this determination attractive force has the determination of repulsive force, since it first lets the distance or empty space arise. Thus conversely repulsive force contains attractive force within itself. If matter through repulsive force occupies space and there is therefore no empty there, repulsive force is rather an attractive force, through which matter is composed and whose extendedness is produced.

The “nature of the thing” consists “in the nullity of the difference of repulsion and attraction” (GW XI, 106f.). From the precise determination of both forces it moreover results “that one [force] had passed over into the other” (GW XI, 107/XXI, 171). This interchange of the determinations of opposed forces sublates the opposing.

Hegel’s critique of the Kantian doctrine of the construction of matter can only apply to the Remark, not to the main text, since the concept of force, which thereby plays an important role, first occurs in the logic of essence. As we shall see, in essential relation the relation of a force to its effects is treated and first in the logic of essence the logic of that critique is grounded.


### B. The Outer and the Inner in Hegel’s Logic of Essence

That the Kantian third pair of concepts of reflection ‘the Inner’ and ‘the Outer’ appears in Hegel after the Kantian fourth, ‘Form’ and ‘Matter,’ is no accident, but logically necessary. Essential relation, to which that of the inner and of the outer belongs, is first to be presented with the concepts of form, matter and content. The first essential relation, the whole and the parts, is a higher version of the relation of content and of form (Enc. 1830, § 135).[^s2.38]

That the themes with which Hegel occupies himself in the chapter of “essential relation” again hang together with the problems of matter is easy to see: the problem of the divisibility of matter, with which Kant occupies himself in the second antinomy (Hegel: “A. The Relation of the Whole and of the Parts”) and the problem of the construction of matter through force, which Hegel already, as just seen, has anticipatorily treated in a Remark of the logic of being (“B. The Relation of Force and of Its Expression”). Hegel’s third subchapter “C. Relation of the Outer and Inner” equals the unity of the first two subchapters. With this relation even that relation between being-in-itself and being-for-another is again—although this time formally—taken up, which is to be further treated in the chapter “Actuality.”[^s2.39]

Still to be remarked is the parallelism of this chapter with the Understanding chapter of the Phenomenology. In this we find the relations of force and of its expression and of the outer and inner phenomenologically presented. Hegel in the logic gives these concepts again as logical categories, i.e. not entangled with one another as in the Phenomenology; rather the concepts each take a systematic place in the logical series of order.

The logic of essence, which is guided by the three-stage logic of reflection, presents the process from immediacy through mediation or relation to self-mediation. Here the same process manifests itself as from substance in the usual sense through its dissolution into relation to substance, which is at once subject. It is therefore no wonder that in the course of the logic of essence the substrate sublates itself into the relation, which becomes the new substance as self-relation: We have on the one hand established that the logic of shine, which determines the approach of the logic of essence, dissolves “being” as shine into the inner relation, reflection into itself. Insofar as this is so essence from the beginning is not hypostatic or Absolute in the conventional sense, but the relation itself, then even self-relation.[^s2.40] What the last and highest stage of essence is, is designated as “Absolute Relation.”[^s2.41] Kant already asserts in the amphiboly chapter with the concepts of reflection ‘Inner and Outer’ that substance is to be understood only as the sum of relations, and with the fourth pair of concepts of reflection ‘Matter and Form’ opposes the traditional manner of thinking of letting matter precede form. Hegel’s logic of essence can count as continuation of this Kantian enterprise.

In the section ‘Appearance’ the process is unfolded in which existence, as the immediacy again coming forth from essence as self-mediation, again dissolves itself into a relation,[^s2.42] namely into essential relation. Its three sub-relations belong to the old stratum of the logic of essence. They are already in the Encyclopedia (1808/09)—even in this sequence—subordinated to the third subchapter ‘Ground and the Grounded.’ Hegel has thus not only relatively early so conceived this relation chapter, but also after the *Science of Logic* always retained it, while he has very much altered the articulation of the first two sections in the three editions of the Encyclopedia. Of the three essential relations only the third, that of the outer and of the inner, is name-identical with the third pair of the Kantian concepts of reflection. Nevertheless easy to see is the connection also of the second relation with these concepts of reflection, because with them likewise force and matter as their expression are thematized. When one finally recalls that Kant with this pair of concepts of reflection treats matter, the connection with the first relation is also recognizable, because the infinite divisibility or inseparability of matter is a traditionally philosophical theme, with which especially Leibniz occupies himself and which he beside the problem of freedom has called one of the two labyrinths.[^s2.43] But Kant has treated this problem not with the concepts of reflection, but mainly in the second antinomy (A434ff./B462ff.), with which Hegel already occupies himself in the doctrine of being of the *Science of Logic*.[^s2.44] Also in the Remark of a part-chapter this theme is pointed to.[^s2.45] But in this chapter matter itself is not thematized, because it has already been sublated in the chapter “Ground” or in the chapter “Appearance” through the concepts “content” and “appearance.”

#### 0. The General Character of Essential Relation

Essential relation is “[t]he truth of appearance” (GW XI, 353), in that it is “the determinate unification of both” (ibid.), i.e. namely of essence and of existence. But it reaches, as Hegel says, “not yet the true third” (ibid.) of both, because this relation still remains external to both and thereby remains a relation.[^s2.46] The inclusion of the pair of concepts ‘form and content’ in the Appearance chapter, which—as seen—occurs in the published Encyclopedias, makes it possible to derive “relation” from this (Enc. 1830, § 134/Enc. 1817, § 82). Thus Hegel in the first relation can equate the whole with content and the parts with form. As to content essential relation has two sides: “the being immediacy and the reflected immediacy” (GW XI, 353). In that these two sides are independent, external relation still prevails, reflection upon the other. Essential relation is moreover to be distinguished from that relation which is the contradiction of the positive and of the negative, since it consists of essence as “beyond” (ibid.) and its existence. In this relation its sides are “broken” (ibid.), and thereby the relation is “a in itself broken” or “an inverted world” (ibid.). For the existence of an essence is rather its other.

That is according to Hegel “the concept of relation” (ibid.). It is already a negative unity of its moments, i.e. of reflected and immediate independence, of the beyond and of the this-side (of appearance). Important is that in this relation substance is not yet present. Neither in the whole as relation nor in the related is the substance that Hegel designates as “the reflected unity of that identity and of the independent existences” (GW XI, 354). It first is when it not only mediates *phaenomenon* and *noumenon*, but reflectingly unifies them. “But first this concept itself is still immediate” (ibid.). Relation is still only an immediate, external connection of its two sides.

#### 1. The Derivation of the Relation of the Outer and of the Inner

##### 1.1. The Relation of the Whole and of the Parts

Hegel treats the third pair of the Kantian concepts of reflection, the outer and the inner, as the third essential relation. Essential relation is with him first the relation of the whole and of the parts, the relation whose extremes relate themselves only externally to one another:[^s2.47] The whole is the dead aggregate (GW XI, 359) of the parts. They are connected only through the “Also” (GW XI, 355).[^s2.48] In this relation of the whole and of the parts, in which Hegel essence-logically dissolves the Kantian antinomy of the divisibility of matter,[^s2.49] finally however the reflexivity of the moments of this relation is unveiled: The whole is on the one hand the consistence of the parts. These first are, insofar as they are in that. But the parts are on the other hand the consistence of the whole, because it exists only as composed of them. In these two sides of the relation the two moments reflect themselves as “tautologies” (GW XI, 356f.). For the whole is not equal to the parts as parts, but to the composition of parts, which is the whole itself. The parts are not equal to the whole as the whole or their unity, but to it as “divided whole,” to the manifold, namely to the parts. “This relation-to-itself of each of the two sides is their independence; but this their independence, which each has for itself, is rather the negation of itself” (GW XI, 357). Here Hegel appeals to “identity” as the first determination of reflection: Each identity of the whole and of the parts arisen here has “this reflection into its other for its truth” (ibid.). Each finds its first in its other. Both are no longer regarded as immediate: “The truth of the relation thus consists in the mediation” (ibid.).

In this relation the immediate has passed over into mediation; it is now positedness. But it is posited as immediate; this positing is the sublating of mediation, the mediation that negates itself and posits the mediated as immediate.[^s2.50]

##### 1.2. The Relation of Force and of Its Expression

###### 1.2.1. The Being-Conditioned of Force

We have now arrived at the relation of force and of its expression, which we want to investigate somewhat more precisely, because it, as indicated, stands in close connection with the third pair of the Kantian concepts of reflection. The two sides of the last relation related themselves only externally to one another; they both had an independent consistence, but are now sublated, posited into a reflection and become moments. Thus the relation of force and of its expression is not that in which an extreme externally belongs to the other like the whole to the parts, but a relation that reflects the other into itself and even presents it.[^s2.51]

\1) Force is something that acts upon an other. It must thus be capable of unfolding an effect outside itself. Such an effect Hegel calls the expression of a force. Here Hegel shows the succession of the relation of the whole and of the parts, since it has sublated and internalized its external relation. This new relation is however first also still external and immediate. Therefore it has to catch up the external relation into the inner.

Thus force at the first stage, which properly is supposed to be a negative unity of itself and of its expression, is understood as “an existing something” (GW XI, 360). In this immediate and external connection this something is the first. As Hegel has previously said, the whole through the return into itself “has ceased to be an external and indifferent to this manifold [i.e. to matter]” (GW XI, 359). Thus now force is “belonging to the existing thing or to a matter” (GW XI, 360). Insofar as this is so force lies only in matter. It is “a quiet determinacy of the thing” (ibid.). Hegel appeals here to the then discussion in natural science, which does not hold force for something inner: Force is said to be “not a self-expressing, but immediately an external” (ibid.). Forces are ascribed to the matters themselves, as “a magnetic, electrical etc. matter” (ibid.); or the ether is the matter to which attractive force in this sense belongs.[^s2.52] Force is only “impressed upon the thing through a foreign violence” (ibid.).[^s2.53]

But the matters that are directly connected with force do not belong here, but to “Appearance,” the “consistence of the thing of matters.” “[S]uch independence has long since passed over into positedness and into appearance” (GW XI, 361).[^s2.54]

\2) Thereupon in the second step of “a. Being-conditioned of Force” the proper relation of force and of its expression is presented: it is the force that as the first posits existing being. It is its ground, i.e. it must have been given earlier than this. Immediate existence is the expressed force. Thus force is “the unity of reflected and of immediate consistence, or of form-unity and of external independence” (GW XI, 361). Of force there is first then to speak when it expresses itself as immediate, which properly is a posited, a mediated. Here lies the contradictoriness of force: It first exists when it expresses itself, i.e. negates itself and makes itself into an outer determinate being. Thus it is “active.” It is thus grasped as the “contradiction repelling itself from itself” or “the self-relating negative unity” (GW XI, 361).

\3) In force there consists the contradiction of first coming to itself through the fact that it negates itself. In this third step however force is conversely “conditioned.” Immediate existence as its own negation is its condition or its presupposition (GW XI, 361). Here yet a further step is made, insofar as expression itself shows itself as a further force. The thing as the expression of force loses its independence or substantiality:
> “This presupposition now is not a thing finding itself over against it [force]; this indifferent independence is sublated in force; as its condition it is an other independent to it. Because it is however not thing, but the independent immediacy here has at the same time determined itself as self-relating negative unity, so it is itself force.” (GW XI, 361)

In order to understand this, one must bear in mind that existence or the thing is only one side of the movement of reflection as self-relation. It is only thereby an existence or immediate, that it negates its being-mediated through itself, i.e. through the negation of negation gains positivity.

Existence or the thing as the condition of force comes to affect force, because force cannot be without its expression. Insofar as existence or the thing thereby lets force arise and is not simply immediate, it itself is also a force: “Force is in this manner relation, in which each side is the same as the other” (GW XI, 361). This relation between two equal moments anticipates the inner; it is “first the inner unity that is in itself” (ibid.), but already unity, for “[t]he being-conditioned through another force is thus in itself the doing of force itself” (ibid.).

The process from the relation between the mediating and the immediate or of force and of its expression to that between the two mediating, the two forces, is also illustrated in the Understanding chapter of the *Phenomenology of Spirit*:
> “That which appears as other, and solicits it both to expression and to return into itself, is […] itself force.” (GW IX, 85)

Here already the word “solicit” is used, which designates the next stage of the relation of force and of its expression. Matter, in which force expresses itself, is first a passivity that receives this. But then it lets force itself happen as “universal medium” of force. Thereby force returns into itself, to negate its own externality, because it exists only insofar as it is nevertheless separated from this. Force returns into itself in that matter affects force. The expression of force in matter and its return into itself are “solicited” by matter. Thereby force is first a force; in principle however this solicitation is also force.[^s2.55] And matter solicits force, because it has thereby become something other than immediate matter, precisely a force.

Matter for its part has just like force two sides: “universal medium” and “the one reflecting itself into itself” (GW IX, 85f.). Insofar as this is so the diversity of force and of its expression passes over into an opposition of two independent forces. In this relation each extreme like in that opposition contains the respective other.

###### 1.2.2. From Solicitation to the Infinity of Force

Hegel’s argumentation has already at the end of the last section suggested the unity of both forces. But still one force seems to presuppose the other. Therefore the presupposed force still lies beyond the proper force, which Hegel calls the “positing activity” or the “reflection returning into itself” (GW XI, 361). First force posited matter, so that this was a posited or a mediated. Since however it was to be grasped as immediate, this positing of matter is a presupposing, a negating of mediation. Thus matter was posited by force or subsequently presupposed. Matter as expression of force is however, as the Phenomenology establishes, itself a force, since it as presupposed posits force. The positing or presupposing has become mutual.

The mutual presupposing is now grasped with the concepts “solicitation” or “impulse.” Force gives another an impulse. Thus force posits itself as an other. Insofar as this is so the activity of force is “the own repelling of itself from itself”: “its own expression” (GW XI, 362).

As in the logic of reflection there are also brought in the solicitation of force two activities, positing and presupposing, into a unitary reflection. Since positing is a self-positing, it remains in this activity identical with itself. Presupposing by contrast is a self-positing-as-an-other, so that the posited loses the property of being posited by an other and becomes something immediate: “But the concept of force is in general the identity of positing and presupposing reflection or of reflected and of immediate unity” (GW XI, 362). Here Hegel distinguishes the concept of force and its reality. In its concept force is the identity of both reflections. In its reality by contrast force has the form-determinations as soliciting or solicited. But these determinations do not belong to its concept. They are to be brought to unity and indeed not externally through comparison, but as “an essential unity of the same” (GW XI, 363).

The process to unity equals the well-known “play of the two forces” in the Understanding chapter of the *Phenomenology of Spirit* (GW IX, 86ff.),[^s2.56] although here in reflection-logical terms:
> “The soliciting [force] relates itself to its other negatively, so that it sublates the externality of the same, it is insofar positing; but it is this only through the presupposition of having an other over against itself; that is, it is soliciting itself only insofar as it has an externality in it, thus insofar as it is solicited. Or it is soliciting only insofar as it is solicited thereto, to be soliciting. Thus conversely the first is solicited only insofar as it itself solicits the other thereto, namely the first, to solicit.” (GW XI, 363)

The constant inversion of the determinations ‘solicited’ or ‘soliciting’ is the constant transition from passivity to activity and conversely (ibid.). It is, as is later summarized, “the expression of force […] only a mediation of the reflected unity with itself” (GW XI, 364). The unity of both forces constitutes, again analogously to the *Phenomenology of Spirit*, the “infinity of force.” There the concepts “the inner and the outer” indeed appear earlier than the concept of infinity, but the following place shows that first in the inner infinity unfolds itself: “Infinity or this absolute unrest of pure self-moving, that what in some manner, for example as being, is determinate, is rather the contrary of this determinacy, has indeed already been the soul of all previous, but in the inner first it itself has freely come forth” (GW IX, 100).

Here the previous process is so summarized, “that the presupposing and the determining returning into itself is one and the same” (GW XI, 365). But the section that is titled ‘The Infinity of Force’ adds no new content; only through the identity of presupposing and of self-determining as reciprocal action of both forces is their connection made clear. The section seems to be intercalated, both in order to present the stage of the relation of force and of its expression that has been reached and in order thereby to prepare for the next chapter the relation of the outer and of the inner. Moreover the last sentence points us to the fact that we have already occupied ourselves with this thematic ‘the outer and the inner’ in the relation of force to its expression: “[F]orce expresses this, that its externality is identical with its inwardness” (GW XI, 364).

#### 2. Exposition of the Relation of the Outer and of the Inner

In Kant the outer was a designation for the relation of substances and the inner for substance. Substance in appearance was determined only as the sum of relations, which were presented as forces. Hegel now derives from the ‘Relation of Force and of Its Expression’ ‘the Relation of the Outer and Inner,’ which is the third and last of essential relation.[^s2.57] Here the inner and the outer are properly not grasped dynamically, but logically in the speculative sense. The relation of the outer and of the inner forms itself in Hegel into a more general relation than the last relations—and thereby into a relation of the two Kantian concepts.

##### 2.1. The Identity of the Outer and of the Inner as Content

The logical structure itself that Hegel here unfolds is almost the same as that of the third section of absolute ground, form and content, which we have already presented.[^s2.58] The identity of the two moments of this relation was already reached at the end of the last relation, in the infinity of force. In this third essential relation the identity is examined according to its two sides, content and form. Then it is shown how these two sides of the identity of the outer and of the inner, which themselves are assumed as the outer and the inner, unify themselves. The logic of the relation of the outer and of the inner is first understandable when one relates it back to that of absolute ground. Here namely the relation of the identity (content) of force and of its expression on the one hand and of their diversity (form-determinations) on the other is unfolded.

The first essential relation of the whole and of the parts was the immediate relation: Each of its moments was independent and only externally connected with one another. With the second essential relation, that of force and of its expression, this immediate outer relation is step by step dissolved. As the infinity of force the identity of both moments was realized, which is a self-expressing and then into itself returning movement. This identity is now, Hegel says somewhat later, an identity “as content” (GW XI, 368), because it functions as foundation of its form-determinations (of the outer and of the inner). This identity is called “the solid unity” (GW XI, 365), which dissolves the determinations of the outer and of the inner into itself. The inner and the outer are now “only One thing,” yet “the absolute thing” (ibid.).

Here the form-determinations of the inner and of the outer have their consistence in their unity as content, which is diverse from them. It is now noteworthy that this unity is distinguished from its form-determinations as diversity and insofar itself has become a form-determination of the inner.[^s2.59] The identity and the diversity of the inner and of the outer have themselves become the inner and the outer and thus fall into an infinite regress. For if these distinguished are again united, the new unity again stands over against that difference. Thus identity is still indifferent against its form-determinations.

##### 2.2. Identity of the Outer and of the Inner as Pure Form

The inner as identity of the outer and of the inner is again distinguished from the outer as form-determination and considered in itself itself a form-determination. Thus the inner and the outer are diverse. But this content as form-determination is still a side of the identity as infinity of force. Hegel here determines the two form-determinations so that the inner is “the form of reflection-into-itself, of essentiality” and the outer as “the form of the immediacy reflected into another, or of inessentiality” (GW XI, 365). The unity of presupposing reflection and of reflection-into-itself, which was shown in the infinity of force, is also the identity of these two form-determinations: “Force in its expression is this, that the presupposing and the determining returning into itself is one and the same” (ibid.). In this second phase the inner as also the outer is grasped as a form-determination. Therefore their unity is also “the pure abstract mediation” (ibid.). Thus this unity is “not their contentful identity” (ibid.), as in the last phase. In this only formal unity “something that is only first an inner is precisely therefore only an outer. Or conversely, something that is only an outer is precisely therefore only an inner” (GW XI, 366). Hegel calls this state of affairs “the immediate inversion of the one of these determinations into the other” (ibid.). According to him this relation already occurs at the beginning of logic: As example he adduces the relation of pure being and nothing, which however was not grasped as a relation. Hegel lets it be recognized that the relation of the whole and of the parts in general was such a relation, even if it remained potential as relation. The same holds for the sphere of being in general and for essence as that which “only first” is itself (GW XI, 367).[^s2.60]

In this relation the inner is “the completion of essence according to form” (GW XI, 366), but it consists only in the relation to the outer as its other. More important is that the inner and the outer not only point to one another, but also to “their third,” their totality or their foundation, which occurred in the first phase (ibid.). It nevertheless lies beyond the unity concerned, in that this is still something to which one must point. Here the inner and the outer are connected with one another only in the “simple contentless point” (ibid.).

##### 2.3. The Unity of Both Identities

The identity as content is an identity that consists indifferently against the difference of the inner and of the outer and even constitutes their foundation. But the foundation opposed itself to its form-determinations. Thereby it again counts as a form-determination and begins an infinite regress. The second identity as pure form is an immediate, unmediated identity of the two form-determinations, a direct inversion of both. But this identity first has nothing to do with content. It is an empty identity. We find here the two concepts of the “inner” that Kant distinguishes in the amphiboly chapter. The inner as the object of the pure understanding is the substance that has no difference from the other or no relation to this. The first identity is such an “inner” that is indifferent against difference or relation. The inner as relation, which for Kant forms the object of sensible experience, is by contrast the inner in the form-determination in Hegel. This is distinguished from that as the object of the pure understanding and only points to it. The inner in the sensible world always stands opposed to the outer and becomes a form-determination. Because one always passes over from one inner to the next, no firm substrate or substance can be reached in appearance (infinite regress).

Now however Hegel asserts that the two identities “are only the sides of One totality” (GW XI, 368) and that they lead to the immediate inversion of the one into the other. The second identity is not only the identity of the two form-determinations, but also at the same time a moment of the unity of the outer and inner. The second identity contains the first as its moment within itself. The infinite regress into which the second identity falls consummates itself only through the fact that the foundation indifferent against the form-determinations is presupposed. Here again an application of the theory of reflection is found. For Hegel the identity as content, which brings about the infinite regress, is only presupposed. The foundation is thereby posited, to be presupposed:
> “Totality as foundation and content is this immediacy reflected into itself only through the presupposing reflection of form, which sublates its difference, and posits itself as indifferent identity, as reflected unity against it.” (ibid.)

Therefore Hegel can now apply to the two identities themselves the logic of the second identity of the form-determinations. The identity as content is nothing other than what form gives itself for its consistence as other. Identity is only a form that is posited indifferent against form. Now the identity of the two identities is that which through the mediation of the other, which properly is the self, relates itself to itself. This identity, which is at once both the immediate identity and a mediated one, is “the inner.” The inner in Hegel is likewise the outer, namely form as the active self-relation that negating itself produces itself.

#### 3. On Substance

What connection has essential relation with the outer and the inner in Kant and with his concept of substance? As was executed in the first chapter, Kant asserts in the amphiboly chapter with these concepts of reflection that substance in space is only the sum of relations. We can only through relations and thereby through force cognize the existence of matter. If the relations were in some manner removed, nothing would be to be found here. Substance in space does not precede its relations, but depends upon these. One can for example through weight or gravitation, which acts upon a thing, cognize the existence of this thing or of its substance. But weight itself is not a substance, but a force that must have acted upon the substance. And this force is only cognizable through the relation of two bodies.

The essence-logical presentation of the relation of force and of its expression stands near to the Kantian conception. Hegel first conceives matter as the expression of force, which does not mean that substance has its force, but that force posits substance. Then substance itself dissolves itself into another force. In the reciprocal action of the first and second force, in the play of both forces as in the *Phenomenology of Spirit*, the difference of force and of matter, which was also assumed as a force, melts into a force that is the negative self-relation. The relation of the outer and of the inner unfolds this process further. The absolute inner, which at the beginning is presented and so presupposed, as Kant thinks it in the object of the pure understanding, cannot be reached in the relation of the relatively outer and of the relatively inner. The process falls, as shown, into an infinite regress. But the absolute inner is only something presupposed (in the reflection-logical sense) in this relation of the relative outer and of the relative inner. It is only a “thought-thing,” something postulated in this relation. Insofar as this is so the absolute inner is to be included in the relative relation. The previous logic already shows the dissolution of the traditional concept of substance, which Kant also wanted to preserve in the noumena.[^s2.61]

But Hegel here does not treat the concept of substance itself. Our interpretation is only plausible when Hegel’s determinations of reflection are connected with the Kantian concepts of reflection. The concept of substance appears in Hegel’s logical system explicitly first in “absolute relation,” in which Hegel unfolds the Kantian categories of relation. But that the last section of essence Actuality, which joins itself to essential relation, as a whole has to do with substance, proves itself from the fact that first it is from the content obvious that its first chapter “The Absolute” has to do with the traditional concept of substance. Hegel especially takes up the concept of substance of Spinoza.[^s2.62] Second Hegel has, from a developmental-historical view, treated modality, with which he occupies himself in the second chapter of the section, as theme of substance: In the Jena System Drafts II it has no independent chapter, and it is treated only in the ‘Relation of Being,’ which represents the later ‘absolute relation.’ Also in the Nuremberg period modality was once treated in the introductory paragraph of the third part of Ontological Logic “Actuality” in the Encyclopedia (1808/09) (GW X, 68, § 43 (Dict.)), then in the paragraph of substance (GW X, 68, §§ 44f. (Dict.)) and finally at the beginning of the dialectic of the unconditioned relations in the Doctrine of Spirit of the Middle Class Logic from the same year (GW X, 46f.(Ms.); §77, 124f. (Dict.)). In the Logic for the Middle Class (1810/11) categories such as ‘possible,’ ‘actual,’ ‘possibility,’ ‘actuality’ and ‘necessity’ occur in substance (GW X, 169ff.; §§ 66-69, GW X, 234f.). Third Hegel in the introductory section to the logic of the concept holds the three steps of absolute relation or of actuality (Encyclopedia), i.e. the three categories of relation, for a movement of substantiality (GW XII, 11ff.).[^s2.63] Finally Hegel says in the logic of essence of the *Science of Logic*:
> “In this wholly become formal difference [of the outer and inner] relation itself goes to ground, and substance or the actual comes forth as the absolute unity of immediate and of reflected existence.” (GW XI, 354)

Hegel determines in the ‘Relation of Substantiality’ first substance traditionally as the unmoved and the accident as the moved, which recalls the Kantian definition of substance as the permanent and of the accident as change.
