But the relation of substance and accident is ambiguous. They are on the one hand distinguished from one another, but on the other hand the accident can exist only in substance, only as its mode. In this ambiguous relation substance is supposed to be something that remains over when all accidents would be taken away. But we cannot speak of substance without entering upon its accidents. Substance is again only something presupposed, demanded by the accidents:
> “This movement of accidentality is the actuosity of substance, as quiet coming-forth of itself. It is not active against something, but only against itself as simple resistance-less element. The sublating of a presupposed is the vanishing shine; first in the doing that sublates the immediate does this immediate itself become, or is that shining; the beginning from itself is first the positing of this self from which the beginning is.” (GW XI, 394f.)

As result it comes to light that the accident is a force bringing forth its substance, and at the same time that substance itself is a force or ‘power’ that, in that it posits those accidents into itself and lets them change, produces itself. Both are active and an independent power that posits itself through the fact that it posits its other. Here one finds the same logic as in essential relation, even if with other concepts, for the relation of substance and of its accidents leads to a negative self-relation.

Hegel further develops the Kantian conception of substance as relation in appearance and thereby dissolves the concept of substance of traditional metaphysics, which Kant still retains with the object of the pure understanding. Substance is not only, as with Kant, relation, but even a self-relation. Hegel’s substance is the self-relating negativity that posits itself as an other and thereby produces itself.

Although Kant already comprehends that substance in the *phaenomena* is nothing but the sum of relations, he nevertheless holds fast to the traditional concept of the relation-free substance, in that he assigns to it another place (*topos*), *noumena*. By contrast according to Hegel the transcendent substance is only presupposed. Since in the world of appearance an infinite regress is unavoidable, a direct inversion of the outer and of the inner shows itself: this is a productive self-related activity.

What significance has Hegel’s understanding of substance or of the inner in the history of modern metaphysics? First he follows the Kantian critique and carries it further. Second he restores a unity of logic and ontology. In this connection one could fault Hegelian logic that it ignores the sensible conditions that for Kant are necessary, and throws philosophy back to a kind of pre-critical metaphysics.

But if phenomenal substance also in Kant can be cognized only through the relations, we cannot directly perceive it. We can assume it like the thing in itself only ideally, thus, strictly speaking, metaphysically. Over against this Hegel gives up this assumption and thereby unfolds the Kantian conception of substance as relation.[^s2.64] Here the Hegelian concept of substance as self-relation arises.

--------------
# Conclusion

As we saw in the first chapter, Kant used the four pairs of concepts of reflection to clarify how his transcendental logic differs both from traditional general logic and from the ontology of school metaphysics. These two distinctions, however, coincide: Leibniz–Wolffian school metaphysics conceived its ontology on the assumption that general, formal logic also suffices ontologically, and that the general predicates of being can be derived from it. Yet Kant does not replace general logic with transcendental logic. Formal-logical principles remain necessary conditions of all correct thinking, but they are no longer sufficient conditions of our cognition.

Kant therefore rejects the ontological principles of Leibniz–Wolffian school metaphysics because they take into account only the formal-logical condition and not the transcendental one—that is, they ignore the difference between our sources of cognition, sensibility and understanding. Even if Kant’s critique of Leibniz does not always hit its mark, and even if Leibniz in part still seems to agree with Kant (see Chapter 2), the achievement of Kant’s transcendental logic lies in having reconceived the relation of logic to the content of cognition.

One may nevertheless ask whether Kant really intends to destroy metaphysics as such, and whether he carries that intention through.[^z.1] First, as regards *metaphysica specialis*, his critique goes only so far as to declare cognition of transcendent objects impossible; it does not assert the impossibility of those objects themselves. The same holds for *metaphysica generalis*, ontology as the science of the cognition of things in general. Only in the case of things in themselves is the claim to cognition at least to be declared problematic. In both kinds of metaphysics, according to Kant, the sensible condition of human cognition must be taken into account: we cannot cognize the immortality of the soul, freedom, God, or things in themselves, because they exceed our sensible faculties.[^z.2] Although he thereby restricts our possibility of cognition and has made metaphysics in its traditional form impossible, his critique remains for him a preparation (propaedeutic) for a new metaphysics,[^z.3] and he still calls his transcendental philosophy “ontology”—yet he does not make good on that announcement.[^z.4] That ontology would contain the “predicables,” which the *Critique of Pure Reason* did not take up.[^z.5]

Although Kant himself thus designed his critique as a forecourt to metaphysics, with his transcendental logic he breaks a path so decisively that his successors can neither return to “unsuspecting” metaphysics nor claim a new metaphysics as a science. Hegel wants, along this path, to replace metaphysics by his logic.

In the final part of this study (Chapters 5 and 6) we followed Hegel’s treatment of the Kantian concepts of reflection in the logic of essence. They form the core categories of the first two sections of that chapter which, at the beginning of the Nuremberg period, separated from ‘actuality’ or the ‘independent relation,’ was called “essence”; in that respect these concepts belong to essence proper (Chapter 3). Hegel’s presentation of these essence-logical concepts in the *Science of Logic* rests on the logic of reflection (Chapter 4). The Kantian concepts of reflection, which for Kant always also mean an engagement with Leibniz or the Wolffian school, are in Hegel’s logic of essence deduced, presented, and dissolved through this movement of reflection.

Henrich sets himself the task of “bringing Kant’s and Hegel’s thinking into a substantive relation to each other and making them compatible within a systematic whole.”[^z.6] He sees a fundamental difference between them: Kant’s transcendental logic is said to concern the natural world, Hegel’s speculative logic the real world. But if by the “real world” he means the world of things in themselves, that is mistaken. The phrase “real world” presupposes that this world is distinguished from an unreal or natural world. Hegel has made that distinction impossible. Our analysis of the essence-logical categories has shown that Hegel rather carries further the principles of transcendental logic and dissolves things in themselves as something presupposed. Hegel’s ontology must rather be compared with the transcendental logic that in Kant concerns the world of experience. Hegel’s monism becomes possible only when it destroys the difference of the two worlds itself: “The more consistently carried through transcendental idealism has recognized the nullity of the ghost of the thing-in-itself still left over by critical philosophy, this abstract shadow separated from all content, and has had the purpose of destroying it completely” (GW XI, 19/XXI, 31—emphasis by T. O.). When the essence represented behind the shine is taken away, the shine is no longer shine. The task of Hegel’s logic is therefore not to bring things in themselves to cognition, but to destroy this empty concept, whereby the character of appearance is dialectically removed from appearance. The shine thereby itself becomes essence.[^z.7]

The achievement of the logic of essence can be understood only in this historico-philosophical perspective:

1. Liberation of ontology from traditional logic: Hegel carries further the Kantian approach of transcendental logic and constructs a new logic that takes the place of transcendental logic.

2. Liberation from traditional substance metaphysics: in Hegel, ontology is transformed from substance ontology, through relational ontology, into the logic of self-relation.

In the amphiboly chapter of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, as concerns *substantia phaenomenon*, Kant dissolves the traditional concept of substance into the concept of relation. Hegel takes up into the logic of essence the concepts of reflection by means of which Kant criticizes traditional ontology in the amphiboly chapter, and develops them further toward the concept of substance as self-relation. This arises from his concept of reflection as negation relating itself to itself. Hegel’s dialectic of the determinations of reflection dissolves, on the one hand, the logical-ontological principles of traditional metaphysics and, on the other, subjects Kant’s critique of those principles—which rests on his doctrine of space and time as purely sensible forms—to a metacritique. For Hegel, the Leibnizian *principium identitatis indiscernibilium* remains at the level of external reflection, the mere comparing of determinations. By contrast, his concept of identity is negation reflected into itself: it first appears in diversity as a determination of external reflection, of comparing, yet already implicitly contains the further process through opposition to contradiction. In opposition Hegel presents his dialectic, which again dissolves into the self-relation of negation the Kantian concept of real opposition—the concept Kant sets over against logical opposition, which in former metaphysics had ontological significance.

Hegel’s treatment of the last two pairs of Kantian concepts of reflection—matter and form, and the inner and the outer—the first in “ground,” the last in “essential relation”—develops Kant’s dissolution of substance into the relation of appearance further toward substance as self-relation, and thereby prepares the concept of the concept. Our brief investigation of substance in the “absolute relation,” which directly presents the genesis of the concept, shows that substance stands in a reflexive relation to its others, the accidents, and that the concept arises as self-relation. The logic of essence grounds, on the one hand, the unity of logic and ontology, yet on the other rejects substance metaphysics. These two aspects are therefore inseparable, because traditional substance metaphysics, as we have seen, rests on the first assumption: the agreement of formal logic and ontology.

Insofar as the logical course of self-determining is at the same time that of self-negation, one must not see here a revival of the old substance. Substance as a quiet foundation of all accidents, or of all activities resting on the logical model of the subject–predicate relation, has been completely destroyed in the logic of essence as an ontology of relation. When Hegel now speaks of the substance that must at the same time be subject, he is no longer speaking—as that equation might lead one to expect—of traditional substance, but of substance as subject: not the subject of all formal logic, but the self-negating subject.[^z.8] Substance is now the movement of self-negating itself, which presupposes no substrate.[^z.9] Presupposing is unmasked in the logic of reflection as positing. Substrates are only a positedness, or a product of this movement itself, and are dissolved into the movement. The course of objective logic thereby reaches a subjectivity that no longer presupposes its other, and that unfolds or develops its determinations and its other (objectivity) within itself not merely implicitly, but already explicitly.

How this result of the logic of essence bears on subjective logic becomes clear when, in the introductory chapter of the logic of the concept, “Of the Concept in General,” Hegel elucidates the “concept of the concept” (GW XII, 16). This is nothing other than what was presented and attained through objective logic, which Hegel calls “the genetic exposition of the concept” (GW XII, 11). In this connection Hegel formulates that the “concept” constitutes the foundation of “being” and of “essence,” of the immediate and of reflection, and is their truth. True, the concept as such is again something immediate—but only because the concept “has made itself into the immediate out of the sublation of mediation” (ibid.). It is the process of the logic of reflection to make itself into the immediate out of the sublation of mediation. The completion of that process is the emergence of the concept as subjectivity.[^z.10]

That famous (and notorious) conception of the concept as the concrete universal[^z.11] can be understood correctly only through the achievement of the logic of essence. This concept is universal because it is equal to itself and thereby a positive that has no negativity, no limit, no restriction. It negates determinateness (negation). Yet the concept is at the same time concrete, because self-equality is the negation of negation. As self-negating it is thoroughly determined—which, in the old metaphysics and still in Kant, fulfills the definition of the individual. This concept is thus the successor of the Leibnizian complete concept (*notio completa*) and at the same time constitutes the principle of individuation, because as self-determining it is thoroughly determined.

In this sense Hegel’s characterization of the logic of the concept as development can be understood: what is to be unfolded is what the concept implies in determinations.[^z.12] Yet the determinations of the concept are not compossible in the Leibnizian sense; they contradict one another.

Only as absolute negation is the concept the concrete universal. Thereby Hegel replaces both the Leibnizian *notio completa* and the Kantian ideal of pure reason as the foundation of thoroughgoing determination. The Hegelian concept of the concept must therefore be understood as the realization of reflection as self-related negativity.

The place of the Hegelian concept in the history of metaphysics also becomes clear when one considers its relation to the Kantian pure I, to transcendental apperception. For Hegel this I provides a model for his concept of the concept.[^z.13] This I establishes the objectivity of the categories.[^z.14] Hegel sees therein the starting point of his logic, whose task is to present being and thinking as united and to ascribe objectivity to thinking: “I penetrates it [the object] thinkingly” (GW XII, 18).

On the other hand, Hegel does not fail to recognize that in the first ‘Transcendental Dialectic,’ the ‘Transcendental Paralogism,’ Kant rejects this I’s claim to being. For Kant the I is not a substance, for it would fall into a circle if it applied its own category of substance to itself. It may apply the empty categories only to sensation-material; therefore—Kant asserts against the rational psychology of school metaphysics—the substantiality or immortality of the soul cannot be proved. The pure I as transcendental apperception stands outside cognition (and self-cognition). This I is solely the function of synthesizing the manifold of all representations.[^z.15] Hegel attacks this claim of Kant’s in the introductory text to the second chapter of the Idea chapter, “Idea of Cognizing.” According to Hegel, in his critique of the rational doctrine of the soul Kant tries to keep the I free from being determined as to content:
> “There thus remains nothing but the simple representation, wholly empty of content for itself: I, of which one cannot even say that it is a concept, but a mere consciousness that accompanies all concepts.” (GW XII, 193)

The I itself, which secures the objectivity of the categories, is therefore not objective. It is a wholly contentless, empty representation.

Hegel’s critique of the contentlessness of pure apperception—which amounts to a demand for the contentfulness of the I, or of the concept—must not be misunderstood as a relapse into the old metaphysics. For Hegel himself, like Kant, criticizes the substantial version of the concept of the I in metaphysics by rejecting the view that the I *has* faculties.[^z.16] “Having” here designates the substance–accident relation, which Hegel uses in connection with the thing and its properties.[^z.17]

Hegel refers to the passage in the *Critique of Pure Reason* where Kant exhibits the circle-problem of self-cognition and calls the circle an “inconvenience”[^z.18]:
> “But it is indeed ridiculous to call this nature of self-consciousness—that I thinks itself, that I cannot be thought without its being I which thinks—an inconvenience and something faulty, a circle: a relation whereby, in immediate empirical self-consciousness, the absolute, eternal nature of that self-consciousness and of the concept reveals itself, and reveals itself for this reason, that self-consciousness is precisely the existent, thus empirically perceptible, pure concept, the absolute relation to itself, which as separating judgment makes itself into the object and alone is this: thereby to make itself into a circle” (GW XII, 194).[^z.19]

Hegel’s dissolution of the circle-problem is thus no dissolution in the vulgar sense: the circle is not to be avoided at all. It is rather the essence of the I, of self-consciousness, and of spirit. In this concept of the concept the constitutive role of the logic of essence is unmistakable—the logic in which reflection as self-related negativity is established.

Hegel’s logic of the concept stands on a new stage that is no longer entangled in shine. Through reflection as self-relation it has been dissolved into the developing concept. The concept is the unity of being and essence. Yet it unfolds their difference within itself without again losing itself. This self-preservation of the concept has become possible through its genesis in the logic of essence.

It is obvious that Hegel here means the metaphysics of the Leibniz–Wolff school, which Kant had designated as dogmatism. Later Hegel also includes Descartes in this first attitude (Enc. 1830, § 76). According to Jaeschke, in the lectures on logic and metaphysics Greek philosophy is also assigned to it (Jaeschke 2003, 264).

Concerning objective logic all seem to agree that it contains the critical presentation of metaphysics. Fulda/Horstmann/Theunissen 1980; Horstmann 1984, 65. In the reply to this question Theunissen keeps open the possibility of ascribing a critical function to the logic of the concept as well (op. cit., 71). But as concerns objective logic, Theunissen on the one hand concedes that untruth is also a necessary moment of truth (Theunissen 1994, 35 and 89). On the other hand his interpretation diverges from ours when he does not admit the fundamental role of the logic of reflection (op. cit., 47). But if the Idea, as he says, is understood as “perfect unity of self-relation and relation to the other” (op. cit., 49), it is not to be mistaken.

Reuter too concedes the “terminologically in part conspicuous agreement” of the amphiboly chapter with the Dissertation, but asserts moreover that Kant here at the same time attempts to set himself apart from it (Reuter 1989, 70ff.). Especially in Sectio V of the Dissertation Broecken finds the precursor of the amphiboly chapter (Broecken 1970, 56), but she rejects the direct tracing of the systematic position of the amphiboly chapter back to this (Broecken 1970, 58). Malter contests this on the ground that the contents of this chapter would presuppose the results of the Transcendental Aesthetic and Analytic (Malter 1981, 291). Kemp Smith indeed mentions the similarities of the themes of the amphiboly chapter and the Dissertation, but also makes the differences clear in passing (Kemp Smith 1918, 419).

[^k1.29]: Erdmann 1878, 37 n.; Heidegger likewise holds this opinion (Heidegger GA III, 472).

[^k1.30]: Vaihinger 1922, 529.

[^k1.31]: Kemp Smith 1918, 423. He relies on Adickes.

[^k1.32]: Kemp Smith, ibid.

[^k1.33]: Zilsel 1913, 431. For this reason this dating of Zilsel’s conflicted with that of Kemp Smith and Adickes. For these ascribed the section A277–8/B333–4, which belongs to the Remark on the Amphiboly, to the earlier time. Zilsel is of the opinion that each individual part as a whole stems from the same time, whereas Adickes and Kemp Smith want to determine the diversity of the time of composition also within a part. Against the dating of the chapter to the pre-critical period argue Malter 1982, 126 and also Heßbrüggen-Walter 2004.

[^k1.34]: Zilsel 1913, 433.
[^k1.35]: See the quotation from the *Prolegomena* in footnote 52. Zilsel himself also cites this place, without agreeing with it. According to Schopenhauer these concepts are “wholly and completely arbitrarily assumed,” whereas the number four corresponds to the categories. (Schopenhauer 1859 I, 564). Not a few of the groundings that Zilsel gives for his dating spring from his own interpretive presuppositions, which prevent an appropriate understanding of the text.
[^k1.36]: The author of the *Darstellung der Amphibolie* also asserted the derivation of the concepts of reflection from the table of categories (Anonymous, 3).

[^k1.37]: Kant’s definitions of reflection are not always agreeing; cf. Reuter 1989, 82f.; Heßbrüggen-Walter 2004.
[^k1.38]: Kant here gives only these four examples and does not show how the other judgments—the categorical, hypothetical, problematic, assertoric, etc.—result from the comparing application of the concepts of reflection, which is not easy to supply. Longuenesse seeks to find the complete correspondences (Longuenesse 1998, 131ff.); also Malter 1982.
[^k1.39]: One recall Kant’s famous sentence in which he defines the word ‘transcendental’: “I call all cognition transcendental that is occupied not so much with objects but with our mode of cognition of objects insofar as this is to be possible a priori” (B25, also A11f.).

[^k1.40]: This use of the understanding would better be designated as “transcendent.” The distinction of “transcendent” and “transcendental” that Kant undertook in the Transcendental Dialectic (A295f./B353f.) he did not maintain.

[^k1.41]: Although it comes only in an appendix, the importance of transcendental reflection and thereby of this chapter becomes clearer when one considers how fundamental the distinction of representations according to their source of cognition, i.e., of the understanding or of the senses, is both for Kantian epistemology in the *Critique of Pure Reason* and for his practical theory. The appendix is obviously in Kant the systematic place of the grounding of that distinction of representations.

[^k1.42]: The difference that Heßbrüggen-Walter makes between “*reflexio*” as deliberation and proper transcendental reflection does not quite become clear. (Heßbrüggen-Walter 2004, 161f.) Malter and Willaschek are also of the opinion that this “deliberation” already means the transcendental. (Malter 1981, 290; Willaschek 1998, 340).

[^k1.43]: On the distinction between the reflecting and determining power of judgment see *Critique of the Power of Judgment* (AA V, 179).

[^k1.44]: Reuter is also of this opinion (Reuter 1989, 82). But he sees it somewhat differently at a later place. He insists on the demarcation of transcendental reflection over against those reflections as “constitutive acts” and thereby understands these objects in general as “object-complexes constituted through categorial synthesis, made determinable for judgment-synthesis, brought into objective space–time relations” (Reuter 1989, 85).

[^k1.45]: Besides Heidegger (GA IX, 472) Liebrucks also holds the thesis: “Transcendental deliberation is the proper business of the *Critique of Pure Reason*” (Liebrucks 1968, 665). The affinity between the two is unmistakable, whether or not it hits Kant’s own understanding. Liebrucks’s interpretation of the amphiboly chapter is marked, what should be critically noted, by the equation of Leibnizian metaphysics with human intuition (666f.) and moreover also by the peculiar introduction of the theme of language that goes past Kant’s text.
[^k1.46]: Cf. George Friedrich Meier: *Auszug aus der Vernunftlehre*. Halle 1752, § 363, in: Kant AA, XVI. Cf. Wolff, *Logica*, §§ 346–348. Aristotle already knew the dictum; see Bochenski 1956, 91f.
[^k1.47]: AA IX, 123. See also Reflexionen 3217 and 3218 in AA XVI, 717.
[^k1.48]: See Chapter 4, 1.

[^k1.49]: By contrast Meier reckons the immediate inferences among the inferences of reason and Wolff among the syllogisms: Meier *Auszug aus der Vernunftlehre*. Halle 1752, § 363; Wolff *Logica*, §§ 346–348.
[^k1.50]: In that respect Broecken’s attempt fails to find Leibniz’s errors, which Kant presents in the amphiboly chapter, in the forms of the syllogism (Broecken 1970, 177). Although she correctly sees that reason constitutes a faculty of inferring (ibid. 174), she forgets that what is at issue here is the use of the understanding and then simply ascribes the errors that Kant finds in Leibniz to syllogistic fallacies. She thus misrecognizes that Kant holds the immediate inference for an inference of the understanding.

[^k1.51]: In that respect Parkinson is right when he asserts that the critique rests on the Transcendental Aesthetic. But that does not make, as he says, the critique of the amphiboly superfluous (Parkinson 1981, 313 and 314).
[^k1.52]: Kant says still more clearly in the *Prolegomena* that the concepts of reflection have been derived from the categories: “Those concepts which I had likewise brought into a table under the name of the concepts of reflection according to the clue of the categories mingle themselves in ontology, without leave and rightful claims, among the pure concepts of the understanding […]” (AA IV, 326).
[^k1.53]: It could be that readers associate with this word ‘concept of reflection’ the concept of ‘reflection’ itself. In this study ‘concepts of reflection’ always designate these eight concepts.
[^k1.54]: See the quotation from the *Prolegomena* in the penultimate footnote. The same holds for Wolff. Cf. Heimsoeth 1971, 3.

[^k1.55]: On the difference of the concepts of reflection from categories: Anonymous: *Darstellung* 1795, 2ff. and 10; Knauer 1881, 4.

[^k1.56]: Heidegger GA XXV, 250. Eisler shows in his *Kant-Lexikon* that the categories for Kant initially also possessed validity as concepts of reflection (Eisler 1930, 295). Cf. Broecken 1970, 63.

[^k1.57]: The concepts of reflection are those “that distinguish themselves from categories by the fact that through those not the object, according to that which constitutes its concept (magnitude, reality), but only the comparison of the representations, which precedes the concept of things, is presented in all its manifoldness” (A269/B325).

[^k1.58]: See also Broecken 1970, 12f.
[^k1.59]: For the precise analysis of the function of the concepts of reflection in the formation of judgments, see Longuenesse 1998, 131ff. Reuter finds the generalization of reflection in the formation of judgments incomprehensible, because it is said to be possible to form analytic judgments without it. But without the achievement of comparing that goes back to reflection, analytic judgments too are impossible, because only through comparing is it cognized whether a mark can be subsumed under the concept of a subject. (Reuter 1989, 86, n. 8).

[^k1.60]: It is improbable that Kant believes that two things at different places can have wholly the same determinations. Kant’s assertion is not that the two things can be equal, but that their diversity is already given spatially before we investigate their determinations. Cf. Fischer 1909, 492; Zilsel 1913, 437.
[^k1.61]: Also in his habilitation writing *Principiorum primorum cognitionis metaphysicae nova dilucidatio* Kant criticizes the *principium identitatis indiscernibilium*, but through the assertion that he ascribes to Leibniz in the amphiboly chapter, that place in space is also a determination of a thing (AA I, 409).

[^k1.62]: “Since he [Leibniz] thus had before his eyes merely their concepts, and not their place in intuition, wherein alone objects can be given, and wholly left out of account the transcendental place of these concepts (whether the object is to be counted under appearances or under things in themselves), it could not turn out otherwise than that he extended his principle of indiscernibles, which holds merely of concepts of things in general, also to the objects of the senses (*mundus phaenomenon*), and believed thereby to have procured no small extension for the cognition of nature” (A271f./B327f.).

[^k1.63]: This logical opposition “consists,” as Kant determines in the writing on negative magnitudes, “in this, that of the very same thing something is at once affirmed and denied. The consequence of this logical connection is nothing at all (*nihil negativum, irrepraesentabile*), as the principle of contradiction” (AA II, 172). Kant also in the transcendental Ideal in the *Critique of Pure Reason* equates transcendental affirmation with Something or being and transcendental negation with non-being (A574/B602).
[^k1.64]: But this concept of realities to be opposed to one another does not hold in the Kantian Ideal of Pure Reason: God is the sum of all realities. Kant asserts that this God contains no negation and therefore no opposition (A575/B603). In that respect Kant with reference to the concept of God stands on the same standpoint as Leibniz, which makes a great difference from Hegel. On this later in the fourth chapter.
[^k1.65]: Cf. Döring 1904, 84; Hess conjectures that Kant thereby wanted to avoid the possible misunderstanding concerning the Analogy of Experience, in which he takes up the category of causality (Hess 1981, 221).
[^k1.66]: Whether this really holds we shall investigate more precisely in the next chapter.

[^k1.67]: E.g. Aristotle: *Metaphysics* (XII, 4, 1070b3ff.; XIV, 1, 1088a21–b2).
[^k1.68]: But Kant has not only thought of this; he was also of the view that this Leibnizian substance-doctrine is only then to be understood consistently if one thinks substance exclusively as an object of the pure understanding.
[^k1.69]: “Substances in general must have something inner, which is thus free from all outer relations, consequently also from composition” (A274/B330).
[^k1.70]: From this the connection of the concepts of reflection “of the inner and the outer” with the transcendental paralogisms becomes clear: In the paralogism chapter the doctrine that holds the soul for a substance is expressly rejected. What I can cognize through inner sense is not the ‘I in itself,’ but only the appearance of the same; just as through outer sense I can cognize not things in themselves, but only their appearance. Leibniz falls, Kant would say, into this fallacy. On the connection between the amphiboly and paralogism chapters, see Behn 1908, 25; Zilsel 1913, 440; Broecken 1970, 190.

[^k1.71]: Cassirer takes into account Kant’s doctrine of matter in the amphiboly chapter, which would exactly correspond to his conception of modern history of philosophy as passing from the concept of substance to that of function, first in the introduction to the *Phänomenologie der Erkenntnis*, the 3rd part of the *Philosophie der symbolischen Formen*, but neither in his book *Kants Leben und Lehre* nor in *Das Erkenntnisproblem in der Philosophie und Wissenschaft der neueren Zeit*. But he there exaggerates the significance of the Kantian conception of matter and form as concepts of reflection when he sees therein a “methodical relativization of the opposition” (Cassirer ECW XIII, 11). On this point, see Graubner 1972, 57f. Cassirer already with Kant takes a step in the direction of Hegel.

[^k1.72]: A181/B225ff., especially A184/B227. Here Kant speaks of “substance (*phaenomenon*).”

[^k1.73]: “[…] so we have even nothing permanent that we could put under the concept of its substance as intuition, except merely matter, […]. Therefore this I also has not the least predicate of intuition which, as permanent, could serve as correlate of the time-determination in inner sense: as for example impenetrability is in matter as empirical intuition” (B278).

[^k1.74]: I contest Tuschling’s thesis that the concept of substance as sum of mere relations does not agree with Kant’s own metaphysics of the world of appearance (Tuschling 1988, esp. 226).

[^k1.75]: “The concept of a substance means the last subject of existence, i.e., that which itself does not again belong merely as predicate to the existence of another. Now matter is the subject of everything that in space may be counted toward the existence of things; for outside it no other subject could be thought than space itself; which however is a concept that still contains nothing existent at all, but merely the necessary conditions of the outer relation of possible objects of outer sense. Thus matter, as the movable in space, is the substance in the same” (AA IV, 503). Schelling takes up this theme of the construction of matter through forces in the *System of Transcendental Idealism*. This is also said to have influenced Hegel. Cf. Wladika 1995, 69ff.
[^k1.76]: Thus Leibniz’s doctrine of pre-established harmony could count for Kant as a *deus ex machina*, a designation that Leibniz himself applied to occasionalism. In this point Hegel seems to be of the same opinion when he says of this Leibnizian doctrine in the lectures on the history of philosophy: “[A]lone God has the privilege that what cannot be comprehended is loaded onto him. The word God is then the makeshift that itself leads only to a unity that is only a named one” (HW XX, 254). Cf. Guyer 1978. Hegel criticizes Leibniz still more fundamentally, because the latter has exhibited no principle in order conversely to let the many proceed from the unity (HW XX, 255).

[^k1.77]: This problem of the agreement of substances occupied Kant already since his earlier period, when he still held Leibniz–Wolffian pluralistic substance-metaphysics. Already in his *nova dilucidatio* of 1755 Kant criticized Leibniz’s doctrine of pre-established harmony (AA I, 412), in that he asserted a *realis actio*. But thereby he likewise did not get by without a divine influence, on which Leibniz too relied (On the “*Principium coexistentiae*” see AA I, 412f.).

[^k1.78]: Moreover Kant still gives further examples of the traditional dealing with these concepts of reflection: the essential determinations of a thing (*essentialia*) correspondingly count as matter, whereas the manner in which the things are connected counts only as form joining afterwards. Also the assumption of an unlimited reality as matter preceded the form of negation as limitation (A266f./B322f.).
[^k1.79]: Cf. A219/B266.
[^k1.80]: He names his own transcendental idealism in the second edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, what is especially to be emphasized, the “formal” (B519). Cf. Okochi 2005.

[^k1.81]: Kant repeated this assertion: “Leibniz took appearances as things in themselves, hence for *intelligibilia*, i.e., objects of the pure understanding […] and then his principle of indiscernibles could indeed not be contested” (A270/B326—emphasis by T. O.). See also: “Indeed, if I know a drop of water as a thing in itself according to all its inner determinations, then I can allow none of them to count as diverse from the other if the whole concept of the same is identical with it” (A272/B328—emphasis by T. O.); cf. Vaihinger 1892, 529; Zilsel 1913, 437; Reuter 1989, 128f.
[^k1.82]: Also in the writing on the progress of metaphysics Kant says that “the noumenon that lies at the ground of that appearance, i.e., the outer object as thing in itself, may perhaps be a simple being” (AA XX, 308). Cf. Reuter 1989, 155. Concerning the fourth pair of concepts of reflection Kant says of the intellectual conception of matter and form: It is “a wholly correct censure if he [the intellectual philosopher—T. O.] assumed that we intuit things as they are” (A267/B323).

[^k1.83]: See e.g.: “But since we can apply none of our concepts of the understanding to it [to the transcendental object—T. O.], this representation remains empty for us, and serves for nothing but to designate the limits of our sensible cognition” (A288f./B345).
[^k1.84]: Adickes too holds that Kant presupposes this correspondence (Adickes 1924, 59 and 119, footnote). Cf. Heimsoeth 1971, 38; Ewing 1938, 198; Findlay 1981, 37–38; Wladika 1995, 129.
[^k1.85]: “Thus his [Leibniz’s] principle of sufficient reason, since he set this in mere concepts, also did not help him in the least to go beyond the principle of analytic judgments, the principle of contradiction, and to extend himself synthetically a priori through reason” (AA XX, 282).
[^k1.86]: Malter 1981, 299; Graubner 1972, 248.

## For part 1.2

[^k2.1]: In order here to forestall a possible misunderstanding, it must be said: Metaphysics in the narrow sense, as *metaphysica specialis*, is as is well known first subjected to critique in the Transcendental Dialectic.
[^k2.2]: E.g. Döring 1904, 103; especially the Leibniz researcher Parkinson sets himself the task of refuting every critique of Kant’s of Leibniz (Parkinson 1981); Cassirer ECW I, 236ff.
[^k2.3]: Adickes 1897, 245; Parkinson 1981, 305f.; Kaehler 1981; McRae 1976; Broecken 1970. We go into this point still more precisely below. Broecken asserts that Kant yet correctly distinguishes Leibniz from the Wolffian school (Broecken 1970, 253).
[^k2.4]: Behn 1908, 19f.; Hess 1981, 222; Reuter 1989, 113; Malter emphasizes, even if he does not deny the validity of the critique for Leibniz, that the critique of the amphiboly chapter properly aims at Wolff and Baumgarten or “Wolffianism” (Malter 1981, esp. 294).

[^k2.5]: Adickes has noted in *Kants Systematik* that Kant takes up the individual concepts of reflection from Baumgarten: identity and diversity from §§ 269–272 in Baumgarten’s *Metaphysica*; agreement and conflict from §§ 135–147 (*reale et negativum*) and §§ 34–36 (*realitates, negationes*); inner and outer from §§ 37, 38 (*externum, internum*) and §§ 191–223 (*substantia et accidens*); determinable and determination from §§ 39–66 and §§ 224–245 (*simplex et compositum*). (Adickes 1887, 112; the paragraph numbers refer to the fourth edition of the *Metaphysica*, which was taken up in volume XII of Kant’s writings in the Academy edition). But Adickes tends not to go into the corresponding concepts themselves, but often only refers to places in Kant and Baumgarten in which the same theme is treated. *Substantia et accidens* e.g. have contentually to do with the concepts of reflection of the inner and the outer, but they rather correspond to the like-named categories substance and accident; something similar holds for the points that are treated under the catchwords “*reale et negativum*,” “*realitas et negationes*,” and “*simplex et compositum*.” Cf. Döring 1904, 89.

[^k2.6]: Both were first published in 1846.
[^k2.7]: Döring 1904, 75; Parkinson 1981, 305; Reuter 1985; Kemp Smith conjectures the direct influence of this writing on Kant (Kemp Smith 1918, 92, 186). Belaval names beside the *Nouveaux Essais* also the polemical writings between Leibniz and Clarke as a further text that Kant knew through his own reading of Leibniz (Belaval 1976, 2).
[^k2.8]: Heimsoeth has posed the task not yet solved to this day: What is at issue is “to pursue this connection [between Leibniz–Wolffian metaphysics and Hegel’s critique of metaphysics, which includes Kantian philosophy—T. O.] in a historico-systematic examination—thus first piece by piece to confront Hegel’s category-concepts with corresponding terms and executions in Wolff […], then however to pursue the overarching connections!” (Heimsoeth 1971, 4). — The present study understands itself as a contribution that attempts to bring this task nearer to its solution.
[^k2.9]: According to Heimsoeth the principle of contradiction in Kant in his Dissertation of 1770 has lost its formerly ontological significance. Summarizing Heimsoeth holds: “Kant’s new fundamental insight that this principle can indeed furnish a necessary, but not a sufficient criterion of the finding of truth, […] leads the later Kant to the complete detachment of this ‘supreme principle’ from all proper questions of being” (Heimsoeth 1971, 8).

[^k2.10]: “The first step that has happened in this investigation of reason is the distinction of analytic from synthetic judgments in general. — Had this been distinctly cognized in Leibniz’s or Wolff’s times, we would find this difference somewhere in a logic or metaphysics that has appeared since then, not only touched upon, but also impressed as important” (AA XX, 265). Correspondingly to the second stage of the progress of metaphysics the question is to belong: How are synthetic judgments a priori possible? and to the third: How is a cognition a priori possible from synthetic judgments? (AA XX, 266).

[^k2.11]: In the writing against Eberhard Kant divides the principle of the principle of sufficient reason according to the criteria of the logical (formal) and of the transcendental (material) (AA VIII, 193f.), and criticizes him therefore “that he wanted to let the in fact material principle of causality steal in by means of the principle of contradiction” (AA VIII, 194). The logical principle of the principle of reason is said to be subordinated to the principle of contradiction, whereas the transcendental proves itself as not derivable therefrom. The principle of sufficient reason in the last sense is said to be “merely a subjective principle, namely merely related to a critique of reason” (AA VIII, 248). Kant is in this writing, differently from in the Progress essay, of the opinion that Leibniz was conscious of the distinction of the analytic and synthetic judgment (AA VIII, 247f.).

[^k2.12]: Russell 1900, Couturat 1901.

[^k2.13]: Cassirer 1902, ECW Vol. 1.

[^k2.14]: Couturat 1902, 57.

[^k2.15]: Couturat 1902, 58.

[^k2.16]: Couturat 1902, 65.

[^k2.17]: Ishiguro 1990; Fichant 1998, 222–223.

[^k2.18]: “*[I]l faut considerer qu’il y a deux grands principes de nos raisonnemens; l’un est le principe de la contradiction. […] l’autre principe est celuy de la raison determinante*” (G VI, 127). — The places from Leibniz’s writings are given according to the following abbreviations of the titles of the texts and with the volume (Roman) and page numbers (Arabic) of the *Philosophische Schriften* edited by Gerhardt or with the paragraph numbers:
	**Disc. metaph**: *Discours de Métaphysique*
	**Meditationes**: *Meditationes de Cognitione, Veritate et Ideis*
	**Monad**: The so-called *Monadology*
	**NE**: *Nouveaux Essais sur l’entendement*
	**Principes**: *Principes de la Nature et de la Grace fondés en raison*
	**SN**: *Système nouveau pour expliquer la nature des substances et leur communication entre elles*
	**Theod**: *Theodicy*
	For the German translation the *Philosophische Werke* in four volumes edited by Cassirer (Hamburg 1996) are used.

[^k2.19]: “Everything involving a contradiction is impossible and everything containing no contradiction is possible.” Leibniz believes, quite traditionally, that the principle of identity and the principle of contradiction are properly one and the same principle. See e.g. *Theod*. 3rd appendix, 14 “*celluy [principe] des Identiques ou de la contradiction*” (G VI, 413); see also the 2nd letter to Clarke (G VII, 355). He derives in the *Nouveaux Essais* the principle of the excluded middle from the principle of contradiction (NE IV, 2, § 1).

[^k2.20]: According to Buchenau’s translation (Leibniz Werke IV, 222). The original text runs: “*une maxime aussi universelle et aussi evidente*” (G VI, 217).

[^k2.21]: “*[S]i deux contradictions peuvent estre vraies en même temps, tout raisonnement devient inutile*.” (To Arnauld, G II, 62) “*Cujus ratio est, quia de notionibus contradictionem involventibus simul possent concludi opposita, quod absurdum est*” (*Meditationes*, G IV, 424). Cf. Martin 1967, 83.

[^k2.22]: *Theod*. I, § 44 “*[…] qui porte que de deux propositions contradictoires, l’une est vraye, l’autre fausse*” (G VI, 127).
[^k2.23]: *Disc. metaph*. § 13. *Theod*. II, 130.
[^k2.24]: *Theod*. I, § 45, II § 122, § 180, III, § 282, § 346, § 367; 3rd objection in the “Short Sketch of the Controversies” in the appendix of the *Theod*. in G VI, 379ff. *Disc. metaph*. § 13.
[^k2.25]: G IV, 437. The same terminology is also found at some places in the *Theodicy* (*Theod*. II, § 169, § 282) and in the 3rd Objection in G VI, 381.
[^k2.26]: *Theod*. G VI, 380.
[^k2.27]: *Theod*. I, § 45; 1st appendix, 3rd objection. *Disc. metaph*. § 30; G VII, 302 et al.

[^k2.28]: Leibniz sometimes restricts this principle to the sentences that are not tautologies (G VII, 199, 309), and sometimes however lets it hold in all cases (G II, 62; G VI, 413; G VII, 295; G VII, 301; 5th writing against Clarke, in G VII, 419). “This important principle holds for all events, and no contrary example can be adduced for it” (*Theod*. I, § 44; cf. *Monad*. § 32). In the second letter to Clarke he asserts that the principle of sufficient reason is required in order to pass from mathematics to physics (G VII, 355f.).

[^k2.29]: Leibniz continues: “*ou bien (ce qui est la même chose) qu’elles ont des preuves a priori de leur verité qui les rendent certaines, et qui monstrent que la connexion du sujet et du predicat de ces propositions a son fondement dans la nature de l’un et de l’autre; mais qu’elles n’ont pas des demonstrations de necessite, puisque ces raisons ne sont fondées que sur le principe de la contingence ou de l’existence des choses, c’est à dire sur ce qui est ou qui paroist le meilleur parmy plusieurs choses également possibles, au lieu que les verités necessaires sont fondées sur le principe de contradiction et sur la possibilité ou impossibilité des essences mêmes, sans avoir egard en cela à la volonte libre de Dieu ou des creatures*” (G IV, 438f.).

[^k2.30]: “*Cependant je diray un mot de la raison de la difference qu’il y a en cecy entre les notions des especes et celles des substances individuelles, plutost par rapport à la volonte divine que par rapport au simple entendement. C’est que les notions specifiques les plus abstraites ne contiennent que des verités necessaires ou eternelles, qui ne dependent point des decrets de Dieu […]; mais les notions des substances individuelles, qui sont completes et capables de distinguer entierement leur sujet, et qui enveloppent par consequent les verites contingentes ou de fait, et les circonstances individuelles du temps, du lieu, et autres, doivent aussi envelopper dans leur notion, prise comme possible, les decrets libres du Dieu, pris aussi comme possibles, parceque ces decrets libres sont les principales sources des existences ou faits; au lieu que les essences sont dans l’entendement divin avant la consideration de la volonté*” (G II, 49). Cf. G II, 38f.

[^k2.31]: Guyer understands Leibniz’s conception of the perfect concept as a precursor of the Hegelian concept. The argumentation for his thesis that Hegel exhibits a contradiction between this perfect concept and the pluralistic substance-theory in Leibniz is very convincing. But for the grounding he refers only to the determinate-being chapter in Hegelian logic, which in my view is inadequate. For a more complete proof of this thesis Hegel’s executions on being-for-itself and on reflection should also be analyzed (Guyer 1978).
[^k2.32]: “*Patet etiam, quae tandem sit Idea vera, quae falsa, vera scilicet cum notio est possibilis, falsa cum contradictionem involvit*” (G IV, 425).
[^k2.33]: In that respect the distinction between *definitio nominalis* and *definitio realis* does not coincide with the distinction of the two kinds of truths.

[^k2.34]: Thereby it is to be noted that Leibniz in contrast to Kant believes that geometrical cognitions are analytic.
[^k2.35]: “*Videbam autem commune esse omni propositioni verae affirmativae universali et singulari, necessariae vel contingenti, ut praedicatum insit subjecto, seu ut praedicati notio in notione subjecti aliqua ratione involvatur*” (*De libertate*, in: *Nouvelles lettres et opuscules inédits*, précédés d’une introduction par L. A. Foucher de Careil, p. 179/Cass. II, p. 655; see also p. 182/pp. 657f.).
[^k2.36]: Frémont calls the one purely logical, necessary inherence ‘*implication*,’ the other ontological ‘*involution*’ or ‘*enveloppement*’ (Cf. her introduction to the ‘*Specimen inventorum de admirandis naturae Generalis arcanis*,’ Frémont 2001, 287).

[^k2.37]: Couturat has called Leibniz’s logic “not only the heart and the soul of his system, but the center of his intellectual activity and the source of all his inventions” (Couturat 1901, xii). Russell exercised sharp critique of this doctrine of Leibniz (Russell 1900, 12ff.). According to him not all sentences can be reduced to the subject–predicate form. See on this Cassirer’s review of Russell’s Leibniz book in: Cassirer ECW I, 178ff. Cassirer asserts by contrast that Leibniz’s philosophy is not exhausted in having researched the significance of the relation of subject and predicate (Cassirer ECW I, 484, 487; see also in his Leibniz book, ECW I, 318f.). Cf. also Martin’s critique of Russell and Couturat (Martin 1967, 34ff.).

[^k2.38]: Kant distinguishes in his polemic against Eberhard, who attempts to prove the principle of sufficient reason through the principle of contradiction, the logical (formal) principle of cognition and the transcendental (material) principle. Whereas that, which runs “Every sentence must have a ground,” is subordinated to the principle of contradiction, this, “Every thing must have its ground,” is the principle “which no human being has ever proved from the principle of contradiction (and in general from mere concepts without relation to sensible intuition), nor will prove” (AA VIII, 193f.). The last-quoted version of the principle of reason obviously applies to the synthetic sentences a priori.
[^k2.39]: *Ontologia*, 15. (Place-references to the writings of Wolff and Baumgarten are given with titles or customary designations and paragraph numbers; see the bibliography.)

[^k2.40]: Cf. the formulation in Wolff: “Something cannot at the same time be and also not be” (*Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 10).
[^k2.41]: Cf. *Deutsche Metaphysik* § 30.
[^k2.42]: In the *Ontologia* he even defines existence as *complementum possibilitatis*. But he also adds that this definition is a mere nominal definition (*Ontologia*, § 174).
[^k2.43]: But of course logically something actual must also be possible (*Deutsche Metaphysik* § 15).
[^k2.44]: On this point see Heimsoeth 1971, 6f.; Cassirer ECW III, 458f.

[^k2.45]: Wolff gives another proof “a priori” in § 70 of the *Ontologia* (*Ontologia*, 70). But this proof appears as less convincing. On this: École 1990, 148. On the problem of the connection of this principle with that of contradiction see École 1990, 151f.
[^k2.46]: “*Id, cujus in se sive absolute spectati oppositum impossibile est, seu contradictionem involvit, dicitur absolute necessarium: illud vero, cujus oppositum non nisi in hypothesi data, seu sub data quadam conditione impossibile aut contradictionem involvit, hypothetice necessarium est*” (*Ontologia* § 302; cf. *Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 575 and *Natürliche Gottesgelahrtheit* § 972).

[^k2.47]: This hangs together with his distance over against Leibniz’s monadology and pananimalism. Wolff ascribes, as we shall see still more precisely below, to the simple thing physical character, i.e., that its composition constitutes a body. And although this simple thing itself is not extended, it is nevertheless not to be understood spiritually and metaphysically, as for example the faculty of representation. The simple thing is rather characterized by the fact that it contains everything that happened to it, happens, and will happen.

[^k2.48]: Baumgarten’s proof of the principle of reason, which Eberhard reproduces against Kant, Kant judges as one “to which now well no one will any longer appeal” (AA VIII, 197).
[^k2.49]: GW XI, 271.

[^k2.50]: According to Parkinson Leibniz means with this principle not the diversity of sensible objects, but of substance, which counts as noumenon (Parkinson 1981, 310).

[^k2.51]: Interestingly this determination of plurality also fits together with that which Hegel ascribes to number (GW XI, 124f./XXI, 193f.). See below.

[^k2.52]: E.g. “*et ratio existentis non est nisi ab existente*” (G VII, 303, also 305). But that of course still does not say that the ground of the negative effect cannot be a positive.
[^k2.53]: “*[J]e reponds qu’il sera encor assez difficile d’expliquer quelle connexion il y peut avoir entre les pensées de l’ame et les costés ou angles de la direction des corps et de plus qu’il y a encor dans la nature une autre loi generale, dont M. des Cartes ne s’est point apperçü, qui n’est pas moins considerable, sgavoir que la même determination ou direction en somme doit tousjours subsister; car je trouve que si on menoit quelque ligne droite que ce soit, par exemple d’orient en occident par un point donné, et si on calculoit toutes les directions de tous les corps du monde autant qu’ils avancent ou reculent dans des lignes paralleles à cette ligne, la difference entre les sommes des quantites de toutes les directions orientales et de toutes les directions occidentales se trouveroit tousjours la même, tant entre certains corps particuliers, si on suppose qu’ils ont seuls commerce entre eux maintenant, qu’à l’égard de tout l’univers, où la difference est tousjours nulle; tout estant parfaitement balance et les directions orientales et occidentales estant parfaitement egales dans l’univers; et si Dieu fait quelque chose contre cette regle, c’est un miracle.*” (Leibniz to Arnauld, April 1687, in G II, 94; see also G IV, 505f.). Traces of this doctrine of Leibniz’s can be recognized, although Kant could not know this letter, in his writing on negative magnitudes. There Kant in the third chapter gives two cosmological principles. The second runs: “All real grounds of the universe, if one sums those that are agreeing and subtracts from one another those that are opposed to one another, give a result that is equal to Zero” (AA II, 197).

[^k2.54]: Parkinson 1981, 310; Paton 1969, 77.
[^k2.55]: Cf. *Theod*. I § 20, II, § 153; *Monad*. § 42; *Principes* § 9.

[^k2.56]: He distinguishes three kinds of evils or bads (*mal*): 1. the metaphysical as simple imperfection, 2. the physical as suffering, and 3. the moral as sin (*Theod*. I, § 21). This distinction of the kinds of evil Wolff and Baumgarten have also taken over.
[^k2.57]: *Theod*. I, § 29, III § 378, *Disc. métaph*. § 30.
[^k2.58]: See also: *Theod*. II, § 199; I, § 8.
[^k2.59]: In *Theod*. I, § 30 Leibniz attempts—not convincingly—also to find the ground for the inertia of matter not in matter, but in form. Later however he gives to understand that the comparison, as concerns this point, remains precisely only a comparison and does not give a sufficient explanation for the existence of evil (§ 380).
[^k2.60]: See also: *Theod*. I, § 32; II, §§ 335, 380. It is remarkable that Leibniz at one place adduces two significations of the expression “real”: Real means 1. the purely positive or 2. privation. This second signification is for our context more interesting, because it seems that this concept of reality stands nearer to the Kantian, although Leibniz here too traces the cause of privation back to the limitedness of finite creatures (*Theod*. 1st appendix, Vth objection).

[^k2.61]: *Theod*. preface, G VI, 37, 47; see also G VI, 377, 383, I 24. On the problem of God’s “permitting” of evil see also already the *Discours de métaphysique*: “*il faut dire que Dieu le permet et non pas qu’il le veut, quoyqu’il y concoure à cause des loix de nature qu’il a establies et parce qu’il en sgait tirer un plus grand bien*” (*Disc. metaph*. § 7, in G IV, 432).

[^k2.62]: It is obvious that Kant has taken up this doctrine into his concept of the transcendental Ideal as sum of all realities. Cf. (Anonymous) *Darstellung* 1794, 17f.

[^k2.63]: That Leibniz, as Hegel understands it (HW XX, 244f.), also holds inorganic natural things like a stone for a—and even darkest—monad, is contested by Martin (Martin 1957/58, 83). In the logic of the concept Hegel indeed once says that Leibniz elevates matter to soulfulness, but for Hegel the monad itself is only “soul-thing” (GW XII, 197).

[^k2.64]: In the polemical writing against Eberhard Kant says: “He [Leibniz] meant [with the monads] not the corporeal world, but its for us cognizable substrate, the intelligible world, which lies merely in the idea of reason” (AA VIII, 248).
[^k2.65]: This division seems to violate the fact that logical necessity was also called metaphysical. Thereby two word-significations must be distinguished: “Metaphysical” in the sense of “absolute,” so that no opposite is possible, and “metaphysical” in the sense of a science that occupies itself with the end or God.

[^k2.66]: Cassirer ECW I, 300.
[^k2.67]: *Specimen dynamicum* Pars I, GM VI, 235.
[^k2.68]: “*inter facultatem agendi actionemque ipsam*.”
[^k2.69]: In *Système nouveau* Leibniz says that he was led to metaphysics through the concept of force: “*[J]e m’apperçus que la seule consideration d’une masse étendue ne suffisoit pas, et qu’il falloit employer encor la notion de la force, qui est tres intelligible, quoyqu’elle soit du ressort de la Metaphysique*” (G IV, 478).
[^k2.70]: Leibniz sometimes does not distinctly distinguish matter and extension; yet he does this in *Entretien de Philarète et d’Ariste, suite du premier entretien d’Ariste et de Théodore*; see especially G VI, 584.

[^k2.71]: Still more detailed in G IV, 392f. On extension and on matter see also G VI, 584; “*solamque adeo extensionem resistentiae quae per materiam diffunditur, hoc nomen apud rigidum examinatorem tueri*” (G IV, 394).

[^k2.72]: See also the untitled text against Descartes of May 1702 (G IV, 393ff., esp. 395ff.).

[^k2.73]: In the *Monadology* Leibniz ascribes to the different degree of perfection of the monads the difference of activity and passivity. (*Monad*. § 49; also *Theod*. I, § 66).

[^k2.74]: “*Atque hoc ipsum substantiale principium est, quod in viventibus anima, in aliis forma substantialis appellatur, et quatenus cum materia substantiam vere unam, seu unum per se constituit, id facit quod ego Monadem appello*” (G IV, 511).
[^k2.75]: See also in the *Système Nouveau*: “*Mais comme l’ame ne doit pas estre employée pour rendre raison du detail de l’oeconomie du corps de l’animal, je jugeay de même qu’il ne falloit pas employer ces formes pour expliquer les problemes particuliers de la nature, quoyqu’elles soyent necessaires pour établir des vrays principes generaux*” (G IV, 479).

[^k2.76]: Still clearer in G IV, 395. “*Vis passiva est ipsa Resistentia, per quam corpus resistit non tantum penetrationi, sed et motui, et per quam fit, ut corpus aliud in locum ejus subire non possit nisi ipso cedente, ipsum vero non cedat nisi motu impellentis nonnihil tardato, atque ita perseverare conetur in priore statu non ita tantum ut sponte non inde discedat, sed ita etiam ut mutanti repugnet. Itaque duo insunt Resistentiae sive Massae: primum Antitypia ut vocant seu impenetrabilitas, deinde resistentia seu quod Keplerus vocat corporum inertiam naturalem*.”

[^k2.77]: Kant evidently shares this view when he says in the polemical writing against Eberhard: “The simple thus in the time-sequence as in space is absolutely impossible, and if Leibniz has sometimes expressed himself so that one could at times interpret his doctrine of simple beings as if he wanted to know matter composed therefrom, then it is fairer to understand him, so long as it is compatible with his expressions, as if he meant under the simple not a part of matter, but the wholly beyond all sensible lying for us completely uncognizable ground of the appearance that we call matter” (AA VIII, 203).
[^k2.78]: But in attraction, which Kant, following Newton, essentially ascribes to matter, Leibniz against Newton sees a divine assistance, i.e., a miracle (5th writing against Clarke in G VII, 417).
[^k2.79]: “*Anima est substantia simplex*.” (*Psych. rational*. § 48).

[^k2.80]: “*Determinationes essentiales sunt id, quod Forma appellari solet, item Causa formalis*” (Wolff *Ontologia*, § 944).
[^k2.81]: Over against this the body is defined through extension, the force of inertia, and the active force (Wolff *Cosmologia*, § 138).

[^k2.82]: In Wolff the moving force is a principle that effects the change of place of a body, whereas the active force constitutes the principle of change in general.

[^k2.83]: But for him no extended can simultaneously have inertia and the force of motion. These are relatively related to one another. If the force of an extended is not equal to the force of the other, the force is the force of motion (*Metaphysica*, § 418).

[^k2.84]: But Leibniz makes clear that he thereby lays the emphasis on the relation of body and soul.
[^k2.85]: Wolff, *Psychol. rational*. § 589, n. and Baumgarten, *Metaphysica*, § 767.

[^k2.86]: See e.g. SN, in G IV, 483, 499.
[^k2.87]: “*Per Systemata explicandi commercium inter mentem & corpus intelligimus hypotheses philosophorum, quas excogitarunt ad reddendam rationem commercii inter animam & corpus intercedentis*” (Wolff, *Psychol. rational*. § 530). “*Sententiae, huic [substantiarum mundanarum commercio] explicando aptae quae videntur, SYSTEMATA EXPLICANDI COMMERCII INTER SUBSTANTIAS MUNDI dicuntur*” (Baumgarten, *Metaphysica*, § 448).
[^k2.88]: Hegel judges Leibnizian philosophy generally “less as a philosophical system than as a hypothesis” (HW XX, 238).

[^k2.89]: In the *Deutsche Metaphysik* it is first asserted in empirical psychology (“Ch. 3, Of the Soul in General, namely what we perceive of it”) that sensation or the deed according to the will give no proofs for the agreement of the soul and the body (see also Wolff *Deutsch. Metaph*. §§ 527ff.). Both, sensation and will, show only “that two things are at the same time” (*Deutsch. Metaph*. § 529). The problem of the agreement of body and soul thus points beyond experience. Therefore it is to be treated not in empirical, but in rational psychology. In cosmology (“Ch. 4, Of the World”) Wolff mentions the “universal harmony of things” once and praises Leibniz’s doctrine; but he there forgoes further executions: “[It] is enough for us that we have proved that it [the universal harmony of things] is present, and that it can be explained in an intelligible manner according to the sense of Herr von Leibniz” (*Deutsch. Metaph*. § 600; cf. § 601).
[^k2.90]: “*Tria hodie in philosophia occurrunt systemata explicandi commercium, quod inter animam atque corpus intercedit, scilicet systema influxus, systema causarum occasionalium systema harmoniae praestabilitae*” (*Psychol. rational*. § 553).

[^k2.91]: Moreover he still says that the doctrine of pre-established harmony is supposed to serve Leibniz only to be able to hold fast to teleology (AA VIII, 250).

[^k2.92]: A23/B37, especially on space cf. A26/B42, on time A32/B49.
[^k2.93]: “*L’Espace bien loin d’estre substance, n’est pas même un Estre. C’est un ordre, comme le temps, un ordre des coexistences, comme le temps est un ordre entre les existences qui ne sont pas ensemble*” (G III, 622, also 612). “*Nempe spatium nihil aliud est quam ordo existendi simul possibilium, uti tempus est ordo existendi successive possibilium*” (G II, 269). “*Le temps n’etant que ce rapport des successions*” (G III, 595). “*Pour moy, j’ay marque plus d’une fois, que je tenois l’Espace pour quelque chose de purement relatif, comme le Temps; pour un ordre des Coexistences, comme le temps est un ordre de successions.*” (3rd writing to Clarke, in G VII, 363).
[^k2.94]: Cf. Martin 1957/58, 90, 92f. He holds space and time for orders of the monads and these monads are alone the absolute realities, but not their orders or relations. But he also sees that reality in a certain sense can be ascribed to space and time (ibid., 94).
[^k2.95]: He writes to de Volder: “*At spatium, ut tempus, non substantiale est quiddam, sed ideale*” (G II, 278). See also the fifth writing against Clarke: “*Puisque l’espace en soy est une chose ideale comme le temps, il faut bien que l’espace hors du monde soit imaginaire, comme les Scholastiques mêmes l’ont bien reconnu*” (G VII, 396).
[^k2.96]: G I, 282.
[^k2.97]: “*[E]o ipso quod corpus mathematicum non potest resolvi in prima constitutiva, id utique non esse reale colligitur, sed mentale quiddam nec aliud designans quam possibilitatem partium, non aliquid actuale*” (to de Volder, in G II, p. 268).

[^k2.98]: Cf. SD II, in GM VI, 247.
[^k2.99]: “*Ces considerations levent les difficultés sur le continu, qui supposent que le continu est quelque chose de reel, et a des parties avant toute division, et que la matiere est une substance*” (G III, 622f.).

[^k2.100]: “*La source de nos embarras sur la composition du Continu vient de ce que nous concevons la matière et l’espace comme des substances, au lieu que les choses materielles en elles mêmes ne sont que des phenomenes bien reglés*” (G III, 612). According to Kaehler Leibniz also distinguishes sensibility and understanding or appearance and things in themselves; but the difference from Kant consists in the fact that Leibniz attempts to ground these distinctions through God (Kaehler 1981, 418).

[^k2.101]: G II, 278; G III, 622; 3rd writing to Clarke, in G VII, 363 et al.

[^k2.102]: “*Horum (monadum – T. O.) ordo inter se nostris phaenomenis expressus constituit temporis spatiique notiones*” (G III, 281; see also Buchenau, 531).

[^k2.103]: Especially the concept of reality distinguishes Kant from Leibniz. The latter does not concede reality to the phenomena, but the former does.

[^k2.104]: This doctrine of ideal space and of ideal time however favored Cassirer’s interpretation of Leibnizian philosophy, which attempts to bring this near to Kant (Cassirer ECW I, esp. 235ff.). Here Cassirer also shows the difference of the space–time doctrines in Leibniz and Kant and also comments on Kant’s misrecognition of their connection in the amphiboly chapter. Döring likewise emphasizes the similarities of both philosophers as concerns these objects. (Döring 1904, 116f.).

[^k2.105]: Wolff however holds Leibniz’s definition of time in the letter to Remond (G III, 612: p. 100 of this work) for insufficient, because there successivity is not mentioned (Wolff, *Ontologia*, § 572).
[^k2.106]: “*Ordo est similitudo obvia in modo, quo res juxta se invicem collocantur, vel se invicem consequuntur*” (Wolff, *Cosmologia*, § 472).
[^k2.107]: In the *Deutsche Metaphysik* Wolff indicates that space is an order. He determines space as “the order of those things […] that are at the same time” (Wolff, *Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 46). He defines a thing as “everything that can be, whether it may be actual or not” (§ 16).
[^k2.108]: But space and time have nothing at all to do with the inner of things (Wolff *Deutsche Metaphysik*, § 49, § 98). Whereas *quantitas*, *magnitudo*, and *figura* are the *accidentia intrinseca* (*Ontologia*, § 784), space and time are the *accidentia extrinseca* (*Ontologia*, § 787, § 788). In that respect they cannot, as in the pre-critical Kant, be regarded as a property of things.

[^k2.109]: Parkinson 1981; Kemp Smith 1918; Broecken 1970; McRae 1976, 126–129; Cassirer ECW I, 235ff.; Kaehler 1981. Martin by contrast holds this critique of Kant’s for justified (Martin 1966, 99f.); also Belaval 1966, 4.
[^k2.110]: Martin conjectures that Kant knew this writing of Leibniz (Martin 1966, 100).

[^k2.111]: He thereby appeals to Aristotle’s concept of *aisthesis koine*; this also means: shape, magnitude, and number beside motion and rest (*De anima*, 418a17; 425a14; 428b22; 431b5). According to Leibniz for this cognition the perceptions from the outer senses must be brought into a unity. This unity is given by inner sense, which Leibniz also calls the power of imagination (‘*l’imagination*’). This imagination contains the outer senses and the common sense within itself.
[^k2.112]: This epistemology of Leibniz is very interesting in comparison with Kant’s: Although Leibniz always equates geometrical necessity with the logical or absolute, he here ascribes to geometry beside the intelligible also a sensible character. In Kant geometry is a synthetic cognition a priori that is traced back only to space as the sensible form of the subject. It would be worthwhile to compare Leibniz’s doctrine of the cognitive faculties more precisely with the Kantian. This however exceeds our theme.

[^k2.113]: Parkinson sees that Kant’s view of the theory of sensibility in Leibniz deviates from this itself (Parkinson 1981, 314).
[^k2.114]: E.g. Parkinson 1981, 305; McRae 1976; cf. Kaehler 1981, 418. By contrast: Broecken 1970, 138; Martin 1966, 99.
[^k2.115]: Wolff here inserts beside these Leibnizian divisions also a new concept: that of the overflowing (*abundans*) concept (*Phil. rational*., § 93).

[^k2.116]: On clarity: § 31; on obscurity: § 32; on distinctness: § 38; on confusion: § 39.


## For part 2.1
[^r1.1]: I restrict myself in this chapter to Hegel’s writings from this period that have to do with logic and metaphysics in the narrow sense, namely with the fragments from the lecture manuscripts (1801/02), Troxler’s transcript, Logic and Metaphysics (1804/05) and with the often discussed ‘logic’ of the *Phenomenology of Spirit*. I thus do not go into Hegel’s writings that, like the natural-right essay, the System of Ethical Life or further the philosophy of nature or of spirit, do not belong directly to logic, on which one can however also follow the development of Hegel’s logical concept, especially of Hegelian dialectic. On this theme: Baum 1986; Düsing 1984; Kimmerle 1982.

[^r1.2]: Fragments to these lectures were rediscovered not too long ago and taken up into volume V of G. W. F. Hegel’s Collected Works. What is at issue are texts (or at least parts of them) that Rosenkranz used in the composition of *Hegel’s Life*, but which thereafter were long lost. Concerning the precise content of this lecture the transcript by Troxler edited by Düsing informs us, who was one of the participants not only in the later broken-off lecture itself, but also in a private collegium of Hegel’s. On this lecture and its transmission see Düsing 1988, Jaeschke 2003, 150ff. Whether Hegel really held the lecture on *introductio in philosophiam* remains doubtful (Kimmerle 1967, 78).

[^r1.3]: Here Hegel refers implicitly to the relation of the *Wissenschaftslehre* to logic in Fichte, in which logic “\[consists] in the abstraction from all content of the *Wissenschaftslehre*” (Fichte’s Works, ed. by I. H. Fichte vol. I, 67). But in the derivation of the deed-act, which constitutes the first principle of the whole *Wissenschaftslehre*, one must appeal to logic, so that necessarily a circle arises (ibid. 66ff., 92).

[^r1.4]: “As such forms of finitude the forms of speculative thinking are now taken up in the logic” (GW V, 272). The infinite forms as object of logic are the forms of speculation grasped by logical reflection.

[^r1.5]: See Baum 1986, 2. “The difference of logic and metaphysics thus rests on the accentuation of opposition or identity of the finite forms constituting the common matter through reflection” (Baum 1986, 159). Jaeschke sees by contrast in the relation of logic and metaphysics a possible inconsistency of Hegel’s then concept, which we on account of the lacking transmission can no longer follow in detail (Jaeschke 1978, 98).

[^r1.6]: Kimmerle in the attempt at reconstruction of the Logic (1801/02) orients himself by the section “Comparison of Schelling’s Principle of Philosophy with Fichte’s” of the *Difference* essay (GW IV, 62ff, esp. 67ff.), in which he finds the presentation of a system-concept, and by the later logic-concept of the Logic and Metaphysics of 1804/05. Trede and Horstmann assert by contrast that the discussion of Hegel’s early Jena system-concept should rather be conducted by means of the section “Various Forms” of the *Difference* essay. It appears more convincing to me, as Jaeschke has worked it out, that in that section with the title “Comparison” what is at issue is not at all a system-concept (cf. Kimmerle 1982; the same 1974; Trede 1972; Horstmann 1972; Jaeschke 2003, 122, 125). Here indeed the task is posed to reason of sublating the oppositions, and it is asserted that their resolution can be found in a system of the indifference of two systems (1. of intelligence and 2. of nature). But even if one would hold this rendering of Schelling’s philosophy of identity for agreeing with Hegel’s then view, there is nevertheless no talk at all of logic or metaphysics, thus also not of their place in a system of philosophy.

[^r1.7]: “Sundering is the source of the need of philosophy” (GW IV, 12). Of the “need of philosophy” there is also talk in the first fragment “introductio in philosophiam” (GW V, 260f.). On the concept of “need” see the developmental-historical attempts thereto by Kozu 1988.

[^r1.8]: Jaeschke 2003, 112.

[^r1.9]: “Insofar as reflection makes itself into its object, its highest law, which is given to it by reason and through which it becomes reason, is its annihilation” (GW IV, 18).

[^r1.10]: On this aspect of the *Difference* essay, see Jaeschke 2003, 109f.

[^r1.11]: “The first now is that we cognize the simple Idea of philosophy itself, and then deduce the division of philosophy. The extended science of the Idea as such will be idealism or logic, which at the same time comprehends within itself how the determinacies of the form that the Idea includes within itself attempt to constitute themselves into absolute ones; i.e. it will, as \[it] as science of the Idea itself is metaphysics, annihilate the false metaphysics of the limited philosophical systems” (GW V, 263).

[^r1.12]: Cf. the editorial report to volume V of Hegel’s Collected Works, p. 654. A division of logic into the analytic, synthetic and the dialectical is further mentioned in Troxler’s transcript. But it corresponds neither to that order of Hegel’s in the fragment, nor to that of the announcement and also not to the presentation of logic in the transcript itself. Cf. Düsing 1988, 159. At the end of this fragment Hegel says: After “we show the specter of skepticism […] to the day, and cognize it in its nakedness, then I shall continue with \[the] presentation of those systems, as of the Kantian and of the Fichtean, that touch our culture more closely, and of which especially that one, even if it now no longer has significant adherents, yet still strongly rages in other sciences” (GW V, 275—emphasis T. O.).

[^r1.13]: Düsing doubts that the word “End of transcendental logic” stems from Hegel (Düsing 1988, 179). But the following second point supports that it was Hegel’s intention to take up and criticize transcendental philosophy.

[^r1.14]: In the *Difference* essay Hegel criticizes Kant’s concept of nature, which against its proper intention seeks to construct matter not dynamically, but mathematically (GW IV, 69, esp. 70).

[^r1.15]: Hegel very probably under the influence of Schelling’s philosophy of nature took up this theme in the Jena period. On the concept of matter of Schelling and Hegel in Jena, see Vieillard-Baron 1980.

[^r1.16]: Düsing 1984, 184f. He appeals to the following decisive place: “[…] simple relation, in that it reflects itself into itself, becomes infinity and first posits itself what it is according to its essence, since previously the dialectical of its moments was only our reflection; that they contradict themselves in their essence, this is now posited as reflection of simple relation into itself, as absolutely dialectical essence, as infinity” (GW VII, 29). But as Düsing rightly sees, the inclusion of our reflection is reached at the end of the logic (Düsing 1984, 186). The first part of this Metaphysics of 1804/1805 is constituted by the principles that are to be found in the logic of essence of the mature logic as “determinations of reflection.”

[^r1.17]: On the analysis of this place see Baum 1986, 98f., 101ff.

[^r1.18]: “*Metaphysica est scientia primorum in humana cognitione principiorum*” (Baumgarten *Metaphysica*, §1).

[^r1.19]: See pp. 152f. and 157ff. of the present work.

[^r1.20]: Admittedly indifference constitutes the last stage of the logic of being, to which essence as reflection joins. But this transition does not succeed. Right at the beginning of the logic of essence, as Henrich rightly asserts (Henrich 1978, 230f.), there is found a regression behind the result of the logic of being. But the developed concept of essence, which then arises through the logic of shine, can no longer be conceived as an indifference. See footnote 61.

[^r1.21]: Precisely therein it becomes clear that Hegel’s then system-concept is doomed to failure and that these system drafts—contrary to their program of separation—make necessary the unification of logic and metaphysics (Cf. Schäfer 2001, 94, 152ff.).

[^r1.22]: In analogy thereto being at the beginning of the logic of essence in the *Science of Logic* is designated as shine (esp. GW XI, 246).

[^r1.23]: In this sense it becomes understandable why cognizing, which has its systematic place in metaphysics, is determined as “the sublating of logic itself, as of dialectic, or of idealism” (GW VII, 127). The relation in which a determination is posited in relation to another and which thereby falls into a dialectical movement belongs to logic. Cf. the first sentence of the Metaphysics: “Logic ceases where the relation ceases” (GW VII, 126). The propositions of logic are thus here not—as later—so conceived that they also then express relations when they have metaphysical contents.

[^r1.24]: By contrast in the *Science of Logic* the tautological proposition is rather held for expression of reflection-into-itself. Düsing overlooks this difference (Düsing 1984, 329). Cf. GW XI, 262ff.; also below pp. 197f.

[^r1.25]: Cf. GW XI, 26f.

[^r1.26]: Cf. GW XI, 269. This problematic will be pursued further below, see pp. 172ff.

[^r1.27]: Hegel determined the task of metaphysics to the effect “that this indifferent other \[becomes] a different for the same, determines itself only as the negation of the same, whereby cognizing, the solely positive, becomes the true In-itself” (GW VII, 126). At the beginning of metaphysics the stage was reached at which the Other ‘for us’ is no longer an Other. The task of metaphysics is then to make the Other accessible for cognizing itself.

[^r1.28]: Pöggeler 1966, 365; Fulda 1965, the same 1966.

[^r1.29]: Fulda 1965, 142ff.; Pöggeler 1966, 362. Pöggeler’s proposal of the correspondence between the composition of the *Phenomenology of Spirit* and the system-concept in the System Drafts III was also discussed in the literature.
