Düsing relates this system-sketch for speculative philosophy both to the Logic and Metaphysics of 1804/05 and to the Phenomenology (Düsing 1984, 157ff.); cf. Schäfer 2001, 165ff., esp. 170.

[^r1.30]: Pöggeler 1966, 362.

[^r1.31]: Pöggeler 1966, 362.

[^r1.32]: “a) speculative philosophy absolute Being, that becomes other (relation) Life and Cognizing — and knowing knowing, Spirit, Knowing of Spirit of itself” (GW VII, 286).

[^r1.33]: GW IX, 72f.

[^r1.34]: Letters I, p. 230. This is also cited by Pöggeler 1966, 362 and Fulda 2003, 96.

[^r1.35]: Hegel begins the Logic for the Middle Class with the doctrine of consciousness, which he let precede the logic, which lets us conjecture that he still ascribes to the Phenomenology in this period a systematic introductory function. That Hegel however lets his Encyclopedia instruction for the upper class begin directly with the logic seems confusing. But he does not hold fast to that introductory conception, even if he says: “Philosophical science presupposes that the separation of the certainty of oneself and of truth has already been sublated, or that spirit no longer belongs to appearance. Science does not seek truth, but is in the truth and the truth itself” (Upper Class Philosophical Preparatory Sciences. Philosophical Encyclopedia. Dictations 1808/09, §2, GW X, 61). The three parts into which Hegel here divides “the whole of science” do not contain the Phenomenology: “The whole of science divides itself into the three parts: 1.) logic, 2. the science of nature, 3.) the science of spirit” (ibid., §3; cf. Kozu 1999, 24).

[^r1.36]: Upper Class Philosophical Preparatory Sciences. Philosophical Encyclopedia. Dictations 1808/09, §§ 28–42, GW X, 65ff.

[^r1.37]: See: Chapter 6 A.

[^r1.38]: Hegel here reduces dialectic only to the doctrine of antinomies and leaves the paralogism and the fallacious inference of the proof of God out of account.

[^r1.39]: Cf. Chapter 6 A.

[^r1.40]: For this logic-instruction Hegel used the subjective logic of the doctrine of spirit from the preceding school year (see the editorial report in GW X, 935).

[^r1.41]: See the reports of Hegel concerning his objects of instruction (GW X, 879, HW IV, 295). Cf. the editorial report in GW X, 916.

[^r1.42]: This lets us recognize that Hegel has altered the concept that is to be inferred from the transmission of his instruction in the school year 1808/09. The questions that have been discussed in previous research concerning Hegel’s development in the Nuremberg period, for example whether these alterations concern the system-concept itself and whether thereby also the systematic function of the *Phenomenology of Spirit* has altered, must here be left to further research. Cf. Kozu 1999.

[^r1.43]: On the basis of these experiences Hegel seems to have come to the conclusion that logic is not an appropriate object of instruction for the pupils of the lower class. He has since then no longer lectured on logic in the lower class. See the private expert opinion to Niethammer of 1812 (GW X, 823).

[^r1.44]: The manuscript for this instruction is only fragmentarily transmitted; the first 28 paragraphs are lacking.

[^r1.45]: The heading “The Concept” for the third part of the Logic for the Middle Class (1810/11) has been subsequently added (GW X, 181, cf. Editorial Report, GW X, 948). But as general object of subjective logic the concept was here determined (ibid.).

[^r1.46]: Still to be mentioned is that the third main point of the logic of being here constitutes not—as still in the doctrine of spirit and the Encyclopedia of the school year 1808/09—infinity, but measure. Moreover the wholly new section “End” has entered as the second of the logic of the concept between the concept and the Idea.

[^r1.47]: In the *Science of Logic* the Kantian antinomies with the exception of the third are taken up only in the Remarks: The first in GW XI, 147ff and GW XXI, 228ff.; the second in GW XI, 113ff. and GW XXI, 179ff.; and the third in GW XII, 157f. Moreover there results a close connection between the fourth antinomy, which relates to the categories of modality, with the section on ‘Actuality’ in the *Science of Logic*.

[^r1.48]: GW X, 442. Hegel continues: “The doctrine of the two former constitutes objective logic in metaphysics, the doctrine of the concepts the proper or subjective logic” (ibid.).

[^r1.49]: Hegel continues: “The former is being in a determinateness, as limit; the second essence in a determination that is mediated through a presupposition of an Other” (GW X, 181). Cf. the belonging dictation for this instruction (GW X, 246).

[^r1.50]: At the beginning of the logic of essence in this manuscript the determinations that are supposed to constitute the object of the logic of essence are called “determinations in the form of reflections.” But in the subsequent revision of the transcripts to this instruction (according to Rameil 1814/15) this word is replaced by “the determinations of reflection.”

[^r1.51]: This expert opinion is said to have been added to Hegel’s letter to Niethammer of 23 October 1812. Cf. the editorial report to this writing (GW X, 1020ff).


## For part 2.2

[^r2.1]: In what follows, in citation indications the number before the slash refers to the page number of GW XI (first edition of the logic of being), and that after to GW XXI (second edition of the logic of being).

[^r2.2]: Cf. GW XI, 31f. 45/66 and the beginning of the logic of essence.

[^r2.3]: Thereby I agree with Theunissen, who asserts this against Henrich (Theunissen 1994, 344f. and 358f.).

[^r2.4]: Thus essence is namely determined against determination. Around this problem circles the beginning of the logic of essence. More precisely in the second section of this chapter.

[^r2.5]: E.g. the logic of being-for-itself like that of infinity already contains reflexive self-relation. They are not simply to be subsumed under the logic of passing-over. Against this Iber holds fast to the principal difference between reflection and affirmative infinity (Iber 1990, 162). But he finds the ground therein that affirmative infinity still has being underlying it, which however is doubtful.

[^r2.6]: The answer to this question, which may have floated before the readers of the logic of essence of 1813, Hegel first gives in a paragraph of ‘a. Something and its Other.’ See footnote 38.

[^r2.7]: Esp. Heidegger GA V, p. 451.

[^r2.8]: “Our discussions accordingly teach the reality (i.e. the objective validity) of space with respect to everything that can outwardly come before us as object” (A27f./B43f.). The reality of space and of time in that sense he calls empirical reality (A28/B44, cf. A35/B52f.).

[^r2.9]: Space and time however have transcendental ideality, “with respect to things when they are considered through reason in themselves, i.e. without taking regard to the constitution of our sensibility” (ibid.).

[^r2.10]: On this below: 1.2.

[^r2.11]: Kant says in the ‘Anticipations of Perception’: “They \[appearances as objects of perception] thus contain besides intuition still the matters for some object in general (whereby something existing in space or in time is represented), i.e. the real of sensation, thus merely subjective representation, of which one can only become conscious that the subject is affected, and which one relates to an object in general, within themselves” (*KrV* B 207f.). But whether something exists or is given one cannot according to Kant anticipate (A167f./B208f., cf. also the postulate of necessity A226ff./B279ff.). Therefore the anticipation here must be restricted to that of intensive magnitude (A166ff./B207ff.).

[^r2.12]: Of course the reality of space and of time from the transcendental view must be distinguished from that of the content of experience, since with the former what is at issue is only the form, with the latter the matter. They obviously have another status of actuality. But in comparison with the traditional significance the two can be thought together under the concept of actuality.

[^r2.13]: ‘Outside us’ is here understood in the empirical sense, not as thing in itself. In this sense Kant in the Refutation of Idealism (in the second edition) attempted to prove the determinate being of objects outside us. Heimsoeth has made clear this distinction of the significance of ‘outside us’ with the Latin expressions ‘*praeter nos*’ and ‘*extra nos*’ (Heimsoeth 1966, 135ff.). Cf. Okochi 2005.

[^r2.14]: Heidegger GA XXIV, 46. Cf. GA V, 451.

[^r2.15]: Concerning the concept of reality in Leibniz, Wolff and Baumgarten we have spoken above in connection with Kant’s real opposition (See the second chapter). Fischer rightly relates the third pair of concepts of reflection, agreement and conflict, to this Ideal of God (Fischer 1909, 493).

[^r2.16]: Kant is inclined to understand the copula “is” in the judgment as being. This inclination favors Heidegger’s interpretation, which wants to find in the copula a trace of original being. But existential sentences must be strictly distinguished from predicative sentences.

[^r2.17]: Hegel later summarizes this thus, “that Kant under existence understands determinate determinate being, whereby something enters into the context of the entire experience, i.e. into the determination of an other-being and into the relation to Other” (GW XI, 324.).

[^r2.18]: “For in general only first in determinateness does the real difference begin: indeterminate being and nothing do not yet have it on them, but only the meant difference” (GW XI, 47).

[^r2.19]: “The proposition \[that being and non-being are the same] contains the pure abstractions of being and nothing; but the application makes a determinate being and determinate nothing out of them. But of determinate being there is, as said, here not talk. A determinate, a finite being is such a one that relates itself to another” (GW XI, 46).

[^r2.20]: This word, which is later used by Heidegger as designation of metaphysics, stems from Kant. Thereto: Henrich 1960, 1.

[^r2.21]: Kant says further that this principle does not rest alone on the principle of contradiction, while the principle of determinability, which is supposed to hold not for the thing, but only for the concept, rests only on it (A571f./B599f.). From this it results that he understands the principle beyond school metaphysics, which supports it only on the principle of contradiction, in his own manner, especially since what is at issue thereby is not the object of the pure understanding or the thing in itself, which according to the amphiboly chapter is supposed to rest only on the principle of contradiction.

[^r2.22]: Cf. “Namely everything that we encounter in singular things is determined in every way: and precisely hereby something becomes a singular thing, because it is determined both in all that which it has within itself, and in that which falls to it externally with respect to other things” (*Deutsche Logik*, § 27); “*Principium individuationis est omnimoda determinatio eorum, quae enti actu insunt*” (*Ontologia*, §229).

[^r2.23]: “*Per Principium individuationis intelligitur ratio sufficiens intrinseca individui. Scholasticis idem venit nomine Haecceitatis. Quamobrem per principium individuationis intelligitur, cur ens aliquod sit singulare (§56)*” (*Ontologia*, §228).

[^r2.24]: “*Complexus omnium determinationum in ente compossibilium est omnimoda eius determinatio. Hinc ens aut est omnimode determinatum, aut minus, §. 10. Illud est singulare, (individuum,) hoc universale*” (*Metaphysica*, §148).

[^r2.25]: Cf. A572/B600 (Anm.).

[^r2.26]: It is to be remarked that reality in the table of categories corresponds to the positive judgment, negation to the negative (A80/B106). But this does not in Kant exclude that the realities can conflict against one another. See the first chapter.

[^r2.27]: That is also the point at which Hegel criticizes Spinoza’s substance-metaphysics. Insofar as the latter first finds no differences in his substance and then introduces the attributes and modes, the negativity is lacking which makes possible the differentiation in the substance (GW XI, 376ff.).

[^r2.28]: “Being-in-itself and being-for-other are the moments or inner differences of determinate being.” (GW XI, 63); “[I]t \[Determinate being] is determinate being, insofar as it has determined itself as being-in-itself and as being-for-other, and is the unity of them as its moments. As reflected determinate being it is reality” (ibid.).

[^r2.29]: “[B]ut in reality as quality with the accent of being a being one, it is hidden that it contains the determinateness, thus also the negation” (GW XXI, 99). “Reality is quality, determinate being; thereby it contains the moment of the Negative, and is alone thereby the determinate that it is” (GW XXI, 100). In the first edition the concept negation does not appear in connection with quality or reality, but first later in connection with alteration. There it is also said that “negation \[is] the true Real and being-in-itself” (GW XI, 77).

[^r2.30]: It is also to be found in Jacobi’s letters on Spinoza, p. 21.

[^r2.31]: “Of the Paralogisms of Pure Reason” (A341/B399ff.). A direct critique of this paralogism is exercised by Hegel in the chapter on the Idea in the logic of the concept (GW XII, 192ff.). See also the concluding chapter of the present work.

[^r2.32]: As older exception: McTaggart 1910.

[^r2.33]: For the logic of reflection: Henrich 1978, Hackenesch 1987; for the logic of essence: Iber 1990, Wölfle 1994, Schmidt 1997; for the whole *Science of Logic*: Fink-Eitel 1978, Hartmann 1999; for the encyclopedic logic Lakebrink 1979; Hackenesch 2000. No commentary, but an overall presentation is given by Düsing 1976.

[^r2.34]: Henrich directs his commentary especially toward that thesis of the *Phenomenology of Spirit*: Substance is at the same time to be thought as subject (Henrich, 1978, 204ff.).

[^r2.35]: With the concept of the concept the structure of self-relating negativity, which is already reached at the beginning of the logic of essence, namely in the logic of reflection, plays a great role. Hegel in the second edition of the logic of being relies more on the terminology of the logic of reflection. The logic of essence that fits this version of the logic of being Hegel could no longer compose. The problem of method of the whole logic, to which Jaeschke (1978) rightly relates the logic of reflection, goes beyond our theme.

[^r2.36]: Henrich 1978; Iber 1990; Wetzel 1971; Wölfle 1994; against this McTaggart 1910.

[^r2.37]: In the Encyclopedia (1830) Hegel says of the logic of essence: “This (the most difficult) part of logic contains mainly the categories of metaphysics and of the sciences in general” (Enc. 1830, § 114).

[^r2.38]: Therefore Hegel could intercalate in the 2nd chapter B. a. “Something and an Other” of the second edition a paragraph where the being-logical “being-for-other” and the essence-logical “positedness” are compared. On account of its importance let the whole paragraph be cited: “Being-in-itself has first being-for-other as its opposing moment; but to the same is also set over against positedness; in this expression there indeed also lies being-for-other, but it contains determinately the already occurred bending-back of that which is not in itself into that which is its being-in-itself, wherein it is positive. Being-in-itself is ordinarily to be taken as an abstract manner of expressing the concept; positing properly first falls into the sphere of essence, of objective reflection; the ground posits that which is grounded through it; the cause still more brings forth an effect, a determinate being whose independence is immediately negated and which has the sense on it of having its matter, its being, in an other. In the sphere of being determinate being only proceeds from becoming, or with Something an Other, with the finite the infinite is posited, but the finite does not bring forth the infinite, does not posit the same. In the sphere of being the self-determining of the concept itself is only first in itself, so it is called a passing-over; also the reflecting determinations of being, such as Something and Other, or the finite and the infinite, although they essentially point to one another, or are as being-for-other, count as qualitative as subsisting for themselves; the Other is, the finite counts just as much as immediately being and standing firm for itself as the infinite; their sense appears as completed also without their Other. The Positive and Negative by contrast, cause and effect, however much they are also taken as being isolated, at the same time have no sense without one another; there is present on them themselves their shining into one another, the shining of its Other in each. — In the different circles of determination and especially in the progress of the exposition, or more closely in the progress of the concept to its exposition, it is a main matter always well to distinguish this, what is still in itself and what is posited, how the determinations are as in the concept and how they are as posited or as being-for-other. This is a difference that belongs only to dialectical development, which metaphysical philosophizing, under which also the critical belongs, does not know; the definitions of metaphysics, like its presuppositions, distinctions and conclusions, want only to assert and bring forth Being and indeed being-in-itself” (GW XXI, 109f.).

[^r2.39]: Enc. 1830, §§ 84 and 112.

[^r2.40]: Jaeschke distinguishes external reflection as the second shape of Hegelian reflection from outer reflection, which is a mental act. This distinction contributes to clarifying the problem of logical method in connection with the concept of reflection (Jaeschke 1978).

[^r2.41]: Transcendental reflection is however not to be held for a reflecting power of judgment. For it orders singular representations under two concepts, *noumenon* or *phaenomenon*, which over against those count as more general.

[^r2.42]: Cf. GW XI, 247.

[^r2.43]: John Locke, *An Essay Concerning Human Understanding*, Book II, Chap. I, § 4, p. 105.

[^r2.44]: Broecken finds a forerunner of Kantian transcendental reflection therein that in Locke the distinction of the two sources of experience itself belongs in reflection (Broecken 1970, 33). Cf. Locke, *An Essay*, II, XXI, §5.

[^r2.45]: Reuter 1989, 34.

[^r2.46]: Broecken emphasizes the significance of comparison in Locke’s reflection for Kantian reflection (Broecken 1970, 36ff.). Criticizing this, Reuter points to its connection with the concept “discerning” (Reuter 1989, 37).

[^r2.47]: Kant distinguishes inner experience through time from pure apperception as self-referential self-consciousness.

[^r2.48]: Leibniz, *Nouveaux Essais sur l’entendement par l’auteur du système de l’harmonie préétablie*, G. V, 45.

[^r2.49]: Ibid., and also livre 2, chapitre 1.

[^r2.50]: This is to be recognized in the following sentence: “*on pourrait peut-être ajouter que les bêtes ont de la perception, et qu’il n’est point nécessaire qu’ils aient de la pensée, c’est-à-dire qu’ils aient de la réflexion ou ce qui en peut être l’objet*” (G V, 121). By contrast for Locke thinking or the understanding (*the understanding*) is a faculty of perception: “The power of Perception is that which we call the Understanding” (Locke, *An essay*, II, XXI; § 5). Moreover Leibniz knows that reflection produces the problem of an infinite regress (G V, 108).

[^r2.51]: “*Menti tribuitur Apperceptio, quatenus perceptionis suae sibi conscia est*” (§ 25).

[^r2.52]: “*Dum super rebus perceptis reflectimus, eas quoque inter se conferimus*” (*Psych. empir.* § 260).

[^r2.53]: This lets one conjecture that he learned this significance of ‘reflection’ through his engagement with the *Nouveaux Essais*.

[^r2.54]: In the introductory chapter of the logic of the concept Hegel sees in Kantian apperception a model of his concept, which unifies subjectivity and objectivity (GW XII, 17f.). But in the Idea chapter Hegel criticizes the critique of the paralogism with which Kant forbids the application of the categories to the I (GW XII, 193ff.). See the concluding chapter.

[^r2.55]: Therefore I do not share Theunissen’s conception, which proceeds from the assumption that the concept of shine already creeps into the Determinate Being chapter (Theunissen 1980, 187ff.).

[^r2.56]: But this correspondence does not mean that the presentation of the logic is one of the process of cognition. Cf. Jaeschke 1978.

[^r2.57]: Thereto Theunissen 1994, 320; critically Iber (1990, 29), who by contrast relates ‘recollection’ to Schelling (loc. cit., 32); Iber as well as Düsing also point to Aristotle’s *ti ên einai* (Iber ibid.; Düsing 1984, 342).

[^r2.58]: Cf. Hegel’s interpretation of Plato in the Lectures on the History of Philosophy (*Vorlesungen*, VIII, 14f./HW XIX, 43ff.). Hegel there speaks of these two significances of the word “recollection,” but he also complains that Plato often uses the word in the empirical sense (*Vorlesungen* VII, 14/HW XIX, 45).

[^r2.59]: Kant also appeals to the Platonic doctrine of the Idea and even of recollection when he introduces the concept of ideas (A313ff./B370ff).

[^r2.60]: That is the ground for the fact that reflection contains external reflection, which belongs to a sublated stage. On the concept ‘self-determining’ Wetzel 1971.

[^r2.61]: Concerning the problem that the logical course here seems to fall back behind what was reached at the end of the logic of being, there has been discussion since the above-named commentary of Henrich. The latter endeavors to show that essence in the double sense, as whole and as part, is the successor of being (Henrich 1978, 235f). Cf. Wölfle 1994, 119.

[^r2.62]: “In the sphere of being, determinate being was being that had the negation on it, and being was the immediate ground and \[the] element of this negation, which therefore itself was the immediate” (GW XI, 255).

[^r2.63]: “The Other is here \[in reflection as essence] thus not being with the negation or limit, but the negation with the negation. But the First against this Other, the Immediate or Being, is only this equality itself of the negation with itself, the negated negation, absolute negativity” (GW XI, 249). Henrich finds in this course a shift of significance of immediacy. His assertion rests on the doctrine of the change of significance in the theory of science (Henrich 1978, 309ff, esp. 311f.). It seems plausible to me that a change of theory occurs in the transition from being to essence that requires the shift of significance of immediacy. But in Hegel the succeeding theory is understood as truth of the preceding. Thus the self-relation of negativity is the truth of mere immediacy.

[^r2.64]: “[I]t \[essence] is thus the identical unity of absolute negativity and of immediacy” (GW XI, 248).

[^r2.65]: “[T]his, to be the negation of a Nothing, constitutes being” (GW XI, 250).

[^r2.66]: At a somewhat later place: “Determinate being is only positedness; this is the proposition of essence concerning determinate being” (GW XI, 255f.).

[^r2.67]: “It \[Being] is merely the position of a thing” (*KrV*, A598/B626).

[^r2.68]: “If I now take the subject (God) together with all its predicates […] and say: God is, or there is a God, I posit no new predicate to the concept of God, but only the subject in itself with all its predicates, and indeed the object in relation to my concept” (*KrV*, A599/B627).

[^r2.69]: Fichte, *Foundation of the Entire Wissenschaftslehre* (1794), in: Fichte’s Works, ed. by I. H. Fichte vol. I, 99

[^r2.70]: “But it \[positedness] is at the same time determined as Negative, as immediately against one, thus against an Other. Thus reflection is determined” (GW XI, 252).

[^r2.71]: Henrich finds this external reflection superfluous precisely for this reason, because it repeats that position that is supposed already to have been overcome (Henrich 1978, 291). But it is necessary that Hegel here places external reflection. For if reflection does not include that position, it must unfold this within itself, because otherwise the relation of both would remain external and real. Moreover the presentation of external reflection is not equal to that of determinate being, because in the latter reflection was indicated as the Third, but could not really be thematized. In essence first reflection itself is thematized.

[^r2.72]: “The self-sameness of reflection, which has the Negative only as Negative, as sublated or posited, is it which gives the same subsistence” (GW XI, 256).

## For part 3.1

[^s1.1]: This lets us conjecture that he would have written a rather strongly modified second edition of the doctrine of essence of the *Science of Logic*.
[^s1.2]: Cf. Duque 2002, 143f.
[^s1.3]: Iber (1990) divides the chapter into six stages: 1. identity and difference, 2. diversity, 3. first and second form of opposition, 4. third form of opposition, which we shall call “proper opposition,” 5. contradiction and 6. ground. This division itself is convincing if one aims at a precise analysis of Hegel’s text itself. Our division however appeals to the historico-ideological context.

[^s1.4]: This assertion of Hegel’s Düsing traces back to Plato’s *Sophist* (Düsing 1984, 218).

[^s1.5]: Iber sees that Hegel here takes up again the dictum in the *Difference* essay “the identity of identity and of diversity” (Iber 1990, 303f.). Indeed the commonality of the task between the *Difference* essay and the later logic is thereby to be recognized; but the resolution of this demand is with the latter wholly other than with the former, which appeals to intellectual intuition.

[^s1.6]: Cf. Kruck 2002, 131.
[^s1.7]: In this I agree with Wölfle and Iber, who set identity and absolute difference into one (Wölfle 1994, 230ff., Iber 1990, 269ff.).
[^s1.8]: Hegel continues: “This is to be considered as the essential nature of reflection and as determinate original ground of all activity and self-movement” (GW XI, 266)

[^s1.9]: “Identity falls apart in itself into diversity” (GW XI, 267).
[^s1.10]: Cf. Iber 1990, 336.
[^s1.11]: “The other of determinate being has immediate being for its ground, in which the negative consists. In reflection however identity with itself, reflected immediacy, constitutes the consistence of the negative and the indifference of the same” (GW XI, 267).

[^s1.12]: This three-poled structure is characteristic of diversity as external reflection. First in the logic of essence is the logic of the passing-over of the logic of being grasped not as two-, but as three-poled, which marks the progress. Wölfle misses this when he criticizes this three-poledness (cf. Wölfle 1994, 235).
[^s1.13]: “This more closely considered, both, identity and difference, as has just been determined, are reflections” (GW XI, 267).

[^s1.14]: “[I]t [positedness] is negation as negation; hereby in itself indeed the self-relating negation and reflection into itself; but only in itself” (GW XI, 268).
[^s1.15]: Hegel later says this clearly: “Likeness and unlikeness constituted the side of positedness, against the compared or the diverse” (GW XI, 270). This is however missed when one understands the diversity of likeness and of unlikeness as derived directly from the difference between reflection-into-itself and positedness (cf. e.g. Fink-Eitel 1978, 106; Iber 1990, 342f.). More on this later.

[^s1.16]: “Reflection in itself is identity, but determined to be indifferent against difference, not not to have difference at all, but to relate itself as identical with itself against it; it is diversity. It is the identity that has so reflected itself into itself that it is properly the one reflection of the two moments into itself; both are reflections into themselves. Identity is this one reflection of both, which has the difference only as an indifferent in it, and is diversity in general” (GW XI, 267).
[^s1.17]: “External reflection by contrast is the determinate difference of the same not as absolute reflection into itself, but as determination, against which the reflection that is in itself is indifferent; its two moments, identity and difference themselves, are thus externally posited, not determinations that are in and for themselves” (GW XI, 268).

[^s1.18]: Insofar as this is so it is clear that Hegel here with likeness does not mean only self-equality, as K. Hartmann asserts (Hartmann 1998, 183).
[^s1.19]: “[T]he determinate difference is the negated absolute difference” (GW XI, 268).

[^s1.20]: “For both are determinations of difference; they are relations to one another, the one, to be what the other is not; like is not unlike, and unlike is not like; and both essentially have this relation, and outside it no significance” (GW XI, 269).

[^s1.21]: Insofar as this is so it appears to me that Iber overestimates the significance of this passage when he already here sees opposition prepared (Iber 1990, 356).

[^s1.22]: Cf. Fink-Eitel 1978, 107.

[^s1.23]: Hegel himself however unfolds this logical process too meagerly for us to be able to follow it without supplements. From this one cannot however yet—as Düsing—draw the consequence that Hegel’s demonstration of the necessary transition from diversity to opposition fails (Düsing 1984, 223). Cf. Wölfle 1994, 238.
[^s1.24]: Wölfle in his attempt at reconstruction of the *Science of Logic* deletes opposition, because he thinks that “opposition and contradiction do not at all distinguish themselves in their structural moments and their logical mediation” (Wölfle 1994, 226). That holds insofar as opposition already, as we shall presently see, implicitly has the structure of contradiction, thus already contains all factors that constitute contradiction. Yet both stages are still distinguished, because first in contradiction it is clearly established that each moment of difference (and now of contradiction) destroys its own independence through the respective own determination. Wölfle misrecognizes this feature specific to contradiction.
[^s1.25]: In the Encyclopedia this proper opposing appears directly (Enc. 1830, § 119).
[^s1.26]: According to Wolff Hegel probably did not engage directly with Kant’s essay on negative magnitudes, but knew the theory only through the then philosophical or natural-scientific discussion (cf. Wolff 1981, 81).

[^s1.27]: Iber also lets the second stage count as real opposition (Iber 1990, 403f., fn. 13 and 410)
[^s1.28]: This reflection was already in the chapter on diversity called “[the] reflection that has alienated itself” (GW XI, 269).

[^s1.29]: Wolff 1981, 119ff.; Iber 1990, 402ff.
[^s1.30]: Cf. Wolff 1981, 91.
[^s1.31]: This will later be investigated still more precisely, see below 186ff.

[^s1.32]: This interchange of the two poles appears in the Understanding chapter of the *Phenomenology of Spirit* as “the inverted world.” Hegel uses examples such as the south and north pole of the magnet, the oxygen pole and hydrogen pole in electricity or the lawful deed and the crime (GW IX, 97). According to Gadamer the first two examples illustrate “merely the reversibility of these relations, thus their dialectical character,” while the last is “an outstanding documentation on a favorite theme of Hegel’s” (Gadamer 1980, 42, 44).
[^s1.33]: Wolff (1981) has especially laid a focus upon the mathematical examples in the Remark. Iber (1990, 406ff.) joins himself to this and also emphasizes the exact correspondence of the division of the main text with that of the Remark.

[^s1.34]: When Hegel distinguishes “opposed [magnitudes] in general” and the “real or indifferent [magnitude],” he proceeds in an appropriate manner, yet he does not always remain consistent thereby.
[^s1.35]: Cf. the “merely opposed [magnitudes] in general” (GW XI, 277).

[^s1.36]: “Not only however are the opposed magnitudes on the one hand merely opposed in general, on the other hand real or indifferent. But although the quantum itself is the indifferently limited being, there nevertheless also occurs in it the positive in itself and the negative in itself” (GW XI, 277).
[^s1.37]: See the 1st chapter of this work.

[^s1.38]: Cf. “so much debt, so much less wealth, and so much wealth is present, so much of the debts sublates itself” (GW XI, 276). See the 2nd chapter of this work.
[^s1.39]: Wolff designates this “a” as “|a|” (Wolff 1981, 91).

[^s1.40]: “[I]ts positedness or the relation to the other in a unity that they themselves are not, is taken back into each” (GW XI, 274).
[^s1.41]: “In themselves they [the positive and the negative] are it [positive and negative—likewise], insofar as one abstracts from their excluding relation to another, and they are taken only according to their determination. […] Something is in itself positive outside the relation to the negative; and something is in itself negative, outside the relation to the positive” (GW XI, 275).

[^s1.42]: “More closely this emerges with multiplication and division” (GW XI, 277).

[^s1.43]: GW XI 58; GW XXI, 94f.
[^s1.44]: Also in the Encyclopedia (1830) it runs: “Both [the positive and the negative] are thus the posited contradiction” (§ 120). In the greater Logic however Hegel expresses this otherwise: “But the positive is only in itself this contradiction, the negative by contrast the posited contradiction” (GW XI, 280).

[^s1.45]: “[B]oth are in themselves the same. Both are it also for themselves” (Enc. 1830, §120).

[^s1.46]: Hegel refers to Remark 1 to becoming (GW XI, 47ff. /GW XXI, 73ff.).

[^s1.47]: “In speculative logic mere understanding-logic is contained and can at once be made from that; nothing is needed for this but to leave out from it the dialectical and the rational; thus it becomes what ordinary logic is” (Enc. 1830, § 82). Cf. Wolff 1981, 158; Hartmann 1999, 25; Wölfle 1994, 218. Also in the Preface to the second edition of the *Science of Logic*: “Therefore the logical science, in that it treats the determinations of thought that in general run through our spirit instinct-like and unconsciously, and even in that they enter into language, remain non-objective, unnoticed, will also be the reconstruction of those which have been raised out through reflection and fixed by it as subjective forms external to the material and content” (GW XXI, 17.).
[^s1.48]: After we have investigated the development of the logic of essence in the Nuremberg period, it has become clear that the propositions for Hegel are “no incidental theme,” as Iber indeed rightly—but without taking into account the developmental history—asserts (Iber 1990, 244). It remains to ask why Hegel prefers the form of the determinations of reflection to the form of the proposition.
[^s1.49]: Hegel here distinguishes proposition and judgment, which belongs to the logic of the concept (GW XI, 259; cf. also GW XXI, 55).

[^s1.50]: E.g. Hegel says: “Everything is, Everything has a determinate being etc. or Everything has a quality, quantity etc.” (GW XI, 259).
[^s1.51]: Hegel says there: “The category is according to its etymology and the definition of Aristotle that which is said, asserted of the existent” (GW XI, 259). It is noteworthy that there suddenly there is talk of ‘categories,’ although he properly speaks of the ‘propositions.’ This lets us conjecture that he wants to say that the propositions are replaced through the doctrine of categories and that it is his logic that presents the true doctrine of categories.
[^s1.52]: When Kang criticizes Iber because the latter sees the form of the proposition in Hegel positively assessed, he rather misunderstands him. Iber designates the reference of the determinations of reflection ‘as axioms’ to the substrate as shine and asserts directly after the passage that Kang refers to that the determinations of reflection constitute the “critique of a substrate-metaphysical understanding of the proposition,” which can be traced back to Aristotle (Kang 1999, 180, n. 71, Iber 1990, 262f.).
[^s1.53]: Hegel is of the view that the content of the proposition is the relation itself, while the content in the judgment is transposed into the predicate. But the form of the proposition demands the subject in order to express this content. The propositions in comparison with the judgment lie near to the determinations of reflection, in that with them what is at issue is the relation, but this form is not suitable for the presentation of this relation (GW XI, 259).
[^s1.54]: Hartmann doubts that the logical propositions really contradict one another and asserts that this assertion first becomes intelligible when the propositions are held for ontological principles (Hartmann 1998, 176).
[^s1.55]: Hegel treats the principle of identity and of contradiction in the Remark to identity, the principle of diversity or of the identity of the indiscernible in the Remark to diversity. The principle of the excluded third is treated in the Encyclopedias in opposing and in the *Science of Logic* in contradiction, although it contentually belongs to opposition.
[^s1.56]: Also in the earlier period the two propositions are treated together, cf. Jena System Drafts II (GW VII, 130; in the Nuremberg period: Logic for the Middle Class (1810/11) § 36 (GW X, 159, 228). But in § 33 of the Encyclopedia for the Upper Class (1808/09) only the principle of identity is mentioned (GW X, 66)).

[^s1.57]: This proposition Hegel formulates as follows: “A cannot at once be A and not-A” (GW XI, 265).
[^s1.58]: Kang overlooks that Hegel asserts that the traditional principle of identity “A is A,” grasped speculatively, already expresses this movement. (Kang 1999, 186): “The principle of identity itself however contains, as was shown in it, the movement of reflection, identity as vanishing of otherness” (GW XI, 265). The proposition proposed by Kang by contrast does not catch up with this ‘vanishing of otherness.’
[^s1.59]: On this Schmidt 1997, 62; cf. pp. 120f. of the present work.
[^s1.60]: See below 207ff.

[^s1.61]: See above 76f.
[^s1.62]: With the principle of identity: A=A “A” was still indeterminate. In it there was no reference to something other. In the propositions of diversity however it is shown: “A is a diverse, thus A is also not A; or A is unlike an other, so it is not A in general, but rather a determinate A” (GW XI, 270).
[^s1.63]: In contrast to Leibniz Hegel holds a tautology for nonsensical. Cf. Ch. II.
[^s1.64]: Hegel in his lectures on the history of philosophy brings his esteem for this principle of Leibniz to expression (HW XX, 255). See: Schmidt 1997, 67. But the critique that Hegel at this place exercises upon external reflection also holds of the proposition as Leibniz develops it.

[^s1.65]: Hegel says in the chapter on quantity: “[T]he many constitute a number, One two, One ten, One hundred etc. [Paragraph] Number thus has as its moments the unit and the amount, and is itself the unity of the same. The former constitutes the moment of continuity, the latter of discretion, as they, in the quantum, are as number” (GW XI, 126; cf. moreover GW XXI, 194).

[^s1.66]: “There also lies in it the dissolution and nullity of the principle of diversity. Two things are not perfectly equal; thus they are like and unlike at once; like already therein that they are things or two in general, for each is a thing and a one just as much as the other, each thus the same as the other; unlike however they are through the assumption” (GW XI, 271).

[^s1.67]: Kant never mentions such a subject of reflection, yet there must be one.
[^s1.68]: “To the contrary and contradictory concepts […] there underlies the determination of reflection of diversity and of opposing” (GW XII, 46).

[^s1.69]: Kang judges this statement of Hegel’s as false (Kang 1999, 129).

[^s1.70]: See especially the third Remark to contradiction (GW XI, 284f.).

[^s1.71]: Insofar as this is so Wolff and Iber are right (Wolff 1981, 102, 104; Iber 1994, 374ff.). Cf. Kang 1999, 205.

[^s1.72]: Iber 1994, 403. Kang misunderstands Iber’s complicated doubled division of the course of opposition (Kang 1999, 194).

[^s1.73]: According to the interpretation of Fink-Eitel the development from contrary to contradictory opposing is unfolded in the opposition chapter itself. But thereby opposition, which is still only contrary, is divided into four: Those three shapes of opposition are all still contrary—which is not convincing (Fink-Eitel 1978, 116ff.).

[^s1.74]: Wolff 1981, 113.

[^s1.75]: Kang rightly says that the turn of phrase “the reflection-logical substrate,” which Wolff first used and which e.g. was taken over by Iber, is “a problematic word-combination.” Thereby however it also becomes clear that he himself has fallen into that misunderstanding against which Wolff precisely wanted to guard, in that he further formulates: “But in Hegel there is no talk at all of such a substrate, of which Wolff speaks. ‘A reflection’ or ‘mediation’ is nothing other than the opposition-relation itself, which presupposes no substrate” (Kang 1999, 193, fn. 80; 197, fn. 84). Kang understands under substrate something that was supposed to precede the relation; but Wolff rightly recognizes that this ‘substrate’ is first a product of reflection (Wolff 1981, 134). For this misunderstanding Wolff himself is nevertheless not entirely without guilt, since he so emphasizes the word ‘substrate.’ On the contrary it belongs to the essential aspects of the presentation of the logic of essence that what is assumed as substrate by external reflection is properly only a product of reflection. The difficulty of the logic of essence, which Hegel himself admits, springs precisely from the fact that the logic must not proceed from a substrate (cf. Rohs 1969, 140). Kang however also overlooks that a substrate comes into view in diversity or in the second stage of opposition, which both themselves belong to external reflection and therefore to the falling-back into the logic of being. Such a substrate should rather be called “being-logical.” Wolff fell into this error because he oriented himself by the example in the Remark, to whose inappropriateness I have already pointed above (Wolff 1981, 112ff. and 154). Schäfer likewise criticizes this turn of phrase (Schäfer 2001, 305).


[^s1.76]: But ground in Leibniz, as Hegel says, is not only the *causa efficiens*, but rather the *causa finalis*, the end. Insofar as the concept of end belongs in the logic of the concept, Hegel can here only point to this (GW XI, 293). In the Teleology chapter of the logic of the concept however the principle of ground is not mentioned. Only the following pointer to the connection of the concept of end with content indicates a connection with ground: “In mechanism they [the indifferent determinacies of the objects—T. O.] become it through the mere form of necessity, whereby their content is indifferent […]. In teleology by contrast the content becomes important, because it presupposes a concept, an in and for itself determinate and thereby self-determining, thus has distinguished from the relation of the differences and of their being-determined through one another, from the form, the unity reflected into itself, an in and for itself determinate, thus a content” (GW XII, 156).

[^s1.77]: He however distinguishes “pure mediation in general” of reflection from “the real mediation” of ground (GW XI, 292). The moments of mediation are now in this independent, while that presented only “the movement of nothing through nothing back to itself” (ibid.).

[^s1.78]: See pp. 50ff. in the present work.

[^s1.79]: See the process of arising of existence from ground at the end of the ground chapter (GW XI, 321ff.).

[^s1.80]: “[E]xistence is the reflection of ground into itself; its identity with itself come about in its negation, thus the mediation that has posited itself identical with itself, and thereby is immediacy” (GW XI, 326).
[^s1.81]: Cf. pp. 172ff. in the present work.
[^s1.82]: “It [the contradiction] is for the other by contrast shoved into subjective reflection, which through its relation and comparison first posits it” (GW XI, 286f.).

[^s1.83]: Cf. Henrich 1978, 306f. and 319f.




## For part 3.2

[^s2.1]: For this “older metaphysics” the editors of volume XI in Hegel’s *Collected Works* point to Plato’s *Parmenides* (GW XI, 417: Remark to 17, 9-13). But therewith a still older time is likewise meant and especially Anaxagoras. Hegel mentions this pre-Socratic philosopher at a somewhat later place: “Anaxagoras is praised as the one who first pronounced the thought that the *nous*, thought, is the principle of the world, that the essence of the world is to be determined as thought. He has thereby laid the foundation for an intellectual view of the world, whose pure shape logic must be” (GW XI, 21/XXI, 34). Anaxagoras is for Hegel the author of metaphysical rationalism, even if “his thrust still goes rather into the blue” (Lectures on the History of Philosophy, HW XVIII, 369).

[^s2.2]: See the Introduction in the present work.


[^s2.3]: It seems to me that Hegel’s concept of the thing in itself here is “the transcendental object,” the correlate of pure apperception (*KrV*, A250). But Hegel is indifferent to this, since the thing in itself is determined through the fact that it is distinguished from appearance: It is assumed equally like the transcendental object as indeterminate substrate of perceptions and insofar presupposed.

[^s2.4]: “The exposition however of what alone can be the true method of philosophical science falls into the treatise of logic itself; for the method is the consciousness of the form of the inner self-movement of its content” (GW XXI, 37, also XI, 24). This is however first completed in the chapter on the absolute Idea (GW XII, 236): “The logical Idea thus has itself as the infinite form for its content” (GW XII, 237).

[^s2.5]: When Hegel in the Introduction of the *Science of Logic* equates the separation of content and of form, which traditional logic erroneously makes, with the separation of truth and of certainty (GW XI, 16/XXI, 28), the *Phenomenology of Spirit* undoubtedly hovers before him, which sublates the separation. He also speaks of it as an example of the application of the method, which itself is the object of logic (GW XI, 24f./XXI, 37). We cannot enter upon the much discussed theme concerning the relation of logic and of the *Phenomenology of Spirit* (Fulda 1965 and 1966; Pöggeler 1966, Kozu 1999), but here at least establish that Hegel on the one hand says that the concept of science itself is the result of the *Phenomenology of Spirit* (GW XI, 20/XXI, 32), that this however on the other hand is an application of the method that logic has for its content. The key perhaps lies in the following sentence: “This concept [of science] thus needs (apart from the fact that it comes forth within logic itself) here no justification, because it has received it there [in the Phenomenology]” (ibid. – emphasis by T. O.). From this sentence, which Hegel left unchanged in the second edition, one can conclude that he ascribes both to the *Phenomenology of Spirit* and to the *Science of Logic* the justification or the “bringing-forth” of the concept of science, even if the question still remains open in what kind of connection the two justifications stand.
[^s2.6]: “[T]he content is in general nothing other than such determinations of absolute form; the content posited through it itself, and therefore also appropriate to it” (GW XII, 25).
[^s2.7]: The concepts form, matter and content are in the published Encyclopedias no longer treated separately in the chapter ‘Ground.’ There it runs: “Ground has as yet no in and for itself determinate content” (Enc. 1830, § 122 Remark, similarly Enc. 1817, § 73, Remark).
[^s2.8]: As we shall see later, the concepts form and matter appear in the three published Encyclopedias not in ‘Ground,’ but in ‘The Thing,’ and form and content first in ‘Appearance.’ Also in the *Science of Logic* the concept of matter appears again in the Appearance chapter with the thing.

[^s2.9]: “This circumstance makes difficult the presentation of reflection in general” (GW XI, 295). This points to the fact that essence, which is now thematized as ground, is no simple substrate of the forms, but begins as reflection without substrate, although a sentence in the German language needs a subject. Cf. Rohs 1969, 140.

[^s2.10]: HW XX, 364; cf. Enc. §262.

[^s2.11]: The content of this first part of course does not exhaust itself in this function. Rohs e.g. offers a convincing interpretation according to which Hegel there engages with Schelling’s philosophy of identity (Rohs 1969, 122ff., esp. on the difference of both see loc. cit., 155ff.).

[^s2.12]: See pp. 56ff. in the present work.

[^s2.13]: Even if Hegel distinguishes ground as real mediation from the pure mediation of reflection (GW XI, 292) and with this mentions the imagination, one may not, as Wölfle (1994, 244), reduce pure reflection to the imagination. For Hegel thereby wants only to explain that the related in reflection as self-relation are not yet independent, while they in ground are already real (ibid., cf. GW XI, 295 and 292).

[^s2.14]: Schmidt disputes Wölfle’s thesis that the structure of the logic of reflection of positing, external and determining reflection supplies the principle of articulation for this section on absolute ground (Schmidt 1997, 89). Thereby Schmidt on the one hand overlooks that Wölfle does not reproduce Hegel’s logic, but reconstructs the logic of reflection (Wölfle 1994, see esp. 246). I however hold—against both—fast to the correspondence of absolute ground with the logic of reflection. The articulation of the section corresponds to the three-poled articulation of reflection, even if ground is named as unity of pure and determining reflections: Essence is inseparable from form, which however posits that; with form and matter the moments become external to one another and thus belong to external reflection; content forms as unity of form and matter the inner self-relation. On the other hand it is no evidence against the correspondence that the logic of reflection developmental-historically appears later than the determinations of reflection (Schmidt, 89, footnote 130). Hegel could first later have made independent as logic of reflection the structure underlying the whole logic of essence. The logic of reflection precedes, as also Wölfle’s assertion wants to be understood, not temporally, but systematically (Schmidt 1997, ibid. and 96, footnote 141). Schmidt seems to assume the logic of the determinations of reflection as foundation of the logic of ground. But first this can only be understood from the inner development of reflection, second he would have to ground his assertion developmental-historically, that ‘Absolute Ground’ like ‘The Determinations of Reflection’ each consists of three parts, although ‘The Determinations of Essence’ in the Nuremberg Logic also still in the school years 1810/11 consists of four constituents. Hartmann therefore sees the reflection-logical schema carried through in this whole section “Essence as Reflection in Itself” (Hartmann 1999, 215).

[^s2.15]: “Essence as the related substrate however is the determinate essence; on account of this positedness it essentially has form in it. – The form-determinations by contrast are now the determinations as in essence; it underlies them, as the indeterminate, which in its determination is indifferent against them” (GW XI, 295).

[^s2.16]: “This is the absolute reciprocal relation of form and of essence, that this simple unity of ground and of the grounded, therein however precisely itself is determinate or negative, and distinguishes itself as foundation from form, but so at the same time itself becomes ground and moment of form” (GW XI, 296). Ground has taken over the contradictory character from contradiction. Ground is opposed to the grounded and at the same time the whole of both moments of itself and of the other.

[^s2.17]: Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, 1029a10-20.
[^s2.18]: Cf. Holz 1991, 36.
[^s2.19]: Rohs poses the question: “Is however now […] also the stronger separation of form and matter to be grounded historically? Has one in the tradition of metaphysics held form and matter for something so to be separated from one another?” and again points to Aristotle (Rohs 1967, 158). This however rather holds for Kant.
[^s2.20]: Reisinger also sees in the concept “pure being” matter thematized (Reisinger 1971, 257f.). When Kang finds in the logic of reflection “the correction of the beginning,” he is right. It shows that negation or mediation is ontologically earlier than the immediate (Kang 1999, 160ff.).

[^s2.21]: This sentence occurs twice: once “posits” and “pre-” are emphasized, once only “posits” (GW XI, 297).
[^s2.22]: It strikes one that here in both relations form appears as grammatical subject, once in the active and another time in the passive sense.

[^s2.23]: See pp. 158f. in the present work.
[^s2.24]: Rohs finds there Hegel’s attitude that subjective activity as well as objective movement as it were stems from the I or from form: “[B]oth are indeed nothing different” (Rohs 1969, 172). He also points to the connection of form with the I in Transcendental Idealism (Rohs 1969, 21; cf. 177).
[^s2.25]: Also in the transcendental theory of cognition of the *Critique of Pure Reason* not matter, but sensibility is assumed as passive form. First the understanding, which is at the same time a form, is activity.

[^s2.26]: “Content is further determinate in itself; not only like matter as the indifferent in general, but as the formed matter, so that the determinations of form have a material, indifferent consistence” (GW XI, 302).
[^s2.27]: “There is no content that would step over against form as absolute negativity,—content is only the seeming other of method or of form” (Rohs 1969, 193).

[^s2.28]: Rohs believes “that the presentation of absolute ground and the presentation of form as such laid into it first give the proper grounding of the science of absolute form” (Rohs 1969, 270). I see the presentation of form as the grounding of the science already prepared in the logic of reflection; I agree with him therein that the fundamental role of form has first here become explicit. 
[^s2.29]: Belaval draws attention to the connection of this chapter with the Kantian concepts of reflection ‘the Outer and the Inner.’ According to him the inner is only another word for the simple, and the outer for the manifold (Belaval 1975, 327f.). In this chapter the thing-in-itself is treated as unity, and from the properties of the thing as the manifold matters are derived.

[^s2.30]: This critique Hegel exercises repeatedly, e.g. in the philosophy of nature (Enc. 1830, §261, Remark).
[^s2.31]: “This thing has the two determinations, first to be this, and second to be the Also. The Also is that which occurs in outer intuition as spatial extension; This however, the negative unity, is the punctuality of the thing. The matters are together in the punctuality, and their Also or the extension is everywhere this punctuality; for the Also as thinghood is essentially also determined as negative unity” (GW XI, 338). Cf. *Phenomenology of Spirit*; GW IX, 83f.
[^s2.32]: But contradiction is not distinguished from the two other concepts of reflection, takes no independent section in difference, but is only mentioned in opposition (cf. Enc. 1817, § 71).

[^s2.33]: Cf. §§ 142 and 150.
[^s2.34]: See pp. 52ff. of the present work.

[^s2.35]: We have already remarked that Hegel also in the early Jena period exercises this critique. I quote the place from the *Difference* essay once more, in order to show that Hegel himself already then recognizes the problematic of the concepts of reflection such as form, matter, force, inner and outer: “Kant however has not let fall the difference of an in itself possible and of a real, nor raised the necessary highest Idea of a sensible understanding to reality, and therefore in his natural science insight into the possibility of the fundamental forces is partly in general an impossible, partly such a natural science, for which nature is a matter, i.e. absolutely opposed, not self-determining, can construct only a mechanics; with the poverty of attractive and repulsive forces, it has already made matter too rich; for force is an inner that produces an outer, a self-positing, = I, and such a thing cannot from the purely idealistic standpoint belong to matter; he comprehends matter merely as the objective, that opposed to the I; those forces are for him not only superfluous, but either purely ideal, and then they are no forces; or transcendent” (GW IV, 69f., emphasis by T. O.). In the Remark to “formal ground” of the *Science of Logic* it is also said that the manner of explanation itself concerning matter through force falls into the error that Hegel’s logic of ground rejects: “With the formal business of this manner of explanation from grounds, one hears at the same time again, despite all explaining from the well-known forces and matters, say that we do not ourselves know the inner essence of these forces and matters” (GW XI, 307).

[^s2.36]: Here arises the structure of reflection that later is unfolded in the logic of essence. In the second edition it is further said: “The self-presupposing in general is in One to posit itself as the negative of itself,—repulsion, and what is presupposed therein is the same as the presupposing—attraction” (GW XXI, 164). This obvious introduction of the reflection-logical categories into the logic of being would have very much altered the logic of essence.
[^s2.37]: Also in the Addition to § 98 of the Encyclopedia: “It is Kant to whom the merit belongs of having completed the conception of matter through considering the same as the unity of repulsion and attraction” (Enc. § 98, Addition), and likewise in the Lectures on the History of Philosophy (HW XX, 364).

[^s2.38]: The chapter that in the *Science of Logic* is designated as ‘Essential Relation’ is in the published Encyclopedias simply called “Relation” (Enc. 1830, §135/Enc. 1817, §83).
[^s2.39]: GW XI, 64/XXI, 108.
[^s2.40]: See the fourth chapter of the present work.

[^s2.41]: But also relation is, since it belongs to essence, to be sublated. Hegel says in the last paragraph of essential relation in the Encyclopedia, which constitutes the transition to “Actuality”: “The empty abstractions through which the one identical content is still supposed to be in the relation sublate themselves in the immediate passing-over, the one in the other” (Enc. 1830, §141/Enc. 1817, §89). The category of relation still belongs to the abstractions that Hegel already at the beginning of the doctrine of essence (Enc. §112) has criticized. This also holds for absolute relation; it must finally be sublated into the subjective concept.
[^s2.42]: Concerning the priority of relation before its related it is said in the ground chapter: “A related first comes forth in ground after the moment of sublated reflection” (GW XI, 295, cf. Schmidt 1997, 90 n. and 91; Rohs 1969, 140). Iber calls his commentary on the two first chapters of the logic of essence “Metaphysics of Absolute Relations” (Iber 1990, see especially: 215f.). Cf. Kang 1999, 178f., fn. 70.
[^s2.43]: This could be an answer to the question that Hartmann poses after the second subsection of the chapter: “Why force?” (Hartmann 1999, 242). He points to Kant’s *Foundations*, again however only in a question-sentence (“Does Hegel think of Kant’s *Foundations*?,” loc. cit., 244: Cf. the footnote of the editor to this place).

[^s2.44]: In the second Remark to ‘Pure Quantity’ (GW XI, 113ff./XXI, 179).
[^s2.45]: GW XI, 358. Also in Enc. 1830, § 136 (Enc. 1817, §84).
[^s2.46]: “Its [of essential relation] identity is therefore only a relation, outside which their independence falls; namely into the sides” (GW XI, 354)


[^s2.47]: Wölfle finds here an inappropriateness of the structure of the chapter ‘Essential Relation’ therein that this first relation is only an external one, while the first moment was always supposed to fit positing reflection (Wölfle 1994, 376f.). Thereby he overlooks that Hegel in the Remark to the ‘Relation of the Outer and of the Inner’ says that the direct inversion of inwardness and externality always appears at the beginning (GW XI, 367). He names as example for this the relation of the whole and of the parts (cf. Enc. 1830, §136/Enc. 1817, § 84, Remark). Hartmann sees in this Remark “very fundamental things” (Hartmann 1999, 248f.).

[^s2.48]: From this it results that this relation corresponds to that of the thing and of the matters in the Existence chapter.

[^s2.49]: “The whole and the parts is the thoughtless relation upon which representation first falls; or objectively it is the dead, mechanical aggregate, which indeed has form-determinations whereby the manifold of its independent matter is related in a unity, which however is external to the same” (GW XI, 359). Cf. Enc. 1830 § 136, Remark.

[^s2.50]: That this also constitutes Hegel’s refutation of atomism can be recognized from the fact that he in the Encyclopedia criticizes the mechanical relation on account of “its superficial form in general” (Enc. 1830, § 136, Remark/Enc. 1817, §84, Remark).

[^s2.51]: The connection of this relation with the concepts ‘form and content’ is more clearly to be recognized in the Encyclopedias (Enc. 1830, § 136, Remark/Enc. 1817, §84, Remark). Even if Hegel strongly modifies this passage in the second and third edition, it remains unmistakable.
[^s2.52]: Cf. the Remark of the editors of volume XI of the GW to this place (GW XI, 438).
[^s2.53]: Also in the philosophy of nature (Enc. 1830, § 261 Remark) Hegel, joining to the doctrine of force in the logic, again criticizes the understanding that only externally connects force and matter: “It further belongs to this concept-less reflection to regard the so-called forces as implanted in matter, that is, as originally external to it, so that precisely this identity of time and of space, which hovers before the determination of reflection of force and which in truth constitutes the essence of matter, is posited as something foreign and contingent to it, brought into it from outside” (ibid.).
[^s2.54]: Thereby Hegel points back to the second part of the Appearance chapter “B. The Consistence of the Thing of Matters,” where matter was treated as light-stuff, color-stuff, smell-stuff etc., so that the properties of the thing as matter become independent and the thing thereby dissolves into appearance (GW XI, 334ff.).

[^s2.55]: “In that it is necessary that it [force] itself be this consistence, or that it express itself, so its expression presents itself so that that other steps to it and solicits it” (GW IX, 85).

[^s2.56]: I quote the following place in order to show the connection of essential relation with the Understanding chapter of the Phenomenology, even if the categories have not yet come to logical clarity: “This whole as whole or universal is what constitutes the inner, the play of forces, as reflection of the same into itself” (GW IX, 88).

[^s2.57]: According to Wölfle the relation of the outer and inner like that of the whole and of the parts underlies the logical structure of the whole logic of essence. That is the ground for the fact that he in his reconstruction of the logic of essence omits this subsection. But this omission has as consequence that the connection of Hegel’s logic of essence with Kant’s amphiboly chapter becomes unrecognizable (Wölfle 1994, 380).
[^s2.58]: Cf. Schmidt 1997, 173; Hartmann also points to ‘Form and Content’ (Hartmann 1999, 245f.). He thereby indicates the systematic problem that sharpens itself through the fact that ‘Form and Content’ in the last two Encyclopedias (1827 & 1830) occur as real moments in the Appearance chapter (ibid.). This can be explained not systematically, but only historico-ideologically.

[^s2.59]: “But this thing as simple identity with itself is diverse from its form-determinations, or these are external to it; it is insofar itself an inner that is diverse from its externality” (GW XI, 365).

[^s2.60]: As example Hegel also mentions the customary word-meaning of ‘essence’ e.g. with ‘school-essence,’ ‘newspaper-essence,’ where essence “is a common that is made through external gathering-together of existing objects, insofar as they are without all essential connection, without organization” (GW XI, 367).

[^s2.61]: The following passage of the Encyclopedia directs itself against the Kantian concept of the thing-in-itself: “It is the ordinary error of reflection to take essence as the merely inner” (Enc. 1830, §140/Enc. 1817, § 87, Remark). Moreover another passage from the same paragraph, which Hegel however first added in the third edition, lets us recognize that also this dialectic of the inner and of the outer stands in close connection with the dialectic of conscience of the Phenomenology and of the *Elements of the Philosophy of Right*: “Man, as he is externally i.e. in his actions (indeed not in his only bodily externality), is he inwardly; and if he is only inwardly i.e. only in intentions, dispositions, virtuous, moral etc. and his outer is not identical therewith, so the one is as hollow and empty as the other” (ibid.).
[^s2.62]: Cf. on the critique of Spinoza in Hegel’s logic: Düsing 1984, 228ff. In the Remark to this chapter Hegel mentions the concept of substance of the Leibnizian monad, whereby he compares it with that of Spinoza. Leibniz has in that point the advantage over Spinoza that he has the principle of negativity, that of self-negating, within himself. The “principle of reflection-into-itself” constitutes the “fundamental determination of the monad.” But over against this there is lacking in him the principle of totality, which again Spinoza’s substance includes (GW XI, 378f.).

[^s2.63]: “The dialectical movement of substance through causality and reciprocal action is therefore the immediate genesis of the concept, through which its becoming is presented” (GW XII, 11). Somewhat later, after he has shown this genesis, Hegel says: “This infinite reflection into itself […] is the completion of substance. But this completion is no longer substance itself, but is a higher, the concept, the subject” (GW XII, 14). Joining to this Hegel recalls the already mentioned Remark to “The Absolute.”

[^s2.64]: “Kant has arrived at the place at which the immediate concept of substance is recognized as concept of relation. But this concept of relation requires for its formal-logical intelligibility again a substance” (Liebrucks 1968, 682).

## For conclusion

[^z.1]: Fulda holds that Kant did not make metaphysics impossible, but set new demands upon it (Fulda 1991). Yet his understanding of the Kantian achievement in the history of metaphysics seems inaccurate on several points—particularly when he claims that Kant extended the ontology which pre-Kantian metaphysics had restricted to the domain of beings to the object of judging thought in general (Fulda 1988, 52; idem 1997, 23; idem 1999, 470, 477). Underlying this claim is a misunderstanding of the ontology of Wolffian school metaphysics. Its concept of ontology springs, as we made clear in Chapter 2, from the conviction that logical principles have directly ontological value—that is, value with respect to beings. The *ens* means being formally-logically possible, thus containing no contradiction. The extent of the *ens* equals that of the thinkable. In that respect it is rather Kant who, at least as concerns the ontology of experience, restricted the concept of the possible: for Kant, “possible” no longer means the formally-logically possible, but the transcendentally-logically possible—what agrees with the formal conditions of experience (A218f./B265f.). Its extent is smaller than that of the concept of possibility in traditional ontology. Moreover, Fulda’s claim that nothing was not treated in the ontology of the school contradicts the facts (Fulda 1988, 51); rather, Baumgarten’s ontology begins with the definition of nothing (Baumgarten, *Metaphysica*, §7; also Wolff, *Ontologia*, §§ 57ff.). As we have seen, Kant takes up Something and Nothing at the end of the amphiboly chapter precisely because they were traditionally ontological categories. Furthermore, although Fulda draws attention to the importance of distinguishing categories and concepts of reflection in Kant, a precise analysis of the amphiboly chapter—important for understanding Kant’s concept of ontology—is lacking (Fulda 1988, 56). Fulda thinks that Kant subjectivizes ontology by means of the doctrine of an object of judging thought in general, in which, on Fulda’s view, the Kantian extension of ontology consists. Thereby he aims to locate Hegel’s ontology not in the logic but in the theory of consciousness. But in Kantian transcendental philosophy one must distinguish, on the one hand, the instance of the categories and predicables, which are directly constitutive for appearances and at the same time, as a priori predicates, make up the content of ontology, and, on the other hand, the instance of the cognitive faculties that ground the objectivity of the categories—that is, their ontological contents and the transcendental psychology that grounds them.

[^z.2]: Henrich holds that for Kant the world of things in themselves and the world of experience do not differ fundamentally in their type of ontology. He claims, however, that Kant gave Hegel the starting point for conceiving a new, universal ontology distinct from “natural ontology,” even though Kant’s critique is directed at the latter (Henrich 1993, 188ff.).

[^z.3]: In that respect Wladika’s assumption that Kant destroyed ontology together with “metaphysics as metaphysics” cannot be maintained (Wladika 1995, 1ff. and 133).

[^z.4]: Cf. pp. 20f. of the present study. Cf. Fulda 1988, 51.

[^z.5]: After introducing the table of categories, Kant says: “For the sake of the latter it is therefore still to be noted that the categories, as the true stem-concepts of the pure understanding, also have their equally pure derived concepts, which in a complete system of transcendental philosophy can by no means be passed over, but with whose mere mention I can be content in a merely critical attempt. [Paragraph] Let me be permitted to call these pure but derived concepts of the understanding the predicables of the pure understanding (in contrast to the predicaments). […] Since it is here not a matter for me of the completeness of the system, but only of the principles for a system, I reserve this supplementation for another occupation” (A81f./B107f.). Here the connection with his system-concept in the Transcendental Architectonic is unmistakable. We have already seen that there Kant equates his transcendental philosophy with ontology. He continues: “One can, however, attain this intention fairly well if one takes the ontological textbooks in hand, and e.g. subordinates to the category of causality the predicables of force, of action, of passion; to that of community those of presence, of resistance; to the predicaments of modality those of arising, perishing, of alteration, etc.” (A82/B108). It follows that his complete “transcendental philosophy,” with the system of predicables, was supposed to replace the ontology of school metaphysics.

[^z.6]: Henrich 1993, 206.

[^z.7]: Theunissen formulates this state of affairs thus: “Positively, then, Hegel sublates transcendental philosophy precisely in the destruction of its metaphysical heritage” (Theunissen 1994, 53).

[^z.8]: Schick likewise understands this thesis of the *Phenomenology of Spirit* as an elevation of substance to the self-determining subject (Schick 1994, 19).

[^z.9]: Thus Hartmann’s striking statement: “His [Hegel’s] ontology is an ontology without substrates” (Hartmann 1999, 35).

[^z.10]: “The step to the self-doubling of reflection is an implication of the concept of essence, but not a thought still logically governable by the concept of essence itself. Only the logic of the concept will be able to say how double reflection is properly to be thought—what being-with-itself-in-its-other properly means” (Henrich 1978, 299). In “The Formative Conditions of Dialectic” Henrich explains this state of affairs from Hegel’s monistic approach (Henrich 1982, see esp. 158).

[^z.11]: Düsing 1984, 322 and 339; cf. 244ff.

[^z.12]: To develop is to unfold or unwind what is folded or wrapped within itself—although according to Kluge the word was never used in this sense (Kluge, *Etymologisches Wörterbuch der deutschen Sprache*, 23rd, expanded ed., Berlin/New York 1999, 224).

[^z.13]: “The concept […] is nothing other than I or self-consciousness” (GW XII, 17). For Hegel, Kant’s greatest achievement—alongside the doctrine of the antinomies—lies in the Transcendental Deduction and its theory of pure apperception: “It belongs among the deepest and most correct insights to be found in the Critique of Reason that the unity constituting the essence of the concept is cognized as the original-synthetic unity of apperception, as the unity of the I think, or of self-consciousness” (GW XII, 17f.).

[^z.14]: “The principles, by contrast, of the objective determination of representations are to be derived alone from the principle of the transcendental unity of apperception. Through the categories, which are these objective determinations, the manifold of given representations is so determined that it is brought to the unity of consciousness” (GW XII, 18).

[^z.15]: *KrV* A345f./B403f.

[^z.16]: “When in the ordinary manner one speaks of the understanding that I have, one understands thereby a faculty or property that stands to the I as the property of a thing stands to the thing itself—an indeterminate substrate that is not the true ground and determining of its property. According to this representation I have concepts and the concept as I also have a coat, color, and other external properties” (GW XII, 17). Hegel continues: “Kant went beyond this external relation of the understanding as the faculty of concepts, and of the concepts themselves, to the I” (ibid.). With Kant, then, he criticizes the psychology of school metaphysics, whose concept of the soul he disparagingly calls a “soul-thing” (e.g. GW XII, 197; Enc. 1830, § 34, § 125; cf. Lecture X, 25f., 47f. and XI, 30f.).

[^z.17]: E.g. GW XI, 330; also the Perception chapter of the *Phenomenology*; Enc. 1830, § 125; Lecture XIII, 9.

[^z.18]: “Through this I, or He, or It (the thing), which thinks, nothing further is now represented than a transcendental subject of thoughts = x, which is cognized only through the thoughts that are its predicates, and of which we, separately, can never have the least concept; around which we therefore turn in a constant circle, since we must already make use of its representation whenever we are to judge anything of it—an inconvenience that cannot be separated from this” (*KrV*, A346/B404). Hegel investigates—almost quoting—precisely this passage from the *Critique of Pure Reason* (GW XI, 193).

[^z.19]: “[T]he experience of the complained-of inconvenience is itself the empirical fact in which the untruth of that abstraction pronounces itself” (GW XII, 195).
